# Introduction

## Purpose

"What is a human being?" is a question that psychology continues to ask today with the same fascination, immediacy, and urgency that animated philosophers centuries ago. Who are we really, how do we engage the rest of the world, and how do we come to know what we know and that we know?

During the 20th century, developmental theorists and researchers have offered a powerful response to these questions, which goes to the heart of epistemological and educational issues. The response of constructivist developmental theory generally is that persons move through qualitatively different ways of knowing who they are, how the world works, and how they know what they know.

During the early epochs of a person's life, the transformations from one way of knowing to another are experienced by the vast majority of people, but the changes are neither deliberate nor conscious at the time. During the college years and later in adulthood, a small minority of people continues to develop still more complex, qualitatively different ways of knowing themselves and the world. At this point, the changes are increasingly more voluntary, deliberate, and conscious, generated in part by the explicit asking of these very questions, and the conscious observation of how one responds to them.

There is great controversy whether such later stages of self-development even exist. This controversy arises because few people undergo these changes, mature adults have been studied far less than children and adolescents, and asking these questions explicitly generates a critique of commonly accepted views of what it means to be a mature adult. Paradoxically, these rarer perspectives on life produce types of knowledge that are incomprehensible to those who have not themselves asked the necessary epistemological questions and experienced the self-transformations occurring in this inquiry process.

However, if these relatively rare persons who do undertake the deliberate inquiry into how we create, maintain, and transform meaning are, in effect, uncovering a valid perspective on who we might become, how we could interact with the world, and how we come to know this, then they can help us understand the normative direction and potential of human learning and development. The very possibility that this might be so makes documenting this process with increasing accuracy significant.

## Purpose of the Study

This study will attempt to create better theory at these less understood levels of adult development. The research has two separate but related goals. It will test whether the highest stage in a widely used ego development theory reflects what we currently know about high-end development and self-understanding. Moreover, it will test whether the existing measurement tool, a specific sentence completion test, adequately describes this area of development. By charting and validating the characteristics and rare qualities of individuals at the highest ego stages, this study hopes to make a substantive contribution to the field of positive adult development. Indeed, it hopes to answer the age-old question of the nature of humanity from the perspective of constructivist developmental psychology.

Early pioneers in postulating and documenting adult mental development beyond early adulthood into mature age used the psychological concept of "ego" as the underlying principle in personality organization that develops and generates coherent meaning throughout life. The ego is said to orchestrate how we perceive inner and outer reality and to coordinate affect, thought, and action.

Conventional ego development theory describes stages of human mental development from childhood to adulthood, which occur mostly unconsciously and as a result of maturation and pervasive cultural conditioning. Conventional stages describe forms of meaning making that seem required for adults to function in the roles of modern societies.

Postconventional ego development, on the other hand, describes the rarer stages of meaning making in which some adults begin to deliberately and consciously wrestle with culturally programmed responses to life. They begin to examine previously taken-for-granted assumptions and explore the fundamental questions about knowing and reality.

The sentence completion test is a measure that is meant to assess people's level of ego development or their ego stage. The theory currently describes three conventional adult stages and three stages beyond these. Two of the postconventional ego stages, the Individualistic and the Autonomous, are fairly well researched and recognized by many other theoreticians, albeit the stages are given different names. The most highly developed ego stage, the Integrated, on the other hand, is rarely described in the literature and ill-defined by the original framework developers' own admission. It attempts to portray self-actualized persons who have permanent, objective, and integrated, yet highly complex self-identities. Because of the emphasis on permanence and stability, the theory cannot account for people who develop a dynamic, fluid self-experience and who question the very assumption of the permanent self and object world. Such themes do appear on sentence completion tests and originally prompted this research into the upper end of ego development theory.

This research focuses on how people's meaning making develops after they have reached the penultimate or Autonomous stage. It constitutes a needed step in a research effort begun decades ago to study mature ego development in response to perceived problems at the high-end of the theory and the measurement instrument.

Overall, the goal of this study is to put the previously suggested reinterpretation of high-end ego development theory and measurement to the test. First, it will explore whether defining postautonomous ego development differently from the original framework allows one to understand high-stage sentence completions and protocols that the original theory cannot explain. Second, it will test empirically whether others can reliably score postautonomous completions to the test with a set of newly created postautonomous scoring categories.

This testing will be accomplished in several steps. First, it will explore the notion that postconventional ego development is characterized by an increasing awareness of the constructed nature of knowledge as well as a concomitant, step-wise deconstruction of the assumptions underlying conventional views of reality and the self. It will assess to what extent other sources in the developmental literature use similar notions to describe mature cognition. It will especially examine theories that postulate postautonomous perspectives in constructivist developmental theory and in transpersonal psychology to analyze what agreement exists between the thematic categories for identifying postautonomous ego stages and the characteristics given in these theories. In effect, it will be seeking to show the content validity of these categories. In the process of examining others' conceptions of high stage development, it will also expand and enhance the definition and description of the categories.

Second, it will test empirically whether these categories are helpful for scoring highest stage sentence completions and protocols by subjecting them to a thorough multiple analysis by others. The database used consisted of 1100 relatively rare postautonomous sentence completions and 476 whole high-end protocols systematically culled from over 4400 protocols in 17 years of scoring tests. The size of this database of high-end material allowed the data to be divided into several subsets for different kinds of analysis. What is more, it provided the database for the kind of analysis that the original framework developers felt could not be done.

To begin, it will ascertain to what degree an expert rater will concur that high-stage responses from a random set of late-conventional responses and above are hard to score with the existing manual. The expert rater will next use the new categories to determine whether they make a difference in the outcome. The rater will also provide feedback about the clarity and completeness of the proposed categories and help optimize the category descriptions through dialogue.

Following that, a team of general raters will score randomly selected data from both data sets (individual completions and whole protocols) as a reliability test. It will report interrater agreement and Cronbach's alpha coefficient. If the outcome warrants, it will determine new cut-off numbers for rating postautonomous protocols.

Finally, it will discuss both the theoretical and empirical results of this study, outline threats to validity, and suggest further research directions.

It is hoped that this research will make several useful contributions to psychology: (a) It will make clear the underlying logic of postconventional adult development including two stages beyond the Autonomous stage. (b) It will provide a concise set of scoring categories for distinguishing between the two proposed postautonomous stages as well as for scoring high-end protocols and sentence completions.

It seems important for educational psychology to have a model of adult ego development consistent with late 20th century conceptions of advanced cognitive growth and integration. Such a model may help us to distinguish and recognize similarities among the mature qualities and reality perspectives found across cultures. In addition, the new thematic distinctions for analyzing high-stage material could be useful to researchers who study mature development in other disciplines.

Last, this research—by exploring the border area between personal and transpersonal development—might move forward an integration of Eastern and Western conceptions of human growth.

## The Gift of Wonderful Ideas

Rare indeed are the moments when one is given a vision, an insight, or a fabulous idea that transforms one's outlook on life in an instant of recognition. There are moments that stand out especially and are reported here because they were pivotal in mental growth and eventually led to the topic of this research. The first great idea was encountered during adolescence, and the second during a course in adult development in mid-thirties.

During adolescence, a language teacher lectured on philosophical thoughts. The teacher was explaining what the philosopher meant when answering the question "What is man?" by responding: "He is nothing compared to infinity and an infinity compared to nothingness." This teacher helped to understand the ideas by translating them into analogous mathematical equations:

1 = person; 0 = nothingness; ∞ = infinity.

Compared to the infinite, man is nothing:
1 / ∞ = 0

Compared to nothingness, man is an infinity:
1 / 0 = ∞


---


# The Study of Human Beings

## Introduction to the Human Condition

Despairing about the many contradictions in personal experience, one might resonate with a description of the human condition that places human experience at a fulcrum, vacillating between greatness and unworthiness. This perspective finds comfort in the idea that humans have the courage to face their paradoxical position in nature and embrace their destiny. This realization often serves as a momentous psychological breakthrough, relating the self-absorbed search for a meaningful interpretation of personal experience to humanity's broader quest for self-understanding. 

An interdisciplinary approach to learning—linking ideas in literature to abstract symbol manipulation in mathematics, and interconnecting philology, philosophy, literature, and mathematics—can help individuals grasp the subtler connections and ambiguities of life. This approach guides restless minds to consider personal experience within broader human and historical contexts, inspiring further study in fields like linguistics and semantics, the study of the meanings of words.

## Ego Development Theory

Ego development theory offers a coherent explanation of human behavior, helping individuals map their own perceptions, reactions, feelings, and thoughts. Its emphasis on subtle differences in language aligns well with the study of semantics. The theory is based on the notion that language is constitutive of reality. It posits that what we say and how we say it indicates how we frame reality. Through our utterances, we reveal what we know that we know and notice, as well as what we choose to ignore or unconsciously leave out. Most interestingly, it can also reveal what does not appear on our mental screen at all, illuminating what is not in our conception of the world and what we do not know we do not know.

This theory integrates many strands of psychology into a coherent approach that makes both rational and intuitive sense. Becoming skilled at scoring sentence completion tests (SCT) reveals that some high-stage responses, although meaningful, do not fit the standard theory or manuals. This discrepancy prompts the systematic collection of high-end protocols and responses to understand why it exists and how to make sense of it. Some theorists have grave doubts that one can make better theory at the upper end, and some recent manuals practically eliminate the highest, integrated stage.

Advocating the constructivist developmental perspective as a meaningful approach to answering the question of "what is human?" and being an expert scorer of the measurement instrument are central to this inquiry. The goal is to test conjectures about high-stage ego development and how to measure it.

## The Study of Human Beings

The basic questions of how we are similar as a species, and how we as individuals and groups differ from each other, continue to be explored in many disciplines of science (biology, anthropology, sociology, philosophy, medicine, and psychology). Within psychology, the questions of similarity and difference have been addressed in many subdisciplines (evolutionary, abnormal, personality, and developmental). The developmental subdiscipline, in turn, has been approached from different angles (such as biological, cognitive, constructivist, and psychopathological).

Whether there are human universals and, if so, what aspects of being human would come under this rubric are among the most debated questions throughout history, with different emphasis placed on either similarities or differences. One area in which universals have been sought throughout Western history is in the mental sphere. Early philosophers identified the concepts of identity, difference, change, and stability as basic categories of mind. Later philosophers expanded the list, trying to define the building blocks of conceptual space, adding categories such as time, place, quantity, quality, and causality.

Three other universals are proposed as essential to understanding human development:
1. Humans have a need for orientation and coherent meaning.
2. Humans tend towards both increasing differentiation (individuation) and greater integration (communion).
3. All humans partake in the language habit.

### The Need for Orientation and Coherent Meaning

From cannibalistic burial rituals to the unschooled mind's everyday theories about why a thrown penny returns to the ground, to the most sophisticated postmodern arguments about why we have no leg to stand on in looking for universals in meaning making, all are attempts at explaining the world and locating ourselves within a larger context. The fundamental need for structuring and explaining experience is undeniably and universally human, no matter what shape it takes. Asserting that there is no viable position from which to judge is, itself, a definite position.

Meaning making is seen a priori as an innate, defining characteristic of human beings. At the same time, researching ego development explores the many ways human beings differ in how they make sense of their experience. Understanding what matters to individuals—what they are able to perceive and conceive of, what is outside their awareness, and how conscious they are of their own meaning-making activity and its limits—is a central question.

### The Balance Between Autonomy and Homonomy

The second universal is the human need to fulfill the double propensity towards autonomy (differentiation, independence, mastery) and homonomy (integration, participation, belonging). Human development can be conceptualized as the balance between these two opposite needs, negotiated differently by different people. This motion can be described in terms of what one can take as object and what one is subject to at each stage, or in terms of what one is separating from and what one is integrating toward. The balance between autonomy and homonomy is negotiated differently among individuals within a culture and at different junctures in life. Whole cultures also differ in how they allocate more value to either individualism or connection. Yet, the need to relate and to individuate is universal.

### The Capacity for Language

The capacity for language, or symbolic representation of experience, is a third universal human characteristic. There is no known culture today without a language. Natural language use and symbol use are prerequisites for participating in human social life and are essential to survival. The measurement tool for ego development is directly based on the assumed relationship between language and meaning system. Test manuals use subtle distinctions in verbal expression to infer differences in how people construe reality.

Understanding the nature and function of language, as well as its innate roots, is paramount in understanding ego development theory and its measurement instrument. A mindful appreciation of the place of language in our experience is an important clue to mature ego development, because language permeates all facets of life and intellectual inquiry. Knowing how language influences what we notice or ignore, what we hold dear or abhor, and what we take for real or deem outside the limits of relevant possibilities, adds an important dimension to our understanding of ourselves and the human condition.

## The Centrality of Language in Ego Development Theory and Measurement

Language is so habitual an aspect of our behavior that we tend to forget how complex it is and how it structures our awareness. Language should be understood in the broadest sense, as symbolic codification or representation of experience in all its varied forms, including gestural, oral, written, and sign language; figurative representations; logic, mathematical, and other abstract codes. Without symbolic representation, today's global reality of instant information exchange could not exist.

All systems of notation or symbolic representation are shorthand for transmitting knowledge and experience among people. To be understood, there must be some consensus on how a symbol relates to the thing symbolized, and this knowledge must be shared. Natural language is the prototypical system of symbolization. Only natural language is universally used to talk about, analyze, reflect upon, and communicate about itself, and about all other symbol systems, as well as about experiences from nonsymbolically mediated states of mind.

The view of language presented here integrates ideas from general semantics, the sociology of knowledge, anthropology, and nativist approaches. While some linguists support the notion that language is an inborn faculty, sociologists elaborate on the human context necessary to activate language and its power in shaping our understanding of reality.

Human babies lack the necessary biological means to survive after birth without a nurturing human environment. The period during which children develop minimal self-sufficiency through interactions with others is also the period during which the "self" is formed. Thus, the individual self cannot be understood apart from the particular social and linguistic contexts in which it was shaped. Children are molded to become members of their group from the day they are born through relentless modeling of behavior and language. What is to be considered important and real in everyday life is always transmitted through language and has coercive power over the child. The child internalizes the world of significant others as the only existent and conceivable world.

For example, a toddler calling a cat a "doggie" is subtly corrected until they learn to distinguish dogs from other four-legged animals. By about age five, children acquire a complex linguistic system adequate to manipulate abstract concepts, such as time and emotions, and to use symbolic sound codifications to interact with others not present. They will also correct a younger sibling who exhibits the dog-cat confusion. By this time, labels appear to children as inherent in the nature of things. Any notion of their conventionality or arbitrariness is lost.

Each culture divides the original, unstructured continuum in different ways according to its specific needs, development, imagination, and context. With every speech act, the members of a language community mutually reinforce the shared conceptual map or worldview for each other. By the time we are in school, we are so deeply habituated into language that we cannot imagine life without it. We generally can remember neither our own preverbal existence nor how we acquired our mother tongue. For most people, the language habit remains unconscious and automatic throughout their lives.

We use language to accomplish a wealth of transactions, from the practical to the fantastic, from the ordinary to the sublime. We use language to identify concrete objects and events around us and to tell the stories of our group and of ourselves. At all times, we use a shared language to assure ourselves and each other of the reality and meaningfulness of our existence. Human existence without language seems utterly unimaginable.

Language is likely the most powerful means by which we are socialized into a specific overarching view of reality. Its social function is to serve as shorthand for transmitting knowledge and experience among its speakers and from generation to generation. For this endeavor to work, there must be some consensus among speakers of how a construct or symbol relates to the thing it symbolizes, and this relationship must be learned for participation in any social group.

Fortunately, the language habit is innate. As long as children's potential for language is activated through regular exposure and training at the maturationally appropriate age, they acquire a specific language and hence will structure experience via its grammar and concepts. Developing more sophisticated language then becomes a central basis for continuing development within a given speech community. The cognitive growth that accompanies children's physical maturation can be described as a process of continuous acquisition of finer and finer symbolic representations, elaborations, and distinctions. This also includes organizing existing concepts into higher orders of abstraction. The more evolved and mature, the more territories of experience become available for symbolic representation and cognitive manipulation.

Just as different cultures segment the continuum in different ways, so different individuals perceive a slightly different segment of the stimuli continuum, depending on the microstructure of their specific bodies (including genetic variables) and their maturational development. They accept stimuli for processing both according to their innate general human makeup, their unique personal constitution, and according to the linguistic screens and limitations imposed on them by their culture.

It seems reasonable to conclude that in order to grow into adults, human beings are initially dependent on a culturally accepted orientation and definition of what is real in life. Once a coherent view of reality is absorbed, it is constitutive of experience and filters out rivaling possibilities. We carry around in our heads a conceptual map of the world, a guidebook to rightness and wrongness, ugliness and beauty, value and worthlessness. Such mental maps seem indispensable because they provide us with rules and scripts for action, and explanations of why things are the way they are. Moreover, they often rationalize, justify, and sometimes even sanctify our existence.

Becoming aware of one's acculturation marks a turning point in development. Once individuals understand that they are interpreters of reality, that they contribute their own meaning to external and internal facts, and that their insight may differ markedly from generally held definitions of what things mean, their explanatory universe has changed dramatically. They have become aware of the inevitability of cultural conditioning and programming. In a step beyond, people can become aware of the deeper bias inherent in the construction and reification of the object world via language. This may include an ever-growing awareness of the processes and mechanisms of thinking and meaning-making themselves.

The experience of the artificiality of such concepts as the "ego" occurs at the most differentiated stages of development. Once one can entertain the idea that the universe is one interconnected whole without boundaries, one has to face one's intrinsic tendency to organize experience—one's need to make cosmos out of chaos—by attributing order and relevance to it. Seeking a way of experiencing and meaning making that is direct, without the filter of language, can become a central existential quest. Ego-development theory, if it is to be a viable explanatory principle, must be able to account for this extraordinary phenomenon.

Language as verbal behavior is a crucial aspect of sentence completion tests. It is indisputable that, by the time we are adults, employing symbolic representations is such an automatic aspect of our behavior that we completely depend on doing so for making sense of the world. Concepts are used as tools to solidify our world and ourselves. If a world of nameable things exists, then 'I' as one of the nameable things exist as well. A pernicious feature of the modern Western configuration of sense of self is that it tends to obscure its historical origins and its interpretation-dependent nature. Thus it perpetuates the illusion that we have selves as we have eyes, hearts, or livers, and makes it very hard to question this notion.

Western psychologists are only now beginning to look into the costs associated with our near total reliance on language as a medium. A view heard increasingly often is that we create symbolic systems of meaning and then forget that they are our creations, and also that language poses a unique problem of analysis by its being both the object and the agent of its study.

There seems to be a growing interdisciplinary consensus that the maps and theories we make about reality by rational means are always reductionist and partial. They can never grasp the whole, intricate unity of the living universe of which we are an inextricable part. The very existence of multiple, sometimes conflicting maps and scientific explanations of human nature helps to point out the relative position and limitation of each of them.

No matter how much we learn and know about the intricate workings of our body-minds through objective, modern science, our existence as biological organisms is impermanent. Our edifices of knowledge are impermanent. They are but intimations of a deeper reality that may not ever be fully accessed empirically and by merely rational means. The reality outside the cave is the real territory, and we must be warned about confusing our conceptual maps with the reality outside. Language is arguably the chief means by which we create such conceptual maps of reality. These maps, in turn, serve as a powerful tool to create a sense of permanence and identity and, by the same token, to ward off our fears of impermanence and insignificance. Being cognizant of this possibility creates an ineluctable dilemma.

Fascination with how we interpret our experience, how we make sense of these existential dilemmas, and how much awareness we have about our own meaning making naturally leads towards constructivist developmental psychology, a branch of psychology that elevates meaning making and the differences among people in this vital activity to a central position.

## Positive Lifespan Development

There are differences in emphasis even within a meaning-centered psychology. Much psychological research in the last century has focused on human suffering and what causes people to fail at making adequate sense of their lives. Only more recently has there been a resurgence of interest into what makes some people navigate the straits of life with skill, success, and grace.

The types and severity of psychopathology, including the failure to create a coherent sense of self, have been classified and described in diagnostic manuals. While psychiatry's aim is mainly to help those with severe pathologies to live more tolerable and safe lives, helping people create more adaptive narratives for their experience has been the domain of much psychotherapy.

On the other hand, we can also focus on what innate proclivities, favorable circumstances, and external supports enhance the capacity of individuals to synthesize a self-supporting story from the myriad, constantly changing challenges and demands of life and to live a life full of vigor and forward motion. The synthetic function of the ego inspires the study of positive adult development, resilience, and wisdom. Narrative coherence may be more important than actual historical truth in people's ability to find meaning in life. The relationship between objective science and the humanities—and even religion—may be analogous: the story we tell about the meaning of a given scientific fact perhaps weighs more heavily for our survival than its absolute truth.

Within the last decade, there has been a growing trend among objective, empirical scientists to advocate a more integrative, cross-disciplinary approach toward the major questions in life, including the question of human nature. Evolution as a scientific explanatory principle has been described as the grandest myth about the universe there ever was, bringing narration and meaning making back into the hard sciences as a basic aspect of human activity.

Experts in Eastern spiritual traditions also articulate a need to bridge science and religion. The objective methods of science for discovering truth and the religious function of generating meaning based on subjective, inner awareness are not only compatible, but complementary. There is an urge for reconciliation among science, the humanities, and religion, an integration that honors all their methods of data collection, analysis, and verification.

Yet long before modern science rediscovered the centrality of a coherent narrative, the peoples of the world created myths about the universe and how they fit into it. These myths inevitably explain where human beings come from (origin), who they are (in relation to the rest of creation), why they are the way they are (nature), why they are here (purpose), and where they are going hereafter. The ancients also had stories about the meaning of the human lifespan.

### Types of Lifespan Theory

The practice of dividing the human lifespan into several phases according to age is as old as the riddle posed by the Sphinx to Oedipus: "What goes first on four legs, then on two, and then on three?" "Man," Oedipus rejoined, thereby describing the simplest theory of lifespan development. Yet noticing the changes in locomotion is but one way of describing the visible changes. Today we are likely to have a more complex, psychological interpretation of the riddle. We associate the crawler with early dependency and animal-like instinctual behavior, the two-legged being with real personhood, higher mental functions, free will and control, and the three-legged creature negatively, with the decline of these capacities, as well as positively, with turning inward and greater wisdom.

Psychological theories on what develops and how growth occurs, especially in children, have proliferated. This is naturally so because the changes are much more visible during the growing-up years. Once one is grown, the changes and adaptations are subtler and become noticeable only after longer stretches of time. However, interest in the adult lifespan has reawakened and, as a result, there are several branches of psychology that specifically deal with positive adult development. There are three types of theories most relevant to this inquiry.

Statistical means have allowed researchers to reduce the seemingly endless ways of characterizing human behavior into a relatively small number of dimensions or factors. Of these more scientific trait theories, the five-factor model is probably the most rigorously tested. The Five-Factor Model purports that five factors—Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness to experience, Agreeableness, and Conscientiousness, derived from factor analysis—are sufficient to account for most personality differences in adulthood.

Some researchers feel that their five-factor model and ego development theory—and the respective methods—ought to be complementary. It stands to reason that given a particular ego stage, some important differences among subjects might be explained by personality traits, and conversely, that individuals with similar personality profiles might still differ significantly based on how they frame their experience (ego development). The ego has been described as "the master trait," second only to intelligence in accounting for human variability. Indeed, it seems that differences in the ego construct are more predictive of behavior in a wider variety of circumstances than differences in other proposed personality traits.

To characterize the ego as a trait, however, is peculiar and may be a result of initial attempts to distinguish this approach from the more common type and trait classifications. Most developmental theorists would not characterize the ego as a trait, but as a psychic organizing function or the synthetic principle in meaning making. The psychological construct of the "ego" is at the core of creating and maintaining a coherent world view. The ego is defined as the autonomous drive towards meaning making. The ego represents the striving of human beings to understand themselves and the world they live in by fitting new experiences into their current meaning system. Overall, the ego labors mightily to create and maintain coherence and vigorously defends against dissonant stimuli. Yet, with increasing ego development, immature defenses give way to more mature ones.

When the ego fails in its mandate to create meaning, humans suffer anxiety, which is the dread of non-being or a basic sense of meaninglessness. Thus the ego is at the very core of meaning making, constantly integrating and absorbing experience and classifying and finding order in everything that touches the individual both externally and internally.

## Stage Theory

Historically, stage models of mental growth or genetic epistemology were proposed early on. The "genetic" or developmental approach outlined how children's reasoning about moral situations changes in foreseeable ways from early childhood through adolescence. These insights were developed by carefully exploring how children of increasing age made sense of their experience in many domains. Children were given practical problems to solve, their actions were watched, and they were asked for explanations of why they did what they did. At other times, they were presented with everyday natural phenomena and concepts, and probed for what they meant by what they said. Responses and observations were recorded and analyzed for common patterns and recurring shifts in different age groups.

Based on the evidence gathered, it was concluded that children's reasoning was not merely an incorrect version of adult thought, but that it was coherent and meaningful in its own right. Reasoning changed in terms of quality over time, and the changes were predictable and deducible from children's actions and explanations. Four major stages of increasing differentiation and integration in the formation of adult cognition were described. This theory covers the first two tiers of the development of meaning making and reasoning. In large strokes, children's thought develops sequentially from sensory-motor to magical thinking through concrete and abstract thought to formal reasoning. The highest form of cognitive integration observed was called formal operations.

As children grow up, they gradually learn to think more abstractly about their experiences. They learn to connect thoughts, feelings, and wishes to specific actions and from such actions to their consequences. That is, people learn to understand time in a linear fashion. Events follow each other in a sequence from past to present to future and are viewed as a causal chain. In the West, reality is treated as an assemblage of various types of permanent, well-defined objects with inherent properties. These are arranged in closed systems, clearly separated as distinct entities from each other and from the observer. Elaborate procedures and heuristics (the scientific arsenal of methods) are employed to experiment with, measure, analyze, and figure out the laws that govern their behavior. If something does not work according to expectations, the problem space is "reduced" into its component parts (independent variables). Then the culprit variable is isolated, so that a solution can be found by using an appropriate existing procedure or by devising a new one based on established scientific principles. There is even an "objective, scientific" writing style which effectively mirrors the separation (or obscures the relationship) between experimenter-observer and that which is being experimented on and observed.

It was projected that people reach formal operations by early adulthood. This stage was seen as the prototype of mature reasoning, necessary for the functioning of modern society. Indeed, formal operations figure prominently in the ideology and education of the West. Adults with this mind frame, also referred to as the modern mind, think of themselves as separate, independent selves, in control of and responsible for their actions. They make decisions based on rational analysis and are actively engaged in orchestrating and controlling the movement of their lives. It is no exaggeration to say that people with a formal operations worldview quite often look at life itself as a task to be accomplished or a technical problem to be solved. In response to this need, there is a wealth of information available about how to solve any problem or achieve any goal. Guides to the five ways of stopping smoking or the twelve steps to enlightenment are readily available.

In general, whenever we encounter a new phenomenon, we describe and categorize it and try to fit it into our existing explanations. If it does not fit, we create explanations for its "deviant" behavior that make it fit into our existing way of knowing. The formal operational reasoner is excellent at finding explanations for any state of affairs. In addition, they can predict what is likely to happen next and manipulate the components to achieve specific outcomes, extrapolating from the known. Conscious analysis of causes and consequences, deliberate choice of methods, and rational decision-making are hallmarks of meaning making at the formal operational or Conscientious stage.

The human actor in this drama sees himself as a clearly separate self-contained system and other human beings as such systems in their own right. In the positive case, this assures every one a kind of integrity and independence; in the less favorable circumstance, it reduces individuals to objects themselves, isolated and at the mercy of being objectified by others.

The fascinating aspect of formal operations is that it trusts that a rational, scientific approach will be adequate to answer all questions, and that what appears as a mystery now, will turn out to be explainable eventually. This positivistic, progressive stance has produced the industrial revolution and evolved into the current global technology, which has solved many dire problems of humanity and created technological wonders unimaginable fifty years ago.

### Methods

One major methodological contribution to developmental psychology is recording responses to the same set of stimuli from many subjects at different ages in order to (a) find common patterns, (b) define these patterns, and next (c) test their predictive power in describing behavior. This approach uses responses to verbal stimuli from many individuals, looks for distinctive patterns, and attempts to order them in a meaningful way along a developmental continuum from lesser to greater complexity, coherence, and higher order integration.

It was also pointed out early on that people as organisms do not merely react to the environment, but that they seek out experiences, actively experiment with them, and interpret them: that is, they construct their meaning. These ideas, exemplary studies, and empirical method were pivotal in spawning the constructive developmental approach in psychology and what is sometimes referred to as the study of positive adult development.

In addition, there was cognizance of the fundamental role of language in the construction of meaning and mastery of life. Specifically, it was observed that the external object world is constructed early in development and then (mis)taken as preexisting and external. When language and thought begin, the neonate is for all practical purposes but one element or entity among others in a universe that he has gradually constructed for himself, and which, hereafter he will experience as external to himself.

However, the construction of the external object world is only the beginning of the "conquering" of reality through symbolic representation. Once in motion, the process of reification or objectification continues unconsciously and covers more and more abstract aspects of experience. Determining the level of awareness people have in regards to the function of language in meaning making may itself be one of the indices of their level of cognitive development. Because the insight into the constitutive role of language in reality perception is such a pivotal aspect of the interpretation of development based on evidence from late-stage sentence completions, the main ideas relevant to such a perspective are summarized below.

### Social Construction of Reality

The idea that language is constitutive of experience is especially elaborated in the literature on the social construction of reality. It also emphasizes the child's mastery of the object world through the acquisition of language. Studies show that linguistic classifying of experience is a universal aspect of human nature. Mental growth can be described as the continuous acquisition of subtler distinctions among concepts, their clustering into complex matrices, and their ordering into hierarchical systems of meaning.

Every culture transmits its own basic assumptions about reality and the relationship between humans and their environment through its language, symbols, and behavioral patterns. Therefore, the acquisition of a language seems absolutely necessary for cognitive growth beyond the infant state to occur. In order to become adult participants in life, children must learn the specific notions about reality of their group, starting from the initial concrete distinctions to the more general and varying types of relationships, role functions, and abstract theories available in the larger society. What is most interesting is that the constructed nature of everyday reality remains hidden from consciousness for most people throughout their lives. The reality created by one's language is taken for granted as the only existent and conceivable world.

By the time children go to school, they can deal with ordinary experience in the summary fashion we all use: They respond to the ever-changing sensory information in terms of the predictable characteristics and properties of the physical world as transmitted to them through the concepts and grammar of their culture. Moreover, once we have internalized these notions about how the world is, we cannot conceive of it in any other way. It is self-evident that the world is as we perceive it. Specific labels appear as inherent in the nature of things and thus become automatic, objective, and permanent. Any notion that the distinctions among things may be arbitrary and that they have been "learned" is veiled by the overwhelming reinforcement of everyday experience. Thus for most people, the language they use indeed mirrors the reality they perceive as existing independent of their descriptions. In other words, to them, the map is the territory, and they come by this notion naturally in the course of growing up in their respective cultures.

### Summary of Stage Theory

The worldview of formal operations is referred to as Newtonian, as scientific and, with positivistic overtones, as a progressive or "modern" stance. People know reality in other words, a different epistemology, or a different worldview. Central to this model is the claim that the stage sequence is unidirectional and that the stages constitute hierarchical integrations. Development is unidirectional because the sequence of stages is posited to be the same for all people. It evolves from the least differentiated to ever more differentiated ways of knowing and relating to the world. It moves from simple to complex in regard to all possible contents. It is hierarchical because each subsequent stage constitutes a transformation of the previous way of interpreting reality.

A new stage integrates the material or content of the previous one as a special case, that is, as an element into its more inclusive meaning system. Each stage is thus a part/whole. It is a whole in its own right, as well as part of a bigger, more expansive system of understanding. This involves "orders of consciousness," or the increasing ability to take as object that which one previously could only construe and experience subjectively. What we were unconsciously embedded in or subject to at one stage, becomes an object that we can consciously organize and reflect upon at the next higher stage. In the most global sense, development can be described as the gradual unfolding of people's capacity to embrace ever-vaster mental horizons and to plumb ever-greater depths of the heart.

## Postconventional Development Theory

### Postconventional Constructivist Models of Human Development

Beginning with the late Sixties, several developmental theorists started to challenge the highest stage of previous models. Looking at formal operations as a step in development rather than its terminus, they suggested that positive development and cognitive differentiation beyond formal operations could occur throughout life. They claimed that (a) mental development does not necessarily stop upon becoming an adult—although it often does—and that (b) formal operations is not the final or the highest order integration possible. Based on their research with adults, they claimed that some people continue to transform their views of reality throughout the lifespan into even more complex and inclusive matrices of reasoning and understanding than those reflected in formal operations. Because these theories expanded the invariant hierarchical sequence upwards, they are sometimes called post-Piagetian, even though the terms postformal or postconventional are more often used.

Postformal theories emphasize contextual and process-oriented forms of knowing, and increasingly turn attention to people's inner life. They explore meaning making not only in terms of its mechanics, but also in terms of its human valence and experience. Some theories distinguish between understanding what is merely rationally defensible and logically consistent from what is perceived as meaningful or wise in mature living. To underline this distinction, the term "postformal" is restricted to theories of cognition that describe more complex, higher-order forms of logical analysis and reasoning, and the term "postconventional" is favored for theories that also deal with issues of meaning, value, and experiential salience.

Some psychologists, of course, take issue with the concept of stage-like development beyond formal operations. Some object to any attempts at revising the original paradigm, claiming that all postformal variants of stage theory are mistaken in principle since the original framework of genetic epistemology is based on a logical empiricist or positivist philosophy of science. This model overemphasizes logic and rational thought, and thus the theory is not adequate to account for development, which is an organic process. Specifically, it is argued that biological processes, like most other fundamental ones, need to be inserted within an extralogical framework of metaphysical concepts and principles (being, becoming, potential, and so on).

In response, it can be argued that logical positivism, rather than being a "type" of theory, can be regarded as a necessary, but not a final "stage" in the development of the theory of science, just as formal operational reasoning is a necessary, but not final equilibration in the development of human understanding. The original theory is accurately described as overemphasizing rational thought, but the possibility of post-Piagetian theories is not mistaken. The method has proven productive beyond its original use. What characterizes postconventional development is precisely that the positivistic worldview of formal operations is superseded by a more organismic, systemic view of reality.

Research over the last twenty years on how mature adults organize experience has made plausible the existence of worldviews that reintegrate nonphysical or psychic aspects (being, becoming, potential) into the logic of psychology. Constructivist theories of adult development describe just how people reintegrate the formerly split-off affective, somatic, intuitive, and nonrational aspects of experience into their meaning making at the postconventional stages.

### Overview of Postconventional Constructivist Developmental Models of Human Development

The capacity for symbolic representation of reality and the clustering and hierarchical ordering of concepts (i.e., the propensity for classification) seem to be universal characteristics of human beings. It is this focus on the active interpretation of reality or construction of meaning that is the bedrock of the constructivist perspective. We perceive, organize, judge, and synthesize input from all sources that reach our awareness in order to orient ourselves and create coherent meaning in life. In addition, constructivist developmental theories argue that we tend to coordinate the many inputs into larger systems of meaning that are internally consistent, and that we do so through a series of increasingly complex and exhaustive reinterpretations of what constitutes reality.

What is most important in selecting a context for exploring the nature of postautonomous ego development is that constructivist developmental theories make useful distinctions and predictions not possible in other branches of psychology. They can explain why people develop contextual, process-oriented, and dialectical forms of knowing that integrate affect, intuition, and rational thought. In particular, they can account for the phenomena of increasing self-awareness and awareness of the process of meaning making itself that some people develop. Neither psychodynamic, ego psychology, information processing, phasic, or trait explanations of adulthood have much to say about these aspects of experience which seem to become of central concern to meaning makers at the postconventional stages of development.

### General Description of Adult Development

The orthogenetic principle for the field of developmental psychology states as a "law" that wherever development occurs it proceeds from a state of relative globality and lack of differentiation to a state of increasing differentiation, articulation, and hierarchic integration. Mental growth, in the most general way, can thus be described as a process of continuous differentiation and elaboration as well as of higher order abstraction and integration.

Sequentiality is a fundamental aspect of constructivist developmental theory. People can understand thinking at their own level or at levels below their own, but not at levels above their own. The more developed a person is, the more realms of experience become available for symbolic representation and cognitive manipulation, that is, the broader the range of experience that can be integrated and made sense of. Thus, fewer and fewer aspects of experience remain hidden, denied, or split off from conscious awareness.

Developmental theories share a belief that the need for meaning and communication drives people to integrate into their existing conceptual frames of reference every difference that enters their awareness, by either labeling it or filtering it out. The defense mechanism by which people screen out phenomena that do not fit their preexisting worldviews is called "selective inattention."

On the other hand, people can also learn to accommodate discrepant stimuli by adapting in two distinct ways. Most growth in adulthood seems to occur within a given stage, i.e., laterally, or by adding more information to what one already knows. The current way of viewing reality is refined, enriched, and modified to include more diverse domains, more contexts and detail, and to establish more connections among them. The higher the stage, the more room for such horizontal expansion seems to exist. On rare occasions, a transformation or vertical change occurs in response to persistent discrepancies that cannot be accommodated through horizontal integration or defensive mechanisms. In that case, the whole previous meaning system may be transformed and restructured into a new, more expansive and inclusive theory. In one way or another, constructivist developmental theory makes a fundamental distinction between horizontal growth, which adds new information or content to the existing worldview, and vertical growth, which transforms or reorganizes the current meaning system into a higher order integration.

Thus, constructivist developmental theory posits that the more evolved and differentiated individuals become, the more elements from more diverse sources and at higher orders of synthesis, they can simultaneously process and integrate into a coherent system of meaning. Such sources of input range from registering multiple channels of sensory information, to others' feelings, behaviors and interactions, to being alert to one's own behavior, reasoning, primary process and secondary ideation, perceptions, emotions, motivations, biases, dreams, intuitions, bodily states, and altered states of consciousness. Interindividual sources in the form of the group mind, or archetypal intimations, are also included as possible input at subtle levels of meaning making, while these sources can be organized into four territories of experience and foci of attention.

Another way of looking at the developmental progression is to state that the greater the differentiation, the greater distance from the objects of attention one has, or the greater one's perspective on experience. At the most advanced levels, whole systems of human behavior and thought—for instance, moral reasoning, physics as a discipline, or language as an automatic habit—are taken as objects of reflection. Individuals can become simultaneously aware of the most concrete and the most ephemeral aspects of their experience. They can recognize the immediate and the infinite in both temporal and spatial terms.

At this point, it may be useful to introduce a figurative model which summarizes current thought about the overall course of development from its early, undifferentiated modes to the most mature and rare ways of meaning making observed. The overall growth trajectory is divided into four tiers: a preconventional, a conventional, a postconventional, and a postpostconventional or ego-transcendent one. Available statistical research places about 10% of adults in the first, 80% in the second, and 10% in the third and fourth tiers. Using such a model allows one to indicate and compare how much of the overall range of the adult development trajectory each theory covers and how many subdivisions at the third tier it postulates.

1. The preconventional tier describes stages of meaning making that most people traverse before age twelve, i.e., they are more characteristic of children and are therefore considered immature, or lacking the complexity required for successful functioning and integration into modern society.
2. The conventional tier represents the stages most commonly found in adolescents and adults. These are the stages most representative of the conventions (the practices, beliefs, norms, and values) of what it means to be an adult in a given culture, hence the label conventional. This is also, therefore, the tier in which differences in local socialization most likely play the biggest role in individuals' differing self-perceptions, both within and across cultures.
3. The postconventional tier, also referred to as postformal or post-Piagetian, has only recently emerged as a viable lens for viewing the psychology of adulthood in Western psychology.
4. A fourth tier, the postpostconventional, metarational, or transcendent one, is even more rarely described by developmental psychologists in the West as part of the full potential of human development. Since this study is limited to the third tier of development, especially its upper-end, or the postautonomous level, a detailed understanding of the fourth tier is not necessary. The fourth tier is relevant to this inquiry, however, inasmuch as it covers the postautonomous level of the trajectory.

The postconventional tier is divided into two levels representing two distinct mindsets: the general systems stage, and a unitive stage. This distinction allows one to classify theories that postulate postconventional stages in theories may differ by whether they focus on stage characteristics.


---


# Adult Meaning Making and Ego Development Theory

## Theoretical Differences in Adult Development

Theories of adult development vary significantly in their focus and methodology. Some frameworks chart movement through stages dynamically, while others adopt a more static approach. Furthermore, theories differ in whether they are primarily data-driven or theory-driven, and whether their empirical foundations rely on interview data or psychometric testing.

Researchers have investigated numerous separate aspects of adult meaning making. Some focus on the mechanics and underlying laws of problem-solving, utilizing specific, narrowly defined cognitive tasks similar to classic practical beaker and balance beam experiments. Others explore reasoning within specific domains, such as moral development, conceptions of the good life, or the domain of faith. Additional research has examined the transition from adolescent to adult thinking, describing intellectual development in college-age populations or the emergence of relativistic thought. In response to earlier studies that relied heavily on male subject populations and were thus open to critiques of gender bias, subsequent research traced development in adult women to test how gender influences developmental trajectories. Finally, some theories focus on the evolving self and its overarching attempt to orchestrate meaning across domains, charting individuals' evolving sense of self or identity as a complex, multidimensional response to personal and cultural factors.

Most of these theories, however, avoid considering adult development beyond the rational, representational realm of cognition. This reluctance may stem from the fact that experience and expertise in transcendent realms become increasingly rare among researchers themselves. Additionally, propositions regarding postrepresentational cognition are difficult to test using standard scientific methods and are therefore challenging to evaluate from the purview of mainstream psychology.

In contrast, the field of transpersonal developmental psychology has attempted to build full-spectrum theories of human development that follow the trajectory from birth through conventional and postconventional stages into the postsymbolic realm and stages of higher consciousness. Rigorous methodologies have been formulated for testing transcendent phenomena, detailing techniques for data collection, empirical evidence, and verification. These transpersonal theorists are particularly relevant because they are more likely than most researchers of postconventional development to include the highest levels of development and the transition to postrepresentational cognition in their models.

## Domain Relationships and the Unified vs. Stranded Debate

Theories also differ in how they view the relationships among different domains of development. To account for the unevenness observed in individual cases, some proponents of constructivist developmental theory allow for a general coherence among domains theoretically, but insist that unevenness occurs frequently in real life and must be accounted for within the theory. Based on observations of adolescents and young adults in counseling environments, some researchers have described specific moves that account for this variability, such as "retreat, escape, and temporizing," while others use concepts like "encapsulation" for the unevenness observed in clients. The issue of whether different dimensions of the self develop more or less in tandem, or whether they can follow significantly different trajectories, remains a critical debate. Comprehensive and complex accounts of the relationship among domains (strands) and waves (major transformational moves) of development have been articulated in integral theories.

Overall, the question of whether development is unified or consists of many strands is still debated, with evidence supporting both positions. The unified position posits that stages are structured wholes forming a single coherent system of logic. Some theories subsume several dimensions of a person under ego development—including impulse control, character development, interpersonal style, conscious preoccupations, and cognitive style—and understand them as developing more or less together. Depending on whether the focus is on micro- or macro-development, one may observe both phases of inconsistent performance and phases with more stage-like, coherent, or consolidated performance patterns. What researchers choose to pay most attention to is often a matter of personal affinity and largely determines what they are apt to find. A richer and more verisimilar understanding of human nature can likely be gleaned only from a tapestry of approaches and perspectives.

By using the concept of the ego as the central processing unit that orchestrates and aims at coherent meaning, it can be demonstrated how concepts of the self and reality evolve together such that a sequence of clearly observable stages of increasing differentiation and integration can be drawn, making the underlying logic visible. At the same time, development must be understood as a gradual and ongoing process with periods of more or less stability, since ego stages are qualitatively different points along a more or less hypothetical continuum. The stages themselves are most visible when comparing large numbers of adults or people at very different stages. Ego stages offer a powerful way of characterizing differences among people.

While the course of development naturally abounds with possibilities for pathology and less-than-optimal negotiation of the multiple demands on the self, it seems undeniable that most individuals try to organize experience into some coherent, unified view of reality. Even in the most severe psychopathologies, one can often discern the striving of the ego to cohere in the first place or to maintain a semblance of coherence. Thus, a focus on positive or ideal adult development and meaning making as a central, synthesizing activity of the self is particularly valuable.

## Critiques of Early Cognitive Stage Theories

Numerous arguments against early cognitive stage theories have been mounted, questioning the cross-cultural universality of the stages, the ages at which people can solve laboratory problems, and the locus of control, which was predominantly placed within the individual. Given current knowledge, it is reasonable to challenge many specifics of these foundational theories. For example, early theorists both underestimated and overestimated the ages at which individuals reach defined stages because much of the theory was based on laboratory tasks. They underestimated them because children who are coached can solve certain problems earlier than surmised. On the other hand, full formal operations rarely occur as early as suggested (age 12), and many adults do not develop them at all. However, the approach of basing theory on careful experimentation, observation, and interviewing—that is, on empirical evidence—remains a powerful avenue for expanding knowledge about the human condition.

## Ego Development Theory and Sentence Completion Testing

Ego development theory and its associated sentence completion measuring instrument form the basis for assessing stages of character development. This approach constituted a breakthrough in cognitive developmental psychology and measurement when it first appeared for three major reasons: it used adult women as a sample population at a time when research and conclusions about adult development were customarily drawn from studying males; it was thoroughly grounded in empirical evidence; and it introduced a novel argument for determining a person's stage of development.

The sentence completion test serves as a vehicle to deduce people's overall frame of reference, or mental map, from their verbal productions. It is fascinating that 36 brief sentence completions can provide a fair idea of the greatest concerns and challenges for a given developmental personality type. It remains astonishing that one can comprehend another in important ways by knowing their developmental stage, and that one can predict their behavior across a number of dimensions. This includes preferences for relating to others, ways of adjudicating in cases of conflict or doubt, and main preoccupations and concerns. Empirical evidence for the predictive power of the instrument comes from extensive research and pilot studies.

### Characteristics of the Test

The sentence completion test assesses a person's ego stage or character development. It is perhaps the most widely used standardized developmental measuring instrument for adults. Its psychometric properties, reliability, and validity have been thoroughly explored by numerous researchers.

The test usually contains 36 sentence stems or sentence beginnings displayed on two pages in numerical order with space for test-takers to enter their responses. They are instructed to complete the set of 36 stems in any way they wish. Thus, it is a semi-projective test, inviting test-takers to impose their own way of structuring reality on the material presented. A salient aspect of semi-projective sentence completion tests is that each sentence stem provides both the framework for the response and a unit by which to compare all responses from many individuals given to that stem. Unlike fully projective tests, the stems limit to some degree what a plausible completion can look like.

The test can be administered in groups, which is likely the most effective method, requiring at most half an hour. It can also be given verbally to people for whom writing may be a challenge, and by telephone when group or mail administration is not possible, with the researcher noting responses verbatim. Non-group administration inevitably introduces some error. Administrators and raters are held to strict adherence to recommended procedures, and problems with non-traditional uses are explicitly detailed.

The recommended 36-item form contains the same stems for both men and women and is constructed so it can be given in two psychometrically equivalent halves for pre/post-test research designs. Many other forms exist, including variations for different target populations, some combining different items or varying their order. The current form aims to provide a variety of themes and optimal sequencing, containing the statistically most discriminating items.

The stems refer to everyday life and were chosen to appeal to and elicit responses from the widest possible range of adults. Examples include:
- "A man's job -"
- "I am -"
- "If my mother -"

These stems tap into how people construe coherent meaning, calling on subjects to project their own way of making sense of experience. The semantic choice (differentiation of content and specific language) and sentence structure are said to reveal the underlying frame of reference or ego stage. The test assumes that language is interpretable, meaningful behavior that mirrors people's ways of responding to life, or even that language is constitutive of reality. This aligns with the notion that no expression of psychic life is arbitrary, lawless, or trifling. If spoken and written language is behavior, differences in verbal expression are interpretable as differences in psychic life or meaning making.

### The Role of Language and Rater Interpretation

The semantic and structural distinctions required to separate similar responses into different stages according to their level of differentiation were tacitly assumed skills, even though participants were not trained linguists. Part of the appeal of the original manuals was the skillfully sequenced sets. For instance, completions to the stem "When people are helpless -":
- "I try to help them"
- "I want to help them"
- "I feel a need to help"

These responses give a clear sense of increasing stimulus-response delay and self-reflection, and were used as examples for three consecutive conventional stages. Students taught how language can zero in on a subject's probable underlying frame of reference can readily sort such sequences intuitively, gaining a sense of the construct of the evolving self. This supports the face validity of the theory and the claim that adequate understanding of the theoretical construct requires knowledge of the scoring system.

However, this approach carries dangers. Researcher bias or limitation can powerfully influence the initial sorting of responses with similar content into different stages, even with consensus among research team members. Using exacting psychometrics afterward to ensure that classes of responses are consonant with the overall rating of a protocol cannot entirely remove the problem due to the circularity of the process; it cannot discover shared rater limitation.

### Sentence Completions as Verbal Behavior

The test is fundamentally based on the notion that meaning is created and communicated through language, and that choice of content and structure models one's conception of reality. The test provides enough glimpses into such choices to give an overall sense of how things cohere for a person.

In comparing structured and projective tests, it has been noted that in structured tests, investigators project their own frame of reference rather than tapping the subjects', which is what reveals ego level. In unstructured tests, one cannot control what the subject will choose to reveal. While testers become adept at interpreting minimal signs, there is always the chance a person will conceal all or respond in a way that conceals their usual ego level.

Concealment and preexisting psychopathology are issues raters must be aware of. Signs of serious disturbance are not necessarily easily detected, though expert raters can often pick up telling incongruities. What can be said with certainty is that the ego level displayed is one an individual has mastered and can employ in a test situation. In most cases, lower responses are not due to concealment but to limitations in the subject. Individuals functioning at lower stage levels will simplify verbal stimuli to a form they can understand and handle. Misreading or not understanding stems indicates low ego level, as some stems require a conventional conception of reality. For example, "Crime and delinquency could be halted if -" is hard for lower-level subjects to understand because it pulls for hypothetical reasoning.

In contrast, postformal individuals commonly choose to respond to some items at lower levels than they are capable of—most often at conventional stages—because doing so is functionally adequate. This flexibility is a sign of higher development. Lower stage responses can also be a form of acting out or avoidance of emotionally loaded stems for subjects at all levels.

Overall, the test measures performance rather than competence. We cannot tell what a participant might have said under other circumstances. Thus, one can assert that a person seems capable of appreciating the world at least at a specific level under test conditions.

Furthermore, there are intrinsic difficulties in assigning behavioral signs to any developmental level. A sign appearing at one level in a tentative version appears at higher levels in increasingly clear and elaborated versions. A thought appearing at one level as a cliché appears in a deeper, more convincing, and more complex version at higher levels. Thus, a manual using verbal signs is valued for its explicit logic and plausibility manifest behind the sequencing of model responses and categories. The primary questions are: "Given a particular ego level, what is the probability of showing this sign?" and conversely, "Given a particular replicated response, what is the probability that the subject comes from a given level?"

Since the highest stages are characterized by originality, spontaneity, and freedom from stereotypy, behavioral signs are difficult to find. Replicated responses are not expected at these levels. It is a weakness of the statistical approach that it works best where there is sufficient data; the end of a qualitative scale escapes its power. The fundamental problem of psychometrics is transforming qualitative data onto a quantitative scale, and identifying the entities worth measuring.

### Manual Construction and Scoring Rules

The manuals grew from an exemplar manual—which just listed responses given for each stage and was not useful for rating new material—to a categorized manual, which separated responses into categories with titles indicating the common element. The published manual is a rationalized category manual, attempting to justify every scoring decision theoretically and empirically. It offers an overview of each stem, a theoretical rationale for category placement, and cross-references for similar content placed at different stages.

Final placement of a category was based on an internal consistency method. If a group of responses rated at a given stage turned out to come predominantly from protocols rated at a different stage, the group was reassigned to the more frequent stage in the next version. The final product has many instances where face validity is striking, but also placements whose logic in terms of a consistent theory of ego evolution is not evident. This is expected when statistical techniques override placement by insightful researchers, especially for rarer, non-replicated completions. Empirical test construction methods rest on replicated responses, which is difficult for free-response tests given human variability.

The manual consists of separate scoring sections for each stem, containing lists of representative content categories with actual completions as examples. Trained raters assign an ego stage to each response by matching the completion with a listed category title. Five rules govern the process:
1. Matching (tried first).
2-4. Rules for scoring compound completions (which may contain elements at the same or different stages).
5. Rules for other eventualities (missing or incomprehensible responses).

Verbatim matches occur mostly at the low end of the scale and through the conventional tier. Beyond that, the length and complexity of responses require the use of Rules 2-4. Skilled reliance on scoring rules becomes increasingly important as responses become more complex and unique with increasing ego development.

The number of recognized stages changed over time, from an initial four to nine, to as many as ten or eleven, and currently eight. Whichever way the continuum is parsed, each consecutive stage represents a coherent, predictable, and recognizable way people differ. The instrument types people according to their ego level, making stage theories quasi-typologies, though they differ because stages follow an invariant sequence.

A final rating of an entire protocol (Total Protocol Rating or TPR) is based on the cumulative distribution characteristics of the individual scores. These distributions are compared to predetermined cut-off numbers (ogives) that distinguish between adjacent stages, and a stage score is assigned according to a specified algorithm. Ideally, a scorer reads the entire protocol to assess whether the mechanically derived score warrants rating the complete protocol at that stage.

The TPR reflects a person's most ego-syntonic and habitual frame of reference, or their "center of gravity." People tend to respond optimally in domains where they are highly motivated. By definition, an ego stage is the view of reality a person most routinely and automatically uses in everyday situations. While there is spread around the center of gravity, one cannot fully understand reasoning beyond one's own. Upward scatter is interpreted as intimations of more complex meaning making, though not sufficiently numerous to show a shift into the adjacent higher level.

### Ego Development Theory Structure

The ego development theory ranges over three tiers: preconventional, conventional, and postconventional. Unlike domain-specific stage theories, it describes a basic or overarching aspect of personality development including cognitive, affective, and behavioral aspects.

The theory uses the ego as the underlying principle in personality organization. The organization or synthetic function is not just something the ego does; it is what the ego is. The ego strives for coherent meaning and orchestrates how reality is perceived. The failure of the ego to integrate is meaninglessness. The fundamental characteristics of the ego are that it is a process, a structure, social in origin, functioning as a whole, and guided by purpose and meaning.

To claim an underlying construct develops, one must show its movement from stage to stage and its changing properties. The theory attempts to account for transformational changes in perspective by proposing a sequence of ego stages.
