These stages form an invariant sequence and represent hierarchical integrations of greater complexity, cognitive differentiation, and integration. A higher stage transforms the previous way of looking at reality, offering a broader, deeper, more integrated perspective.

However, the theory has been critiqued for lacking a rigorous, coherent definition of the ego construct and the logic underlying successive stages. It lacks an explicit logic of what makes one position higher or more mature than another, making it difficult to justify a sequence of hierarchical stages.

### Problems with the Manual and Theory

The most distinctive feature of the theory and instrument is that the theory evolved out of the measurement instrument. The manual is based on years of cumulative experimentation, with observations from successive studies influencing the conceptualization of the theory itself.

Developmental theory heavily derived from empirical evidence and based on a probabilistic rationale is prone to specific problems. It cannot readily account for data from the extremes of the scale (due to lack of evidence) or novel data. It is strongest in the middle range where data is adequate. The reliance on statistical conclusions without known rationale, permitting data to decide, is seen by some as less arbitrary, but it leaves questions about whether other approaches could have produced similar results.

The lack of theoretical logic is evident in the manual's categories. Identical completions to very similar stimuli can be listed at two different stages. Explaining almost the same stimulus-response sequence one way for one stem and differently for another raises questions about overall coherence. The stage descriptions are often like sketches or snapshots, making little attempt to be comprehensive or explain the movement from one stage to the next.

### High-End Problems and Proposed Revisions

Rating high-end protocols presents a significant challenge because the theory and manuals do not clearly distinguish between the two highest stages (Autonomous and Integrated). The Integrated stage often does not seem to be a transformation of the previous stage, but merely an elaboration or original combination of material from lower stages. Most examples of the ultimate stage do not contain content or structure different from the penultimate Autonomous stage.

However, a handful of examples at the Integrated stage seem genuinely different in tenor, content, and structure, warranting consideration as a "postautonomous" stage. For example, a response to the stem "I am -":
- "I am - finally, in the long run, mostly unfathomable, but I enjoy the process of trying to fathom."

This response reflects a fluid, deconstructed self-view, enjoying the process without concern for a definable outcome or the desire for objectivity common at prior stages. It requires a transformation and awareness of one's own mental processes not documented earlier. Yet, there was no general ego development theory to account for this awareness. The manuals highlight "the search for identity" as the characteristic of the Integrated stage, but this response abdicates that search in favor of witnessing the ongoing process of self-becoming.

To address these limitations, systematic collection of rare "postautonomous" responses led to proposed revisions. Postconventional ego development can be characterized by an increasing awareness of the constructed nature of knowledge and a step-wise deconstruction of assumptions undergirding conventional views of reality. The search for an objective, nondistorted view of reality is superseded by an understanding of the futility of such a goal. Attention turns to how meaning is continuously reconstructed and how all constructs are artifacts of linguistic codification.

Other examples of such responses to "I am -" include:
- "- which is pretty wondrous to me all by itself as it unfolds"
- "- alive, trundling along, making sense as best as can, diversifying & expanding while consolidating & contracting"

Analysis of these responses led to the proposal of two distinct new stages with several categories each. For example, criteria for the Construct-aware stage look for signs that subjects are cognitively aware of the constructed nature of "labeled" reality and concerned with ambiguities and paradoxes inherent in constructing meaning. The experience of alert, detached witnessing of one's changing self-identifications is a criterion for the more integrated Universal or Unitive stage.

Unlike existing stem-specific categories, these proposed postautonomous categories are at a higher level of abstraction and apply across all stems, reflecting stage-specific themes. This approach allows for a theoretically coherent and plausible explanation of the data. At the Construct-aware stage, individuals recognize the pattern of forming coherent maps of reality by consecutive approximations and the underlying human need for permanence that drives reification. At the Universal/Unitive stage, people understand the fundamental instability and illusion of the permanent object world more deeply, integrating this awareness into a new mode of living and a more sober appraisal of the purpose of rational behavior and language.

### The Second Edition Revisions

The 1996 Second Edition of the manual was intended as the final, authoritative version. It introduced a single-volume, "unisex" format based on data from both males and females, with examples reflecting current public attitudes. Recalibrating stems referring to women brought them into line with contemporary social realities, acknowledging that the social expression of development varies across cultures and times.

However, several changes present challenges:
1. **Stage Relabeling:** Stages were relabeled E2 to E9, truncating the number of levels from ten or eleven down to eight. While eliminating labeling inconsistencies, this poses a serious transposing challenge for longitudinal studies with great potential for clerical error.
2. **Introduction of Themes:** Themes were introduced to guide raters, effectively adding an extra step in the decision tree. Since it is not systematically carried through, it is not necessarily more efficient.
3. **Altered Ogive Rules:** The rules for assigning Total Protocol Ratings became less stringent for obtaining a postconventional rating, requiring only six ratings beyond the highest conventional stage (E6). Borderline ogives were eliminated.
4. **Combinatorial Categories:** New "combinatorial" categories at the postconventional tier invite mechanical application, overruling the rule that compound responses must generate a higher level of conceptual complexity. A response with several E6 elements might be rated E8 merely because of the combination, confounding conceptual complexity with the assembly of ideas and weakening demands for postconventional ratings.
5. **Dropped Stages:** Stages that raters had difficulty rating or that were ill-defined were dropped to streamline the instrument.

Relying primarily on data and psychometrics runs the risk of ignoring vital aspects of the overall underlying pattern. When a stage makes theoretical sense and can be explained as a distinctive position in the developmental sequence, it ought to be included whether or not it draws many responses. Data-driven theory is only as good as the data it is based on.


---


# Development Theory and the Challenges of High-End Stages

A fundamental purpose of development theory is to provide a plausible model of how adults differ from each other in a systematic fashion. It should explain how they develop increasingly complex maps of meaning, what they choose to focus on or deal with at each stage, and how they reason their positions. Thus, combining data with interpretation and insight is essential for producing predictive and useful theory. Nonetheless, the framework developers seem to weigh the frequency of stage assignments and expedience increasingly more heavily than theoretical consistency in their latest version of the manual and theory. By renaming and renumbering the stages, some of the confusion resulting from earlier modifications was avoided on the one hand, but on the other, the theory as a whole became weaker. This move was intended to make the task of learning how to score easier for novice raters, but it puts practicality above the integrity and coherence of the theory.

## High-End Problems Persisting in the Second Edition

Because the placement of examples in the manual is based on probabilistic considerations, the manual is good at distinguishing among adults at the most common stages. It is less so for the extremes at either side of the distribution curve. It is weak at the lower end because examples are few and the test requires that respondents be minimally verbal to respond to the stimuli. However, this is not a grave problem for the low-end because responding inappropriately and misunderstanding the stimulus items are in part signs for low ego development. The paucity in numbers and distinctions of the examples mirrors the impoverished and confused thought of low ego stages.

The problem at the upper end, however, is very different and more serious. The next section cites many more of the framework developers' pronouncements verbatim than other sections because it is at the high-end of the scale that their explanations are least consistent, perceptive, and defensible.

### Lack of Clarity and Sparse Examples

In 1993, the framework developers asserted that:

> More problematic than (...) is description of the highest stage. There bias is all. I try to be vague [ ? ? ] about the highest stage, and I discourage our raters from assigning the highest score (integrated), because I find the rules are almost always misinterpreted, even by our most reliable and conscientious collaborators (...). It is also the level on which there is least agreement with other similar stage conceptions (p. 59).

The framework developers continue to cite lack of "agreement" (p. 7) and "lack of data" (p. 6) as the main reasons for the vagueness of the Integrated stage. As a practical matter, the Second Edition offers considerably fewer postautonomous examples than previous versions. It contains only 18 examples in the introductory chapters and the scoring section combined. Thus, 30 out of 36 stems currently provide no examples for the Integrated stage. As for the manual, the framework developers acknowledge that the few examples actually listed may not be very helpful to raters in scoring higher stages. They remark that:

> because replicated responses are rare at E8 (Autonomous) and nonexistent at E9 (I-6; Integrated), this chapter [Manifestations of ego level in sentence completions] is about as helpful as the item manuals for those levels (p. 9).

One begins to wonder why the Integrated stage has not been dropped altogether from the manual as other stages have, given its theoretical fuzziness and the lack of empirical support.

### The Problem of Identity

As in earlier versions, the sole discriminating characteristic for the Integrated stage is given as the "search for identity."

> The one new category at E9 is the search for identity. Clichés about "who I am" or being oneself do not qualify, only responses that on other grounds would be classed at least as E8. The problem of identity appears in terms of reconciliation of roles, striving for autonomy, individuality and self-fulfillment, and recognizing other people's need for theirs" (p. 25)

All of the above concerns are also listed as characteristic of the Autonomous self. Moreover, concerns with identity (self-assessment) appear at all stages and at all levels of differentiation since ego as underlying construct is said to reveal the current synthesis of "who I am." Identity therefore appears at level E8 for instance in "A man's job - search for identity" and in "For a woman a career is - a means of achieving identity" as category titles. To clarify the distinction between the search for identity at E8 and E9, the framework developers declare that, at E8 "people express their respect for other people's need for autonomy," but "the problem of identity appears only in general or cliché form; full appreciation of identity as a problem is present at E9" (p.23).

What they understand a "full appreciation of identity as a problem" to mean must be deduced from two examples at their Integrated stage E9 in the "Manifestation ..." chapter.

> My mother and I - get along, but I need to get away to develop myself, I praise her because she is a woman, and a person. I am a person but not yet a woman.
>
> When a child will not join in group activities - it may be because he feels no identity with the group, is too self-conscious, or has no real interest in the activity. (p.25)

Though the first completion is touching, it only expresses an insight that is most likely from an Individualistic (E7) perspective. It could possibly be rated at E8 for the quality of "admiration" that is clearly expressed. The girl tries to find her identity as a woman within the context of and in contradistinction to a special significant other. The second response should be rated at E7 according to scoring Rule 2 because it combines lower material in an interesting way that seems overall more complex than its parts taken alone. Neither of these responses seems to warrant a postautonomous rating since they give no indication of a frame of reference that is qualitatively different from and more integrated than an Autonomous one.

One is tempted to interpret the first response above as a "full appreciation of identity as a problem," or "the search for identity as a problem," but this clearly is not what the framework developers have in mind. In their framework, the most evolved persons are aiming for a clear enduring self-sense that—although it may be emotionally rich and cognitively complex—is based on an "objective" appraisal of oneself in the world. There is no indication anywhere in their writing that they have considered how the expression "search for identity" itself inherently frames their reality as assuming that (a) a permanent objective identity can be found and (b) that finding one is a desirable goal. The "problem of identity" for the framework developers is how to best accomplish the endeavor or at least approximate it. The possibility of questioning the meaning of such a search is not entertained.

### Lack of Distinction Between the Autonomous and the Integrated Stage

Because they have not provided useful guidance, distinctions, and examples in the past for rating the Integrated level, the framework developers find that:

> There are major differences among qualified raters both as to the description of this level and application of the description in particular cases (p. 7).

They therefore suggest that for practical purposes their highest rating, the Integrated stage, be eliminated (p. 24), or collapsed with the Autonomous stage because of its rarity. It accounts for "probably less than 1%" (p.6) of SCT protocols from an urban population. Even if one agreed with their interpretation of what constitutes the "highest" stage of ego development, it is not clear that statistical rarity alone warrants ignoring the possible theoretical distinctions that the study of these rarer views might produce. The framework developers are justified, of course, from an actuarial perspective. Most research projects with adults have predominantly conventional subjects, so that distinctions at the high-end are unnecessary for scoring them. To know what characterizes most people in an adult population is important for many reasons, but is it more important than to know what the overall trajectory of development might be and to understand what characterizes those at the high-end of the scale as the framework developers assert? One wonders what happened to their earlier recognition that: "theoretical insights" at the "growing tips of theory" are possible only by retaining "rare, unreplicated responses" (p. 14).

Furthermore, it is a curious aspect of an otherwise admirably concise and objective writing style throughout their work as well as in the Second Edition that the framework developers feel compelled to attribute "questionable" motives to high-end writers of long, complex responses as well as to those who would want to understand and rate such material:

> Protocols with a very large number of long responses, even if they are mostly rated E8 or E9, are not comparable with those on which the manual is based. They may have been written to display that their authors know the "right" answers, or to teach lessons to whoever reads the test. In any case, they appear to be addressed to a different directive than simply completing sentences. (p. 25)

For some people that may be the case, but for others that directive may be ego-syntonic, in the service of expressing their deepest concerns as they experience them, or, in the framework developers' words, addressing "life's universal problems" (p. 32). The struggle to express in words the complexity of one's understanding in response to a simple test situation is just one of those "universal problems" that does seem to appear frequently in postautonomous protocols. Rather than dismissing it, it deserves to be investigated. What has changed in the way postautonomous subjects understand themselves? Why do they talk explicitly about their struggles with complexity issues whereas subjects at earlier stages ignore them? These are the types of questions that guide one in discovering recurring themes in high-end responses and that differentiate high-stage protocols from earlier ones in a unique, qualitatively distinctive way.

In addition, the framework developers also suspect those who are particularly interested in the higher stages of merely seeking self-affirmation or of downright strangeness.

> The highest stages are fascinating in part because they embody much of what each of us aspires to or believes that he or she has achieved (p.1).
>
> Many professional colleagues are enthralled with studying the highest possible levels (p. 87).
>
> Even if people of the highest ego level became psychologists, would they really want to spend their time scoring SCTs? (p. 59).

The response to the question is, why not? A Buddhist might observe that every activity that is done with one's full-presence of mind and heart is an expression of being. Any job is as important or unimportant as any other. There is no difference. Thus, the framework developers tend to dismiss questions of meaning making which might be essential concerns in life for some individuals, and they devalue anybody's interest in these matters. Granted, few people give responses that fall into the postautonomous range, but their completions promise to be especially illuminating in mapping out a more full-bodied theory of mature ego development. The above remarks seem part of the framework developers' overall adherence to a linear, analytic model of science, one that declares anything that can't be readily measured as outside the realm of scientific purview or meaningful inquiry and therefore of little value.

### No New Theory in Spite of Whole Field of Research

The Second Edition along with the technical companion volume consists of a defense of previous methodological moves as well as of current strategies. It shows no evidence that the overall theory of ego development has been reexamined since its first publication. The most recent cite in the Second Edition is a 1968 work on intellectual development. Other than the framework developers' own publications, there is no consideration of any work done by later authors. Most significantly, the Second Edition doesn't even mention other relevant work in the field of adult development.

This insistence on keeping ego development separate and distinct from other movements in the field is also peculiar to the framework developers, and perhaps expresses the zeitgeist. Their ego development theory as it stands, cannot adequately allow for the evolution and transformation of its own system even as it can acknowledge dynamism and movement as an important element in growth. Thus, it would be a fascinating task for a psychobiographer to assess in which way ego development theory is a child of its specific progenitor and of her historical-intellectual context and why it may be at odds with the current postconventional research arena and a more postmodern scientific paradigm. Although tongue-in-cheek, the framework developers' phrase, "the obdurate vagary of human beings always escaping our labels," (p. 57) summarizes in a nutshell their sentiments towards the extraordinary and the unac-"countable".

Given the substantial evidence of the limits of both the theory at the upper-end and the inadequacy of the Second Edition as a scoring tool for postautonomous development, it seems that a test of the proposed revisions is appropriate.

## Summary of Revisions of Ego Development Theory

This section, which summarizes prior work in ego development theory, is subdivided into three parts. First, it highlights four salient aspects of the overall conception of ego development which systematizes and extends ego development theory. Among them, it will mention 'the expanding perspective on the self' as the underlying construct that develops throughout the stages. Second, it will point out a distinctive pattern that emerged from examining the whole sequence of stages in large numbers of SCTs. It proposes that ego stages alternate in their overall emphasis and balance between stages of differentiation and stages of integration (separateness and connectedness). This pattern is introduced here because it originally guided the hypothesis for an intermediate stage of differentiation between the integrated Autonomous and "integrated" Integrated stage. Third, it will sketch the overall cognitive dynamics in postconventional ego development and present two new stages, the Construct-aware stage 5/6 (C9) and the Unitive stage 6 (C10) as part of the whole trajectory of expanding perspectives. It is suggested that these be used in lieu of the Integrated stage E9 (I-6) when dealing with high-end samples. For reasons of comparison and distinction, an abstract of the Autonomous stage 5 (E8) is also included in this section.

### Ego Development Theory

#### Definition of Terms Used

Key terms are defined noting the similarities and differences between these definitions and other researchers'. First, the critical term "ego": Some aspects of the use of the term ego and "self" seem similar to the superego (e.g. the development of impulse control, managing affective states, and conflict resolution). However, the term "ego" is used here to denote the underlying principle in personality organization that strives for coherent meaning and orchestrates how we perceive reality. "Ego development is the term for the common element in the stage sequence and the corresponding dimension of individual differences" (p. 6).

The term self is used interchangeably with person and individual whereas by ego, it refers to two main dimensions: ego as process and ego as representation. The function of the ego as process is to organize, synthesize and integrate experience from both external and internal sources and to mediate among them. The ego is the central processing unit within the rational, personal, symbol-mediated realm of experience. The ego as representation (the I and the me as one's identity) can be understood as the result of this effort at integration. The created identity thus changes with the changing levels of integration. Interestingly, rare high-end SCTs seem to reflect the possibility of becoming a witness to both the ego's efforts at processing and to the changing results of the process of "identity formation."

The fundamental arbitrariness and limits of defining and reifying experience were introduced earlier and will be taken up again as a separate theme in the validation study.

#### A Multidimensional Psychological System

Ego development theory describes the ego as an overarching, multifaceted construct. Human development has been described as emerging from a synthesis of doing, being, and thinking, although this general pattern would be qualified by adding that the balances among the dimensions are likely to shift along the growth path. Following this, ego development theory can be usefully described as a psychological system with at least three different, but interrelated dimensions that coexist at all times. The affective dimension deals with emotions and the experience of being in this world. The cognitive dimension addresses the question of how a person learns, reasons, and knows about himself or herself and the world. The operative dimension looks at what people see as the purpose of life, what needs they act upon, and what ends they are moving towards. These three dimensions of experience have correlates in all human cultures and represent ontological, epistemological and teleological concerns.

It is maintained that the stage descriptions "emphasize the affective and motivational aspects of personality growth, such as impulse control, conscious preoccupations, interpersonal orientation, and character development" (p. 5). Current researchers have different views of what special weight should be given to cognitive style or cognitive complexity as an aspect of the concept of ego development. Since the perspective on the 'self' has been chosen as the element that develops, it appears that the cognitive aspect drives the others in this approach. It can be argued that this is more likely an artifact of the lens chosen than an aspect of development itself.

#### The Linguistic Foundation

Another crucial aspect of ego development theory and measurement is its linguistic foundation. It seems indisputable today that the capacity for symbolic representation is innate in human beings and that its activation is indispensable for growth to occur. For verbal projective tests to be useful, one has to assume that there is a "probabilistic" relationship between that which is experienced and expressed and the signs used for doing so.

As discussed previously in the section on the centrality of language, ego-development theory postulates (a) that the content and the structure of people's language reveals their conceptual frame of reference, and (b) that their underlying cognitive structure can be deduced from a matrix of linguistic signs. Most importantly, it assumes that individuals function at the level that explains the world best to themselves in areas that are relevant to their lives.

The analysis of a person's use of language in the SCT for both its conceptual content and its structure provides the researcher with a set of clues to the person's underlying self-theory and Weltanschauung. This is so because language is internalized so completely and at such an early age that it acts as an unconscious filter for a vast number of stimuli. We can therefore evaluate what the conscious sphere of people is likely to be from what they spontaneously choose to express on an SCT, and often, more saliently, by what is missing because it is outside their conceptual framework. Some material may be filtered out defensively because it is threatening to one's current self-system. But more often, distinctions don't appear because they don't register at all in that person's range of awareness. By definition, an ego stage is that frame of reference that a person most automatically and routinely uses in everyday situations.

If we accept the premise that one's language subtly mirrors one's conceptual or ego stage, the question arises whether ego development theory can be shown to follow a regular order of expanding views of reality. The 'Perspectives on the self' is an attempt at elucidating the movement through the stages. 'Perspectives on the self' can be used as the consistent element from which to view the whole sequence of ego development. This movement from a simple, undifferentiated self-state to ever more complex self-identifications follows the overall move described as part of the orthogenetic principle. The level of perspective increases at each stage (quantitative aspect of change) and, in turn, greater perspective allows for a different understanding of what is looked at (qualitative nature of change) (p.117).

In 1984, the theory was critiqued qua developmental stage theory for lacking an underlying structural logic. A distinction was introduced between "hard" and "soft" stage models. What hard structural stages gain, it was written, "is precision in their articulation of a structural logic of stages that will survive the ever-changing growth of psychological knowledge about the self, its functions and development" (p. 384).

In response to this critique, it is suggested that the evolving 'perspective on the self' demonstrates the inner logic of ego development from the Symbiotic stage through the proposed postautonomous stages. It is argued that spelling out the structure of an underlying regularly expanding self-perspective moves ego development theory from a "soft" stage theory to one more akin to a "hard" stage type according to the distinction.

These 'evolving perspectives on the self' are similar to orders of consciousness in that they distinguish between what is subject (and thus hidden from view, or unavailable for consideration and negotiation) and what can be taken as object, "looked at," or dealt with at each stage of ego development. Thus, the 'perspective on the self' actually provides a logic or scaffold that underlies the ego stages. Unlike the previous construct, (1) it explains the dynamic unfolding of the self; (2) provides a test of whether a stage is qualitatively different from a previous one, and (3) serves as a predictor.

By structuring ego development theory via a unifying construct that can be shown to expand regularly, one of the problems with the theory is eliminated. As it stands now, this version of the theory explains both the strengths and the limits of each meaning-making stage and anticipates the specific issues that adults are likely to have to negotiate at the next level in their development. It predicts what content and processes they will be able to notice, perceive, act and reflect upon, organize, and synthesize at the current stage that they did not perceive before. That kind of mental growth is best conceptualized as "making what was subject into object so that we can 'have it' rather that 'be had' by it" (p.34).

This reconceptualization of ego development theory fleshes out the descriptions of the qualitatively different points on the continuum provided by the framework developers. It explains how people form systems of coherent meaning and how they move from one interpretation of reality to the next by tracking the changes between the stages. Looking for regularities in the whole sequence of ego stages, is one way to systematize the theory.

### The Alternating Pattern Between Stages of Differentiation and Integration

According to developmental tenets, when individuals grow beyond the confines of a particular stage, they may redefine themselves within the new frame of reference of the next stage. After carefully studying many SCT protocols as well as the theory and scoring manuals, it appeared that these redefinitions seem to alternate in a regular fashion between stages of differentiation and integration. The continuous renegotiation of the balance between differentiation and assimilation, or the "double trend towards autonomy" (differentiation, separation, mastery, independence) "and homonomy" (integration, participation, relatedness, connection) throughout development has been commented on as a fundamental tendency in human beings.

According to developmental principles, ego stages can be thought of as organic wholes, as relatively stable balances within which adults stay settled for long periods of time, often for a lifetime. It is for this reason that the positions of differentiation in ego development theory can be considered "stages," or relatively permanent positions, rather than transitional phases between more enduring stages. This model describes a logical sequence of ten such stages of differentiation and integration, which range over three tiers of the four tier developmental model. The stages represent stable hypothetical equilibria along the continuum. Other models, on the other hand, only consider the integrated points on the continuum as qualitatively different stages proper.

The pattern of the alternating stages of differentiation and integration can be summarized as follows. Individuals at the earliest two stages in ego development theory are too undifferentiated for the separation of the double trend toward autonomy and homonomy to emerge fully. Beginning with the Self-protective stage, however, one can distinguish between stages that favor separation and those that emphasize relatedness.

The Rule-oriented, Self-aware, Individualistic, and Construct-aware Stages (A3, 3/4(E5), 4/5(E7), and 5/6 (C9)) are oppositional stages or stages of differentiation. People at these stages emphasize their difference and uniqueness in relation to the previous stage. In each case, they are beholden to that former way of meaning making by explicitly setting themselves apart from it while they are, at the same time, able to observe it from a new level of perspective. They tend to stress the boundaries, the distinctions, and the differences to the previous way of perceiving themselves and the world. They also tend to affirm their independence although, depending on the stage, at different levels of cognitive complexity and abstraction. Because their basic need for relatedness is not fulfilled, they generally express more negative affect and more tension than persons at the even stages 3 (E4), 4 (E6), 5 (E8), and 6 (C10).

### Alternating Stages of Differentiation and Integration

On one hand, ego development theory at the postconventional tier deals with people's ability to represent the self and reality with increasing accuracy, objectivity, and non-self-defensiveness. From one ego stage to the next, people become more individuated and more complex according to the pattern of continuing differentiation-integration begun in infancy. They can draw multiple kinds of distinctions and connections among stimuli from ever more diverse sources and meaningfully integrate them into a coherent frame of reference for themselves. Overall, this movement describes the increasing mental flexibility and personal maturity evidenced at the postconventional stages.

The second movement, in contrast, is best described as a step-wise deconstruction of the clear-cut subject-object boundaries that were established in the process of forming the adult, independent, rational self and the permanent object world. This process of deconstruction of the subject/object separation seems to happen in two steps, which are elaborated below.

#### The Systems Level of Postconventional Development

At the systems level, individuals begin to realize that they interpret what things mean and that their interpretations are both culturally determined and influenced by personal experience. What something means depends on one's relative position in regard to it. This change is generally referred to as a systems view of reality. Although the objects themselves are still viewed as permanent, what they mean is now seen as constructed. Variables are seen as inevitably interdependent rather than independent, causality experienced as cyclical rather than linear, and boundaries of objects as open and flexible rather than closed depending on one's definition of what is to be considered within a system or outside. At the Individualistic stage 4/5 (E7), the importance of context and point of view first enters as a crucial element into people's thinking and understanding. A main concern of this stage is to lay bare the assumptions embedded in the conventions absorbed at earlier stages. Stage 4/5 (E7) represents systematic operations in the General Stage Model. The Autonomous stage 5 (E8) expands cognition to metasystematic operations as it actively integrates different frameworks or subpersonalities into a coherent new self-theory that coordinates different even contradictory subidentities into a core self.

#### The Postautonomous Level of Postformal Development

At the second level of postconventional development individuals come to realize that all objects are human-made constructs, especially such abstract constructs as the ego, three-dimensional space and time. All are based on a reification of the underlying phenomenological flux and on layers upon layers of symbolic abstraction. Even the concept of a concrete object such as "an ordinary chair" is an enormous simplification of actual experience: No two people's mental picture of a chair is the same and no two chairs in the world are identical. The concept we usually have in our minds is only one of many layers of possible ways of knowing a chair. Entirely different descriptions would come from a chemist or a carpenter.

As described earlier in this chapter, the framework developers posit one postautonomous stage, the Integrated stage E9 (I-6), for development beyond the Autonomous stage. To systematize the theory and eliminate the acknowledged vagueness of stage E9, two stages were introduced to replace it: the Construct-aware stage 5/6 (C9) and the Unitive stage 6 (C10). These were based equally on high-end SCT data and conceived of by extrapolating from the existing pattern of alternating differentiation and integration of the previous sequence in ego development theory.

In general, people at the postautonomous level tend to experience and view reality as an undifferentiated phenomenological continuum, as Unity consciousness, the creative Ground, "das All," the Tao, or whatever other terms human beings have coined to express their intimation of the underlying unity. Some of the anticipated concerns of men and women at these two highest stages of ego development are (a) to deal with the fundamental paradoxes in human nature, (b) to grapple with one's need to make order out of chaos by abstraction, classification and reification, (c) to face one's unavoidable automatic mental habits, and (d) to realize that "biases" are universally embedded in the way we create meaning via language.

Since the proposed revisions of ego development theory and measurement at the postautonomous level are being tested, earlier ego stages below the Autonomous stage are not discussed.

Below, an overview of postconventional ego development is first given in order to provide a framework for the individual higher stages. A summary of the Autonomous stage and a more extensive description of the two stages following it in the scheme are then provided. The Autonomous stage is included in order to highlight how postautonomous stages differ from it.

### The Autonomous, the Construct-Aware and the Unitive Stages

#### The Autonomous Stage: Stage 5 (E8)

Persons at the Autonomous stage realize that they may notice different conflicting aspects of themselves at different times but, unlike persons at stage 4/5(E7) who may despair about ever knowing who they really are, Autonomous individuals become able to "own" more of the contradictory parts of themselves. They can integrate previously compartmentalized sub-identities of the self into a coherent new whole or core identity. The crucial new element is generativity, the commitment to generate a meaningful life for oneself through self-determination, self-actualization, and self-definition—the hallmarks of an Autonomous person. A strong, integrated ego with mostly mature defenses is the basis for such commitments as well as the experience of being part of a meaningful community of similarly committed and principled people.

Autonomous individuals have much insight into themselves and into others. They therefore tend to believe that human beings can be known even across cultural boundaries because, according to their experience, underneath people are essentially alike. Because Autonomous persons have found a relative balance between inner and outer, body and mind, thought and feelings, they generally display high self-esteem on SCTs. They are convinced that higher development is better and closer to truth. They are therefore often invested in helping others to grow. Higher is believed to be better because the more differentiated and the more autonomous persons become, the more they can claim that they have a nondistorted (true) and realistic view of themselves and the world.

**The Autonomous or Strategist Stage 5 (E8)**
*   **Life purpose:** Realm: Society, others with similar values, like-principled.
*   **Time frame:** own "history, lifetime"
*   **Cognition:** metasystematic operations, general systems view, internalized
*   **Preoccupations:** own development, self-actualization, self-fulfillment; process, holistic view, body-mind integration (centaur)
*   **Positive equilibration:** well-being, high self-esteem, autonomous, tolerant, insightful, growth-oriented; committed to universal principles
*   **Truth:** can be approximated, higher stage is better since not all arguments and positions are of equal quality. Higher is thus more adequate to deal with complexity.

#### The Construct-Aware Stage 5/6 (C9)

Construct-aware adults start to wonder about the meaningfulness of more and more complex thought structures and integrations such as can be imagined with a fifth or nth person perspective. They start to realize the automatic nature of human map making in the representational domain and understand the logical loops and recursions that one can get into when trying to be as accurate as possible within the language-mediated realm. They are becoming aware of the absurdities to which unbridled complexity and logical arguments can lead. Some writers are wizards at creating such complex, nestled absurdities. An example from a collection of poems is given here to illustrate how self-perspective taking can be carried to the extreme.

> Jack sees Jill can't see Jill can't see and that Jill can't see Jack can see and see he sees what Jill can't see she can't.
> Jack tries to get Jill to realize that there may be something she can't see she can't see. (p. 68)
>
> Jill thinks Jill sees it and can see that Jack thinks Jill thinks she sees it but Jack thinks Jill can't see she can't.
> Jill thinks Jack can't see he can't see what he thinks he can, and that he can't see (therefore) that she sees what he thinks he sees but doesn't. (p. 82)

Adults at the Construct-aware stage realize that the "ego" has functioned both as an integrator for all stimuli (process of meaning making) and as a central point of reference (product of permanent self and object world). Once they realize this fundamental egocentricity, it may be felt as a constraint to further growth. Postautonomous persons often reject the self-centeredness and self-importance of the previous stage as they realize their relative personal insignificance in terms of the totality of human experience. They yearn to transcend their own ever-watchful, conscious egos. From past encounters they have come to know of a state of being (peak experience) which is fundamentally different from all previous ways of knowing. It seems to contain the answer to their yearnings.

During peak experiences one is no longer the center of one's world construction as at prior stages, but just a witness to oneself as an experiencing being. The paradox of being a rational, separate individual locus of consciousness while also feeling interconnected and part of a deeper, non-individualized, all-pervasive consciousness, is one of the existential conflicts of the Construct-aware stage. It exemplifies the tension of the polar opposites residing in the human breast that has been pondered over when exploring human nature.

Construct-aware adults sometimes struggle to find a balance between feeling their unique self-experience and concomitant sense of importance in life, and seeing themselves just as a minute speck in the eternal scheme of things. They are keenly aware of the paradox, but incapable of finding a position from which it can be synthesized. They live with great inner tensions. They may become preoccupied with notions of noncontrol, tolerance and acceptance as they find themselves trapped in desiring to be free from desire, and intolerant of intolerance. People at earlier stages do not register such intractable paradoxes or double binds and therefore are not expressing them on their SCTs.

At the Autonomous stage 5, individuals think of themselves as separate individuals with the mission to individuate or actualize themselves. They routinely think, feel, assess, label, coordinate and interpret experience from what seems to be a stable, enduring core self. Individuals at the Construct-aware stage 5/6 develop a more critical stance towards these automatic processes. They begin to understand that much of their mental habits are "programmed" and automatic. They realize, for instance, that concepts and their definitions are based on arbitrary conventions that make reality appear fixed and static in ways it never is.

Once the fluidity of experience is realized, the ego can no longer unconsciously organize coherent meaning from experience, but becomes aware of itself as an organizer and as a temporary, though necessary and useful construct. This stage is therefore sometimes referred to as Ego-aware as well as Construct-aware. Only after a stable, autonomous self-sense is present, can adults become fully aware of the human activity of meaning making through the arbitrary delineation, objectification and labeling of reality without loosing their footing. Now concepts can be seen for what they are: as illusions—as effective but nevertheless arbitrary maps.

On the one hand, Construct-aware adults know empirically and intuitively that there is no clear subject/object separation, no either/or, yet they are stymied by trying to transcend this state of affairs. At the Autonomous stage 5 (E8), the identification with polar opposites is still sequential (on waking I am cross, but after tea I am more civil) or context-dependent (I seem to be patient with Peter, and driven at work). Now any dichotomies are integrated, and non-defensively appreciated as two sides of the same coin (I am both cross and civil at the same time), because each concept only exists through the other. Construct-aware individuals understand this to be true mostly on a rational level, however, and a desire to identify themselves generally with the positive side of the paradox can be heard in their SCTs.

As people become able to perceive and process more information from more and more diverse sources, they become increasingly aware of how complex all phenomena and relationships among them are. Starting at the Autonomous stage, people try to describe this experience through ever more complex matrices of abstract thought, reflection and interpretation. The sudden increase at the Construct-aware stage of responses that fit the higher complexity rule (Rule 2) for compound sentences is probably a result of this intensified search for accuracy and a defense against a budding sense of reality's elusiveness.

At the Construct-aware stage, people show a heightened awareness that the mental habits of thinking, expecting, defending and fearing are problematic in themselves. While the Autonomous ego effectively, but unconsciously, coordinated its rich experience to support a balanced self-sense, the postautonomous ego is no longer sure that it is—and wants to be—in control. It sees itself trapped by automatic mental habits which, on a deeper level, it has found inadequate. How does this realization bear on one's self-understanding?

The framework developers offer no examples or explanations for this kind of cognitive insight into linguistic conditioning and mental habits. At their stages E8 (I-5) and E9 (I-6) persons take for granted that proper reflection and rational analysis will eventually lead them to discover the underlying true self. To the contrary, it is argued that at the postautonomous level, rational thought is no longer accepted as a given, but becomes itself an object of intense questioning and exploration.

Some Construct-aware adults know that there are no unproblematic formulations for describing one's self-experience. For one, trying to define oneself is felt to be self-limiting. Secondly, the previous sense of an enduring, underlying core self is now seen as each individual's personal and temporary self-narrative or guiding fiction in need of constant revision. Such insights constitute a new level of cognitive differentiation beyond the Autonomous stage, which are not recognized as a possibility in the theory. At the Construct-aware stage 5/6 (C9), the previous way of self-understanding is actively rejected and a new kind of self-knowledge sought.

At this juncture, people become entranced by the inner world and the workings of their minds and start to watch the intricate proceedings with great curiosity and absorption. Stage 5/6 (C9) persons don't just think about thinking; that feat is possible earlier in development. Now they want to understand the mechanics of how they think and interpret reality while they are doing so. More importantly, they realize how impossible it is for them not to think and analyze at every waking moment. Thus, the question of identity changes from a question with a possible answer to a question about such questioning itself. The question "who am I truly and fundamentally?" (the search for identity) changes to "what is the meaning behind the experience of continually yearning for a more accurate self-definition?" To some adults the paradox of trying to find out the nature of thought by using thought becomes obvious. Others realize (a) the historical process of creating a coherent self-identity through successive approximations and (b) the futility of ever getting at the source through further intensive self-analysis.

Ego-aware individuals intellectually recognize the pattern of forming self-identities by consecutive approximations driven by the underlying human need for certitude and stability. At the integration following the Construct-aware stage, the essential instability of self-representations is both experienced and acknowledged on a deeper level. The ability to abstain from automatically trying to explain everything characterizes self-understanding at the Unitive stage.

Postautonomous individuals may also wonder about their own hubris, their tendency for overvaluing the second half of the equation. They may become disheartened about the depth of their ego attachment and the seeming impossibility to go beyond it through effort and vigilance. Moreover, they realize that learning and knowing more about themselves and about reality in the rational mode does not lead to greater insight and wisdom for themselves. Nevertheless, because they know that striving for more self-knowledge is part of the human condition, they truly appreciate others' need to make sense of their lives within their own means. They therefore have a deeper tolerance for others' solutions to life without being blind to their obvious shortcomings or ignoring the profound differences among people as those at the Autonomous stage tend to do. Sometimes they express a sense of envy at the simplicity or innocence (not-knowing) of those less aware because the burden of knowledge and insight may weigh heavily on them.

Nonetheless, as Construct-aware adults they are capable of arriving at a positive equilibration within these fundamental conflicts: They fulfill their perceived or chosen destiny independently and courageously in full realization of their basic despair and aloneness. They may learn to embrace their fate (karma) whole-heartedly.

Since postautonomous adults are, by definition, already autonomous and familiar with the possibility of self-deception and defensive maneuvers, they do not need to deny the value of the concept of the self and of rational behavior once they recognize their limitations. Instead, human mental activity and language are acknowledged as critical tools for human adaptation and interaction, but no longer unexamined and taken for granted as they were earlier. In the representational realm, we humans always create paradoxes. By labeling, comparing, measuring, analyzing, and predicting, attention is inevitably taken away from what really is, to what we presume there is.

When individuals realize that their sense of a coherent self has been built on a partial, filtered perception of reality, they naturally desire to experience the underlying, unfiltered reality. They want to be liberated from the bondage of rational thought and the pseudo-grounding afforded by the representational self. They wish to be free from restricting self-definitions. They desire to see life afresh, without the preconceived ideas and mental behaviors that were supported by the social-linguistic environment and perfected through acculturation and lifelong habit. But they also become aware of how difficult it is to transcend one's unconscious and ingrained habits of the mind especially since a predisposition for mental behavior appears to be hardwired.

Construct-aware adults seem to realize that their self-identity is always and only a temporary construct. Hence, they become less invested in the idea of an individual ego that serves the unconscious function of creating a stable self-identity. They see through the mental habits of analyzing, comparing, measuring, and labeling as a means to reify and map experience. They understand the need for a different approach to knowing, one that responds to the immediate, unfiltered experience of what is. This new way of knowing requires an attitude of complete openness: One that is free from wishing for any particular outcome, and free from the automatic habits of representational thought. Paradoxically, the very desire for freedom from any particular idea of how the world should be, keeps one fettered within that frame of reference. A genuine and radical openness—an openness that is completely detached from any desired outcome—is the essence of a different mode of experience. In short, the next step requires that one's yearning for a stable self-identity be let go and that one's accustomed way of growing, knowing and learning be fundamentally transformed.

#### The Unitive Stage 6 (C10)

The Unitive stage represents such an entirely new way of perceiving human existence and consciousness. The previous way of viewing reality almost exclusively through the lens of one's separate personhood is transformed. The new paradigm is multiperspectival and universal. Unitive individuals experience themselves and others as part of ongoing humanity, embedded in the creative ground, fulfilling their evolutionary, karmic or divine destiny depending on their orientation towards the ultimate.

An autonomous, well-integrated ego is the prerequisite for the development to unitive forms of self-cognition. It has been said concisely: "You have to be some-body before you can be no-body" (p.11). Autonomous/Integrated individuals see themselves and are usually experienced by others as "somebody." They show high self-esteem. Construct-aware individuals become aware of the anthropocentric self-importance of the earlier stance. They may feel torn between high and low self-esteem. As this paradox has been expressed, "[Man] is nothing compared to infinity, and an infinity compared to nothingness." Construct-aware individuals feel competent and powerful when comparing their highly developed mental capacities and their ability to understand the complexities of life with those of most others. But, at peak moments, when they see through the illusion of the stable, independent self and the dysfunctional, unconscious aspects of rational behavior, they may feel annihilated, that is "like nothing." The drama is the more salient, because at the Construct-aware stage individuals have achieved a measure of ego-maturity that many outside observers would tend to admire. However, Construct-aware subjects know that they are fettered within deep-rooted mental habits that prevent them from developing the different kind of self-experience and way of knowing that they yearn for. Struggling valiantly to relinquish control, desire, and attachments, they inevitably fail. For the essence of the new way of understanding are effortlessness, non-control, non-attachment, and radical openness.

Unlike people at previous stages, the Unitive person no longer feels a need to reach after fact and reason. "Objective" self-knowledge no longer satisfies the need for constancy as it does for the highest stages in the theory. Instead, unfiltered experience or the perception of ongoing process, rhythm and flux provide inner stability and affirmation.

The self-sense of the Unitive stage is fluid, "undulating," based on people's trust in the intrinsic value and processes of life. For those at the Unitive stage, one can truly speak of a different, higher stage integration according to the axioms of cognitive-development theory. It also requires a new vocabulary to describe the experience, a feature that was considered as a possible sign of a distinctive stage (p. 11). At the unitive stage, a whole logical/psychological system (the rational, representational domain in human development) has become the content—an element or object in a higher-order, postrepresentational integration.

In Unitive self-experience, individuals see through the function of the ego to objectify and reify the self by defining (delimiting) it. They experience the self in its moment to moment transformation and therefore consciously decline to satisfy the implicit demand for objective self-identification. They understand that the striving for individual permanence is an impossible and unnecessary dream in the face of their experience of the continuous change in states of awareness.

They also see the ego with its striving for independence and for permanent, objective identity as just one way among others of how one is conscious of being. Thus, the representational self has been deconstructed and given way to a whole new mode of perception.

In contrast to the Construct-aware stage 5/6 (C9), adults at the Unitive stage have replaced habitual, conscious mental processing by immersing themselves in the immediate, ongoing flow of experience. They can consistently maintain an awareness of their passing thoughts, feelings, behavior, perceptions and states of alertness. They


---


# The Unitive Stage

At this level of development, individuals function primarily as non-judgmental witnesses to their own ongoing process of becoming. They observe the various roles they play in life as secondary to their core state of being. 

This stage is characterized by a timeless, infinite frame of reference where the primary preoccupation is a non-controlling, conscious witnessing of the flux of experience and mental states. The positive equilibrium achieved here involves an accepting, unassuming presence—a full empathetic ability to be with whatever arises without ego-defending or interfering. Truth is experienced as immanent; it is the experiential reality of interconnectedness and non-separateness, where existence is understood as changing states of awareness within a timeless spirit.

Their openness to ongoing experience, combined with a conscious refusal to reify and codify that experience, makes this stage fundamentally and structurally different from all previous stages of ego development. Furthermore, people with this fluid, transcendent sense of self appear free from the anxiety accompanying the "not-knowing" that characterizes earlier stages. Rational awareness or consciousness is no longer perceived as a shackle, but simply as another phenomenon that moves between foreground and background depending on momentary focus.

By this stage, individuals no longer consciously attempt to overcome rational mental habits. Instead, they have relaxed enough to remain open to naked experience and mental activities as they unfold. The paradox of feeling both related to others and entirely separate and unique is now integrated. These feelings are experienced as changing perceptions and equal manifestations of being, without undue tension.

At this level of integration, adults can observe themselves and other beings simultaneously from multiple points of view, shifting focus effortlessly among various objects of attention and states of awareness. They appear to operate within a cosmic time frame that embraces all of history as well as the future. They feel deeply embedded in the processes of nature: birth, growth, death, joy, and pain are all viewed as natural occurrences—patterns of change within the flux of time.

## Open Boundaries and Attunement

Persons at this stage transcend narrow ego-boundaries. They possess open boundaries and exhibit "attunement awareness," which is an explicit immersion in the ongoing, indeterminate process of being. Truth is seen as immanent in the universe and can be apprehended through this ready, open-process stance, but it cannot be grasped by effort or purely rational means.

Crucially, from this unitive perspective, higher developmental stages are not viewed as "better" than lower ones. All stages are recognized as necessary parts of an interconnected reality and an overall evolutionary process where everything is exactly as it should be. 

These thinkers also accept themselves "as is" in a non-controlling manner. Regardless of how great their achievements might be, they remain aware that these are merely a drop in the vast pool of ongoing human endeavors. Furthermore, no matter how often they may fall short, they do not dwell on their failings but simply move on into the next moment.

The reality they relate to most profoundly is the undifferentiated phenomenological continuum, the creative ground, or unified consciousness. Every object, word, thought, and theory is recognized as a human construct—a way of separating out and creating boundaries where none inherently exist and where none need to exist. 

The quest for meaning and connection is understood as an essential aspect of the human condition. Individuals at this stage feel interconnected with others because they understand and share that fundamental condition. Due to this unitive ability, they are highly tolerant and compassionate, feeling an affinity with all expressions of life. They respect the essence in others and therefore do not need them to be different than they are. They can appreciate the absolute perfection of the simplest organism and marvel at the beauty, power, and diversity manifest in creation.

For these individuals, peak experiences no longer possess an out-of-this-world quality; rather, they have become a habitual way of being and experiencing. The present is experienced as the space where past and future interpenetrate. Total openness releases them to be in tune with truth and beauty, allowing for visionary experiences—the ability to comprehend things holistically rather than solely through the filter of the rational mind. 

Expressed differently, individuals at this stage can access reality directly and "immediately," as well as through symbolic representation. The key difference is their constant awareness of both modes. While individuals at the preceding stage do experience unitive awareness spontaneously, they do so only sporadically or when deliberately invited through meditation or other consciousness-altering practices. Because of their ability to concentrate on their own internal processes, such "flow" states may happen more frequently than at conventional stages. While access to the numinous and altered states of consciousness is possible from all stages through various gateways, individuals at this unitive stage begin to sustain the perspective as a consistent home base.

The concepts of this stage can be refined to eventually become part of one unified field containing all possible supertheories, theories, paradigms, systems, and elements at whatever order of abstraction and complexity, both within and beyond human perception and imagination. This unified field is the source and end-state beyond which no further development or more all-encompassing field exists. It is the uniquely human naming process that pulls out (abstracts) from this unified field continuum that which is inherently not separable or distinct.

The assessment framework can only discover signs of a beginning unitive frame of mind; it cannot "measure" it in the same way it measures preunitive stages, because the framework itself is based on language. Individuals identified at this stage are likely to have inklings of this kind of knowing—an initial familiarity, rather than the most profound awareness possible. The path from early to fully realized unity is acknowledged as a long and arduous one.

## Summary of Post-Autonomous Stages

The portraits of the hypothesized post-autonomous stages were drawn from theoretical considerations and empirical responses. The guiding questions for analyzing these responses were: What view of reality would prompt a person to make these statements? Given the knowledge of the evolving self and the autonomous stage, what would a transformation fulfilling strict higher-stage criteria look like? Is it possible for the pattern of increasing differentiation-integration in the rational domain to reach a logical limit beyond which further transformations no longer matter? What would a transformation of the entire constructivist developmental pattern look like?

The proposal to add two new stages—a construct-aware stage and a unitive stage—to the upper end of the developmental theory answers these questions. Self-identifications at these two stages are structurally different from previously established highest stages, and from each other. In each case, the dominant structure of awareness of the lower stage becomes an element in the next stage. 

First, individuals become conscious of their own development. They can look back and describe the transformations of their self-understanding over time by becoming aware of the mechanics by which they construct meaning and reality. In a step beyond that, they become able to transcend the symbolic realm and immerse themselves in the fluid, non-evaluative mode of unfiltered experience.

With the postulation of the construct-aware stage as a missing link, the inner logic of the developmental theory can be mapped from the earliest stages to the unitive stage. The evolving perspective on the self demonstrates one possible underlying logic for this development. Additionally, increasing awareness of the power and dangers of language plays an important role in post-autonomous understanding. Only at this level do adults fully understand that every concept and theory is based on an arbitrary but useful anthropocentric segmentation of the underlying unified field or creative ground. Thus, describing individuals' growing awareness of the reification process in meaning-making offers an additional way to characterize developmental differences.

Each of the two new stages hierarchically integrates the previous way of meaning-making. The construct-aware stage represents the highest observed self-view in the rational, symbolically mediated realm of cognition. The unitive stage, with its fluid, deconstructed self-view, appears to sit at the threshold of the realm of post-symbolic, metaphysical, or transpersonal consciousness.

## Overall Patterns in Development

Development can be described in terms of two patterns: the regularly expanding perspective on the self, and the continuous renegotiation of the balance between differentiation and integration at each successive stage. 

An overall shift from increasing differentiation to increasing integration marks the transition from conventional to post-conventional stages. The first two tiers of development consist of a movement away from symbiotic union toward increasing differentiation and separation of the self. The post-conventional stages show an overall trend of assimilation and integration toward a new conscious embeddedness in, or union with, an ultimate reality.

During the early tiers, the budding self learns to know, define, and establish itself through ever-finer segmentations of the originally undifferentiated inner and outer world. The acquisition of the third-person perspective enables people to see themselves for the first time as fully separate persons; they become self-conscious. By extrapolation, they also become aware of others as selves with unique, separate personhood. 

Simultaneously, the unconscious linguistic mechanism of reification—which automatically transforms learned concepts into objects existing in the world independently of the observer—is most pronounced at these middle stages. "Objectivity" (the denial of subject-object interrelationship) is a necessary condition for the abstract reasoning and scientific, rational analysis that becomes fully developed at the conscientious stage.

The pattern of increasing differentiation from newborn symbiosis to adult abstract, analytic, logical functioning may be superseded by a movement toward greater deobjectification and reassimilation at the post-conventional stages, culminating in the openness and self-transcendent awareness of the unitive stage.

The change from a linear paradigm (perspective) to a concept of field (vision) between the construct-aware and unitive stages is a striking characteristic of this conception of development. The pattern up to the construct-aware stage can be visualized as a growing branch structure, with the self at the center and layers of increasing ramifications at the periphery. 

In general, human beings navigate life by searching for ever-more adequate representations of who they are and what the world looks like. The shift to the unitive stage represents a quantum leap in experience and outlook, discontinuous from the previous pattern of gradually reframing and expanding one's perspective. An unbounded neural network or a field that contains within itself many theories and models of possible definitions of self and reality better represents the multidirectional, multiperspectival, and non-egocentric mode of those functioning at the highest stage. The transformation from the linear expanding rational paradigm to the transcendent view can be likened to the change from an earthbound perspective to one from a spaceship in orbit, contemplating earth as a tiny part of the cosmic panorama.

## Conclusion on Theoretical Structure

By introducing the construct of the "perspective on the self," the developmental theory can be seen as akin to a hard structural theory. This perspective can be logically mapped and figuratively represented throughout the scale, fulfilling the structural demands for a stage theory: the stages follow a hierarchical sequence, and the same construct is used to show its transformation throughout the sequence.

Replacing the previously established highest stage with the construct-aware and unitive stages provides greater understanding of development at the post-autonomous level. The construct-aware stage serves as the missing link in a genetic epistemology covering the personal realm of experience. The sequence follows a pattern where each stage logically subsumes the previous stage as an element in its new meaning structure.

Increasing awareness of the constructed nature of reality characterizes the post-conventional stages. In its most ambitious expression, the theory postulates that development may lead naturally and logically to intimations of ego-transcendence.

However, there have been few signs in the assessment completions that people develop beyond a preliminary though explicit awareness and tolerance for uncensored perceptual input. The unitive stage cannot be defined more clearly from the gathered evidence, as the instrument is inadequate for distinguishing between the wisdom of mature integration and authentic transcendence as defined in theories of higher consciousness. Thus, the unitive stage is best considered a threshold position between the personal and transpersonal tiers.

Developing through the full range of stages and concomitant transformations may be regarded as an inevitable, legitimate, and non-meditative path through the gates of rationality onto the path of higher, post-symbolic consciousness. The radical openness to unfiltered witnessing, the absence of anxiety in the face of "not-knowing," and a fluid self-understanding provide evidence for this hypothesis.

Several factors suggest the unitive stage is a gateway for a whole new cycle of possible stages. Empirically, adults at this stage have a beginning understanding of the transcendent realm. Awareness of the desire for unity, embeddedness, or permanence is sometimes experienced as a source of disequilibrium or subtle attachment. Other limitations are inherent in Western notions of time and space, and distinctions between waking reality and other consciousness states. Whether a person has fully transcended ordinary consciousness cannot be determined within the constraints of the sentence completion assessment; only through in-depth interviewing and physiological tests could one ascertain how consistently these individuals embody a witnessing awareness that remains alert throughout sleep and waking reality.

The developmental theory has no terminus; it is open to further revision and refinement. This view is consistent with the semantic argument that all thoughts and theories are constructs—useful maps for orientation, but alterable to fit new insights. However, the theory does have a terminus if one looks at the increasing perspective on the self and takes it to its logical conclusion: development ends at the point where the process becomes aware of its own need for self-perpetuation through self-representation—when it becomes transparent to itself.

Evidence makes it plausible that adults can become conscious of these inner processes and mental habits. Some reveal their recognition of the limits of the rational mode of understanding. Even though conscious of what is happening, they may or may not experience the letting go that is the prerequisite for a transcendent, immediate experience of what is.

## Scoring Categories: Construct-Aware Stage

The scoring categories were developed from a database of over 1,900 protocols, isolating responses that showed greater complexity, demonstrated insights into the limitations of thought or self-perception, or evidenced awareness of the constructed nature of reality. The analysis involved asking what central theme or preoccupation was present, what beliefs were questioned, the complexity of sentence structure, and what kind of experience and view of reality would make the response possible.

There are four categories for the construct-aware stage.

### Category 1: Complex Matrix

The first category is structural. Persons at this stage feel compelled to give responses that objectively mirror the complexity of their reality perception.

**Description:** Complex matrix of possibilities, reasons, feelings and contrasts, often rational, abstract, distanced in an effort to accurately describe the complexity of reality as perceived.

These completions are often several lines long, employing various stylistic devices and punctuation marks to reflect the perceived complex matrix of experience and handle nestled arguments.

**Examples:**
* "When I am with x woman—how can I generalize? Sometimes I am over-whelmed, sometimes bored; sometimes incredibly aware of a sexual electricity, often times primarily involved in business dealings; sometimes arguing, sometimes enjoying her company and sometimes wishing I were somewhere else."
* "A good father—accepts the individuality and limitations of his children, recognizes that they too have problems, and manages to be sympathetic at a distance."
* "A wife should—develop herself according to a fairly sure list of priorities which will enable her to prepare for her own future as one in which she will be respected and loved for the real good she has done in her life and the lives of those dependent on her."
* "A woman should always—be as true to her principles & ideals as she can be if she is to avoid tremendous incongruence—of course she can't do that, and there are no objective shoulds—we all do whatever we can/must."

With increasing understanding, people feel a greater need to express themselves comprehensively, often writing in margins or using tiny script to convey their internal complexity.

### Category 2: Immediacy and Conflict

**Description:** Immediacy, experiencing and expressing conflict, paradox and contradictions non-defensively, without needing to resolve them.

This category captures the ability to express the actual thoughts and feelings of a person as they occur. Unlike earlier stages that put problems into a good light, here conflict and contradictions are expressed directly without apology or hindsight.

**Examples:**
* "Crime and delinquency could be halted—oh fuck it cant be halted; it can possibly be ameliorated, and the question seems trivial as I sit here in a country where terrorists are killing whole villages and leveling homes with bulldozers, and (...) army officers are possibly raping (...) women—anger and despair are responses to these realities, and to crime and delinquency too."
* "When people are helpless—they particularly need our compassion but may well find precisely this the greatest burden of their helplessness."
* "If my mother—could not be so frightened of the dark things in life (death, sorrow) she might feel less pain, or at least pain that she could get a handle on (or pain her daughter could watch her deal with more to her daughter's comfort.)"

### Category 3: Paradoxes and Thought Processes

**Description:** Aware of existential and psychological paradoxes, thinking about own thinking process and habits, aware of loops, recursions and logical paradoxes as inherent in any symbol system (not as instances) stream of consciousness, free associations, comments on various levels of own awareness or experienced lack thereof.

This category covers aspects of conscious awareness from states of mind to the inevitable incongruities discovered in reasoning. Although the limits of rational thought are experienced, the hope and need for explanations remains evident.

**Examples:**
* "At times he worried about—anything and at times he worried about nothing, and the worry was in his mind not in its object."
* "I am—sensitive, honest, striving to always love others, reflective, sometimes to the point of being unable to get out of endless loops, striving to take responsibility for myself individually as well as trying to improve the world."
* "I feel sorry—for all of those who cant understand or accept those different from themselves; and for myself when I must include myself in this group."
* "A man should always—look for the larger implications than just his own point of view, particularly when he is most sure of the absolute rightness of that point of view."
* "The thing I like about myself—my concern to be honest with myself, a claim that may itself be a delusion."
