### Category 4: Constructed Nature of Reality

**Description:** Explicit awareness of perceptions, definitions, labeling, assumptions, frame of reference, paradigms and structure in meaning making. Aware of the constructed nature of self and reality.

People at this stage realize that labels and definitions are arbitrary conventions that make reality appear fixed and static. They recognize that words have neither a single nor an objective meaning, nor a separate definition independent of the relations of equivalence and contrast among them. They realize definitions are not fixed, are constructed over time, and exist only against the backdrop of other words.

**Examples:**
* "A man's job—is traditionally been the role of 'breadwinner', but such simplistic labeling ignores an almost infinite number of possibilities, limited only by that man's conceptual model of himself and his world."
* "I feel sorry—(meaning remorseful) is often confused with 'I feel sorry' (meaning sympathy)—both important to feel, to speak, and to clarify."
* "I am—a confusing complex of contradictions—both good/bad, assured/self-doubting, aloof/warm at the same time. There are no easy formulations to the way I am."

When respondents mention how they construct meaning, they show awareness of the constructed nature of everyday reality. They can imagine multiple realities, not just multiple interpretations of the same reality.

**Further Examples:**
* "When they talked about sex, I—compared the way they viewed it (sex) with the way I viewed it. Did they see it as Beautiful? Normal? Base? It was a reflection on their view of the world."
* "Rules are—artificial constructs that we sometimes use to explain (away) reality or to control (the flow of) events—and as our creation, they may thus exist, change, or not exist as we wish I need [sic]."
* "Education—means, to me, at this moment, an unfolding process of coming to make sense of the world."

At this stage, polar opposites reside within the individual, and which side is focused on is understood as a matter of framing reality, not reality itself.

**Example of framing:**
* "The worst thing about being a woman—is accepting your position as a woman and an individual, but once found ceases to be the worst and becomes the best."

The idea of underlying unity is understood on a rational level, and the evaluative mode prevails. The frustrated desire to explain reality while aware of the illusion of order explains the tension often found in these protocols.

## Scoring Categories: Unitive Stage

There are five categories for the unitive stage. Once adults see through their need for logical coherence and explainable order as means to perpetuate their sense of self-permanence, a palpable affective shift occurs. Freedom from excessive categorizing allows a new freedom towards being and witnessing.

### Category 1: Wide Range and Positive Affect

**Description:** Wide range of thought on human relationships (self not as center) often with unique, positive affect and gratitude.

This new way of knowing is characterized by a sense of admiration for the marvelous way life already is, at every moment, in all its expressions.

**Examples:**
* "Raising a family—has been and is a wonderful experience. The fact of growth, progress, evolution is right there before your eyes in a very intimate way."
* "I feel sorry—for those who have not learned how to appreciate the good & bad experiences in life & look forward to each day of life with the feeling that it is wonderful to be alive."
* "When I was younger—I didn't know so much about this world, therefore I didn't grieve about its problems so much and didn't appreciate and wonder at its marvels."
* "The thing I like about myself—is the way I have developed a keen sense of humor—gift of my mother—while simultaneously maintaining a philosophical perspective of my relative position in the universe, obviously a gift from my father."
* "If my mother—has one abiding hope, it is that her children continue to grow toward grace and perfection."

### Category 2: Tolerance and Acceptance

**Description:** High tolerance, acceptance of self and world "as is," openness to life, change, process, rhythm, self in flux.

This category speaks to the freedom from judging and the experience of life as flux, where nothing stays still.

**Examples:**
* "The thing I like about myself—... a very good question, one I'm trying to answer every day! My adaptability, my ability to accept (and even embrace) change."
* "My conscience bothers me if—upon reflection, I find that I wasn't open to all the possibilities of a situation—if I did not see the full reality, not feel the flow and therefore tried to control the outcome."
* "Sometimes he wished that—he always remembered that life is always this wonderful even when the more underlying challenging events of his life temporarily shade the permanent foundation of happiness and growing fulfillment that are his reality."
* "Raising a family—can break your heart and be wonderful too. One sometimes learns almost too late to accept and love and not try to change."

### Category 3: Universal Connectedness

**Description:** Universal connectedness; profound expression of self as part of larger world, humankind, history, ongoing creation.

This describes a sense of connection that goes beyond the personal realm (family, race, nation) and becomes trans-personal, embedded in historical times or a reality outside individual embodied existence.

**Examples:**
* "Women are lucky—they have always shaped children through the ages, and therefore the world."
* "A man feels good when—he acts in a responsible, caring manner towards the world and himself, accepting good and bad as part of the whole."
* "Being with other people—is awkward at times, pleasant at others, transcendent when we glimpse beyond the shifting surface to the depths of a common humanity."

### Category 4: Fundamental Reflections

**Description:** Fundamental feelings, thoughts and reflections about human existence, faith, life, death, joy and suffering, conscience and consciousness, mystery of being etc.

Responses here address themes at their most fundamental level of meaning, requiring cognitive complexity to articulate.

**Examples:**
* "Usually he felt that sex—had been removed from its relation to love and life, & used as a medium to promote sales, & he felt deeply resentful of it & its contribution to the dehumanization of people."
* "Women are lucky because—as a broad generality, they seem to be more in touch with the realities and eternal verities of life."
* "Being with other people—is the very substance of life itself. It may be invigorating, simulating, wearing or frustrating—but is rarely boring. The range of human behavior and interaction is endlessly intriguing."
* "The worst thing about being a woman—is struggling to make our voice heard in the world—our values expressed + realized in action, our power felt and celebrated."
* "The thing I like about myself—all of myself at different times. My ability to appreciate pain and see humanness in the world's destructiveness."
* "When people are helpless—I'm ambivalent. I think maybe the idea of being helpless is one of the most horrifying to all of us on a fundamental level."

### Category 5: Unitive Thought and Metaphor

**Description:** Instance of unitive thought or metaphor: shifting focus effortlessly between near and far, mundane and sublime, temporal and eternal, serious and ridiculous, individuated and transcendent self as well as fluid transition between different states of consciousness.

This represents the ability to simultaneously perceive the particularity and universality of a fact—seeing it as here and now, yet also eternal. Persons at this stage can take multiple points of view and shift focus instantly.

**Examples:**
* "At times she worried about—the fate of the world—would this little blue marble, suspended in space, ultimately self-destruct?"
* "I am—someone and everyone, special and ordinary, one tiny part of vast cosmic system who has the potential to make a difference."
* "When I am criticized—praise and blame are alike & indifferent to me - as a revelation of what I am, but valuable as a revelation of another's feeling."
* "I am—at times a question mark, at times just a period, but many times an exciting exclamation mark."
* "At times she worried about—money, health, the state of the world, and whether her son needed new shoes right now."
* "Usually he felt that sex—was anything from a dirty game to a lofty expression of a beautiful relationship depending on who 'he' is."

This category differs from the complex matrix category by exemplifying the ability to shift focus effortlessly among many perspectives, rather than offering panoramic surveys from a single vantage point.


---


# Chapter 3: Postautonomous Ego Development: Its Nature

## Section 1: Methods

### Introduction

Having finished with the preliminaries, the focus now shifts to the actual studies that form the center of this research. So far, the need for conceptualizing postautonomous ego development differently from the framework developers has been established. Ego development theory was located within the broader context of adult lifespan development literature, especially constructivist developmental theories. Problems at the upper end of the original ego development theory and its measurement instrument were pointed out in some detail. As potential solutions to these problems, an alternative interpretation of ego development theory and a modification of the instrument at the high end were described. It was suggested that the amorphous Integrated stage might be profitably replaced by two stages, a Construct-aware stage and a Unitive stage. With the introduction of this refinement at the upper end of the scale, the goal was to systematize ego development theory and to clarify the psychologic behind the transformations of meaning making that seem to occur after the Autonomous stage.

Both the theoretical and the measuring problems with the original highest stage appear to have been carried over into the recent Second Edition of the manual. Thus, it seems fitting at this point to test the plausibility of the proposed alternative interpretation of high-end ego development empirically and ascertain the content validity of the suggested scoring categories for measuring postautonomous ego development. The reliability of the coding categories will be tested in the next chapter.

To organize the reporting of these several studies, they are divided into two chapters reflecting the different overall approaches. This chapter describes the qualitative approaches used to probe the content validity of the proposed high-end stage distinctions and scoring categories and then discusses the outcomes of the inquiry. The subsequent chapter maps the multiple quantitative ways in which measurement issues were explored and tested, and reports those results.

In the current chapter, the hypothesis that postconventional ego development is characterized by an increasing awareness of the constructed nature of reality as well as a concomitant, step-wise deconstruction of the assumptions underlying conventional views of reality and the self is first tested. To accomplish the first goal, selected sources in the postconventional and transpersonal psychology literature are analyzed. How these texts characterize postconventional development overall, and postautonomous development in particular, is investigated. Second, the specific agreement that exists between the characteristics given in these theories for postautonomous development and the thematic categories for identifying high-end ego stages introduced in the previous chapter is traced. In effect, the goal is to demonstrate the content validity of these themes by posing the following research questions and exploring each in a different way:

1. To what extent does the conception of postautonomous ego development seem consistent with the conceptions of mature development in the current literature about the mature self, particularly in postconventional constructivist and transpersonal psychology?
2. In addition, how well do the proposed scoring categories for identifying the two postautonomous ego stages concur with the specific attributes given for postautonomous development in the chosen literature?

To answer these questions, current notions about postconventional development will first be summarized. Next, the rationale and the resulting criteria by which the theories for the qualitative analysis were chosen will be specified. Section three of this chapter contains the summaries of the selected theories addressing aspects relevant to the inquiry into the overall conceptualization of postautonomous development and discusses their significance. Fourth, to answer the second question, these sources are analyzed for the presence of the specific content themes that are used in the proposed scoring categories for postautonomous sentence completions. Finally, the result of this second inquiry will be reported and its implications discussed.

### General Proposed Features of Postconventional Ego Development

It may be helpful here to recall the two different dynamics that can account for the trends observed at the postconventional tier. Postconventional development can be looked at from two seemingly contradictory perspectives. On one hand, postconventional development continues the pattern of alternating stages of differentiation and integration toward greater complexity that can accommodate the ever-expanding experiential universe of the self. Thus, one's knowledge of reality becomes broader, deeper, and more adequate as fewer and fewer of the discrepant stimuli need to be fended off in order to preserve the integrity of the current meaning system. Increasing complexity and differentiation are the hallmark of this dynamic. On the other hand, postconventional development can be explained as a stepwise deconstruction of the previously unconsciously constructed permanent object world. It is suggested that these two lenses are complementary and necessary for describing different aspects of postconventional development.

There is another important difference between the two trends. While the layers of cognitive abstraction can be logically extended to the nth degree or ad infinitum (or ad absurdum), there is no additional benefit in meaning beyond a certain point as cognitive complexity alone does not account for deeper insight into the human condition. As one researcher remarked:

> Abstractions are already so far removed from actions in the real world that further developmental levels might well be not merely useless, but maladaptive.

On the other hand, the second dynamic looks at meaning making itself from a metaperspective and thus it can account for the awakening of wisdom in relation to cognitive complexity.

### Increasing Cognitive Complexity

In some form or other, all postconventional theories posit a systems stage or a dialectical form of thought. Unlike formal operations, which treat parts of systems as separate, the systems approach takes into account the interdependence of parts within a system and the interdependence between a system and its context. There is also a branch of science called "systems theory" which deals with phenomena from a systems perspective. However, a distinction is made between systems theory and a systems view because the latter considers aspects of meaning in addition to the structure and mechanics of complex interacting systems.

A systems view allows people to recognize their own cultural conditioning and participation in interpreting reality. Things mean what they do because of our experience with them in given personal, cultural, and historical contexts. In a systems view, researchers cannot be "objective," but are always participants in what is observed. Moreover, the whole system is considered to be greater than the sum of its component parts. In human affairs, systems thinkers look at relationships rather than isolated individuals and aim for plausible interpretations rather than objective truth and certainty. They do not merely rely on the ever-increasing arsenal of conventional scientific methods, but describe and investigate the means by which they arrive at their hypotheses (heuristics). There are two main types of systems theories. Whereas theories that take a dialectical approach seem to focus more on the processes by which knowledge is being transformed and acquired, theories that postulate a systems view take the interdependence of variables as a starting point and focus on the nature and the outcome of the proposed transformations.

Most theories with a systems view concentrate on the first postconventional level. They postulate that the self is not an isolated entity but part of a system. It is socially constructed and in a dynamic relationship to its contexts. In the fields of sociology and anthropology, a systems view is now commonly seen as more adequate in describing the self than is looking at separate individuals. The narrative focus in literature and therapy also stresses a self-conception that is constantly reconstructed in language acts and social interactions. One researcher makes a powerful argument against the notion that we can ever gain a non-distorted, non-fictionalized view of ourselves. He points to the continuing tension between the modern tenets of a knowable, objective reality and the subjective enterprise of human meaning making as a fundamental problem of our times.

In the more organismic, interdependent notions of the self, boundaries are less rigidly drawn between subject and object. Although most adult developmental theories allow for more dialectical, context-sensitive interpretations of reality in adulthood, they still consider the search for a stable, unified self-identity as the goal of individuation, one that can integrate many of the subtler tensions and inconsistencies that become apparent with greater cognitive complexity. As pointed out before, the framework developers, especially, see the attainment of an objective, integrated identity as the goal of development. They equate their hypothesized highest stage vaguely with a set of characteristics introduced to describe self-actualizing persons.

### Stepwise Deconstruction of Permanent Self-Identity (Object Permanence)

Evidence from the sentence completion tests indicates that for some adults the search for an integrated, enduring self-identity is superseded by a more immediate, fluid and process-oriented self-view. It seems that the systemic view can give way to a completely flexible, non-reified notion of self in a rare transformation beyond the systems view of reality. In one interpretation of the fifth order of consciousness, for instance, adults at the interindividual stage are described as having such a process-oriented experience of the self. Few additional Western constructivist developmental psychologists have tried to conceptualize the frame of mind of individuals who go beyond a systems view. This broader epistemology allows people to see through their tendency to reify reality and the self and to feel most alive and genuine in an indeterminate process of witnessing, and ultimately of being.

In contrast, transpersonal psychology has long advocated a dereified, dematerialized view of reality as the goal of development. Several researchers have all put forth theories that the permanent self is an illusion. Both Buddhism and Vedic schools of human development argue that the separate, independent ego can be transcended in favor of an immediate, direct experience of a higher Self.

### Summary

It is suggested here that describing postconventional development in terms of a stepwise deconstruction of the constructed aspects of our symbolically mediated views of reality leads to a more comprehensive understanding of mature development than looking at increasing structural complexity only. In this view, postconventional development outlines the disidentification from the culturally supported fiction of the separation between knower and known. It has been argued elsewhere that seeing through one's cultural and linguistic conditioning is a logical outcome of paying attention to the processes of meaning making that can lead one closer and closer to the transcendent realm of intuitive, non-representational ways of knowing.

Becoming aware of how we automatically acquire our different cultural programs for interpreting reality seems to be a first step towards mature insight. Beyond that, becoming aware of how cultures, in general, depend on natural language to create their respective realities seems to be an additional and important step towards the eventual transcendence of the symbolic mode of reality perception.

At least in theory, the decoupling from one's automatic and exclusive symbolic mediation of experience allows one to reconnect with the underlying reality in a fuller and more direct way. This different way of apperception has been explored in the field of transpersonal developmental psychology. A common theme among transpersonalists who try to describe the metarational realm of higher stages of consciousness is the paradox of having to communicate the "ineffable" of the subtlest inner experiences through the filter of language.

### Choosing Relevant Theories

Having given a general background for how postconventional adult development is generally approached, the criteria by which the developmental literature for this research was chosen are now stipulated. Choosing a small number of representative theories that were likely to inform the inquiry seemed reasonable since looking in the most likely places for confirmation or disconfirmation of one's ideas or hypotheses is more efficient and effective. If one wants to catch a rare tropical fish, it is best to start in the tropics in an area where similar fish have already been caught. Thus, the review is not meant to be comprehensive and may pass over a number of theorists or theories that also speak to the questions.

As argued earlier, only a constructivist developmental approach is likely to view human development in terms of a stage-like progression in which each stage supercedes its predecessors and subsumes the former way of generating coherent meaning into a larger, more comprehensive perspective. Thus, it is to these theories one must turn if one is interested in exploring meaning making in its most complex manifestations.

Constructivist developmental theory holds that development is unidirectional. Persons progress from lower to higher without skipping any stages. Each stage builds on the foundation of the prior stage and becomes the basis for the next transformation. The stages are not just unidirectional, they are also hierarchical as each stage subsumes earlier development under its new, overarching system. In addition, if we look at how many adults reside at each of these stages regardless of the number of stages postulated, the distributions tend to be bell-like. There are considerably more individuals in the middle stages than at either of the extremes. Based on numbers collected from many research projects, most adults (about 80%) reside in the second tier of development whereas no more than 10% to 15% are estimated to inhabit the third tier. Neither the Subject-Object Instrument nor the sentence completion test, however, can distinguish between very advanced self-development in the personal realm and achievement of ego-transcendent insights. Since the stages develop in sequence and take time to traverse, it is assumed that the most mature stages are rarely reached before mid- or late adulthood—if at all.

It matters whether one focuses on development within the personal realm (the first three tiers of development) or whether one conceives of development beyond the personal realm (tier four). In the first case, one is satisfied if one can formulate a reasonable trajectory for the development from the infant's undifferentiated being to the insights articulated in the voices of society's most thoughtful and mature representatives. In the second case, only the postulation of an ultimate non-personal Self seems to do justice to the depth of realization possible. The "wisdom of the ego" seems a mere shadow on the wall compared to the wisdom or the witnessing capacity of the transpersonal Self. Precisely because such transpersonal theories see the personal realm itself as a less developed form of knowing than the transcendent perspective, they have likely articulated the limits of such lesser understanding more clearly than those who cannot stand outside of it.

Thus, theories which posit no development beyond the second tier in adulthood or theories that are based on college age students are unlikely to contain material for this inquiry. Theories that restrict their postconventional development to a systems view are probably also unproductive. One theory addressing women's ways of knowing is an example of this type of theory. It addresses the need of knowers to understand their own contribution to interpreting reality, but it does not question the underlying assumptions of the meaning-making process itself.

Because the interest was in broad differences in the ways people organize their understanding of life, domain-specific theories were also omitted. This includes theories of moral development in spite of their groundbreaking influence in shaping the field of postconventional psychology and their postulation of stages of "postprincipled" moral reasoning.

Ego development is a personality theory that encompasses many of the domains that others have studied separately. Thus, the first general selection criterion—the construct of self or ego as an overarching principle in development (or non-domain specificity)—was chosen for optimal comparability with the original ego development theory.

The second criterion—postautonomous content—excludes all those theories in the field of postconventional development which focus on describing either the transition from conventional to postconventional development or those who are dealing with systems-type meaning making only. Some material that came from theories about human growth that are not strictly stage theories, such as schemas of dialectical thinking, was also included. Nonetheless, these were chosen because they show sensitivity to the questions raised. The third criterion is a focus on meaning rather than reasoning alone, although level of awareness regarding one's reasoning habits can be in the service of deeper understanding.

To answer question one, it was first examined whether the overall hypothesis about the postconventional tier of ego development is supported by other researchers writing about stages of advanced human development. In particular, it was verified whether others observe that people become increasingly aware of the constructed nature of reality and as a result start to question and deconstruct their formerly held assumptions about the permanent object world and the construct of an enduring self. The theories of a group of constructivist developmental researchers were analyzed. How each theorist conceptualizes postconventional development overall and describes his/her highest stages was examined. In particular, it was noted what themes and forms of reasoning they believe occur after systems thinking has been incorporated.

Because psychologies spanning all four tiers of development are more likely to have a well-defined perspective on the postautonomous segment of the developmental continuum, representatives from two different transpersonal perspectives were also turned to. The goal was to see how they depict mature personal development and to what degree the concern with the illusion of the constructed nature of reality is shared between them. Therefore, how these theories define self development at the high-end of the third tier in the personal realm and to what extent the categories matched their accounts of the nature of advanced ego development was analyzed.

Although spiritual or ego-transcendent aspects of development figure prominently in these transpersonal theories, ego-transcendent stages per se were not looked at because they are outside the scope of this thesis. Thus the basic strategy was to establish the content validity of the postautonomous stage distinctions and categories by anchoring them in the above literature on the mature self and by evaluating to what extent the way of conceiving of postconventional ego development differently from the framework developers is akin to other current views of mature development in both constructivist developmental and transpersonal psychology.

A conceptualization of postconventional ego development as a deconstruction of the formerly constructed permanent self is problematic if there is little consensus among the selected authors as to what the most mature self-views in the personal realm look like. The notions about postautonomous development can be readily proven false if there is evidence that individuals at earlier ego stages are capable of making the kinds of distinctions at the required level of complexity that, it is suggested, are reserved for the most advanced meaning makers.

In summary, whether the notions of postautonomous ego development are plausible will be explored by concentrating on theories that (1) posit an ego or self as a "general" organizing principle across domains, (2) investigate reality conceptions beyond the general systems stage, or show signs of postautonomous concerns, and (3) focus on understanding rather than reasoning alone.

## Section 2: Review of Theories with Postautonomous Elements

### Postconventional Theories

The following is a selective and more detailed review of the authors whose writings were examined for both their overall view of postconventional development as well as for specific themes and signs that characterize possible postautonomous development. Each of these theories was evaluated along the following parameters. First, how does it parse postconventional development into stages, what is its overall view of postconventional development and what main distinctions does it make to assess postconventionality? Second, how much is it itself aware of the distance between the level of formal abstraction in which the theory is expressed and the underlying reality it attempts to describe?

To highlight the distinction made earlier between postconventional and postformal models, the review will begin with a general model of hierarchical complexity. While the postconventional models explore issues of epistemology and meaning, this model focuses on structural aspects of higher cognition.

#### (1) The Metasystematic and the Crossparadigmatic Stages in the General Model of Hierarchical Complexity

In 1984, a general stage model of development was first introduced. It was intended to unify the budding field of postformal psychology by providing a rigorous theoretical scaffold for the many then emerging strands of postformal (neo-Piagetian) adult development theory. Although the general stage model has not gained the discipline-wide acceptance that was hoped for, it does serve as a guidepost for other theories in terms of structural rigor.

This model is the quintessential postformal model since it describes logical operations and relies on mathematics to describe its findings. Although not a theory of the development of the self, it is non-domain specific, it posits two stages beyond a systems view, and it covers some postautonomous concerns. Even though its focus is on reasoning rather than meaning, it is included here because of its claim of being a metatheory, superordinate to all others in the postconventional field.

By setting up clearly delineated, hierarchically ordered problems or tasks, the model was operationalized and systematized to be internally consistent and follow the same logic of differentiation across the entire developmental sequence. People are rated to be at the highest stage at which they can successfully solve stage-specific cognitive tasks. The relationships among the stages are expressed in formal mathematical terms. Thus, the output of the processes of one stage become the input for the processes of the next stage in a much more precise and systematic way than is possible in more qualitative theories.

The current model and its scoring system claim to be a universal system that can score anything since it can be applied to "almost any text or discourse in any domain." According to the developers, the stage sequence is grounded in the hierarchical complexity criteria of mathematical models, information science (signal detection theory), and analytical behaviorism. "Hierarchical complexity" refers to the number of recursions that the coordinating actions must perform on a set of primary elements in a task. The tasks are uniquely defined for each stage and follow each other in a strictly sequential order. A statistical method is used to assess the coherence of responses at any order of complexity and the degree to which transitions between orders of complexity appear to be stage-like or discontinuous.

Overall, the model postulates four stages beyond formal operations. Two represent systems views of reality (systematic; metasystematic) and two represent postsystems views (paradigmatic; crossparadigmatic).

The systematic stage allows one to see and describe the properties and transformation within a system; the metasystematic stage deals with relating whole systems to each other, culminating in the formulation of a metasystem or framework for coordinating several systems. The crossparadigmatic stage, finally, coordinates or unifies whole paradigms or frameworks of knowledge into a superordinate field. The latter two take the forms of "super-metasystems and fields."

Examples given for the highest two stages are taken from the history of science. The scoring system rates those scientists highest who created new fields of inquiry by crossing previously separate scientific paradigms into new wholes. One scientist is given as an example—along with others—as a representative of the crossparadigmatic stage. According to the researchers, this scientist belongs to the crossparadigmatic group because he created the new field of the "limits of knowing" by integrating mathematics and epistemology.

The disconnect between theories of the growth of cognitive-logical sophistication and theories concerned with the individual's living understanding of reality as described by ego development theory, is evident here. One wonders how these personalities would have fared on a sentence completion test or in a subject/object interview. In light of what is known about these scientists' lives from the many biographies about them, it is doubtful they were particularly insightful about reality, other people, and their own inner workings. It is clear that crossparadigmatic achievement in science does not necessarily translate into mature ego development.

Unless the developers can show that their structural analysis can pick up the subtleties of a deconstructed, fluid view of reality in which the rational, symbol-mediated realm is considered a useful subset, but not the end-all of making sense of things, they can hardly claim their system can be applied to "almost any text or discourse in any domain." At the very least, one would want the exceptions in "almost" more clearly spelled out.

If we are interested in what it means to be a human being, we want to be able to account for how different people make sense of experience on a lived level. The quality of insight cannot be assessed by an analytical model like this one. If we want to specify and assess the formal properties and the logical consistency of stage theories, then this model fulfills a useful role.

This model was introduced here for two reasons. On the surface, it fits the selection criteria. It was also intended to emphasize one of the difficulties in choosing theories. Despite its claims that it can rate anything, the scoring system is unlikely to do justice to the kind of thought that characterizes the Unitive stage. The model is reminiscent of concerns of people at the Construct-aware stage because it favors complexity and aims at rigor and logical consistency. It is unlike Construct-aware thinking, however, because it remains unaware of its own unquestioned attitude towards the means by which it makes its arguments. It never inquires into the adequacy of formal mathematical notation and statistical analysis as means to describe aspects of experience. They are simply taken for granted. Map and territory are considered commensurate. Because the model does not question the limits of the means by which it makes its claims, it cannot serve for the second part of this chapter which seeks to validate the thematic content of the postautonomous categories in theories with postautonomous content.

Thus, the focus turns next to the framework developers whose theory is concerned with meaning and therefore offers an opportunity to clarify some of the contrasts.

#### (2) The Autonomous and the Integrated Ego Stages in Ego Development Theory

The original ego development theory and its measuring instrument, the sentence completion test, have already been introduced in abundant detail. To repeat, the ego development theory fulfills the criteria stipulated earlier for relevance to this thesis. Ego development is a constructivist developmental stage theory. It is non-domain specific because it posits the multifaceted construct of ego as the central processing and synthesizing faculty. It focuses on the changing meaning at each subsequent stage, and it includes a stage beyond the Autonomous, the Integrated stage. Thus, it is really postautonomous, reaching into the second level of the third tier of development.

Ego development theory seems to resonate with many people, especially its description of the phenomenon of the search for identity and the many changing forms it takes. It has a quality of verisimilitude or closeness to life which a theory like the general model of hierarchical complexity lacks though it may be more rigorously defined and theoretically coherent.

The current conception of the final stage has been analyzed in much detail. According to the framework developers' own assessment, it is ill-defined, and there is no consensus as how to rate it even among experienced raters. As a consequence, there are very few examples given for this stage and several of these are simply combinations of content and reasoning that is available at earlier stages. Nonetheless, some of the examples given in the manuals do fit the criteria for a higher stage rating. To make this point, several concrete examples from the manuals were included in the previous section in which the scoring categories were discussed. Below, the portions of the theoretical characterization of the Integrated stage that call for more attention according to the proposed categories are indicated with italics:

> Characteristics of Autonomous responses that are prominent at the Integrated stage, usually in combination, are existential humor and a feeling for paradox, respect for others' autonomy, search for self-fulfillment, value for justice and idealism, opposition to prejudice, coping with inner conflict, reconciliation of role conflicts, appreciation of sex in context of mutuality, and *(1) reconciliation to one's destiny*. Some form of conceptual complexity is always present, whether in terms of conflict, contradiction, alternative construction of situations, or subtler complexities (such as the idea of potential for development or of *(2) intolerance of prejudice*). Responses at this level are often vivid, touching, or poetic. Many unite the specific and the general, and *(3) concern for inner life (presented as differentiated self-perceptions)* and outer life (usually relationships with others). Generally, responses combine two or more thoughts that would separately be rated at the Autonomous stage.

(1) Only the themes of "reconciliation to one's destiny," and the ability to "unite the specific and the general" are part of the categories for the Unitive stage which were independently derived from a different data set than the Integrated stage features.

(2) Although, at first glance, the theme of "intolerance of prejudice" seems to fit a construct-aware category which identifies the awareness of inevitable paradox as a sign of postautonomous development, there is no sign that the framework developers are actually aware of the internal contradiction that a recognition of one's intolerance for intolerance (prejudice against prejudice) generates as the following example for the Integrated stage indicates:

> I feel sorry—for the disadvantaged, particularly the negro to the point I am outraged and intolerant of prejudiced people.

It seems that they simply mean that expressing one's distaste for prejudice can be a sign of Integrated development although issues of social justice begin to appear at the conventional stages.

(3) "Concern for inner life (presented as differentiated self-perceptions)" could be a salient indicator if it was made clear in which way this concern is addressed differently at the Autonomous and the Integrated stages. The examples given in the new edition do not allow such distinctions. The example above about a person's outrage at inequities does not show any deeper or additional self-perception than is possible at the Autonomous stage. The same is true of the next case:

> The thing I like about myself is—that I let my "child" out; that I don't waste time judging the individuals I meet; and that I think independently and creatively.

This kind of relishing of one's independence from conventionality is symptomatic of the Individualistic stage. However, does it show concern for inner life and differentiated self-perception? The rush to non-judgment "I don't waste time" seems to describe a person who is not very aware of her own tendencies. Seeing oneself as a system of "fixed" subpersonalities (child, parent, etc.) in the vein of transactional analysis occurs as early as the Conscientious level. An integrated rating seems unjustifiable on psychological grounds. Based on the complexity rule, an Autonomous rating would be considered at best.

The only unique characteristic given for the Integrated stage is "the problem of identity." It was initially assumed that this meant that postautonomous individuals actually questioned their preoccupation with identity because of the phrasing. However, there is no evidence in the sentence completions that any other definition of self was ever considered than that of a durable category or objective self-identity. "The problem of identity," therefore, merely refers to a heightened awareness that one has not, as of yet, gained that desired, mature, balanced, non-defensive self-identification one envisions as the Integrated example below demonstrates:

> My mother and I—get along, but I need to get away to develop myself. I praise her because she is a woman, and a person. I am a person but not yet a woman.

This young woman has a clear idea what it means for her to be a woman. She will presumably recognize and be satisfied when she reaches that goal. Yet evidence from the sentence completion test attests that individuals can develop a perspective on their own meaning making that allows them to realize that self and reality are being continuously reconstructed in an "illusive" effort to attain an objective, coherent "unified" self-view.

As already mentioned, the original theory cannot account for people who become interested in a dynamic, fluid self-experience. Nor can it explain responses that show that an individual is attracted to modes of reasoning that draw energy and insight from the very irreconcilability of polarities and ambiguities. Such themes do appear on sentence completion test protocols and seem to be recognized as features of advanced development in other theories.

It is possible that the kind of postautonomous responses that were observed did not appear in any of the samples from which the original ego development theory and the instrument were constructed because of their rarity. Lack of data at the extremes is an inherent source of error for empirically-driven developmental theories, which can only be as comprehensive as their data. In addition, the researchers' own limited awareness (rater bias) is addressed as another possible reason for not recognizing existing nuances and cognitive differentiations:

> To the extent that ego development is a hierarchical variable, the difficulty we have of understanding, describing and judging the highest stage is plausible. In principle, a person does not fully understand the relevant thinking of people much above his or her own.

Whatever the reasons, and they are likely multiple, the original ego development theory fails to make coherent distinctions beyond the Autonomous stage. The Integrated stage does not constitute a qualitative transformation of earlier forms of meaning making. For the most part, the rationale given for including a response at the Integrated stage is unclear or unconvincing. In the previous section, the few cases in which examples for the Integrated stage do seem to capture criteria that parallel the post-autonomous distinctions were noted.

#### (3) Dialectical Thinking as a Metasystematic Form of Cognitive Organization

Although a full-scale development theory is not offered, a complex matrix of what are called schemata or movements in thought was devised to describe mature ways of reasoning. According to this framework, "the dialectical schemata framework" is "a cognitive psychological conception of dialectical thinking."

The schemas range from the end of tier two (formal operations) through most of tier three although no endpoint is given. It is argued that dialectical thought as a whole, especially the meta-formal aspects of it, constitutes a metasystematic form of understanding reality since it is concerned with the relationships among variables. The schemata are non-domain specific and describe how people differ in their reasoning about a broad topic. However, meaning making is central to this intellectual enterprise:

> Dialectical thinking can be understood as efforts at comprehension relying on application of an implicit model of dialectic, developmental transformation via constitutive and interactive relationships, to the phenomenal world.

Rather than proposing a stage sequence, dialectical thought is organized into 24 general moves-in-thought or cognitive schemata. These schemata are drawn from recorded interviews with college age students and their professors. They thus include the voices of mature adults. The schemata are classified into motion-oriented, form-oriented, relationship-oriented ones, plus a higher-order group that integrates the other three by "describing relationships among forms, and transformations through forms, as well as the process of form construction."

These schemas address eight out of the nine scoring criteria or postautonomous concerns as defined. Infinite regress in logical arguments and awareness of the constructed nature of knowledge are two of the most common issues that came up in the interviews.

Dialectical thought is differentiated from other types of postformal thought by focusing on the organizing principle or "process by which structured wholes, forms, or systems, evolve and change." Dialectic is defined as a transformation (= movement through forms) in which the relationship between the notion of form and the notion of movement are central, that is the relationship itself "has a role in making the parties what they are." This is illustrated with the definition of the concept 'road': "If no vehicles traveled on the road, it would not be a road. (...) Thus, its being a road depends on its particular relationship to vehicles."

This is a fine example of the kind of awareness emerging at the Construct-aware stage. It shows an understanding that concepts are interrelated, depending on each other for their existence and focuses specifically on the complex problem of definitions.

The following are some of the schemas that address the issues of greater complexity and of the constructed nature of reality. Both schema 4 and schema 15, for instance, deal with definitions. They make explicit that the meaning of any assertion, concept or object of reflection depends on all of the things to which the assertion, concept, or the object is related to and differentiated against (including their background and negations).

Schema 7 points out problems with objectifying or reification, that is, of treating as separate and static, entities that are part of interrelated, dynamic processes. It speaks to our tendency to see named objects as external givens rather than as constructs. It is further suggested that people who are aware of this pattern can avoid objectification and reification by:

> using language in such a way that one is likely to maintain awareness of motion, process, or constitutive relations in general, and of the processes of human activity and mental abstraction or construction in particular. This is difficult in the English language, since the use of the nominative case, which is required by the grammar, carries connotations which often result in all of the effects described ... above. (...) One can often detect an effort to avoid hypostatization by the awkwardness of the language entailed.

Schema 6 deals with the practical, actively-constructed nature of knowledge and its function as a map of "uncharted complexity" and thus clearly speaks to the map-territory issue. It is classified as a motion-oriented schema because people can become self-consciously aware of how thinking about their thinking influences the course of their thinking. Thus, thought can become "aware of the possibilities for change or motion within itself."

Among the meta-formal schemata, 16, 17, 18 and 23 refer to contradiction and paradox as means to transforming (transcending) established mindsets by challenging the person conscious of them to a "developmental resolution as a transformation to greater complexity." The basic rhythm of thesis-antithesis-synthesis of schema 1 is, of course, the same whether applied to single instances or to whole systems of thought, which is the case for the meta-formal schemata.

Unlike the framework developers, it is realized that:

> (...) disequilibrium is valued over any particular form of equilibrium, since it provides a source of transformation which is capable of freeing one from the limits with which any particular form of equilibrium is viewed as necessarily associated.

This preference for uncertainty reflects the attitude of the person who is conscious of the paradox of living in language. The very awareness of the inherent tension points beyond it. Thus living in paradox feels freeing and vital compared to any kind of closure which is seen as limiting and deadly by the construct-aware person.

Though all dialectic schemata have been located in the general realm of postformal thought, the hunch is that they could be shown to be typical of several different stages, some emerging as byproducts of formal operations, others becoming salient only towards the end of differentiation in the postformal realm. In summary, some schemata described seem to parallel the criteria found to characterize postautonomous protocols. Overall, the schemas become more complex with higher list numbers. At the same time, dialectical thinking includes a deconstruction of formerly held static and rigid understandings of events, and reinterprets them as context-dependent, interactive, multiply determined. Dialectical analyses "draw attention to the limits of the context in which formal analyses are applicable."

In this formulation, the general model of hierarchical complexity would not be dialectical because it does not draw attention to the limits of the context in which its analyses are applicable. Becoming aware of the limits of the means by which one formerly made sense of experience is a feature of postautonomous psychological differentiation. In addition, empirical evidence showed that professors tended to use dialectical schemas in general more often than their students do. They also employed more of the more advanced schemas, while graduate students, in turn, used more dialectal schemas than their undergraduate peers. Thus, as predicted, the more advanced schemata (i.e. those most in line with postautonomous insights) seem to occur with greater life experience and at an older age. They show not only greater cognitive complexity, they also indicate an increasing awareness of the constructed nature of reality, and of our tendency to reify what is in flux. Thus, these observations support the contention that the mental moves that created earlier truths are deconstructed at later stages of development.

#### (4) The Interindividual Stage

The theory of the evolving self is probably the one theory most similar to the original ego development theory overall, in that the self as a meaning maker is accompanied from its beginnings to its most mature expressions. It postulates that the "subject-object relationship" is the "general underlying principle... which organizes" how one makes sense of cognitive, affective, interpersonal and intrapersonal experience differently at each of the five epistemological positions described. Unlike the original theory, however, there is much more willingness to account for and describe not only the stages themselves, but to portray convincingly how people develop from one stage to the next. The emphasis on the victory of each stage over the previous way of meaning making, and the inevitable price for development that must be paid, gives the works of the two theories a very different feel.

This theory is more psychologically nuanced and comprehensive than the original ego development theory for the following reasons: It addresses both the ontological question of what it means to exist in this world and the epistemological question of how we make sense of our existence. It explains how we construct meaning by embracing ever more of our external and internal experience. It empathetically portrays the changes in positive experience, motivating concerns, and difficulties at different stages while it explains these changes using the subject-object transformation as the underlying cognitive mechanism. For the latter, a descriptive itinerary is provided of how meaning evolves "according to regular principles of stability and change" or "in which the output of the processes of one stage become the input for the processes of the next stage." Humans seem to evolve via a continuous dialectic between related opposites of all kinds: between self and other, between preservation and expansion, differentiation and integration, individuation and participation towards greater overall flexibility and coherence between the organism and the surround.

In its first formulation, an overall theory of ways of knowing or orders of consciousness is outlined that describes five such orders: two preconventional, two conventional and one postconventional. Each of these constitutes a higher order integration.


---


The framework developer's model incorporates the previous perspective as a component within a new, broader outlook. The proposed stages focus on relatively stable periods of meaning organization within the continuous process of development. The overall progression alternates between favoring relative independence and relative inclusion, similar to patterns observed in other models of ego development. Of the five proposed stages of consciousness, the final stage, termed the Interindividual or fifth order of consciousness, is broadly postconventional. Thus, the theory spans the first three tiers of development. The term "interindividual" chosen for this final stage suggests relevance to the concept of deconstruction, as "inter" implies "between," hinting at a permeability of boundaries.

In many respects, this theory shares similarities with other comprehensive models of ego development. Both describe the evolving self from birth to mature adulthood and attempt to provide a comprehensive sequence of stages. Recognizing that the self is multi-faceted, these theories address not only cognition but also affect and action, describing their differentiation and integration through dynamic interaction. Despite covering similar ground, the theories differ in significant ways. The instrument used to measure the orders of consciousness not only assesses the five theoretically defined stages but also identifies four transition steps between each stage. These steps are evaluated impressionistically along a continuum, ranging from initial differentiation from the old stage to shifting toward a new organizational structure. Consequently, an individual can be assessed as being at a specific stage or at one of four intermediate positions.

The postconventional territory examined in this framework aligns approximately with the highest stages and positions in other developmental schemes. Despite being conceptualized as a single stage, the theory contains components that can be considered postautonomous.

In a subsequent publication, the framework developer introduces the concept of two distinct positions between the fourth and fifth orders of consciousness: a deconstructive form of postmodernism and a subsequent reconstructive form. The antimodernist position is described as a phase of differentiation prior to integration, characterized by energy devoted to maintaining a valuable disjunction and articulating the rationale for doing so. This antimodern position parallels the oppositional stance found in other individualistic stages. It explicitly rejects the tenets of the preceding stage, which is characterized by well-guarded boundaries. While it excels at exposing the discrepancies and limitations of the previous mindset, it lacks the capacity to integrate them into a new, coherent whole.

The theory posits that approximately ten percent of adults may develop beyond conventional views of reality to an interindividual or trans-systemic order of comprehension, transitioning from a modern to a postmodern view of reality. In developmental terms, at least a postsystemic perspective is required for an individual to view universal human behaviors, such as meaning-making, as objects of reflection. Individuals at this stage realize that the logical power of any system is flawed and incomplete when viewed from the outside. They understand that every system or "way of knowing" is inherently decisive, including some elements while excluding others. Consequently, every way of knowing is simultaneously a way of not knowing. Each discipline is recognized as an ideology that offers explanatory power but inevitably advantages certain perspectives while disadvantaging others.

The realization that "each way of knowing is a way of not knowing" captures the essence of postautonomous meaning-making, addressing several aspects of construct-awareness simultaneously. It acknowledges the dynamic interrelationship between foreground and background, or between polar opposites. It accepts the relative nature and inherent limitations of any intellectual discipline. Postautonomous awareness focuses not on the content of knowledge, but on the process of how sense is made of the world.

When individuals examine their own process of observation, they realize that what appears clear and distinct can change simply by shifting focus across the visual field. The focal area becomes prominent, while the unfocused area becomes dim and indistinct. This highlights the subjective, deliberate, and directed nature of attention, thoughts, and reactions, as well as the inherent cost of any choices made. This cost involves a lack of clarity, or even blindness, in areas outside the focus of attention.

The framework developer explicitly addresses issues of boundaries, polar opposites, and the continuous process of reconstruction, valuing the ongoing challenge to absolutes and boundaries. A key question is whether one assumes the a priori existence of separate elements and systems—a formal-operational view of reality—or focuses on the process by which knowledge about reality is generated. At the fifth order of consciousness, individuals realize that any abstraction, whether a single element or a complex theory, is always a partial representation of the whole, existing only in relation to what the human perceiver has differentiated it from.

As meaning-makers, humans inevitably seek an overarching theory that encompasses all potentialities, providing certainty and closure. It is not until the fifth order of consciousness that individuals become capable of reflecting on their need for meaning and order, and their active involvement in creating these through continuous recreations or more encompassing theories.

Regarding the interdependent nature of polar opposites (any concept and its negation), the theory addresses this postautonomous concern by discussing the dualistic equivalency between the absolute ideology of the institutional self and the absolute antiabsolutism of the deconstructive postmodern stance. Absolutism and antiabsolutism form a dualistic pair, much like dualism and non-dualism. The framework points to a superordinate possibility beyond dualistic splitting, where the tendency to split is internalized as an expression of one's own partialness and incompleteness.

According to the theory, it is this awareness of partialness, continuously extended to larger contexts, that drives the human endeavor of meaning-making toward wholeness and the transcending of boundaries. Similar to other perspectives, the theory maintains that individuals with a reconstructive mindset find vitality in dismantling every new conceptual boundary that enters awareness, viewing closure and fixed boundaries as restrictive. It can be assumed that individuals with this insight have developed a fully postautonomous stance toward experience. While one might argue that the purpose of reconstruction—creating increasingly better theories—is to eventually arrive at a complete theory, a truly reconstructive view would likely associate such a final "victory" with stagnation or death. As long as life continues, the process of reconstruction must also continue.

The framework developer is acutely aware of the reifying tendency embedded in language, which often frames reality as static, separate things rather than ongoing, indeterminate processes. This is highlighted by pointing out the dual meaning of the word "meaning" as both "making sense" and "mattering or being of consequence." This distinction summarizes the core of the theory in both its epistemological and ontological dimensions.

It is more difficult to locate within this theory the movement from an initial relativistic reaction against cultural conditioning to the more profound doubt characteristic of the construct-aware stage. Based on sentence completion data, it appears that some individuals develop a balanced stage between the absolute relativism of the antimodern position and the postautonomous stance anchored in the dialectical process of continuous construction, deconstruction, and reconstruction. At this intermediate integration, individuals recognize that the antimodern stance is a reaction to the rigid boundaries of formal operations, but they do not yet fully grasp the fleeting nature of all reality construction. Instead, they resolve the resulting uncertainty through reasoned choices and commitments, reminiscent of concepts like "developing commitments" or principled moral reasoning.

Individuals at this reconstructive equilibrium believe they must and can generate and live by their own overarching theory, principles, and self-created identity. Unlike earlier stages, this stable self-system is metasystematic, allowing for multiple connections, nonlinear causality, and feedback from other systems in creating its version of reality. It provides a new, self-guided inner stability and relative certainty. The autonomous self represents such an intermediate balance. Individuals at this stage consciously strive to form a stable core self by integrating new awareness while steadfastly holding onto a belief in "the" self and aspiring to actualize it.

However, individuals at this intermediate equilibrium cannot yet relinquish the notion of an overarching self-system. They cannot fully examine the processes of meaning creation or the universal temptation to objectify reality by actively defining it. They are still searching for a coherent identity. While they may accept the philosophical premise that life is fundamentally motion, they remain seduced by language into experiencing themselves and the world as static entities that move. In the terms of the theory, they still believe in their "somethingness," albeit in a sophisticated, dynamic way. This "something" represents a well-articulated, complex, committed, and relatively tolerant self that is aware of its interdependence with other selves.

Unlike other models where the highest stages remain at this intermediate balance, this theory postulates that individuals at the interindividual order of consciousness can become aware of their reifying tendencies. Furthermore, the most advanced individuals are expected to relish and draw vitality from the awareness of the partialness of all views and interpretations of reality. Thus, the theory supports the proposition that advanced development includes both complexification and the continuous deconstruction of prior assumptions at increasingly deeper levels of recognition.

Unfortunately, the theory and its associated measurement instrument do not provide actual examples of the highest, interindividual stage. However, two hypothetical examples constructed by the test makers, rated close to but below the highest stage, exhibit characteristics of postautonomous awareness. The first example involves a reflection on what it means to be helpful:

"No matter how exciting my ideas are, my holding them so dearly actually deadens my vitality; it drains the juice that makes me alive, even the me who invents the ideas to begin with."

The researchers interpret this self-reflection as indicative of a unitive conception of reality, suggesting that life is viewed as a continuous flow of experiencing and participating. Being stuck is seen as falling out of this flow, and the goal is to return to it. While the test makers conceive of a way of knowing that remains in flux, the hypothetical test taker is still struggling to remain in motion, remaining "loyal to the beach." In postautonomous terms, this represents a typical construct-aware struggle: having intimations of a fluid way of being but still being drawn to the safety of the familiar self, accompanied by a yearning to remain in the flow.

The second hypothetical example is even closer to the posited interindividual stage:

"I almost have to keep from letting myself get too involved with my first take on things because, whatever actual merit it may have, I'm in danger of making it into the truth. And I also have a tendency not to want to see some other whole way I'm actually operating or feeling, because I might get pulled into making that the truth."

In this example, the speaker is fully conscious of her absolutizing tendencies and remarks on her own mental habits. By attempting to counteract the first tendency, she realizes she creates a new stumbling block.

The concept of variables as constructs leads to the notion of illusion. All human-made distinctions are part of reality but do not encompass all of reality. Ordinary waking reality is seen as an illusion because individuals mistakenly believe their identifications and distinctions are reality itself.

Another theorist specifically addresses the shared boundaries of polar opposites, noting that one cannot delineate "good" without simultaneously delineating "evil." This highlights the interdependent nature of basic dichotomies and the automatic dualistic splitting that occurs when naming the world. From a top-down perspective, reality is viewed as neither one nor many, neither infinite nor finite, neither whole nor part; everything is interconnected. This represents the final deconstruction of the permanent object world and the concomitant release of the concept of a permanent self, characteristic of the unitive stage. While individuals at systems stages can investigate the content and cultural assumptions guiding their beliefs, those at unitary stages become aware of the underlying process of meaning-making itself.

The portrayal of the unitary stage as one where individuals become fully conscious of the constructed nature of objects and their boundaries, and recognize the essential unity of the universe, goes further than many other adult development theories in deconstructing the formal-operational view of reality and illustrating the limits of reasoning based on rational, symbol-mediated knowing.

In summarizing the analysis of non-domain-specific models of adult development, two different explanations of postconventional development emerge. The first characterizes it in terms of increasingly complex and differentiated hierarchical systems. The second posits a simultaneous, step-wise deconstruction of formal operational concepts. While some theories describe development primarily as increasing complexity, differentiation, and integration at higher levels of abstraction, others specify the increasing ability to see through the meaning-making process itself as the most salient aspect of postconventional development.

Some models, while examining the ability to reflect on whole fields of thought, take the object world created by language for granted and do not examine the premises of symbolic logic used to develop their abstract models. Other theories are also complexifying, describing the capacity to integrate more dimensions of experience, comparing advanced stages to self-actualizing persons. However, these characterizations often do not progress beyond a systems view of reality, and their measurement instruments may not account for the possibility that individuals can become aware of the constructed nature of reality.

Conversely, other models clearly consider both the increasing complexity of thought and the increasing awareness of the constructed nature of reality as essential aspects of mature growth. The trans-systematic ability to reflect on whole internally consistent systems of thought and dialectical reasoning are key features. While these advanced stages are more complex and differentiated, their most salient features are the awareness of reifying tendencies and the underlying unity and flux of experience. Some models advocate for greater integration of outer and inner territories of experience, describing a transcognitive awareness similar to a witnessing stance, focused on the moment but characterized by relentless questioning.

The necessity of deconstructing the conventional view of reality, with its linear and analytical tenets, is explicitly addressed by some theorists. A unitary view of reality is defined as preeminent, where reasoning and symbolic representation are seen as specific forms of a more integrated, metaphysical apperception of reality. This deconstruction process involves two steps: first, realizing that objects have shared boundaries, causality is cyclical, and cultural conditioning exists; second, realizing the fundamental unity of reality, the illusion of boundaries, and the reifying aspects of language. This top-down approach, akin to Eastern philosophy, describes reality as an undifferentiated whole, viewing systems theory and formal operational thought as increasing concretizations and distortions of reality.

It is noted that theorists focused on psychometrics or quantitative analysis tend to overemphasize the structural, measurable aspects of development at the expense of qualitative, content-related aspects. Consequently, they may overlook the possibility that the very means of argumentation and measurement can be questioned at advanced levels of cognitive insight. This limitation is predictable from a constructivist perspective, as a scientist's choice of topic, theory, and methodology reflects their own conception of reality. As one perspective notes, a theory is primarily a way of looking at the world, not a form of knowledge of how the world actually is.

Turning to transpersonal, four-tier models of development that span all tiers, these models provide insights into postconventional and postautonomous development. One such model, derived from Vedic psychology and introduced to the West, proposes a fully developed, four-tier model of consciousness. The fourth, non-ego-centered, postsymbolic tier is considered structurally and functionally higher than the third tier, being more adaptive, stable, accurate, and comprehensive. These higher states are viewed as a natural continuation of human development.

According to this theory, underlying all development is an abstract, silent, completely unified field of consciousness, the self-sufficient source of all mental processes. This transcendent self is non-domain specific, postautonomous, and meaning-providing. All representational processes (perception, thought, feeling, intuition) are seen as fluctuations or concrete manifestations of this underlying field. While they coexist at all times, stages above an individual's current developmental level may be too subtle to register in conscious awareness.

This theory also emphasizes the role of language in meaning-making, distinguishing between presymbolic, symbolic, and postsymbolic modes of knowing. The shift from preverbal to verbal processing is seen as monumental, paralleling the shift from personal, representational knowing to transcendent, postconceptual processing. A parallel is drawn between the conditioning required for language acquisition and the systematic training needed for postlanguage technologies to acquire fourth-tier, ego-transcendent ways of knowing. This aligns with the observation that individuals at advanced stages begin to realize how deeply they are conditioned into symbolic thought and how difficult it is to transcend it. Shared symbol systems draw awareness away from direct experience toward a filtered, culturally specific version of reality.

The model proposes a hierarchical sequence of increasingly abstract, functionally integrated faculties or "intrinsic levels of mind." These latent capacities unfold progressively, paralleling ordinary developmental stages and extending to transcendent stages. The levels are structured from gross to subtle: sensori-motor faculties, desire (early representations), the thinking mind (concrete), and the discriminating intellect (abstract and formal operations) cover the first two conventional tiers. The third tier includes two levels: feeling/intuition and ego.

At the first postconventional level, feeling and intuition reenter meaning-making as valuable sources of information for assessing the whole organism. No longer split off from reason, feeling enriches experience and supports decision-making. This level is more flexible and relational than intellect alone, involving subtler, rapid, holistic, intuitive modes of functioning less dominated by linguistic expression and sequential formal reasoning. This parallels open-system, context-sensitive, conflict-tolerant stages in other models.

The ego, as a level of mind, occupies the most advanced position in the personal representational realm and is developmentally closest to the transcendental self. It is defined as the most refined value of life, the experiencer that understands, feels, and thinks. The thinking faculty is the mind; the understanding and discriminating faculty is the intellect; the feeling faculty is the heart. This conception aligns with views of the ego as the faculty that synthesizes experience into a coherent whole, allowing for detached reflection or mature wisdom. However, the ego remains localized and limited by its inability to know itself directly, a struggle evidenced in advanced developmental assessments.

The goal of associated practices is to allow the self to unfold naturally toward its source in transcendent consciousness by freeing the individual from stress and entrenched habitual patterns. Self-actualization is considered a waypoint on the route to higher consciousness. The theory emphasizes that the capacity for higher forms of consciousness is innate but emerges only through exposure to and practice in postrepresentational ways of knowing. Continued practice fosters further differentiation within the postsymbolic realm. Cognitive growth is accompanied by the continuing differentiation of language and thought in the symbolic realm and the progressively deeper reintegration of knower and known in the postsymbolic realm.

This perspective supports the interpretation that postconventional development involves increasing awareness of the limits of rational, language-mediated meaning-making. Unlike conceptions that view the search for an objective, integrated self as the goal of development, postautonomous development leads to the discovery of the illusion of such an endeavor and the necessity for an entirely different mode of knowing. Longitudinal research indicates that individuals engaged in specific meditative practices achieve postconventional stages at significantly higher rates, supporting the hypothesis that development results from the experience of deeper, more complete levels of subjective existence.

Another comprehensive model traces the evolution of consciousness through cultural history, concluding that reality is hierarchical, composed of successively higher levels of reality (or decreasing illusion), ranging from the material plane to ultimate spiritual realization. This grand theory seeks to integrate all other explanations, positing that the universe begins and ends with spirit as the ultimate ground. It includes the concept of involution, where spirit manifests in grosser forms before returning to itself through evolution. Evolution is seen as reflecting a deep structure immanent at both phylogenetic and ontogenetic levels.

This complex model outlines nine levels of individual consciousness across three major divisions (body, mind, and spirit). Recognizing language as crucial in the ascendance of mind over body, it views numerical subdivisions as convenient mapping tools rather than inherent realities. The first five structures cover the conventional tiers, the sixth stage (vision-logic) represents the third tier, and the final three structures comprise contemplative or transpersonal development.

The model distinguishes between enduring structures (like linguistic competence and cognitive capacity) and transitional structures (like self-identity and moral stages), which are replaced as development progresses. At the core is a self-system responsible for mediating, organizing, and navigating experience. The self-system does not follow an invariant sequence, though specific lines like self-identity may unfold in a stage-like fashion.
