Vision-logic, representing the postconventional tier, integrates aspects of experience into networks of relationships or interrelated systems. Termed "panoramic logic," it apprehends how ideas influence each other as an integral whole. This stage covers several advanced developmental positions, including aspects of systems thinking and unitary thought. The model further argues for a union of science and religion, elaborating on the "linguistic turn" as a key ingredient of postrational development.


---


# The Realization of the Language Habit

The realization that our maps are not the territory, no matter what level of differentiation and complexity we achieve in mapping our understanding of the world, is a crucial insight. It addresses the understanding that words mean what they mean only within the totality of all other words in an interrelated web of meaning. The discovery of the power and problem of the language habit is a spontaneous and profoundly captivating aspect of the Construct-aware stage. On the other hand, people seem to realize earlier at the Individualist and Autonomous stages that they are part of a vast background of cultural signs, and that in many cases they have no clue as to where it all came from. Overall, both cultural and linguistic conditioning becomes visible at the third tier of development. In other words, it is suggested that during vision-logic the previously taken for granted way of meaning making gets deconstructed and its limitations self-consciously explored and exposed.

Another aspect of vision-logic is its acceptance of contextualism, diversity and multiple perspectives as the means for opening up the rigid boundaries and self-contained, monological truths of the formal-operational self. Vision-logic emphasizes adding up all the perspectives, privileging none, and thus attempts to grasp the integral, the whole, the multiple contexts that endlessly disclose the cosmos, not in a rigid or absolutist fashion, but in a fluidly holonic and multidimensional tapestry.

The discovery of the fundamental partialness of our views at the third tier of development is reiterated, but vision-logic is explicitly seen as part of the bigger trend by which spirit rediscovers itself. Vision-logic is considered the most awake of the stages of the awakening mind in the personal realm and forms a link to the transpersonal, fourth tier of spirit. That other Unitive concepts are also part of vision-logic is evident from the description of the "existential level" and its stage-specific pathologies. The "Centaur" (animal body and human mind in one) represents the self-stage of vision-logic. The Cartesian body/mind split of the previous stage is healed, as boundaries are softened and data from all available sources, not just pure reason, are employed to navigate life. The major concerns of the existential or Centaur self are listed as: "personal autonomy and integration; authenticity; and self-actualization." Associated affects are: a concern for overall meaning in life (or-being-in-the world); a grappling with personal mortality and finitude and finding a courage-to-be in the face of lonely and unexpected death. Where the formal mind begins to conceive of life's possibilities and take flight in this new-found freedom, the existential mind (via vision-logic) adds up the possibilities and finds this: personal life is "a brief spark in the cosmic void." How the existential self handles the new potentials of autonomy and self-actualization, and how it grapples with the problems of finitude, mortality, and apparent meaninglessness - these are the central factors in F-6 pathology.

The courage-to-be despite one's awareness of mortality and finitude, as well as the courage to create coherent meaning in the face of the elusiveness of representations, these are the qualities particular to the postautonomous ego differentiation. Only at the existential level are they expressed with such clarity and poignancy. Adults at the system level of thought tend to relish the powers afforded by this new way of thinking which releases creativity and gives more freedom to roam than formal operations. They embody the first equation in the Pascalian paradox: "I am an infinity compared to nothingness."

It is at the Construct-aware ego stage that the opposite side "I am a nothing compared to infinity" starts to resonate at a profound level. When personal life is seen "as a brief spark in the cosmic void," then the person is ripe for existential despair and anxiety as well as for a new, and different kind of integration. It is the Unitive ego stage, which allows the conjoining of both sides of the Pascalian paradox without undue tension and without the need for experience to be different than it is.

In summary, the conceptualization of postconventional development as a step-wise deconstruction of formerly held truths is supported. Features of vision-logic include both cultural and linguistic conditioning. It is suggested that contextualism and cultural relativism can be understood at the first level of postconventional development, while the discovery of our universal linguistic habituation is more characteristic of the postautonomous level. Most importantly, vision-logic is part of the Great "Nest" of Being. It adds higher cognition and affect (feeling and intuition in the Vedic formulation) as well as insights from body and non-waking realities to the tools of the earlier, merely rational mind. At its most advanced, vision-logic opens the door into the spiritual, transpersonal, non-egoic, non-representational realm of being.

# Discussion of High-End Developmental Theories

Postconventional development can be looked at from two seemingly contradictory perspectives. First, postconventional development continues the pattern of alternating stages of differentiation and integration toward greater and greater hierarchical complexity accommodating an ever-expanding experiential universe. At the same time, postconventional development can be understood as a stepwise deconstruction of the previously unconsciously constructed permanent object world with its closed boundaries, linear causality and subject/object separation. Both views provide useful lenses into aspects of postconventional personality development, but only the latter is adequate to describe the insights of people at the most advanced ego stages as seen on rare sentence completion tests (SCTs).

Eight selected sources, six constructivist developmental and two transpersonal, were examined to find out how they accounted for the postconventional terrain in general and for the stages beyond a systems view in particular. Four of these models also have their own measuring instrument. The evidence from investigating these eight sources revealed that two constructivist models do not include the notion of the gradual deconstruction of the conventionally acquired mindset as part of their understanding of highest stage development. They do not do so, however, for different reasons.

## The General Model of Hierarchical Complexity

The General Model of Hierarchical Complexity (GMHC) looks exclusively at cognitive development. It focuses on the structure of the underlying logic and argumentation evident in the material that is evaluated as to stage. Some of their studies of systems level performance are based on subjects' ability to translate their own comprehension of the material into abstract symbols, for instance, in the Four Stories Problem. For their highest stage, the crossparadigmatic, only anecdotal or historical evidence is offered.

Nonetheless, some parallels exist. One of the criteria for a person to be rated at the Construct-aware stage is the ability to look at one's own life and self-identity as a continuous pattern of identification, disidentification and re-identification at more complex and integrated levels, that is, to recognize that identity formation as a psychological endeavor is based on the paradigm of consecutive approximations or as a historical, evolutionary process. The similarity here is to the conception of crossparadigmatic stage insofar as some people can take a perspective on the whole field of "development" by relating, for instance, ontogenetic (personality), phylogenetic (species), and cultural evolution (ideas).

On the other hand, the GMHC would fail to explain an unwillingness to "structure" experience because the rational need to explain everything has been transcended. Individuals at the Autonomous stage tend to see life in terms of integrating multiple self-systems into one self-actualized self while those at the Construct-aware stage notice the range of transformations that they have moved through and the likelihood of continuing transformations. Finally, people at the Unitive stage understand the non-necessity of striving for such identifications in the first place. They understand the limits inherent in arriving at an adequate self-theory based on the reification of experience. It is suggested that GMHC cannot adjudicate such instances of insight because it is entirely grounded in the rational, personal mindset, unaware of the constraints of its own reasoning.

Instead, the GMHC deals with the abstract formulations of underlying mental operations at ever more inclusive orders of complexity. Little empirical evidence outside of the history of science of paradigmatic or crossparadigmatic cognition is given. Even though Gödel is cited as an exemplar of their crossparadigmatic stage, the GMHC has yet to show any signs of awareness that there is a difference between reality and its description via symbolic representation and mathematical abstraction, and between knower and that which is known. Ironically, it was Gödel who argued that the problems of definition cannot be solved even within the most carefully constructed symbol systems. Thus, the mathematical and statistical rigor that is being used in support of the claims of the GHMC is not adequate to do justice to the broadly defined psychological phenomenon of adult development that the GMHC claims to explain.

It is declared that "if the field of endeavor represents the most complex element of epistemology for which their is any empirical evidence, then crossparadigmatic operations represent the present terminus of genetic epistemology." In other words, their systematizing seems to have reached a limit because of the present (meaning current) lack of evidence. However, they do not recognize that this limit is inherent in their approach which relies on structural evidence and rational analysis. Since the GMHC values cognitive complexity over insight, it cannot conceive of metarational modes of apperception that could integrate an understanding of the limits of symbolic analysis.

## The Loevinger Model

The translation of a qualitative phenomenon (how different people make various meaning of the same task) into a quantitative result in ways that maximizes output and efficiency and minimizes rater bias is part of the overall appeal of this model and instrument. By using a semi-projective sentence completion test and a rationalized manual, some of the problems of other measures including the GMHC are avoided. First, the test is the same for all respondents; second, responses are spontaneous and not probed; third, rater bias is less likely because of the matching procedure; and fourth, rating can be accomplished without the rater himself or herself being a complex reasoner. This is decidedly not the case for rating postconventional material with the GMHC scoring instrument.

On the other hand, the creation of rationalized manuals is very much dependent on the insights of those who originally interpreted and "rationalized" the sentence completions as well as the quality of the data used. As for the theory, it seems to reflect the theoreticians' own take on reality. It is imbedded in a modern, scientific view of reality that can only take a limited perspective on its own means of meaning making. Thus, at the high end of the scale, probabilistic considerations are used to defend placement choices rather than theoretical coherence, soundness, and plausibility. Reflecting both of these issues, the theory as well as the Second Edition is more effective for dealing with the lower two tier of ego development, but problematic at the third tier. The theory is least adequate at the postautonomous level because it only gives the fuzziest notions of what such meaning making would look like. The criteria given for the ultimate stage do not distinguish it from earlier stages, and, of greater theoretical concern, they cannot account for "deconstructed" views of reality.

## Other Theories

The theories of the four other constructivist developmental psychologists investigated clearly indicate that the hallmarks of the highest forms of self-development and understanding include greater complexity and higher order integrations as well as an increasing realization of the underlying mechanics of meaning making in the rational realm. All four posit that people can become aware of reification or objectification. These are the means by which we make lasting and solid what in actual experience is fleeting and insubstantial. In contrast, the concern for "finding" an objective, well-balanced and integrated identity is put as the most unique sign for the highest stage whereas "losing" one's need for identity seeking is more characteristic of postautonomous insight according to the other observers of mature views of reality and self development.

All four authors also count as markers of high-end development an awareness that paradox is inevitable, that polar opposites are defined through each other, and that language is a filter that stands between the experiencer and lived experience, or between knower and known. As people gain understanding of these mental habits while they occur, they begin to relate to them in a more conscious way. Whereas they are critical and often frustrated with their own automatic rational behavior at the Construct-aware stage, they can gain a detached perspective on it at the Unitive stage.

There are no clear dividing lines between one stage and the next except in the abstract model. This is only a problem from a traditional scientific point of view, which demands comparability of test samples. Here, it is taken as further support that, in the end, such divisions are arbitrary and based on the needs, purposes and methods of their creators.

Thus, one third tier stage is postulated, but given multiple attributes which include early postconventional as well as mature postconventional characteristics. Currently, the interpretation seems to be the most elaborate third tier self-theory with four theoretically defined steps between conventional and transpersonal development. Four postconventional cognitive differentiation-integrations across domains are also posited, but they focus predominantly on the higher complexity aspect of advanced reasoning. By ignoring the constructed nature of the means by which they create their arguments, that is by dismissing the possibility of the bankruptcy of the whole complexifying endeavor, they stop at the highest level for which there is some structural evidence.

Only theories that acknowledge both increasing complexity and increasing awareness of the constructed nature of reality carry in themselves the seed for a different, altogether new paradigm of cognition. The shift from the unconscious, preverbal world of the infant to the world negotiated through language is as monumental a transformation as the shift from the personal, representational realm of consciousness to the transpersonal, postrepresentational.

# Testing Content Validity of the Scoring Categories

Following the general evaluation of the theoretical terrain of postautonomous development, the proposed criteria for identifying high-stage protocols were tested empirically. There were two reasons for this step. First, it was necessary to verify whether the proposed categories were sufficient for describing postautonomous development. Second, this move seemed useful in light of potentially creating a compendium to the Second Edition for rating postconventional protocols.

Therefore, it was examined in detail how the categories compared to the characteristics articulated in the writings of the relevant authors analyzed in the previous section in their respective descriptions of the postautonomous terrain. One theory was replaced with another that also fulfilled the selection criteria.

The choice of these texts was based on the likelihood that they addressed postautonomous issues. Thus, the theoretical framework chapters as well as the specific part of the writings in which the selected authors dealt with their respective postconventional-postautonomous stages were looked at. Although read carefully, the aim was not at catching every instance of correspondence. Thus, there may be some not noticed or noted down. Here the attempt is only to ascertain whether the thematic categories are indeed part of the repertoire of describing higher stages of development by others, not to find all instances of their occurrence. However, novel characteristics that had not appeared in the database were looked for.

To structure the task, the themes from the existing category descriptions were used as a guiding framework. So far, the nine thematic categories appear to be necessary and sufficient to identify the unique characteristics of the two proposed postautonomous ego stages and to distinguish between them and earlier stages. As mentioned before, the categories are stage-specific and could prove to be useful for identifying postautonomous material outside the narrow context of sentence completions.

At this stage of the project, the aim was to test whether concrete corroboration of the proposed criteria could be found rather than the more general conceptual parallels and differences investigated in the previous section. The numbers of pages consulted for each author are given in Table 3.4. These refer to their treatment of the postconventional/postautonomous area of development only, not the complete works. The following questions were put to each text:

Do the proposed scoring criteria actually appear as postautonomous characteristics in the works of the theoreticians examined for their general views on postconventional and postautonomous development? Do these authors list any characteristics missed? Comparatively, how often are specific categories mentioned?

First, it was wanted to find out whether the themes of the scoring categories actually appeared in the targeted texts. Second, it was needed to make sure that salient postautonomous themes or concerns had not been left out because they did not appear on the SCTs of the data collection from which the categories were empirically derived. Clearly with rare empirical data, there is little confidence at the outset that the evidence will cover most types of responses that would be relevant for creating a comprehensive stage characterization. Therefore, this analysis of the targeted texts also included reading them for potentially new subthemes or entirely new categories. Third, knowing which of the existing categories are mentioned most often among the other thinkers in this field would give some additional weight to their relative importance. The question whether to keep a rare category for the sake of comprehensiveness or whether to list only those that appear a minimal percentage of the time is a further consideration in manual construction.

It is a rare benefit of empirical work that it occasionally produces insights unlikely to occur in a more theoretical context. Construct-aware category C9 (5/6v) was derived from analyzing hundreds of postconventional SCT responses. The sometimes exceedingly complex completions in postautonomous protocols tipped off to the Construct-aware individuals' struggle with complexity issues. Thus in this specific instance, it is the unique, complex structure of a response that identifies it as Construct-aware rather than the content which is more salient in other categories. It is anticipated that the expression of complexity may be part of the description of high-end development in other theories, but not its specific structural markers that are most striking in sentence completions.

To accomplish the test of the adequacy of the scoring categories, the steps below were followed.

## Method

1. The current category descriptions are umbrella types - that is, they are highly condensed summaries of the observed characteristics pertaining to a given master characteristic. To facilitate and fine-tune analysis, the compact categories were broken down into several component subthemes.
2. A multi-column table was devised for recording the topics, themes and subthemes to be tested. The grid represents a category by author matrix. Empty cells were included to have room to modify existing text and to add new themes. See Table 3.3 for a schematic representation of the grid.
3. Next, the selected texts were read and any postautonomous characteristics they mentioned were flagged. The amount of text analyzed differed for different authors. Some material were chapters on postconventional development in theoretical works, others were summary articles on third tier development.
4. Whenever a match was found between a theme on the y-axis of the grid and a target text, it was entered into the corresponding stage, category and subtheme cell, color-coded according to the author and work. Each item was identified by author, text and page number. Where possible, the distinctions between the criteria for the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage were maintained. Since authors vary in the number of distinctions they make at the high end of their scales, it was planned to note where a feature seemed postautonomous but a clear stage distinction was not possible.
5. Finally, the tabulated frequency of correspondences was summarized and the outcome analyzed for patterns. The summary of the tally by category is given below as well as a discussion of the evidence.

## Results and Discussion of Text Analysis

The actual searching of relevant text for the presence of the proposed categories and for finding new features that might be useful in characterizing postautonomous responses did not produce radically new descriptors. 1398 pages of potentially relevant text surfaced 264 instances of criteria for describing postautonomous development. Many of the subtheme cells stayed empty. Therefore, the discussion will be at the level of category themes only. In short, these are as follows:

**Construct-aware stage 5/6 (C9)**
*   Complex matrix (non linear list); abstract, structural signs
*   Evaluation of multilayered intrapsychic and interpersonal dynamics
*   Exploration and evaluation of habits of the mind; paradox, infinite regress; attention to process, attention to attention
*   Reference to the constructed nature of reality; the language habit; definitions and frames of reference

**Unitive stage C10 (6)**
*   6.1 Explicit gratitude for other beings and life in their whole complexity
*   6.2 Acceptance of change, process, world and people as they are
*   6.3 Non-trivial expression of universal connectedness
*   6.4 Fundamental thoughts and feelings about the human condition
*   6.5 Unitive ability, embracing polar opposites and multiple perspectives

It was difficult to place an item into a Construct-aware or a Unitive category. However, 2 instances were found where the Ironist stage is described which partially overlap with the Unitive stage and partially lie outside its parameters. Given the lesser frequency of adults at the Unitive stage, it is natural that there were fewer mentions at the Unitive stage overall.

All nine categories were represented across the texts, but not every author recognized the same categories as indicative for the most advanced kind of development. The two Construct-aware categories with the most hits were 9x which refers to the questioning and evaluating of the habits of the mind, and 9y which makes reference to the constructed nature of reality and its active framing.

Only one author mentioned the complexity category 9v as an indicator of dialectical thought. Unitive ability (10z), the second category with structural indicators was recognized twice by one author, once by another and once by a third as symptomatic of advanced development.

Two authors had eight out of the nine categories represented, while another described seven of them. Thus, the categories do seem to capture elements of postautonomous behavior that are evident to others combing that territory. One author produced the most evidence overall: This seems understandable since his 24 detailed schemas are based on actual excerpts from interview material. As evaluation criteria, the schemas are conceptually closer to scoring categories than the general personality descriptions offered by the other authors.

The Construct-aware category (9y) was mentioned by six out of seven theoreticians, and gathered 38% of the responses. This rubric deals with the constructed nature of reality and includes seeing symbolic representation of reality as a potential "problem" because it "reifies" experience and acts as a screen between reality and reality as perceived by the human observer. Since this seems to be the most easily recognized index of postautonomous development having named the whole stage by this characteristic seems appropriate. 25% of the correspondences came from category (9x) which pays attention to the process of meaning making and to thought habits, such as the recognition of inevitable paradox and infinite regress in logic as well as to issues of conscious attention. 23 instances of category (9w) were found, which describes individuals with an explicit and subtle recognition of the complex interdependence of intrapersonal and interpersonal dynamics.

The Unitive characteristics were presented by three authors as more mature than the other criteria mentioned in the personal realm. The two most common categories for the Unitive stage were (10w) and (10x). Category 10w refers to the letting go of evaluative behavior and being able to witness whatever transpires in the moment. At the Unitive stage, the pleasure principle no longer seems to hold. All experience becomes both meaningful and not meaningful, or neither (non-dual perception). The "unitive" connection across all possible boundaries (geography, time, race, intelligence etc.) is typical of stage 10x and was also recognized as a relevant marker by all seven authors.

The selected literature also contained a few instances of meaningful postautonomous distinctions and subthemes that had not yet been adequately articulated. These were entered as a new subtheme in the relevant umbrella category. There were no entirely "new" categories in this selected sample. After the numerical comparison was completed the umbrella descriptions for the categories were amended to better reflect the characteristics recorded on the list. Their refinement is an important subtext of this study as will be more evident in the next chapter. It is an ongoing effort geared toward simplifying the praxis of actual high-stage scoring.

Perhaps this is not an ideal description because those who are at this order of consciousness probably view reality the way they do because they can do no other, thus "honor" has little to do with their behavior. Indeed, every stage is "honorable" in that it lives at the order it has mastered. Victories would be acknowledged and celebrated wherever they occur on the growth trajectory.

Some critics find fault with many of the concrete details of the effort at synthesis. However, the inevitable price in accuracy to be paid for grand "orienting generalizations or conclusions" is acknowledged. As one is disposed to explore the beauty and intricacies of spirit manifested in the detail, the counterpoint of the vast and integrative vision is particularly appreciated.

The addition of a third, intuited witness-self or anterior self to the dyad of the proximate self (I) and distal self (me) is found as especially meaningful, illuminating and promising for future research in the spiritual line of development.

An alternative, and less successful attempt at a grand integration is offered. The difference is mainly that it remains subtly unaware of its treatment of the cosmos as an "it" rather than a "thou" or the eternal "I".

Gödel "proved" that all symbol systems are self-referential and produce non-contradictory propositions only within their own axiomatic confines. Any analysis of them from an outside frame of reference will produce inconsistencies or "undecidable propositions".

Examples of how the General Hierarchical Complexity Scoring System (GHCSS) Handout can be applied across domains show both the powerful and the limiting aspects of the system. The example material is from probed interview studies. The highest examples shown are rated at the metasystematic level 5b. One study is on the moral climate in universities. Some of the text strings rated at 5b might be rated at the Construct-aware ego stage because of the awareness of paradox and contradiction among propositions (C9x) and because of the complexity (nestling) of thoughts (C9v).

On the other hand, an example given for 5b from a life experience interview would be rated lower at E6 or E7 if the higher complexity rule is applied to combinations of conventional elements. The privileging of structure/complexity over actual meaning is also the main critique of the Second Edition. Looking only at the structure of reasoning, hides the fact that it is the rater who assigns "the level of abstractness" to an element produced by the interviewee. It is a matter of definition.

Let me give a concrete example for a 5b rating:

DO YOU FEEL YOU ARE SATISFIED WITH YOUR CLOSE RELATIONSHIP?
"Difficult to give a "Yes" or "No." Probably the best answer is in some ways. On the positive side, "s. o." [my significant other] relationship has lasted > 6 years; on the negative side, I don't feel as though I am consistently able to maintain the level of intimacy I would like, primarily because of my work schedule."

The content and concerns of this response are entirely conventional. Responsibility is placed on technicalities (my schedule). The subject lives in the formal operational world of sharp self-other distinctions and reified objects (intimacy is a clearly defined thing of which one can have more or less). All of these would be signs of a conventional view of reality. Whether "significant other" is interpreted to mean "the concrete significant other I have" or "significant others that are part of relationships in general" depends on the rater. Combined with a precise number of years, it would tend to be seen here as a concrete reference. The issue in all of this is to point out that all scoring systems that are based on text interpretation are vulnerable to rater bias. Each concept can be assigned several different levels of "abstractness" depending on the context and aim of the interpreters.

# Postautonomous Ego Development: Its Measurement

## Section 1: General Methods

### Outline

As has been shown in the previous chapter, parallels with others' views of postautonomous development can be readily established on a theoretical plane. Both constructivist developmental theorists and researchers studying Eastern paths to maturity suggest that people can develop a stance towards reality that goes beyond the Western rational confines towards ego-transcendence. As individuals recognize ever more profoundly the traps and "unreality" of the world woven with words at the Construct-aware stage, some eventually integrate that understanding into a new, metarational worldview at the Unitive stage. It is astonishing that such sentiments towards experience occur on SCTs at all. However, can the presence of the two proposed advanced ego stages be actually measured by empirical means?

Chapter 4 documents the multiple ways in which measurement issues were explored and tested. The first section describes the overall research design, sampling procedures, data collection and description. Each of the three sections that follow is devoted to a specific set of research questions: The second section addresses individual response agreement and category refinement issues. The third section deals with the central issue of measuring interrater agreement for whole protocols. The fourth section discusses how cut-off numbers for assigning high-end ego scores were determined and addresses general psychometric issues. Each section describes the methods used to answer the research questions, and reports and discusses the results of the respective studies.

To explore the viability of measuring postautonomous development, the following three sets of questions were posed:

1.  How would an experienced scorer rate a random set of postconventional responses with the Second Edition? Which ones would they flag as hard-to-score? What reasons would they give for any difficulties? If they were given the criteria for scoring, would there be any difference in the results? What refinements of the category descriptions could be made through mutual discussion and feedback?
2.  What levels of interrater agreement could be achieved for both individual sentence completions and total protocol ratings when novice raters use the category descriptions, stage distinctions and scoring procedure?
3.  To what extent does using these new scoring categories and stage distinctions alter the distribution characteristics of postautonomous protocols? Do these warrant a revision of the cut-off numbers at the high end, and if so, what would be the best way to translate the observed distribution patterns into numerical guidelines for scoring postautonomous protocols?

This chapter begins by describing some technical aspects of administering the long-term project of data gathering and entry. For a summary of the two previous waves of data use, the reader is referred to Chapter 2. There, the earlier modification of the theory was described and a set of scoring categories for rating postautonomous protocols that have been used since 1987, but never empirically tested for reliability, was introduced.

### The Sample

When looking for rare fish, checking other people's nets is an efficient way to catch interesting specimens. However, this approach also introduces many difficulties as people fish in different waters, use differently gauged nets and sort their catches by different properties.

A long-term, large-scale effort was necessary to find enough rare postautonomous data, both individual responses and total protocols, to analyze them for salient patterns. From 1980 to July 1997, 4420 SCT protocols (consisting of over 130,000 items) from 185 different projects were scored. These projects involved subjects that came from all walks of life (including prisoners, priests, professional managers, undergraduate and graduate students, professors, accountants, administrators and artists, young delinquents and senior executives). Their ages ranged from adolescence to mature adulthood (range 16 to 82). Projects originated from across America, Canada, Australia and the United Kingdom. It is an opportunistic sample with no subsample being systematically representative of any population. The size of the overall sample, the broad age range of the subjects, and the wide geographic provenance make this a very productive sample nonetheless.

The 4420 protocols were scored between 1980 and 1997 and yielded 1100 individual responses (0.8%) that seemed to fall into the postautonomous range. These are the responses that were complex and meaningful, but did not sufficiently fit existing ego development theory, or match the stage or category descriptions of the Autonomous and Integrated stages. Copies of 476 (about one in ten) mostly postconventional protocols were also retained for further analysis.

Throughout the data collection, two tallies were generated for each project. First, the scores from each protocol were entered into an individual, two-part scoring sheet. It contained the individual ratings, alternative scores and comments as well as summary statistics: These include simple and cumulative item distributions, ogive score, total weighted score (TWS), total protocol rating (TPR) and percentage of responses at the conventional stages.

**Figure 4.1 Sampling Procedure**

*   **Original Data from which Dissertation Sample was Drawn:** 4420 SCT Protocols (~130,000 completions) scored from 1980 to 1997
*   **Dissertation Sample:**
    *   228 24-items Form 81-items only (87+ protocols)
    *   41 36-items <85
    *   187 36-items All forms E5-C10
*   **Final Samples for Reliability Studies:**
    *   145 individual completions to refine high-end categories
    *   60 Protocols for Interrater Agreement Study
    *   476 mostly postconventional protocols (10.8%)

Also noted were SES and final comments about any unusual observations made on reading the whole protocol. Second, each set of individual scoring sheets was summarized in a master tally. It contained the stage distributions for each protocol as well as the automatic ogives, total weighted scores and final total protocol ratings. Where available, data on gender, age, levels of education, professional occupation, and comments on the ratings were included.

Both individual and project tallies were regularly shared with the respective project administrators as part of the scoring contract to allow the clients to perform independent replication and reliability studies. Each time a project was worked on, the project leader was asked for permission to retain copies of either individual postautonomous completions or entire high-end protocols. In addition, a few other protocols were selected mainly for teaching purposes. To guarantee anonymity, the original identification number and salient project information were kept in a separate file. To insure anonymity further, it was promised not to use and make public more than a few lines from any one protocol.


---


# Rewritten Content

The remaining 61 protocols were excluded from this project because they originated from protocols with 15 items or fewer, or they were below the stage 3/4 level.

## Problems with this approach

As noted in the description of the manuals in Chapter 2, there is an unfortunate proliferation of protocol forms, item combinations, and manuals. Over the years, the framework developers have produced unpublished adjunct manuals for items specific to girls and boys, items specific to men, and preliminary manuals in preparation for the revision. Although the recommended length of a protocol is 36 items, forms with 12, 18, 24, 27, 36, and 44 items have been encountered. The 1970 manual likely began the trend of experimentation by offering six different test forms for different subject groups and 48 different stems. In addition, items that appear on all the forms are sequenced differently from form to form. Thus, researchers often order the stems to suit their own purposes and add a few items of their own liking. Sometimes these additional items have poor manuals or no published manuals at all.

Partially to remedy this situation, the framework developers introduced a definite 36-item version to replace all previous forms in 1981. Unfortunately, this move did not stop other researchers from using their own forms and, as a result, 55 different stems have been scored and tracked over the years. To manage them, a master list for all stems was created in 1985. Items 1-36 are those from the 1981 form, the same ones used in the Second Edition. Items 37-55 represent stems used in old manuals or in other projects for which manuals exist. Therefore, both the master list number and its actual location number in the sequence of the particular form for each stem and response pair were entered.

## The raters

Whenever one deals with postconventional stages, the issue of rater bias (limitation) becomes even more relevant than for ordinary samples. According to the framework developers, the logic of ego theory dictates that only those with a relatively high ego level can become good raters of high-level protocols. The chief access to a person's ego level is precisely that it limits what he or she can conceive and perceive; that limitation holds for raters as well as for subjects.

Thus, it was anticipated that one of the problems with testing high-end material would be to find skilled raters who could learn how to rate and identify (to perceive) the proposed postautonomous stage and category distinctions. There was special concern about the ability of the experienced rater to be able to "speculate about and conceive" of high-end development on his/her own. A person was needed who could evaluate the new scoring manual and who could compare the usefulness of its high-end scoring categories with the proposed ones. This was important since it was hoped to benefit from the experienced rater's insights into the theory for revising and fine-tuning the high-end scoring categories.

Finding raters for this project was indeed very difficult. In March of 1998, a conference on ego development was attended in celebration of a prominent researcher's eightieth birthday. The hope for leads to possible raters was unsuccessful. Only after several more months of looking was an expert rater finally found, a woman who had been trained in 1980 and whose own protocol had been rated at the high end years earlier. This seasoned scorer rated two sets of individual responses twice. Discussions about her rating experience were taped in three two-hour and one one-hour meetings over a six-month period.

Finding raters who could commit to scoring 60 whole protocols proved to be even more difficult. After several months of searching unsuccessfully for raters, a group had to be trained over the summer. A flyer was distributed and advertised on an email system inviting readers to forward the message to whomever they thought might be interested in scoring. The email quickly resulted in nine responses. These volunteers were interviewed to explain the project and needs and answer their questions. Five individuals signed a preliminary contract which stipulated what was offered in terms of training and potential employment and the trainees' responsibility in terms of attending training sessions, doing homework and participating in the project if chosen based on the result of a test at the end of the training series. When all was said and done, the actual scoring task was completed by two participants.

## Rater Training

The training workshop was held on three Saturdays in May and June of 1998. It included short lectures, many handouts, and intensive discussion of the actual process of scoring and protocol rating. Participants were instructed in the theory of ego development and high-end individuation. As homework, they scored the self-training items and half of the practice protocols in the Second Edition. They then practiced scoring during the sessions, first with individual items, then with whole protocols. They needed to learn how to use not only the Second Edition but also the old manuals since the interrater agreement study contained several stems no longer carried in the current manual. Furthermore, in the second session, the proposed postautonomous stages and scoring categories were introduced. The ensuing discussions on theory and measurement issues were intense and inspiring as all participants got caught up in the intricacies and beauty of making fine semantic distinctions, matching the responses with examples in the manuals and connecting the response characteristics to differences in people's ego development.

As if scoring of high-stage material alone was not difficult enough, participants had to learn to manage multiple scoring systems. They dealt gracefully with the hardship of having to translate the numeric stage names from the old manuals instantly into the differing numeric names of the Second Edition. The participants suggested renaming the proposed stages 5/6 and 6 to C9 and C10 to avoid confusion with the framework developer's stage E6 (Conscientious) from the Second Edition and to distinguish between her highest stage E9 and the revisions. This advice was incorporated in the original reporting to make discussion and comparisons easier for the trainees. It still left the problem of the actual scoring with different manuals. Although complicating matters, this is typical of what can happen with a widely used measure that has had many forms and that has gone through a radical renaming of its components late in the game.

Three women completed all three training sessions and scored two 36-item protocols as a readiness test. All three did well enough and agreed to continue in the project. Though satisfactory overall, the respective tests showed systematic scoring errors. Thus, individual sessions were scheduled to work on the specific problems of each scorer and to deepen her understanding of high-end distinctions before starting on the interrater agreement study proper. The final research team of newly trained raters consisted of three women: a director-level university administrator, a management consultant specializing in transformative learning, and a non-native speaker of English interested in the psychology of creative writers.

With this background information in place, the actual studies that were designed to test empirically the proposed "enhancements" to the scoring method at the postautonomous level of ego development are now discussed.

## SECTION 2: TEST OF INTERRATER AGREEMENT FOR INDIVIDUAL COMPLETIONS

### Methods

To begin, it was ascertained to what degree an experienced rater would concur that high-stage responses from a random set of late-conventional responses and above 4 to 6 are hard to score with the established method.

1. How would an experienced scorer rate a random set of postconventional responses with the Second Edition? Which ones would s/he flag as hard-to-score? What reasons would s/he give for any difficulties? If given the proposed criteria for scoring, would there be any difference in the results? What refinements of the category descriptions could be made through mutual discussion and feedback?

To test the claim that high-end completions are hard-to-score with the Second Edition, an experienced rater was employed. The process proceeded in a bootstrapping fashion, alternating between (a) independent scoring tests that resulted in reportable numbers for interrater agreement and (b) tape-recorded explorative conversations about the expert rater's reaction to the scoring experience to further understanding of possible problems and to hone the high-end category descriptions.

Two sets of individual responses matched for stem and stage, Set A and Set B, totaling 145 responses, were prepared. The final sets contained only stems that (a) were in the Second Edition, (b) came from the 12 stems with the most responses, and (c) were higher than stage 3. In an attempt to be rigorous in creating a sample that was both random and matched, an "elaborate" process was gone through. Though practiced even with high-end protocols, the experienced rater had not yet worked with the Second Edition. Since the actual matching procedure has not changed from the old manuals to the Second Edition, it was assumed that the transition from one to the other would be easy. Set A of the individual responses was meant to serve as a learning experience for her in using the Second Edition, set B as an actual test of the usefulness of the proposed categories in improving ratings of high-stage material. A series of separate, reportable rounds of scoring and subsequent taped dialogues about them were proceeded with.

Step 1: The experienced rater scored the 73 items of Set A independently with the Second Edition according to the instructions and "caveats" for rating high-stage completions and protocols. The rater was also invited to indicate on the scoring sheet when and for which reasons she found any completion hard-to-score. It was predicted that disagreements would increase with increasing ego level and complexity of the responses. Interrater agreement between the initial ratings with the Second Edition and hers was measured and reported as a baseline.

Step 2: Next, a meeting was held to discuss her responses to the Second Edition and her ratings and comments to the completions. She was introduced to the proposed interpretation of postconventional ego development and instructed in the application of the high-stage scoring categories by sharing the respective book chapters and through conversation.

Step 3: The experienced scorer rated Set A once more, this time using both the Second Edition and the proposed scoring categories. Again initial agreement was measured and reported.

Step 4: A second meeting was held to compare results. Instances of disagreement were examined and either resolved through discussion or her feedback was used to modify classification categories and/or improve category descriptions. Differences were considered resolved when it was agreed that the proposed descriptions effectively characterized and differentiated high-end responses both from earlier responses and between the two postautonomous stages. A similar procedure was also followed for step 6.

Step 5: The experienced rater scored the 72 completions of Set B with the Second Edition and the refined postautonomous categories. Initial agreement was assessed and reported including Pearson Correlations to indicate the level of agreement between raters.

Step 6: The results were once more discussed, compared and this time differences compromised. The compromised scores were used for the final round of individual interrater reliability testing which included the novice raters.

Set A of the individual responses was used in the initial training of the new raters to help them gain facility with the Second Edition. Set B was added to their independent scoring task to expand the interrater agreement test for individual completions. The results were compared in pairs for each of the completions with the compromised rating from the experienced rater and the researcher, then reported and analyzed.

Given the small number of data in each set, testing for interrater agreement for individual postautonomous categories seemed unproductive. Since a main feature of such responses is their complexity, many fit more than one category within a given stage. According to the framework developers, "such differences [in assigning a category within a stage] are not considered errors". The overall aim of the manuals including the postautonomous categories is to correctly identify the stage to which a completion belongs. It was reasoned that if the experienced scorer and the scoring team could use the proposed high-end categories and distinctions to rate individual completions reliably at a given stage, there would be some evidence of their fit and their effectiveness. If agreement remained low, the probable reasons for this outcome were planned to be evaluated. It is not uncommon that several rounds of testing and refining coding categories are needed in a new test instrument to achieve even moderate agreement. Testing the efficacy of the high-end criteria first before scoring whole protocols with them was additionally intended to allow for flexibility in the research design. It might have made sense to stop the empirical testing at this juncture, had the outcome been completely negative, that is, if scoring with these new categories proved less accurate than scoring with the existing method.

### Results

The probability values for all correlations reported in the interrater agreement studies both for individual items and for whole protocols were statistically significant at p < .0001. Since the probabilities are the same throughout, they will not be shown in the tables and the symbol *** will be used in the text to indicate the level of statistical significance.

**Table 4.2 PEARSON CORRELATIONS FOR EXPERIENCED RATERS (R1, R2)**
| Set B | Round 1 | Round 2 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Rater | R1*R2 | Compromised @ Rcomp |
| r = | .80 | |

In the learning phase for the expert rater (R2; Set A, Round 1), there was no appreciable difference between scoring with the categories of the New Edition only (Round 1; r = .91***) and scoring for the first time with the addition of the proposed postautonomous categories and stage distinctions (Round 2; r = .93***).

This was expected since initial agreement between the experienced rater and the researcher was very high. This does not mean, however, that the Second Edition is as useful alone as scoring with the benefit of the added distinctions of the new scoring categories. While, in Round 1, the experienced rater and the researcher scored 12 and 13 completions respectively at the Integrated stage, they scored 29 and 26 responses from the same item set at the two postautonomous stages after they were introduced (Round 2). This may be an indication that the new categories indeed allow for making distinctions that are not possible with the Second Edition.

**Table 4.3 PEARSON CORRELATIONS BETWEEN THE COMPROMISED RATING (Rcomp) AND THE NOVICE RATERS (R3, R4) IN PAIRS**
| N=72 | Set B, Round 2 | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Rater | Rcomp*R3 | Rcomp*R4 | R3*R4 |
| r = | .85 | .69 | .69 |

Round 1 of Set B served to give the expert rater further experience with the new categories. It also allowed arrival at a set of compromised scores after differences were discussed. Agreement was reached in all but three cases. These compromised scores (Rcomp) were then used as a basis for comparison with the novice raters (R3, R4). The second set of individual sentence completions (Set B), scored by both the experienced raters and the novice raters, fared less well than Set A. The scores ranged from r = .80*** for the two experienced raters (see Table 4.2) to r = 0.69*** for the two trainees paired with the compromised scores.

Agreement within one stage ranged from 69.4% between the experienced raters to 90.3% for Rater 3 and the compromised scores. Rater 3 had a talent for making high-stage distinctions. Rater 4 remained reliable, but more uncertain about the distinctions between the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage. Indeed, she did not rate any responses at the highest stage in Rounds 1 or 2.

### Discussion

Because of the random sampling procedure, data Set B was harder to score and contained some very "strange" and very complex responses. These were rated by some scorers using Rule 5 for unscorable responses at the compromise stage 3, and given higher "guess" scores by others. While isolated "odd" completions do occur on protocols occasionally, their impact is negligible in the context of the other items on a protocol. In isolation, however, they may unduly influence the outcome. This may partially explain the lower agreement between outcomes for Set A and Set B. In addition, Rater 4 had some difficulty in picking up the signals for the highest stage as mentioned above.

During discussions with the expert rater about her experience with the Second Edition, she spontaneously expressed her disappointment with it on several occasions. The following comments about stages 5 and the highest stage are from the discussion after the expert rater had used the Second Edition for the first time:

"There were so few criteria in the new manual that I couldn't justify a particular rating."

"The new combination rules don't make any logical sense for my matrix. The new rules conflict with the old ones, which were fine."

Later she expressed a preference in some instances for coding with the proposed categories because they actually provided themes that capture some of the qualitative difference of responses from the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage:

"Some responses just seemed too ambiguous to interpret without looking at the total protocol. But I am fascinated by your descriptions of the felt experience of these more complex levels. I especially appreciate your portrait of the hope and effort to paint a complete picture, to make lists, as if things are, ultimately, listable-describable and knowable-once and for all. I like the vividness of the contrast you make with what comes after that: a stance more like relinquishment and acceptance, and even pleasure in incompleteness and indescribableness."

Overall, she found it difficult to migrate to the new stage names and considerable effort was spent on entering and translating the scores, and trying to communicate across the confusing labels.

As hoped, the experienced rater had her own conceptions of highest stage ego development. Much of the discussion time was taken up with trying to put into words what the thinking was behind the categories and how distinctions were made among stages 5, 5/6, and 6. The expert rater, in turn, asked for clarification and challenged some interpretations. Being able to discuss the nuances of the higher stages with a knowledgeable and thoughtful partner is a real pleasure and often more quickly productive than private musings. Initially, the expert rater's conception of the Unitive stage was more idealistic than the proposed one, and allowed for no signs of doubt or ambivalence. Given the proposed view of development this is an unrealistic, though widely shared view that the framework developers commented on as well:

"Many theorists are tempted to believe that there is an ultimate stage of life that combines all virtues and is a paragon of good adjustment, a state of grace which most of us believe we have achieved or are on the verge of achieving. We did find some responses by persons at high ego levels that indicated a well-adjusted life or attitude toward life."

Full-spectrum theories of development based on Eastern traditions have, of course, postulated an ultimate reality of complete union, a state (not a stage) in which distinctions are meaningless. They also postulate intermediate stages beyond the third tier. These ego-transcendent stages are, by definition, of a different nature than the kind of "grace" which the framework developers refer to, and which can be gained in the personal realm through ego development. Even if a subject were at one of the transcendent stages of consciousness, it is unlikely that the subtleties would be picked up by an instrument like the sentence completion test. A probed interview would offer a more effective way to explore the nature of development beyond the personal realm.

As long as people develop, the changes they undergo inevitably pose opportunities for both adjustment and maladjustment as each stage solves some problems (gains new freedoms) and is subject to new limits. What is experienced as a challenge at each stage, is likely to change with the changing reality perception. In fact, there exists a carefully elaborated literature on the difficulties and pathologies of the stages at the fourth, transpersonal tier of development. In the Western mental health field, developmental psychopathology is relatively new and underexplored. As far as is known, nobody has yet done systematic research into what mental difficulties are most prevalent at the third tier of ego development. Suffice it to say in the context of this project, that the postautonomous stages are not conceived of as problem-free or as immune to mental disorders.

In the design of this project, the feedback from the experienced rater was to be the final step in optimizing the category selection and description. However, due to the long delay between meetings, the conversations with the training workshop participants turned out to be as salient and important in sharpening the category descriptions as the feedback from the experienced rater. These descriptions themselves have not changed in substance, but for some categories, a title phrase has been added to highlight the main feature.

While the experienced scorer was familiar with the conception of the Autonomous stage from the earlier manuals and from the theory, the new raters were reluctant to use the stage 5 rating because they could not deduce the stage character from the few examples given for it. In all paired comparisons, the greatest disagreement occurred in relation to stage 5. Indeed, when the contingency tables were examined, R3 and R4 had zero agreement in assigning this stage to individual completions while the compromised Rcomp pairings with both R3 and R4 showed more scatter for rating the Autonomous stage than for any other stage.

The expert scorer, assessing her experience with the Second Edition at the end of her involvement with the project, wrote the following:

"The new manual (...) may make it easier to learn to score simpler protocols. But, this manual reads like 'WUSCT Lite.' It's as if the author wanted to make a neat package, and the messy parts didn't fit. (...) I found the old manual richer in its presentation of a commonly-found response in the heading, with variations below, so that one could get a general idea of how respondents might understand a stem. The new manual also belies the complexity of the more cognitively complex stages by giving so few examples."

### Conclusion

In response to the research question whether using the proposed category and stage descriptions made a difference to an experienced scorer using them in conjunction with the Second Edition, the answer is not decisive. First, adding the new categories, did not make an appreciable difference statistically. This is not unusual when initial agreement is over 90 percent since there is little room for improvement. On the other hand, there was better agreement for the postautonomous stages than there was for the Autonomous stage. It appears to be the weakest link in the stage sequence that combines the earlier stages with the proposed higher stages. Moreover, agreement on isolated individual sentence completions is not necessarily a valid indicator for how well the scoring categories will function in assessing ego stage in the context of whole protocols.

In light of these considerations, there was enough agreement between raters on the first try to progress to the next phase and test the revised high-end scoring categories with a set of full-length protocols as planned.

## SECTION 3: TEST OF INTERRATER AGREEMENT FOR SIXTY 36-ITEM HIGH-END PROTOCOLS

The first study showed that scoring individual, out-of-context, high-end sentence completions can be done with satisfactory results using the proposed postautonomous scoring categories. Naturally, high-end responses take on their full significance only in the context of a whole protocol. Thus, the next step involved testing to what degree the raters would agree in scoring whole protocols.

2. What levels of interrater agreement can be achieved in total protocol ratings when trained raters use the revised category descriptions, stage distinctions and scoring procedure?

### Sample

A selective sample was necessary in order to test the postautonomous categories empirically. Despite the years of effort to find high-end protocols, the numbers are still very small. Since the framework developers' requirements for inclusion in their highest stage are softer, a few protocols that came from other researchers that used the "standard" cut-off numbers did not qualify as postautonomous protocols for this sample.

To test protocol interrater agreement, the 187 out of 231 36-item protocols that had rated in the upper-half of the scale with the old manuals based on more rigorous cut-off numbers were selected. They were transcribed into a consistent format into tables including word counts for each protocol and both the consecutive and the master list item numbers were noted.

Since the goal was to use about 12 protocols from each of the five stages, every third protocol from the stage 4, every fourth of 4/5, every other from 5/6 and almost all from the rare 6 group among the 36-item protocols were picked. Originally, analysis by gender was not a specific goal. However, by chance, the resulting groups were almost evenly matched for gender so that such a comparison became an option.

**Table 4.4 SAMPLE (N=60) STRATIFIED FOR OLD TPR**
| Current Stage names | 4 | 4/5 | 5 | 5/6 | 6 | Total |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Distn N=187 | 47 | 57 | 44 | 26 | 12 | |
| Goal by stage | 12 | 12 | 12 | 12 | 12 | 60 |
| Actual distribution by old TPR | 12 | 14 | 12 | 13 | 9 | 60 |
| F/m ratio | 5/7 | 8/6 | 7/5 | 4/9 | 7/2 | 31/29 |

Next the 60 protocols were sorted into four batches of 15 protocols each, making sure that each batch contained some protocols from each stage in random order. According to standard procedure, all the completions of each batch were then combined and sorted by stem to insure that the initial rating of individual completions was out of context. Since these protocols came from many different 36-item forms, 54 out of the 55 items on the master list appeared among them and thus the sequence for each stem was naturally scrambled. When the completions all come from the same protocol form, one has to randomize the order of items for a given stem additionally to avoid context bias. This was a rare occasion where having data from many forms turned out to be a small benefit.

As soon as the batches were ready in the summer of 1998, and before giving them to the team, they were rated with the Second Edition and the results entered into a spreadsheet. A comparison with the old ratings can thus be reported. While doing so, the support materials for the assistants were prepared. This step helped appreciate just how taxing a task they had agreed to accomplish.

The raters used the Second Edition for all stems that it included. They also relied on their copies of old manuals for the ten stems with the most occurrences no longer part of the Second Edition. The rest of the stems—at most three per protocol—for which they had no manuals at all had to be rated based on their knowledge of the theory only. This is admissible by rating Rule 4 which states:

"In the case of a meaningful response, where there is no appropriate category and Rules 2 and 3 [for compound sentences] do not apply, use the general theory to arrive at a rating."

Next, each batch of 540 items was sorted in increasing order by stem from the master list. Each response was identified by the batch number as well as its position number on the original protocol to facilitate entry into the scoring sheet at a later stage of the procedure. A log was retained which matched the batch numbers with the original protocol numbers from the protocol list. Throughout this process, only the ID# and the batch numbers were used to safeguard the anonymity of the subjects as well as to protect the raters from any information about the subjects that might influence them.

### Scoring

Three raters started the independent scoring effort in August of 1998. After all items of a batch were scored, the participants had to enter each response into the correct cell on the scoring sheets provided. This again was a difficult task since the actual protocols differed considerably in the sequence of items. A test of success was when all 36 cells in a scoring sheet were filled and no duplicates and no empty cells remained. In spite of vigilance, this step was found to be time-consuming and especially prone to clerical error.

The next step for the raters was to calculate the simple and the cumulative response distributions for each protocol and to enter the information in the individual scoring sheet. After that, they determined the automatic preliminary ogive scores and the total weighted score for each protocol.

To arrive at a final TPR, the protocol is read as a whole to check whether the overall content reflects the theoretical characteristics of the probable stage and whether the protocol fulfills the additional requirements for being scored at that stage. For assigning postconventional ogives, more stringent cut-off numbers were used than the framework developers'. The higher the protocol, the more variety of structure and content, depth and complexity is required.

To assign TPRs, the raters also considered the required spread of high-stage categories for highest stage ratings. More detailed information will be given in Section 4 on determining cut-off numbers for high-end TPRs. Finally, the raters filled out a one page summary tally for each batch of 15 protocols.

When a finished batch was received, the item by item results from the individual scoring tallies and the summary information were entered into a spreadsheet. Doing it by batches was of practical advantage as it enabled data entry as it arrived rather than all at once and thus to get a feel how the project was progressing. Two raters finished the assignment within three months. Both scored sixty 36-item protocols and Set B of the individual completions. The third rater opted out after discovering that she did not have the patience for scoring minutiae although she had an affinity for the theory. This reaction can be readily understood by anyone who has rated protocols. While one needs to be somewhat compulsive to do a thorough matching job and systematic in entering data, a tendency to overanalyze the responses does not lead to improved ratings.

The final worksheet contained the individual scores for each of the 60 protocols, the automatic ogive, the TPR, and the TWS from the two independent raters, the researcher, and the scores from the previous rating with the old manuals. Next the total protocol ratings were compared for percentage agreement and reported both overall and in pairs.

Because of the proliferation of different forms, an item by item comparison was not done. Cronbach's alpha coefficient served as a measure of consistency. Exact agreement as well as agreement within one stage are also good indicators of interrater agreement. These were also calculated and will be reported below. The results from this statistical analysis were to tell how relatively reliable the postautonomous categories and stage distinctions are in scoring high-end protocols and in assigning final scores.

### Are stages 5/6 and 6 really different stages on a continuum?

To test whether the two new stages are both different from each other and higher than the Autonomous stage, the distribution curves for each kind of aggregate TPR type were compared. These distributions were drawn based on the results from scoring the 60 protocols. First, if the distribution moves towards the higher end overall with increasing TPR, there is some indication that the categories can detect the predicted order of the newly defined stages. Second, if there are differences between the different stages, their distribution curves should be discontinuous and have clearly separate peaks.

### Results

#### Sample analysis

The sample consisted of 31 women and 29 men between the ages of 16 and 79. 78% were between the ages of 26 and 65. In general they were highly educated with 34 of them having a Master's degree or higher.

Twenty-two subjects were in the management sector (8 mid managers, 2 supervisors, 6 executives, 2 professors of management, and 3 management consultants, as well as 1 entrepreneur). 25 were students at the time of taking the test at various levels of education. An artist, a priest, a professor of sociology, a military officer, a physicist, a physician, a couple of counselors and teachers were also among the sample. Two individuals were retired. Thus, education and management were heavily represented in this opportunistic sample. On the other hand, subjects for the protocol sample belonged to 45 different projects and came from across the USA, Canada and the UK which introduced some desired variability.

**Table 4.5 DEMOGRAPHIC CHARACTERISTICS OF SAMPLE: AGE AND EDUCATION (N=60) (f=31, m=29)**
| Age | <19 | 19-25 | 26-35 | 36-45 | 46-65 | 66+ | Unknown |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| f | 3 | 0 | 10 | 14 | 3 | 0 | 1 |
| m | 0 | 1 | 12 | 7 | 8 | 1 | 0 |

| Education | <HS | <BS/BA | <Masters | Masters/+ | ABD | Ph.D. | Unknown |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| f | 3 | 6 | 3 | 9 | 1 | 2 | 7 |
| m | 0 | 5 | 5 | 6 | 3 | 7 | 3 |

When studying high-stage performance, it is natural that people with more education are more likely to be included. The real surprises were a 17-year-old girl at a Magnet Arts High School, and a 47-year-old entrepreneur with a High School education. Both their protocols scored at the Construct-aware stage. A 26-year-old military officer scored at the Unitive stage. Overall seven out of eight stage 6 protocols came from people aged 26 to 44, the priest's age was unknown. Thus, the average of 33.7 is ten years below the expected age for a Unitive worldview. It had previously been assumed that it would occur only at or beyond middle age.

Except for the priest's, the Unitive protocols all came from graduate students but from different schools. Thus, the intellectual environment is likely to have played a significant role in their views and the ease with which they responded to the simple stimuli with complex utterances. A longitudinal study would be especially interesting in these cases to test whether (a) the participants will settle on a lower ego level in a less supportive and challenging environment after leaving school and/or (b) "graduate school" simply allowed them to be who they were earlier than they might have been in less conducive circumstances, or (c) other factors played a decisive role.

#### Interrater agreement

To test interrater agreement the paired Pearson correlations for TPRs for the two raters and the researcher were determined as well as Cronbach's alpha coefficient, which estimates internal-consistency or homogeneity.

**Table 4.6 PEARSON CORRELATIONS FOR TPRs BY THREE RATERS (R1, R3, R4) N=60**
| | Rater 1 | Rater 3 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Rater 3 | .95 | |
| Rater 4 | .84 | .79 |

Paired agreement for TPR between the two trained raters and the researcher was high with p values = .0001 for all correlations. Even Rater 4, who had some difficulty making the distinctions at the highest stage, did well. She agreed with the researcher r = .84, and with Rater 3, r = .79.

Cronbach's alpha coefficient was 0.95 for TPRs and 0.96 for the TWSs. An alpha of .95 shows that others can indeed learn to make the distinctions at the high end of the scale reliably using the new scoring criteria for stages 5/6 and 6.

The sums of the TPRs for the three raters were clustered tightly at 473, 474, and 469 respectively with corresponding means of 7.9, 7.9, 7.8 and Standard Deviations of 1.30 to 1.38. The average protocol of the sample was almost at the Autonomous stage 5. This was expected as the protocols had been selected based on ratings with the old manuals to fall about evenly into the five highest stages. The actual agreement between the old ratings (dating from 1980-1995) and the ratings of the same protocols with the Second Edition in July of 1998 was high. The Pearson Correlations were r = .91*** for TPR and r = .95*** for TWS.

A pattern output table, comparing the agreement patterns for three raters on 60 protocols, is another way of showing internal consistency. Complete agreement for all three raters occurred in 26 (43.3%) cases. There was three-way agreement within one stage in 30 cases (50%). In only four cases was the difference between the highest and the lowest score for a given protocol two stages, and in no instance was disagreement greater than two. Thus there was 93.3% three-way agreement within one stage, a most remarkable outcome for (1) having used stringent cut-off numbers, and (2) for having been achieved with a sample that, by all definitions, was difficult to score.

If looking at total agreement without distinguishing between stages 5/6 and 6, there was three-way 61.7% total agreement and 100% agreement within 1 stage. That agreement is higher when fewer distinctions are made, stands to reason. Overall, the demonstrated high consistency of agreement among all three raters testifies to the reliability and discriminative power of the distinctions that were introduced.

#### Sequentiality and discreteness of the new stages

The following numbers and the corresponding graph of the distribution curves for each ego stage (type of TPR) shows how, with increasing ego stage, the percentage of responses at a given stage shifts to the right. With each shift, fewer responses occur at the lower levels and more at increasingly higher ones in a clearly clustered pattern.

Table 4.7 makes vivid the wide range of completions seen in many high-stage protocols. When the distributions for each ego stage type are aggregated as they are here, the effect is even more noticeable. The widest spread occurred at the Autonomous stage where the twelve protocols had completions ranging from 2 to 6. The narrow spread for 3/4 could be an artifact of the N=1, but it also represents a general tendency of the conventional personality to adhere to the narrow parameters of conventionality. Not unusually, one encounters a protocol that is entirely located at the conventional tier.

**Table 4.7 FREQUENCY DISTRIBUTION BY TPR: PERCENTAGES OF ITEMS SCORED WITHIN EACH CATEGORY, BY STAGE. (N=2160 item ratings from 60 protocols)**
