As can be seen in Figure 4.2 below, the overall distributions are consistent with predictions as well as the gradual shift of the center of the distributions toward the right with increasing TPR. This reflects the shifting center of meaning making at successively higher ego stages. The graph shows that the distribution curves for the different personality types (ego stages) are clearly discontinuous and distinct from each other as each type of TPR peaks in a different area of the distribution space. The prediction that the distribution patterns for different ego stages are distinct is supported by the evidence.

For a stage theory to be hierarchical, the stages have to be distinct and sequential. Stages 5, 5/6 and 6 follow each other in a sequential order and are discontinuous according to the percentage frequency distributions for the different groups of TPRs. Therefore, the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage seem to fulfill the stage requirements for being higher order hierarchical integrations.

### Discussion

Is such high interrater agreement for TPRs replicable? Unlike scoring average protocols which can be "learned" by following the self-training exercises in the Second Edition and some discussion with other raters, learning to successfully score the high-end categories and stage distinctions requires thorough training, explicit theory and probably, minimally, a Conscientious ego stage. Even with intensive training, the raters tend to make systematic errors in the areas that are beyond their own ken. These can be picked up and addressed in a training situation, but would otherwise remain undetected. In this case, Rater 3 who had a special aptitude for the instrument, initially tended to overuse stage 5/6 (false positives) while Rater 4 generally missed the signs for 6 (false negatives). In addition, Rater 4 preferred the 4/5 rating over any other.

**Figure 4.2 DISTRIBUTION OF PERCENTAGE ITEM FREQUENCY BY TPR**

Problems with identifying the Autonomous stage: Both novice scorers avoided using the 5 rating for the individual completions because they said they found the manual too vague and inconclusive. They reported that they could not deduce the Autonomous personality type from the examples and descriptions given in the Second Edition. This uncertainty is again reflected in the contingency tables for whole protocols: Predictably, some disagreement occurred for assigning the highest, Unitive stage score since it is the most difficult to identify and most apt to reflect rater limitation. Between Rater 1 and Rater 3, there was still more agreement than disagreement (71%).

In contrast, the consistent disagreement for the Autonomous stage, seems more problematic. While Rater 1 and Rater 3 agreed 11 times, but disagreed 4 times, (73%) in assigning stage 5, the agreement for stage 5 between R1*R4 and R3*R4 were 18.8% and 15.8% respectively. For no other stages in the sequence was disagreement greater than agreement. Moreover, there didn't seem to be a pattern in the instances of low agreement. That the greatest rater uncertainty occurred around 5, seems to be a sign that there may be a problem with the New Edition in defining and illustrating the nature of the Autonomous stage.

This situation is partly due to the many new combinatorial categories for the Autonomous stage. The old manual left the assessment to the rater of whether a compound response was multi-part and more complex or merely multi-part by providing clear Scoring Rules for compound sentences. In 1993, the framework developers discussed this issue in the following manner, consistent with the higher complexity requirement:

"One of the toughest problems was how to score responses comprising two or more elements each of which could be scored alone. (...) Where two or more responses contrast in such a way that the combination generates paradox or greater complexity, then the rating is one level higher than for the highest element. The detailed rules parallel the rules of skill theory."

The Second Edition continues to list the same scoring rules, but then offers many new, combinatorial high-end categories. These simply add several conventional elements together regardless of whether the meaning they create is of a higher complexity than the constituting parts. Recipe-style assignment of postconventional ratings is clearly in conflict with the concept of ego development. It is the qualitative differences that make the difference among stages, not just the number of elements. As the expert rater commented earlier, by retaining the rules and introducing the combinatorial categories, many conflicts have been introduced. Given that all three scorers found fault with the Second Edition for some of the same reasons enumerated in Chapter 2, the Autonomous stage and the combinatorial categories are an area that perhaps needs to be reconsidered.

#### Rater bias

Since the distinctions at the postautonomous level are subtle and out of the range of most people's frame of reference, the possibility of rater limitation bias was a private concern at the beginning of this reliability study. Would the novice scorers be able to learn to make the distinctions quite possibly several stages above their own?

The experienced rater's score was known to be high. Thus, it could be anticipated that she would be able to assess high-end distinctions. No such expectations existed for the workshop participants found by email advertising. The more remarkable is the result and the more admirable was the performance of the two assistants. They became proficient in ego development theory as well as in the category and the stage distinctions for the two new postautonomous stages. They learned how to manage two scoring systems (old and new) with conflicting names and rules. Finally, they had to score 60 difficult protocols, and do all this in the short time of six months.

As mentioned in Chapter 2, unlike the established categories, the scoring categories for stages 5/6 and 6 are not stem-specific. Instead, they apply across the test to all items. Beginning at the postconventional tier, protocols become increasingly unique, richly varied in thought and emotional texture as well as in sentence structure. Because matches are improbable at this level of abstraction and complexity, it made more sense to extract characteristics for them at the most general level. The framework developers provide only 18 examples for the Integrated stage in the Second Edition, with most stems having no entry. Granted, dealing with both stem-specific and stage-wide categories for scoring high protocols, may be an unorthodox solution, but from the evidence so far, it may be more workable than the minimalist approach used in the Second Edition.

### Conclusion

People interested in using the proposed high-stage scoring categories and stage distinctions can learn to use them reliably for scoring high-end tests with appropriate training. Indeed, using the postautonomous distinctions and categories may give more distinct and valid results than using the Second Edition alone for projects with high-end protocols. Especially if postconventional ego development is conceived of as the gradual deconstruction of self-boundaries, consensus of what constitutes the best examples of mature ego development may increase among those exploring this area if the proposed high-stage characteristics are taken into account.

Finally, given the results of the interrater agreement study for rating whole protocols, there is strong evidence to refute the "null hypothesis" that rating high-end completions cannot be done reliably because systematic criteria for assessing postautonomous completions cannot be found.

In this section interrater agreement for sixty high-end protocols was tested, and a positive conclusion was drawn as to (a) the viability of measuring the proposed Construct-aware and the Unitive stages and (b) as to their emergence in the predicted hierarchical sequence.

The remaining step of the measurement chapter is to determine fitting cut-off numbers for assigning total protocol ratings to the two new postautonomous stages.

## SECTION 4: DETERMINING CUT-OFF NUMBERS

3. To what extent does using these new scoring categories and stage distinctions alter the distribution characteristics of postautonomous protocols? Do these changes warrant a revision of the cut-off numbers at the high end, and if so, what is a reasonable way to translate the distribution patterns into numerical guidelines for scoring postautonomous protocols?

Again, this step was designed to be explored only if warranted by the outcomes of the previous step. Since the protocol interrater agreement was sound, examining what rationale other than Bayes theorem might be used to determine cut-off numbers for assigning postautonomous ratings to 36-item protocols was proceeded with. It was felt that the numbers needed to be more in tune with people whose "center of gravity" seems to be at these two highest stages.

One of the many ways stage measurements differ is in the algorithm they use to determine what final stage score to give the collected material evidence. No matter what the method—structured interview, multiple choice test, signal detection procedure, or sentence completion tests—human beings tend to give responses at more than one level. By probing or inviting the participants to go deeper, one hopes that they will respond at their highest, most thoughtful level. One thus rates a test at the highest stage that is exhibited if enough evidence at that stage is present and not enough at the next higher level. How many instances are deemed to be "enough" is, of course, the big question, which gets answered by different researchers differently.

The unprobed test tends to elicit responses from a wider range of stages than probed instruments as indicated in Table 4.7. Low tests may be narrowly spread because of their authors' cognitive limitations, conventional ones are often entirely contained within the conventional tier, but most protocols range over 4 or more stages. Indeed, in the context of a whole high-end protocol, having the freedom to respond spontaneously without hiding one's occasional raw feelings can be a sign of non-defensiveness rather than impulsivity even though the particular element of the response might be rated at a preconventional stage.

To do justice to the tendency to give responses in a bell-like distribution curve, the ogive method (ogives = cumulative frequency polygons) was developed to determine ego stage using the cumulative distributions of the individual responses as a starting point.

Overall, there is agreement that ogives better reflect the variations in the distributions of real protocols than choosing either the highest level displayed or the most often used (mode) as a criterion for assigning a final score. As has been seen, the higher the stage, the more the center of the distribution curve moves to the right.

On the other hand, the requirement of two highest-stage item scores for a protocol to be rated at the highest stage is found to be too low. According to the criteria, the confidence level for two items is only 0.84. It reaches 0.98 for three items. In experience, people who function at the postautonomous stages respond more frequently with postautonomous responses. It is affirmed that "the most convincing protocols have three or four ratings" at the highest stage.

The postautonomous protocols that were examined showed distributions with significantly more high responses than anticipated. This makes sense if the ego stage of a person is understood to represent his or her core functioning or favored way of dealing with reality. When read as a whole, protocols with only two or three responses at the highest stage simply did not seem to fit the overall feel or expected profile of a person who has developed a postautonomous frame of mind. Even though the percentage of people at stages 5/6 and 6 is low (< 2%), those who are at them seem to clearly speak from that vantage point. Therefore, it was felt that cutoff numbers needed to be related to the actual number of postautonomous responses observed in postautonomous protocols, rather than merely based on probabilistic calculations.


---


# Postconventional and Postautonomous Ego Development: Theory and Measurement

## Methodological Considerations in Stage Assignment

As previously discussed, test creators must inevitably choose the final parameters by which they distinguish among stages in any method of assigning developmental stage scores to sets of empirical evidence. Whether this evidence consists of interview transcripts, behavioral observations, or sentence completions, the final choice of what constitutes evidence and how many pieces of evidence are required to draw a conclusion is a necessary step. This choice is also arbitrary—not in the sense of being random or capricious, but in the sense of being arbitrated, reasoned, and deliberated. It is a matter of choice and not inevitable; it is not the only choice reasonable people could make. Once a scheme is in place, this simple fact often seems to be forgotten. Especially in the realm of wisdom and mature human meaning-making, overly logical and mathematically-derived conclusions seem inadequate. This argument has been made previously in critiquing models of hierarchical complexity. For similar reasons, it is made here regarding the method of assigning final ratings to protocols at the high extreme. The mismatch between method and theory seems problematic.

### The Use of Bayes' Theorem

The reasoning for requiring only two responses at the highest stage for rating a protocol at the Integrated stage continues to be based on probabilistic grounds. The method, its costs, and its benefits were evaluated. Bayes' theorem was used to determine how many signs (completions) would be minimally necessary to assert that the protocol having the determined number of signs was likely to be at the extreme stage under consideration. The argument still relies on the low-end, Impulsive stage as a model case. The striking qualitative differences between low and high stages are ignored, and both ends are treated as interchangeable with respect to the statistical method. Below, it is spelled out how such probabilistic reasoning is tied to a concatenation of "ifs" and adjustable percentages for the proportion of protocols with or without the sign and the proportion of probable true and false positives.

According to this reasoning, the probability of correctly identifying a protocol as being at the highest stage is 34% if there is only one sign, and if it is assumed (a) that overall 5% of all responses are at the highest stage and, (b) that on average, false positives occur 5% of the time, and false negatives 50%. That is, with one sign, the confidence that a protocol is at the highest stage is only about 1/3. If there are two clear and "independent" signs (completions) for the highest stage, the odds of correctly identifying a protocol as being at that stage increase to 84% (which is 34% better than by chance), and if there are three signs, to 98%. Thus, three signs rather than two seem a more adequate choice as a minimal requirement even within this argument.

If the more realistic, empirically based 1% for each of the postautonomous stages is chosen, the probability figures for correctly identifying a high-end protocol change as follows: One sign = 10%, two signs = 50%, three signs = 91%, and four signs = 99%. Thus, two signs would be no better than assigning a high stage by chance. To require minimally four signs for rating a protocol at the Construct-aware or Unitive stages seems a better choice.

The decision to settle on only two signs was presumably made so that at least a few protocols made the cut, given that there were so few high-end protocols and consensus was lacking. The current suggestion of combining the Integrated with the Autonomous stage is reasonable if the projected highest stage is indeed as poorly understood and so rare as to be close to nonexistent.

However, as has been shown, this is no longer the case. The most insightful subjects in the sample seemed to have a clear and coherent voice coming from their postautonomous reality perspectives. If it is held that ego stages reflect the habitual frame of reference of their "owners," which is for them the most meaningful and coherent explanation for the way things are, one would expect to see a more consistent use of the preferred epistemology even in a test situation.

Having established that the postautonomous stage distinctions and categories have merit in rating high-end protocols, the ranges and averages of Construct-aware and Unitive completions for the protocols in this project were calculated. However, to determine final cut-off numbers for the entire postconventional tier, including the more frequent Individualistic and Autonomous stages, one would want to use the larger database from which this sample was drawn.

### Results

The following cut-off numbers were determined by examining the frequency distributions for all protocols at a given higher stage, and then determining the range and averaging the numbers.

Total Protocol Ratings (TPRs) for the Construct-aware and Unitive stages in this study were based on the "a-minimum-of-four" cut-off rule. Thus, the range starts with four. Four signs represent a 99% confidence that the protocol is at the given postautonomous stage based on Bayes' theorem and the realistic 1 percent occurrence for each of the postautonomous stages in the original large sample.

Given the average number of postautonomous responses of 6.1 at the Unitive stage, and 11.1 at the Construct-aware stage for the Unitive stage, requiring at least four Unitive stage completions at the Construct-aware stage for a Unitive stage rating seems sensible. Combined with the rationale of Bayes' theorem, this is stringent, but not unduly so. At least four ratings (average 9.3 at the Construct-aware stage) at the Autonomous stage looks appropriate too for a TPR at the Construct-aware stage. The minimally four ratings above the Autonomous stage may be either from the Construct-aware stage alone or distributed between the Construct-aware and Unitive stages. Additional conditions need to be fulfilled as well for a Construct-aware TPR. If ego stage represents a person's main frame of reference, then fewer than four responses at either the Construct-aware or Unitive stages may be seen as part of the natural spread or variability of developing human beings, not as an indication of their having reached those stages.

### Conclusion

Using ogives is an elegant solution to the problem of allocating stage scores as it "respects" the stage spread that characteristically occurs on the unprobed sentence completion test. Moreover, employing Bayes' theorem for the problem of determining cut-off numbers at the high-end was a creative move at the original time. On the other hand, Bayesian reasoning alone was found to be inappropriate at the upper end given the definition of ego stages as that frame of mind a person most readily employs in everyday situations. Anecdotal evidence from pilot interviews confirms that postautonomous adults really shift their attention away from Autonomous reasoning. They do not wish to go back to the previous, more limited way of being because it no longer seems adequate and satisfying.

In the end, only a good understanding of the processes governing ego development and a secure sense of the differences among different ego stages will result in making ego development scoring with the sentence completion test the successful translation of a qualitative into a corresponding quantitative entity that it is meant to be. But quantitative methods must remain a handmaiden to theory, not lead it. In this view, "measuring" ego development cannot be divorced from the raters' interpretation much beyond the conventional stages. What a carefully designed instrument can do is help to make the process leading up to the final rating as systematic and as bias-free as possible.

The revised manual does this passably for the lower stages, but it fails to do justice to the complexity and richness of the third tier of development, especially at the most mature positions. There, the ego becomes aware of its own shenanigans and learns to remain witness to the process of meaning-making rather than to constantly reach for rhyme and reason. Witnessing allows the habits of incessant self-evaluating and automatic categorizing of all other phenomena to be seen for what they are: temporarily successful means of perpetuating the illusion of object permanence.

## Discussion and Conclusion

### Summary of Thesis and Results

This research explored the rare phenomenon of postautonomous ego development in order to create more adequate theory and measurement of this little-understood area of adult development. The goal was to provide an alternative to the earlier conception of high-end ego development.

In light of two decades of research in the field of advanced adult development and new data from the sentence completion test, it seemed important to reassess both the theory and the instrument at the highest levels of observed ego development. In particular, the oft-repeated contention that postautonomous ego development can be vaguely described at best, and that systematic criteria for assessing it in sentence completions cannot be found, was challenged.

This whole long-term project started out based on some intriguing high-end responses to the sentence completion test. These unique responses did not fit existing ego development theory, nor could they be scored with the manual in a consistent or satisfactory manner. What were merely hunches initially about late-stage development became testable propositions as high-end sentence completions were systematically gathered and analyzed over more than a decade in order to shed some light on this neglected research area of developmental theory.

Overall, this research was conceived as a secondary analysis and testing of propositions about high-end ego development theory and measurement that were published earlier. At the start of this study, a reinterpretation of high-end ego development had been proposed, but not yet validated, in response to perceived problems and limitations of the earlier theory. It was recommended that the Integrated stage be replaced with two stages: a Construct-aware stage and a Unitary (Unitive) stage. These stages were characterized by increasing levels of deconstruction of the concept of a permanent object world (including the search for an enduring and objective self-identity) with increasing understanding of the constructed nature of everyday reality. Moreover, it was posited that, based on sentence completion data, the highest observable stage, the Unitive stage, shared some features with beginning ego-transcendent conceptions of reality as described in the spiritual traditions of the East and in contemporary transpersonal psychology.

The plausibility of such an interpretation of late-stage conceptions of reality was explored in the context of the current literature of postconventional development in both constructivist and transpersonal developmental psychology. For maximum comparability with the Autonomous and Integrated stages, theories were selected that (a) are non-domain specific, (b) investigate reality conceptions beyond the general systems stage, or indicate postautonomous concerns, and (c) focus on meaning-making and insight into the human condition rather than merely on cognitive complexity and abstract reasoning.

First, it was tested whether a different conceptualization of post-conventional development might be more productive in explaining some of the rare sentence completions that could not be meaningfully interpreted within the earlier psychological framework. The relevant works of authors who address postautonomous human development were analyzed for their explanations of development beyond conventional wisdom and beyond the well-integrated Autonomous ego stage.

In addition, how these researchers characterized people at the postautonomous level of development was investigated, and their attributes were compared with those listed in the scoring categories. There appeared to be a number of salient correspondences among these characterizations. Recognition of the constructed nature of reality and the limits of language, attention to the process of meaning-making in the rational realm—and especially with such concerns as the nature of polar opposites, paradox, and infinite regress—were mentioned as most characteristic of the first postautonomous level. Acceptance of change and process as basic aspects of experience and insight into the unity of the reality underlying all constructed differentiations was seen as the most indicative features of the next stage of the developmental trajectory.

Evidence from others' theories as well as their portrayal of late-stage characteristics verified the merit of the conceptualization of high-end ego development overall. Given the relatively few theorists who discuss the postautonomous developmental sector combined with the differing number of divisions they make at the postconventional tier, the question remains open of just how close the correspondences are. What can be said with some certainty is that this version of ego development theory is more or less convergent with the earlier theory up to the Conscientious stage. At the Conscientious stage, however, the theories start to differ. It is believed that divergent validity for this interpretation has been established since it has been shown that it can accommodate views of reality on sentence completion tests that elude the earlier theory.

Regarding the critique of the sentence completion test for measuring mature ego development, a set of nine scoring categories had been proposed for assessing and distinguishing between the Construct-aware and the Unitive ego stage. These categories were based on a thorough analysis of empirical data from the sentence completion test, but they had not yet been examined for reliability. Therefore, it was tested whether others could understand and reliably use the proposed scoring categories in rating high-end protocols. The high interrater agreement achieved confirmed the usefulness and reliability of the proposed scoring criteria. The overall concordance of results in this study is taken as further validation of the proposed high-end modification of ego development theory and measurement.

The high interrater agreement (Cronbach's alpha of .95 for Total Protocol Rating) would indicate that one can not only document and describe with greater clarity and accuracy how persons at the upper end of ego development create, maintain, and transform their perspectives on reality, but that one can actually measure this process. This research contributes to the present understanding of the nature of late-stage human development. Furthermore, this characterization of postconventional ego development may currently be the most detailed account of the process of mature self-development. It is based on empirical evidence and leads readily from a Western conception of development to Eastern models. It begins to bridge "bottom-up" conceptions of mature development as increasingly complex self-differentiations and integrations with the "top-down" conceptions of the permanent self as a transitional phenomenon that may be recognized as an illusion and potentially transcended.

### Discussion

"Measuring" personality and human behavior is difficult in the first place because of the complexity and variability, or the "obdurate vagary" of human beings. Yet, such measuring of psychological phenomena is at the very heart of psychometrics. The lasting contribution to psychology of the earlier framework is in having devised a novel way "to translate qualitative aspects of behavior to quantitative and to reduce diverse observations to single scores."

#### Benefits and Limitations of the Conception of Postautonomous Ego Development

The outcome of this study confirms that envisioning postconventional ego development as a continuation of differentiation and integration at ever higher levels of complexity alone is not sufficient to account for the kinds of reality conceptions that can occur after an Autonomous stage equilibration has been achieved. Such development can be better explained if one starts with the premise that people do not just continue to complexify the way they make sense of reality. Instead, they also become ever more conscious of (a) previously unexamined assumptions, (b) defensive maneuvers to safeguard the psychic status quo, and (c) the depth of their cultural and linguistic programming.

Researchers in the social sciences now generally agree about some of the problems and constraints of the conventional scientific or modern research paradigm, especially if applied to living organisms. They find that analytic-formal operational reasoning relies too exclusively on the rational faculty while it ignores other essential components of meaning-making such as affect and context. They comment that modern rational science (including psychology) divides subject and object, investigator and problem, knower and known into independent variables to be analyzed and explained separately in its attempt to be "objective." Instead, they insist that variables are interdependent and that the observer cannot be separated from that which is observed. They therefore suggest a systems view of reality as a more adequate paradigm for studying social phenomena.

In a similar vein, constructivist developmental theorists posit that adults at the first postconventional level of development may also recognize the systemic nature of reality. This new understanding is generally described in the literature as two different, but complementary and coexisting forms of thought. Systems thinking proper focuses more on structure. It answers questions of how parts of a system relate and influence each other, and how different systems are nestled within each other. Within a general systems view of reality, researchers acknowledge the multiple relationships among variables and the existing networks of interpretations.

The dialectical form of postconventional reasoning, on the other hand, emphasizes the process by which we make sense of knowledge and observations. It explains the regular and continuing process of meaning-making through thesis, antithesis, and synthesis; or construction, deconstruction, and reconstruction. Both these forms of systems awareness generate their own limits: The systems thinker can never answer the question of where the boundaries between observed entities are to be drawn as each "entity" is itself a whole and part of a larger whole, one system nestled inside another—ad infinitum. The dialectical thinker eventually begins to wonder whether and at what point the dialectical process might reach its functional limit.

In a differentiation beyond the systems level, people become aware of these quandaries. Postautonomous individuals begin to be fascinated with questions of how their minds work and how they make sense of reality through ever more complex theories, rational maneuvers, and interpretations. They become especially concerned with the many automatic mental habits that they unconsciously metabolized through a lifetime of cultural conditioning. Of these, the language habits of dualistic splitting and reification are probably the most intractable and the last ones to be recognized.

Few theoreticians have ventured into explication of the second level of postconventional development, the postsystems, or postautonomous one. As has been shown, they tend to agree that some form of recognition of the constructed nature of reality and of the inevitable problems created by our dependence on symbolic representations are always present at these more advanced stages of development. An appreciation of inevitable paradox in the rational realm and the complementarity of polar opposites are also often mentioned. Another characteristic of highly evolved people is the depth at which they experience and understand the interdependence of self and others and the complexity of thoughts, experiences, feelings, and memories at play.

Moreover, the more highly evolved an individual, the clearer become the non-distinctiveness of the "permanent object world" and the impermanence of the precious self. Regarding postautonomous descriptors, the transpersonal authors also portray the last phase of personal development as showing increased mind-body-feeling integration and greater awareness of the constructed nature of reality.

People at the postautonomous level reveal on sentence completion tests that they are increasingly concerned about their ingrained behavior patterns. They are keenly aware of the human tendencies to reify and complexify. As social beings habituated into language, they often realize that they share these tendencies with all other human beings. What's more, they can acknowledge that these tendencies may be a futile attempt at arresting a profound sense of impermanence and insubstantiality. An early harbinger of postmodernism gave exquisite expression to this awakening:

> Man's freedom is a fabricated freedom, and he pays a price for it. He must at all times defend the utter fragility of his delicately constituted fiction, deny its artificiality. (...) It is one of the most remarkable achievements of thought, of self-scrutiny, that the most anxiety-prone animal of all could come to see through himself and discover the fictional nature of his action world.

Anyone who has tried to stop the mind from producing discursive thought knows how fascinating and frustrating this seemingly independent process can be. We cannot help but discern, classify, and label experience. No matter how hard we try, we can rarely just witness life as it unfolds without reacting to and evaluating what we believe is happening.

It is the transpersonal theories and Eastern ways of conceptualizing human nature that offer a viable explanation for this state of human affairs and a perspective beyond the dilemma. Zen Buddhism, for instance, has developed a technique by which students are confronted with their attachment to discursive reasoning. Essential to the Zen koan is "paradox," i.e., that which is "beyond" "thinking," and transcends the logical or conceptual. Since the koan eludes solution by means of reasoning, it requires an intuitive leap to another level of comprehension beyond the logical contradictions and the dualistic splitting inherent in the rational, representational mode of knowing.

Individuals who have known this other way of being tell us that the direct experience of reality is ultimately more meaningful and satisfying to them than any previous worldview filtered through the watchful ego. Yet in the same way that infants need years of practice and modeling to learn to see the phenomenal flux as the world of permanent objects according to their particular linguistic surround, so the achievement of Subject permanence seems to be greatly aided by the committed discipleship to a given spiritual path. The steady practice of accessing and experiencing transcendent states of awareness in meditation is understood as a way of unlearning, or deconditioning the prior more limited form of reality apperception via language and rational deliberation. It is a gradual path toward a different, more inclusive and expansive mode of knowing. Subtle distinctions among the meditative approaches, however, exist as to the description of the ego transcendent stages. Only at the proposed ultimate level of being, even the subtlest distinctions are said to disappear and become meaningless. This realization was expressed in the following lines: "All distinctions are mind, by mind, in mind, of mind / No distinctions no mind to distinguish." Unity realization is also mirrored in the words of an ancient Buddhist precept: "Insight is the ultimate illusion."

It is wondered what implications it would have for psychology if both the capacity for symbolic representation of experience and for the transcendence of the mediated form of relating and meaning-making were considered as innate, that is, as part of human nature.

Although the Integrated individual is likened to the self-actualizing person, neither the theory nor the instrument, which is meant to illustrate the characteristics of people at a given stage, makes clear how the Integrated self's frame of reference differs qualitatively from the Autonomous one. More problematic yet, the theory is silent about the ego becoming aware of its self-perpetuating tendencies and of the possibility of transcending the rational, discursive mindset.

Similar limitations hold for the proponents of purely analytical constructivist models of development. They cannot detect the kind of profound integration that results in the simplicity that is occasionally evident in Unitive protocols. Because they take the representational, symbolically mediated reality as the only kind of reality that exists, they are limited to assessing the structural complexity of rational thought. They cannot detect its fundamental limitations. As a result, purely cognitive approaches tend to privilege intellectual prowess, logical sophistication, complexity, and formal aspects of meaning-making at the expense of deeper insight and wisdom.

Constructivist developmental theories that focus on meaning are not limited in the same way by the method as structural models are. They are, on the other hand, not free from influences of the researcher's own development. Interestingly, it is not necessarily obvious in a given case whether such limitations occur by unconscious researcher bias or by conscious choice. It is assumed that mindfulness would allow some mature people not to have to reveal everything they understand in the service of a continuing dialogue across diverse boundaries.

One of the problems of comparing this conceptualization with other theories containing postautonomous components has been the relatively few distinctions that are being made in general in the third, postconventional tier of development. The Interindividual stage as well as the Centauric self ranges over several of the steps considered distinct in postconventional development. The "unhappiness" expressed in an Individualistic protocol has an entirely different tone (annoyance) than the existential despair sometimes evident in Construct-aware sentence completions. They originate from different perceptions of what is at stake. While the individualist may harbor hopes for potential agreement behind the facade of multiple interpretations, the Construct-aware person is uncertain whether the concept of agreement is even meaningful in human affairs. In turn, their shared dystonic affect is different from the sense of certainty, commitment to affirm order, and high self-esteem typical of Autonomous persons.

Finally, when respondents show enough evidence on their sentence completion tests that they have let go of any concepts of how the world ought to be, and, instead, show an acceptance of experience as it unfolds, they are said to be at the Unitive stage, the last stage detectable with the sentence completion test.

The Unitive stage allows individuals to be with or witness rather than label experience. This capacity for witnessing invites comparison with non-attached, transpersonal modes of being. If people can develop a non-evaluative perspective on their own as a natural outcome of profound self-inquiry in the personal domain, should the Unitive stage be considered to belong to the fourth, ego-transcendent tier of development?

There is no way to tell from the available evidence whether Unitive persons consistently or only occasionally escape the entrapments of the habits of the mind. We cannot know from an instrument like the sentence completion test whether an individual has realized Subject permanence. According to transpersonal literature, higher stages of consciousness have clear physiological correlates that are crucial in assessing whether or not and to what degree a person has achieved ego-transcendence. Such physiological changes can and have been empirically documented, especially in regards to the Transcendental Mediation technique. It is therefore not believed that the sentence completion test is ever likely to offer enough evidence to determine the status of higher consciousness of people at the Unitive ego stage. Nor is the sentence completion test a fitting and useful tool for further exploring and testing adult development at the transition to and at the transpersonal tier.

Thus, insufficient evidence is one of the salient problems of the theory of the Unitive stage. The level of "vagueness" that was originally found needed addressing regarding the Integrated ego stage has now been pushed up a few notches. It is believed that this interpretation offers both a more accurate conceptualization of postautonomous ego development and a more veridical description of the experience of postautonomous individuals than earlier theory. Yet, it is hoped it will, in turn, be extended again as more is learned. It may also change radically when entirely new perspectives become available. What seems to be a definite advance in psychology is that this particular reconceptualization of high-end ego development provides a productive way of linking insights from conventional Western and transpersonal psychology.

Some quarters inevitably object that normative theories in general and higher stages in particular "unfairly" privilege complexity and insights which have nothing to do with ordinary people's lives. A model is followed in responding to this challenge. Only a theory that contains in itself the means for detecting its own limitations and partialness while it simultaneously honors the possibility of greater inclusiveness and integration can really do justice to the universal human need for orientation and for relationship to an Ultimate Reality or transcendent value.

Unlike the nihilistic tenor of much postmodern deconstructivism, ego development theory sounds a hopeful note (a spirited no to no) as long as it retains its continuously self-examining, boundary-opening stance. From the perspective of ego development theory, the nihilistic type of postmodern deconstructivism is an understandable reaction to some of the excesses of the modern mindset, but not the final position at which one must inevitably arrive. Postmodern deconstructivism creates just the kind of double bind that becomes painfully obvious only at the Construct-aware stage. It defends its relativistic stance that there are no absolutes—absolutely. Similar to people at the Individualistic stage, proponents of this position cannot yet detect the fallacy of their arguments. From a developmental point of view, absolute relativism or postmodern deconstructivism may be understood as an epistemological step on the way to a relativism that is aware of its own partialness as an ideology.

Given these insights, postconventional development in the personal realm can be described as a step-by-step deconstruction of the modern rational, analytical mindset. Such development includes a conscious uncoupling from the conventions that lead to its acquisition as well as the gradual divestiture of absolutizing tendencies as these are part of the reification paradigm.

In addition, developmental theory that extends beyond the rational domain also offers a response to people's frustration with the many unexpected and disastrous side effects and long-term dangers of much technology that grew out of the positivistic tenets of modernity. Transpersonal conceptions of human nature as more than material can serve as possible alternatives. Already many people from all walks of life feel drawn to Eastern spirituality and indigenous practices of meaning-making because these promise both connection to an ultimate as well as inner wealth and contentment, more lasting and profound than any material satisfaction.

At the same time, the relationship between people's ego stage and their readiness for benefiting from the influx of the numinous remains to be explored. The propensity of the ego to usurp the spiritual has been powerfully spoken about. It seems plausible that the higher the ego stage in the personal realm, the less likely transcendent experiences are distorted to enhance the ego. Beginning with the Construct-aware stage, such tendencies would actually be anticipated, unearthed, and "jousted with."

### Scoring with the New Criteria

#### Training Issues

As reported, collecting relevant data and finding people willing to learn to score a random sample of complex protocols from the upper half of the scale was difficult. The training sessions were encouraging because the debate was vigorous and illuminating. Still, being aware of the caveat that there are major differences among qualified raters for rating the highest stage, the study was designed for all eventualities. Since the novice raters had had little time to digest the theory and to practice scoring beyond the training sessions, modest performances were anticipated. The very high interrater agreement for rating whole protocols was thus a genuine surprise.

A Cronbach alpha coefficient of .95 for total protocol ratings, and .96 for total weighted scores is considered strong evidence for the consistency of rater agreement. But what does it mean that these novice raters, each independently, achieved interrater reliabilities with this very difficult sample as good or better than those of experienced teams with more average populations?

The first concern is naturally whether such a result could be replicated with a different set of raters. Given that neither of the other raters was known before the training, there was no control over what talent or dedication they brought to the task. Even though one of the novice raters consistently overlooked the subtle distinctions between the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage responses when she was on her own, she did seem to grasp them when supported in training. It is to her credit that she did not use Unitive stage scores where she did not see them since she was well aware of the purpose of this study. In spite of this difficulty, she mastered the theory and the combined instruments sufficiently overall to be able to score very reliably.

Both raters received intensive instruction not only in how to score in general and in how to use the high-end categories, but they also got a thorough dose of the interpretation of ego development. It is positive that self-training alone is not feasible for gaining adequate scoring proficiency with the high-end categories. One probably also needs to have at least a formal operational frame of reference, as well as an interest in character development and semantic nuances to learn to apply them consistently. Doing the self-training homework in the revised manual before and during the workshop in addition to about twenty-five hours of intensive training may be enough for a person to become proficient in both general and high-end scoring. For such a complex measure, this is a very brief period.

There is some sense that enthusiasm, experience, and expertise partially account for the positive outcome. This is, of course, a problem if others are interested in learning to score with the new criteria. Thus, a possible future goal is to operationalize the teaching approach and to create a scoring manual for high-end protocols. There would also be a need to think about how to assure the responsible use of the high-end scoring criteria by others. The liberties people have taken with the original measurement stand as a warning.

#### Usefulness of the New Categories

When entering the field of developmental psychology, there was some confidence in the ability to draw theory from a careful reading of salient and seemingly reasonable high-stage sentence completions. Every time such a response was encountered, the question was asked: what kind of view of reality would a person have to have in order to make this statement? It was trusted that, with patience, enough evidence could slowly be accumulated to allow for some meaningful generalizations to this question. There was much less confidence initially whether a way could be found to systematically measure these rare, unreplicated responses.

As indicated, it has been argued that improving the measure at the upper end could not be done because of the paucity of data, the uniqueness of the completions, and because of likely researcher bias.

There was faith that, with patience, the first two hurdles could be overcome. In which way the third blinds to alternative interpretations and other problems, is left to others to point out. Comments from researchers concerned with these matters are awaited and welcomed. Especially meaningful would be reactions from those whose meaning-making is being mapped. How well do these categories capture postautonomous people's actual lived experience? Can they relate to the characterization? Does it ring true, false, or partially adequate? How do people identified as being at the same high ego stage actually understand each other, and how do they differ? The unique, non-replicated responses in postautonomous protocols are an indication that people at the highest stages have much more room to roam and to manifest in different ways than people at earlier, more conventional reality views.

The earlier scoring categories are narrowly defined and differ for each of the 36 sentence stems on the test. They are strictly item-specific and are therefore applicable only within the context of the sentence completion test. In contrast, it has been suggested that the stage-wide, thematic categories for identifying the postautonomous stages may be useful beyond the sentence completion test. While they were empirically derived, they seem to characterize mature human development in general. Employing the categories in an in-depth interview context, for instance, to explore whether highly developed individuals in other cultures hold similar views of reality would be a valuable extension of the developmental inquiry into human nature.

### Using the Revised Manual

#### Unexpected Results

An interesting and unexpected result of this study is that the Autonomous stage seems to be as poorly described in the revised manual and less well documented than in the older manuals as the Integrated stage. The novice raters were not able to deduce the Autonomous personality type from the information and examples given. They were especially confused by the many combinatorial categories. The training group raised many more questions about how to recognize the Autonomous stage than anticipated given experience in training with prior manuals. The expert rater and the novice raters alike complained about the lack of definition and distinctive examples for the Autonomous stage.

That the greatest interrater disagreement occurred in regard to Autonomous ratings in the protocol interrater agreement study seems to corroborate the seriousness of the problem. As the expert rater remarked in summing up her encounter with the revised manual, "What saddens me is that it [the theory] is less clearly stated than in the older manual, so fewer people can take the ball and run with it." Currently, the characterization of the Autonomous stage in the manual is too inconsistent for reliable results. There is little doubt that more consistent and "telling" categories for the Autonomous stage could be created from existing sentence completion test data in the same way that the criteria for the two postautonomous stages were developed.

#### Research with High-End Protocols

Several additional changes at the third tier of the revised manual make scoring postconventional protocols more unreliable. First, the use of many "recipe" categories has effectively erased the earlier meaningful scoring rules for compound sentence completions. Second, the ogive rules have been further loosened. Now only 6 out of 36 responses need to be beyond the conventional stage for a protocol as a whole to be rated as postconventional. In this view, the distinctions at the postconventional tier have been watered down over time rather than becoming sharper and more explicit.

The concerns can be summarized. First, the new recipe-style, combinatorial categories eliminate the meaningful distinctions made in scoring rules between compound sentence completions that do generate a higher level of complexity and those that do not. In the revised manual, many examples with conventional ideas that do not "generate a higher level of conceptual complexity" are now rated at the postconventional tier disregarding the existing rule and the underlying logic. When such a crucial aspect of developmental theory as the complexity demand for higher ratings is no longer reflected in the measurement methodology, one wonders whether the construct validity of the whole scheme has been diminished.

Furthermore, in several cases, the group of workshop participants could not find reasonable explanations for the placement of other examples in the "rationalized" revised manual. Some responses that can readily be differentiated from each other by stage are bunched together under one category heading; others are assigned to different stages even though they reflect a very similar frame of reference both semantically and structurally. As a group, there was disagreement with the manual in general, and the combinatorial categories in particular, many more times than was expected from experience with the older manuals.

Second, the bar for protocols to be rated at the postconventional tier has again been lowered in the revised manual. Stricter cut-off numbers are argued for at all postconventional stages for the same reasons that they were opted for at the highest two stages. To say that someone functions at a specific ego stage makes sense only when the evidence from the sentence completion test supports at least some preference or reasonable proportion of responses coming from the target level.

Third, the problem with using Bayes' theorem to determine the minimal number of 'signs' necessary to assign a score at the statistically rare extremes of the scale has been discussed. Both ends of the developmental trajectory are treated as interchangeable concerning the statistical method and thus, the vast qualitative differences between low and high stages are not honored. Simple average frequency distribution counts in protocols that appear to consistently reflect a given postconventional perspective are as good a choice to determine how many responses one might reasonably expect on average at a specific postconventional stage than more complex probabilistic methods.

Fourth, whatever number the test makers decide on for the automatic cut-off rules, they are a starting point, a ballpark estimate of where a protocol is likely to be. They do not take the quality of the whole protocol into account. The sentence completion test is meant to assess people's ego stage, that is, to distinguish among the different types of meaning-making from its earliest to its most mature expression. The higher the protocol, the more unique the responses and the more the subtlest, qualitative differences make the difference between one stage and the next. The diversity of content and sentence structure, the richness and presence of stage-specific themes and concerns, cognitive complexity, style of delivery, relational space and time awareness are essential for assigning an advanced stage score.

Any attempt at making scoring easier, less thoughtful, less dependent on one's in-depth knowledge of the underlying psychological processes is bound to weaken the distinctions that differentiate the stages. The trend of the revised manual to "mechanize" scoring by using combinatorial categories and by relying on automatic ogive rules does a disservice to the intricacies and original power of the conception of ego development. No matter how effectively statistical means are employed to assist in the assignment of a preliminary stage score, the final assessment of postconventional protocols ought to be based on a thorough understanding of developmental theory. The final step of reading a whole protocol and arriving at a Total Protocol Rating by taking into account all the available evidence can simply not be discarded without losing much of the quality that made ego development theory and the original test so powerful and engaging.

As the expert rater observed, "this manual reads like WISC-Lite!" It appears that the test creators introduced some of these changes to make the instrument easier to learn and the results easier to input into an electronic format for analysis as well as more widely accessible and commercially successful. All psychometricians have to make difficult decisions in which practicality, psychometric adequacy, demands from clients, financial concerns, and personal preferences play a role. Because the revised manual reflects even less well than earlier manuals the distinctions among postconventional ego stages, many of the changes that have been introduced are regretted.

### Endnotes

1. A preferred list of stems was also devised. Starting in 1984, a 24-item professional form was developed, which is now expanded to 36 items. This was done after busy executives repeatedly complained about the available forms as too boring (too many family items and sexual matters) and too few items relevant to adult professional lives. "When a child won't join in group activities" was replaced with "when people step out of line at work" and "a good father" with "a good boss." Both of these items have a manual. Especially postconventional individuals readily notice the overall tenor of the test (Freudian legacy) and get annoyed or bored with the repetition and narrow focus.

2. This fixed one problem, only to cause another. There are several major constructivist developmental theories whose numeric stage names approximately paralleled those of the earlier theory. The parallelism is now lost through this relabeling. Especially since this is a cosmetic change, not a revision of the theory itself, it seems that some consideration of other research in the field would have been an important criterion when contemplating major changes. In this revision, the old stage labels are used and the more recent ones are added in parentheses.

3. For this study, only 14-item protocols were used in order to avoid duplication with responses used for whole protocols. First, protocols rated lower than the Conscientious-Conformist stage with the old manuals were deleted, assuming that there would be enough conventional material left given the known distribution of the protocols. 20 protocols were randomly selected and sorted by stage. All responses lower than the Conformist stage were eliminated because testing these was not at issue. Next, 45 responses from the postautonomous individual response data pool were added in order to even out the numbers for the two highest stages for each stem. All of the responses from the protocols and the individual postautonomous response list were combined into one data set of 427 items and sorted by stem and stage. Stems that did not occur in the revised manual were deleted. Among the remaining 36 stems, the twelve with the most responses were chosen, again eliminating the rest. This left a list of 217 items from 12 stems ordered by stage ranging from the Conformist stage to the Unitive stage. From that final list, a grid was created with columns A and B for each item and responses from the list were assigned alternatively to either Set A or B in the order they appeared in such a way that they were spread among the ego levels and evenly matched between Set A and B.

4. A procedure for this step has been published (a downloadable Macro in Excel). It has been found that sentence completions at the high-end of the scale regularly are longer than the maximum length for text strings per cell in Excel. In addition, 55 different stems had to be accounted for. Thus, the macro could not be taken advantage of.

5. Sample ogive argument for the postconventional tier: In order to be rated at the Construct-aware stage, a protocol must have no more than 32 ratings (previously 34) at the Autonomous stage, that is at least 4 ratings (previously 2 ratings) above the Autonomous stage. If the protocol has more than 32 ratings at the Autonomous stage, one checks the next lower stage and so on. In order to be rated at the Autonomous stage, a protocol must have no more than 30 (previously 31) item ratings at the Individualistic stage. In order to be rated at the Individualistic stage, a protocol must have no more than 27 at the Conscientious stage (previously 28, now 30 item ratings).

6. It is one of the disadvantages of using studies from other projects that one can often not feasibly do follow-up studies when interesting questions like this one about a population arise.

7. It is pointed out that the new raters did use scores below the Conformist stage (9 instances) where appropriate. This gives some indication that they were alert to the fluctuations and did not merely rate everything high because they expected "high." To test whether that tendency was present, one Conformist protocol was included in the data set, which had quite a different tenor than the Conscientious ones without alerting the raters to this fact. One rater recognized that the protocol was at the Conformist stage, the other rated it at the Conscientious stage, but had a very low total weighted score for it.

8. Although disappointment with the revised manual may have influenced the trainees, enthusiasm for the method and theory overall clearly also made them willing to dig deep and learn to argue scoring matters at a level of great sophistication and insight. Overall, there seem to be more examples in the revised manual which are placed at given stages that cannot be "rationalized." In several cases during the practice exercises in the revised manual, there was agreement as a group, after thorough deliberation, at which stage to rate a response while disagreeing with the revised manual.

9. The revised manual gives 11 examples for the Integrated stage divided among 6 stems: 1, 2, 6, 10, 12, and 33 as well as a few more in the demonstration chapter. This number is down from 49 examples across 25 stems in the old manual.

10. When dealing with shorter protocol forms, one postautonomous completion will make the difference between an Autonomous or an Integrated rating in the revised model. That scorers are reluctant to make such a judgment call is understandable. Thus, all interested parties seem to agree that for projects that may have postconventional protocols, the 36-item test form is to be preferred.

11. Calculations if P=1%, Q=99%, p1=50%; p2=5%.
How sure are we with 1 sign that a protocol is at the rare Construct-aware stage?
Hp = P(p1) / [P(p1) + Q(p2)] = (0.01)(0.5) / [(0.01)(0.5) + (0.99)(0.05)] = 0.005 / (0.005 + 0.0495) = 0.005 / 0.0545 = 0.09 ~ 10%
How sure are we with 2 signs?
Hp = P(p1)^2 / [P(p1)^2 + Q(p2)^2] = (0.01)(0.5)(0.5) / [(0.01)(0.5)(0.5) + (0.99)(0.05)(0.05)] = 0.50 ~ 50%
How sure are we with 3 signs?
Hp = P(p1)^3 / [P(p1)^3 + Q(p2)^3] = 0.91 ~ 91%
How sure are we with 4 signs? ~ 99%
P = Population at highest stage
Q = Population not at highest stage
p1 = estimated true positive
p2 = estimated false positive
P/Q > p2/p1 = 0.01/0.99 > 0.05/0.5
Q = 99%, p2 = 5%, P = 1%, p1 = 50%
Earlier calculations if P = 5%, Q = 95% then 1 sign ~ 34%; 2 signs ~ 84%; 3 signs ~ 98%.

12. Given recent research, the current cut-off numbers seem inadequate. People at the Construct-aware and the Unitive stage often produce as many high-end responses as seen in earlier stages. They really do look at the world from these more complex perspectives in a consistent and convincing way. It is assumed that in the future, the numbers will be revised again and perhaps the Bayes theorem argument left behind as the highest stages are becoming more common. Unfortunately, the sentence completion test and its cousins cannot measure stages much beyond the linguistically mediated realm precisely because sentence completion tests are language dependent. Other methods will have to be used or developed to measure ego-transcendent stages.


---


The previous scoring manual may be satisfactory for rating first and second tier protocols, but it is inadequate in the way it describes and adjudicates postconventional development. As a result, the likelihood that a conventional protocol is now being "mismeasured" as postconventional has increased considerably. It would seem that relying on the new postautonomous distinctions and more stringent requirements for assigning postconventional stages may be advantageous for projects that explore postconventional populations. Studies that investigate individuals in professional leadership roles, for instance, regularly contain a good proportion of postconventional protocols. Nineteen out of the 25 managers in the sample were rated postconventional regardless of their status (middle management to executives) and regardless of the size of the company (small, medium, and very large corporations). Studies with senior executive teams that are interested in playing a positive role in transforming their organizations have produced several high-end sentence completion tests. Furthermore, such postconventional executives have been shown by multiple measurement criteria to be more effective at changing their organizations in positive directions than their conventional peers.

Given the many problems of the previous manual at the postconventional tier, it is justifiable to advocate for the new measuring categories and scoring rules as well as the use of the older manuals for dealing with projects that have predominantly postconventional subjects. It is also recommended to use the 36-item "professional" sentence completion test form because it is more relevant and interesting to professional test takers, and thus more likely to elicit authentic responses.

### Conclusion

Even though it cannot be claimed that the Unitive ego stage denotes the achievement of ego transcendence as defined by transpersonal psychology, the revision of ego development theory at the postautonomous level is clearly an advance over previous models which do not allow for the possibility of development beyond the personal realm. The Construct-aware and the Unitive stages constitute real transformations in meaning making beyond the Autonomous stage while previous theories describe postautonomous meaning making poorly and provide insufficient evidence for what such development looks like. The interpretation of high-stage ego development presented here has proven to be robust because it was derived from and continues to be supported by empirical evidence.

The new scoring criteria uniquely characterize the two postautonomous ego stages and serve at the same time for measuring them. They allow raters to distinguish postautonomous responses from each other and from lower stage completions regardless of the stem. Their thematic content can also be used to ascertain that the whole protocol shows postautonomous characteristics, not just the individual completions.

In general, it is argued that for an analysis of how people create coherent meaning in their lives, theoretical consistency, verisimilitude and plausibility are more important than probabilistic considerations in the creation of theory and measuring tools. It will be interesting to compare postconventional stage scores derived from (a) interview-based assessments, (b) the previous manual alone and (c) combined with the new postautonomous categories and the more stringent ogive rules.

Unless the whole endeavor of understanding human beings is driven by insightful theory the "mismeasure of man" too readily results. If a considerable number of conventional people are rated at postconventional stages, we do not have an accurate and effective way to assess important differences among people. If, on the other hand, a person seems to embody a particular postconventional self-definition or speaks from its center of gravity by giving ample evidence of its presence, then we can place him or her at that stage with more justification.

It is also emphasized that technical decisions pertaining to measurement and manual construction are fundamentally arbitrary, that is, dependent on the instrument creators' experience and judgement. Thus, they await confirmation or disconfirmation from testing and observation in the field. There is little doubt that meaningful theory should lead measurement and not the other way around. Logical coherence and plausibility should weigh as much as statistical significance when we are trying to explain human beings.

It is not certain what impact this study will have on actual scoring practice. The modifications of ego development theory at the upper end are not needed in most projects using the sentence completion test because these rarely contain postautonomous protocols. The previous manual remains adequate and efficient for most research and assessment situations. On the other hand, for those interested in studying postconventional ego development, having a set of criteria by which to explore the characteristics of people at the highest stages may prove to be a valuable analytical tool.

### Impact on Education

The study of exemplary individuals, those who are a step ahead of their fellow beings in insight, tolerance, and wisdom, is often inspirational. Based on sentence completion tests, postautonomous adults, even though rare, do live, work and play among us. As people who have developed a dynamic, fluid way of meaning making, they are perhaps especially well equipped to deal with the exigencies in training and education that face leaders in today's world. Mature insight into the process by which experience is reified in language may allow them to build bridges (or become the bridges) for and be mentors to those not yet aware or ready for this kind of understanding. Being free to move in and out of frames of reference, they can better appreciate the needs of diverse groups for unique identification, while promoting a collaborative focus on what connects us across geography, history, language, culture, ideology, race, development, and personal experience.

In an age where more and more adults return to learning throughout the lifespan, knowledge of the spectrum of different worldviews they may have is important for pedagogic reasons: First, institutions of higher learning need to know how to understand and support those with mature self-views since their needs are different from the younger college populations of the past. Second, the discipline of psychology ought to include teaching concepts of postconventional adult development to those who, in their professional and personal roles, are in a position to influence other adults' lives. In the organizational development sector, people with postconventional, more flexible self-views, long-term perspectives, and awareness of the constructed nature of knowledge have already been identified as more likely to lead others into the global economic and social order effectively and responsibly. Third, our knowledge of adult development influences what institutions and learning opportunities we create for adults. How well we support them, in turn, bears upon the future of society because of adults' omnipresent influence on children as parents, teachers, role models, and policy makers.

On the eve of the third millennium, it behooves anyone looking at a field of inquiry to anticipate at least some of the challenges awaiting those who toil in it. With the advent of the electronic information age and instant worldwide communication and commerce, people around the globe are exposed to the sights and sounds of other cultures and other languages as never before. The kind of conventional socialization described in earlier literature, in which children are initially exposed to one fairly coherent worldview, is now a rarity. As researchers point out, we no longer live in isolated cultures. Children everywhere in the world encounter other lifestyles, and other ways of being either personally by participating in pluralistic societies or by being targeted by global enterprise and communication channels.

All of this has created a formidable new challenge for the development of a sense of groundedness and orientation. Unlike earlier periods in history we now have to generate a sense of meaning out of many diverse, and often incompatible or contradictory beliefs and behaviors. No longer can we follow in the footsteps of our elders. Each generation finds itself creating its own assemblage of symbols and meanings, often chosen from many cultures and lifestyles and from tools, artifacts and memes that never existed before. It is truly a wonder how young people under these novel conditions actually do develop a coherent story about themselves, about human existence and about the meaning of life.

For some, the multitude of options might be destabilizing and disabling. For others, the new contingencies might call forth creative responses beyond our powers of imagination. The new multicultural, symbol-saturated, electronically interconnected reality may well provide opportunities for discovering new paths to expressing human nature.

Among the adults of our generation who have formed identities in the middle of the twentieth century, the current climate may support us in becoming more aware of the process of acculturation and the power of language in shaping reality. As each individual is challenged to orient himself or herself in this brave new world and to find a way to create a meaningful existence, so is psychology challenged to document and make sense of the as yet uncharted ways new generations will negotiate the basic human task of meaning making.

To summarize, this research has clarified and tested earlier hypotheses about how human beings differ in the ways they make sense of reality at the postconventional tier of development, and especially at its high end.

First, the reconceptualization of postconventional development as a step-wise deconstruction of the assumptions acquired in the first two tiers of development has been supported by the characterization of mature development in other contemporary development theories, both constructivist developmental and transpersonal. In addition, conceiving of the Construct-aware and the Unitive ego stages in the manner done here, may be a step towards an integration of Eastern and Western models of human growth.

Second, there is strong evidence from the interrater agreement studies that the empirically derived, thematic, stage-wide scoring criteria are useful and reliable for rating postautonomous sentence completion test protocols.

Beyond the above considerations, the new stage distinctions and classification categories for analyzing what constitutes mature insight and wisdom may be helpful for researching verbal material from other social science contexts. Thus, it is hoped that this study makes a substantive contribution to the field of positive adult development.

The conception of advanced ego development presented here, which was derived from actual sentence completion tests, is in excellent company. The rare postautonomous sentence completions—spontaneous as they were—give powerful empirical support to the notion that lifespan mental growth consists of a process of a sequential shedding of old thought habits, ego-attachments and illusions in favor of a growing embrace of life as it is. This idea is eloquently expressed by developmental theorists:

> In fact, transforming our epistemologies, liberating ourselves from that in which we were embedded, making what was subject into object so that we can "have it" rather that "be had" by it—this is the most powerful way I know to conceptualize the growth of the mind. It is a way of conceptualizing the growth of the mind that is as faithful to the self-psychology of the West as to the "wisdom literature" of the East. The roshis and lamas speak to the growth of the mind in terms of our developing ability to relate to what we were formerly attached to.

The research indicates that full ego development in the personal realm may open the portal to ego-transcendence as described in the literature on human insight potential of both transpersonal psychology and the "received" schools of ancient knowledge. It is suggested that the ego can become transparent to itself and "ripen" towards ego-transcendence at the high end of ego development.

At the dawn of a new millennium, psychology as a discipline needs to have a framework of the overall trajectory of human development consonant with contemporary integral conceptions of consciousness. Having such a model may help us to transcend the modern legacy of fragmentation within and among disciplines. We can refocus our attention on consciousness and meaning making as central to human experience and create an integral vision of the human condition that embodies science, art and religion.

### Addendum

There are many questions yet to be explored and researched in the field of mature ego development. These range from qualitative assessments of the function of ego stage in people's work, love, and play at present and over the life span. They need to address differences and similarities among individual, age cohorts, and specific groups of people and the emergent global society.

What, for instance, is the relationship between high ego development and mental and physical health, resilience, wisdom, tolerance of ambiguity, equanimity, as well as creativity and spirituality in individual cases and in specific groups of people? Are their characteristic cultural, gender, and race differences that persist beyond the Autonomous ego stage and into the transpersonal realm? What indicators are present in childhood that predict the long-term potential for mature ego development? If at all, how does mental performance and awareness change towards the end of life in people who score high at middle age?

To what degree do current social institutions and economic practices support or hinder development?

### Appendices

*   Learning style: visual, auditory, kinesthetic
*   Jungian-based: extraversion-introversion, intuition-sensing, feeling-thinking, perceiving-judging

*   Temperament: receptive, contemplative, responsive-reactive, outgoing, proactive
*   Five factor model: Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness to experience, Agreeableness, Conscientiousness

**Performance**

*   Right brain: intuitive, holistic, pattern recognition, non-verbal
*   Pattern recognition
*   Intelligence
*   Multiple intelligences: spatial/visual, body/kinesthetic, logical/mathematical, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic, musical
*   Sternberg: analytical, practical, creative

*   Physical-health: metabolism, stress tolerance, senses
*   Mental health: neuroticism, substance use, stress tolerance, psychopathology
*   Native intelligence (IQ): memory access, memory speed, perceptual sensibility, perceptual acuity, memory capacity
