#### Scoring Categories for Ego Stages C9 and C10

**Stage 5/6 (C9): Construct-aware Stage**

*   **5/6,1 (9v) Complex matrix of content (panoramic view; not linear lists)**
    Array of contrasting possibilities, reasons, responses given in order to approximate and express the complexity of one's insights, thoughts, feelings and observations (sometimes abstract, distanced or hyper-rational). [This is a stage specific manifestation of Rating Rule 2. Complexity may be signaled by the conscious use of dashes, semicolons, parentheses, underlines and quotation marks etc. to structure long "unwieldy" completions and to signal one's awareness of subtle distinctions. Rule: If all the responses above E8 in a protocol are from category 9v, it is rated at E8.]
*   **5/6,2 (9w) Exploration & evaluation of intrapsychic & interpersonal dynamics**
    Wide range of personal thought, feelings, concerns and deep insights into one's own and others' psychological functioning and interdependence; aware of the ambiguities, simultaneous benefits and costs, and double binds in human relationships; aware of the stages and process of identity-formation.
*   **5/6,3 (9x) Exploration and evaluation of habits of mind**
    Evidence of observing own thinking process and thought habits such as automatic judging, circular reasoning, infinite regress, defensive mind games; aware of existential and logical paradoxes; comments on various levels of own awareness or felt lack thereof; intellectual understanding of the nature of polar opposites as necessitating each other.
*   **5/6,4 (9y) Reference to constructed nature of reality**
    Specific reference to and questioning of underlying assumptions, multiple perceptions, frames of reference, paradigms and process of meaning making. Explicit about multiple, multi-layered and changing meanings of words and definitions. Aware of constructed nature of self and reality. [Also comments on how sentence completion test stems and "measurements" frame and limit the respondent.]

**Stage C10 (6): Unitive Stage**

*   **6,1 (10v) Wide range of thought on human relationships (self not as center)**
    with unique positive affect and gratitude for people as they are; appreciation of their shadow sides and struggles as mirrors of all humanity.
*   **6,2 (10w) Expression of high tolerance, acceptance of self and world "as is"**
    openness to life, change, process, rhythm, flux, self-in-transformation; letting go of judging need; signs of non-attachment and embracing of polar opposites.
*   **6,3 (10x) Non-trivial expression of universal connectedness**
    self as part of larger world, humankind, womanhood, history, manifestation of creative process.
*   **6,4 (10y) Fundamental thoughts and feelings about the human condition**
    including but not limited to the wonder of being, creation/destruction, joy and suffering, life and death, sexuality; nature; global concerns, conscience, consciousness.
*   **6,5 (10z) Unitive ability**
    Shifting focus effortlessly between near and far (geographically, historically, developmentally), the mundane and the sublime, the somber and the ridiculous, now and eternity, between different states of consciousness without attachment to any one view or position. [Sometimes playful light touch; illuminating metaphor: "seeing a world in a grain of sand."]

#### Three Dimensions of Ego Development

**1. Operative (behavioral) dimension**
*   **Issues addressed:** DOING (coping, needs & ends, purpose)
*   **Questions:** How do people behave? What are the needs they act upon, and what ends do they move towards? How do they do to cope and master their lives?

**2. Affective dimension**
*   **Issues addressed:** BEING (awareness, experience, affect)
*   **Questions:** How do individuals feel about their lives? How do they express affect? How are events experienced and digested? What is felt as stress or as problematic? What are the favored defenses? What is the range of awareness and what remains hidden from consciousness?

**3. Cognitive dimension**
*   **Issues addressed:** THINKING (conceptions, knowledge, interpretation)
*   **Questions:** How do individuals structure experience, how do they explain things, make sense of their experience? What is the logic behind their perspectives on the self and the world? How do they know what they know and what questions do they ask, what do they focus on, and what remains hidden?

#### The Evolving Perspective on the Self: A Brief Overview

**The First Tier or the Preconventional Stages**

Stage 1 (E1) through Delta (E3) represent the child's normal development from birth to about age 12, as well as stages of arrested development in adults. Stage Delta (E3) and Delta/3 include the acquisition of primary and beginning concrete operations, respectively.

*   **Stage 1 (E1)**, also called Presocial or Symbiotic, describes adults who are unaware of themselves as separate individuals. They may be nonverbal, driven by basic needs and fundamentally helpless with others.
*   **Stage 2 (E2)**, or Impulsive, describes individuals who show signs of beginning use of language simultaneously with the ego as reflected in such statements as "I want" and "mine." They are concerned with safety and the gratification of basic needs. It is the first stage measured by the sentence completion test and shows the first-person perspective. The inability to understand fully the verbal stimuli of the test is a sign of this stage.
*   **Stage Delta (E3)**, or the Self-protective stage, describes people who see the world only from the perspective of their own wants and needs. To get what they want, they need to control others and safeguard their interests. It is the first stage of purposeful social interaction. "Opportunists" see the world from an "I win/you lose" perspective. Power is used where useful: "Might makes right."
*   **Stage Delta/3**, or the Rule-oriented stage, describes individuals who are discovering the second-person perspective. They have a vacillating point of view. Sometimes the question is: "How do I look to others?" and at other times: "How do they look to me?" Comparing is restricted to concrete and external aspects of self and others. Interest in being part of groups (greater power) and following rules.

**The Second Tier Or The Conventional Stages 3 (E4), 3/4 (E5) And 4 (E6)**

Stages 3, 3/4, and 4 (E4, E5, and E6) cover the ego stages of most people after age 12. Stage 3 (E4) represents concrete operations, and stage 3/4 (E5) abstract operations. Stage 4 (E6) is considered the adult stage per se in modern Western cultures and comprises functioning at the formal operational level. For stages 3/4 (E5) and 4 (E6), reality is defined as having closed boundaries, permanent objects, linear causality, and independent variables. Eighty percent of the average adult American population scores at these three conventional stages. Based on data since 1980, stage 3 (E4) has become less common with the women's movement having taken hold.

*   **The Conformist Stage E4**, describes persons with an early adolescent frame of mind. They identify themselves mostly as members of familiar groups. The boundaries between self and others are confused. But unlike people at the Self-protective stage, there is real concern for the well-being of others. One takes responsibility for others. Dependency needs are high. Fear of rejection leads Conformists to be overly nice and to repress negative feelings. There is unquestioned acceptance of the Family and in-groups (such as peer groups, family values, club, church) and loyalty is important. The unfamiliar (out-groups) is rejected and easily maligned. External social status and material goods are important as indices of one's value. Simple shoulds and oughts are adhered to, but now include more socially desirable behavior. Experience is concrete, practical, and reactions immediate without much reflection.
*   **The Self-Conscious Stage E5 (3/4)**, characterizes people who are able to step back and look at themselves as objects for the first time. They can take the third-person perspective and begin to observe themselves. Generally, however, the focus is directed outside the self, on others. Conventional morality and self-righteousness strong. Stage 3/4 (E5) people often assert and express their newly discovered personhood albeit in traditional terms. They try to differentiate themselves from the previous familiar context. Being able to stand outside oneself permits beginning self-reflection. 3/4 (E5) persons begin to recognize that others have different selves and thoughts, and that they can look at you as an object as well.

**Self-definition across Conventional Stages:**
*   **Stage E4 (3): Conformist Stage:** Concrete operations: several external features: vital statistics, rudimentary internal states, negative suppressed.
*   **Stage E5 (3/4): Self-conscious Stage:** Abstract operations: clusters of external attributes, simple traits, beginning introspection; beginning sense of separate self-identity and unique personhood.
*   **Stage E6 (4): Conscientious Stage:** Formal operations: self as system of roles and clusters of traits: prototype personality; individual self-agency; aware of recent past and future, and causality.

A third person perspective also allows for abstract operations and mental manipulation of abstract objects. Stage 3/4 individuals (E5) can generate permutations or many solutions to a problem, known as "the yes-but" syndrome, but they cannot yet prioritize among variables. Beginning awareness of time as a linear progression and, therefore, beginning to see self as having enduring traits, i.e., an identifiable character or identity. Conventional shoulds and oughts and cultural norms are more successfully internalized. Belief in Authority and the Truth are strong. Self-aware persons are concerned with duty, responsibility, right action. Technicians and bureaucrats often inhabit this frame of mind with excellent results for society.

*   **Stage 4 (E6)**, or the Conscientious stage, adds the concept of linear time (sequentiality) as a conscious object to the third-person perspective and expands the meaningful social context to others within the same society with similar ideologies and aspirations. At stage 4 (E6) one starts to explore the nature of oneself in terms of traits through more ongoing introspection. Aware of self as having definite traits that distinguish one uniquely from others. One learns to understand oneself backwards (responsibility, guilt) and forwards in time (plans, dreams) within the roles (prototypes) and functions provided by one's culture. Stage 4 (E6) individuals are interested in reasons, causes, goals, costs, consequences, and the effective use of time. Aware of others as individuals with unique personalities, and thus contract agreements and mutually beneficial arrangements. At stage 4 (E6), one may deeply believe in social progress and human perfectibility. This often translates into a genuine effort at making a difference in the world through action, and mobilizing others around one's causes and beliefs. Clear sense of identity and being in charge of oneself. Life seen as a task to be mastered. Formal operations and abstract rationality are at their peak. There may be a conviction that the proper analytical, scientific methods will eventually lead to the discovery of how things really are, that is, to the discovery of the laws of everything and therefore the solutions to all problems. Formal Operational 4 (E6) persons represents "the Adult" as defined by Western industrialized society and as supported by modern institutions from education to jurisprudence. Because of the expanded view, the Conscientious person plans, prioritizes, and optimizes procedures to achieve goals. One needs society to function smoothly, in order to achieve one's desires. Great need to improve.

**The Individualist Stage 4/5 (E7)**

*   **Realm:** self as unique entity
*   **Time frame:** here and now
*   **Cognition:** systematic operations, systems theory concepts perceived
*   **Preoccupations:** celebrate one's unique difference from others
*   **Positive equilibration:** vivid individualism
*   **Truth:** can never be found. Everything is relative. concentrate and relish experience in the present.
*   **Perspective:** 4th person perspective -> Relativism: discovery of cultural conditioning and the possibility of self-deception.

Individualists (E7) realize that reality is not out there, separate from the viewer as previously felt, but connected to the person who experiences it. Increasing ability to see how things are related and influence each other in non-linear ways. Others admired for their individuality and creative solutions to living. Discovery of cultural and personal "assumptions" and own tendency towards defensive moves.

#### Expanding Relational Space and Time in Ego Development

**Relational space (kinship with)**
*   Family of sentient beings
*   Global village, humankind
*   Diverse cultures of choice; like-'spirited' people
*   Own society, culture: others with similar goals, values: like-"minded" people
*   Own kin, family, clan, tribe, club, neighborhood, professional group
*   Providing ones; Mother

**Timeframe**
*   Time-space continuum
*   One's whole life in historical time
*   Self in linear time: defined by recent past, present and future
*   Present
*   Immediate needs; unaware of time
*   Womb

#### Comparative Table of Ego Stages E8, C9, and C10

**Stage Autonomous E8 (5)**
*   **Example from SCT:** I am - a well-balanced professional human being, definitely on the path of self-actualization and self-fulfillment
*   **Focus:** Self-development, self-actualization: creating a meaningful, coherent, objective self-identity
*   **Self-definition:** Autonomous, multiple roles; self-generated core-identity; aware of many defenses and expression of inner conflict. Sense of self-esteem, empowerment
*   **Dominant Center of Awareness:** Rational mind and intellect; thought as mediated through language (symbolic codification, representation)
*   **Range of Awareness:** Rational analysis with awareness of the mechanics of thought, symbolic codification, construction of meaning, contemplation of limitations of present way of knowing -> existential paradox.
*   **Method of Knowing:** Reasoning, rational analysis aided by some intuition; one assesses, evaluate, judges, compare, measures, contrasts, and predicts
*   **Goal:** To be the most one can be

**Stage Construct-aware C9 (5/6)**
*   **Example from SCT:** I am - sensitive, honest, striving to always love others, reflective, sometimes to the point of being unable to get out of endless loops, striving to take responsibility for myself (...)
*   **Focus:** Exploring the habits and processes of mind and the way one makes sense of experience through cognition and language
*   **Self-definition:** Complex matrix of self-identifications, at the same time questioning their adequacy. Description of self in stages (approximations) and critique of conventional labeling
*   **Dominant Center of Awareness:** Rational mind plus intimations of transcendent awareness, and intuitive knowledge during peak moments
*   **Range of Awareness:** Aware of the limits of symbolic codification and rational thought; aware of ego and conventional reality as constructs. Keenly aware of difference between map and territory
*   **Method of Knowing:** To be aware of body/mind as system, aware of context dependency and personal interpretation of internal and external events
*   **Goal:** To be aware

**Stage Unitive C10 (6)**
*   **Example from SCT:** I am - alive, trundling along, making sense as best as I can, diversifying & expanding while consolidating & contracting
*   **Focus:** Non-evaluating, integrative witnessing of ongoing process of experience
*   **Self-definition:** Description of self as in constant flux and transformation; transcendent awareness; I am no(-)body, no(-)thing
*   **Dominant Center of Awareness:** Metarational, postrepresentational, immediate, integrative awareness and direct experience of what it is
*   **Range of Awareness:** Aware of perceptional flux and changing levels of awareness; life as is; aware of "illusion" of permanent, individual self and object world. Cognizant of witness-Self
*   **Method of Knowing:** Contemplative, witnessing of continuous flux; subjective experience of non-symbolic mode of direct knowing and apperception; intellect and intuition are used but not overvalued.
*   **Goal:** To be

# Appendix: Training and Scoring Agreement Form

**Research Assistant Commitment**

The prospective research assistant agrees to serve in this capacity for the lead researcher, provided they qualify following the completion of a training workshop focused on scoring the sentence completion instrument. Qualification is contingent upon the level of proficiency demonstrated in scoring the instrument using the standard manual alongside the newly developed high-end categories.

The training phase mandates attendance at three training sessions, each lasting four hours. During this period, the assistant must independently study the scoring manual and relevant theoretical articles distributed during the initial meeting (with the cost of materials, including the manual, noted as $x). Furthermore, the assistant is required to complete assigned practice homework between training sessions to maximize their scoring proficiency.

The research phase requires the independent rating of approximately 36-item sentence completion protocols for an interrater reliability study. The assistant must maintain all required scoring records and execute the research tasks to the highest standard within the designated schedule. The assistant must also express a willingness to extend their work beyond the deadline if unforeseen, mutually negotiated circumstances arise. Upon selection post-training, the assistant will receive compensation at a rate of $(x) per protocol, payable upon the completion of the assigned tasks.

**Lead Researcher Commitment**

The lead researcher commits to conducting an intensive workshop on scoring the sentence completion instrument and supplying handouts containing scoring clues and comprehensive instructions. The researcher will ensure a safe and comfortable environment for the training sessions and remain available upon request to help participants master the scoring skills and understand the underlying theory outside of scheduled workshop hours. 

The researcher agrees to adhere strictly to the established schedule, barring any mutually agreed-upon modifications. Following the workshop, the researcher will select three research assistants from those who demonstrate the highest proficiency in scoring the instrument. The researcher will provide compensation of $(x) per protocol once the independent scoring task is finished, and will acknowledge the contributions of the assistants in the final research document, should they desire such recognition.

# Scoring Hints and Guidelines

Before finalizing a stage assignment, raters are advised to double-check any unusual ratings or outliers—specifically, those scores that appear significantly higher or lower than the majority of responses within a given protocol. Raters should consult the scoring clues for the assigned stage to verify whether the salient indicators are indeed present. 

Additionally, raters must evaluate whether the Total Weighted Score (TWS) falls within the expected normal range. If it does not, the underlying reasons must be investigated. For instance, a small number of very low scores can artificially depress the TWS, even if the protocol is generally high-scoring overall. Conversely, a highly consistent protocol featuring a narrow distribution of scores, where a large proportion of responses (50% or more) align with the target stage, will typically yield a higher TWS than the standard guidelines might suggest. Raters are instructed to rely on the overall weight of the evidence: the more frequently an individual's responses reflect a specific stage, the more confidently it can be assumed that this stage represents their true developmental center of gravity.

Raters must assign a final Total Protocol Rating (TPR) by synthesizing data from the ogive, the Total Weighted Score (TWS), and a holistic assessment of the entire protocol. To assign a rating at the highest developmental tiers, specific additional conditions must be satisfied. For the highest stage rating (e.g., Stage 6 or C10), protocols reaching this unitive level typically feature an abundance of responses at the preceding stage (E8) and often, though not necessarily always, contain numerous responses at the intermediate high stage (5/6 or C9). For the intermediate high stage rating (e.g., Stage 5/6 or C9), the protocol must meet the baseline requirements for the E8 stage and must also include at least two categories distinct from the specific "5/6, 1 (9v)" category, or alternatively, include some responses that score at the highest stage (6 or C10). If a protocol's high-level responses are exclusively confined to the "5/6, 1 (9v)" category, the overall protocol should be capped and rated at the E8 stage.

# Instructions for Rating Advanced Protocols

Raters are provided with an initial batch of 15 complete protocols (labeled B1-1 through B1-15), representing developmental stages ranging from the autonomous level (E6) through the highest unitive levels (C10), presented in a randomized order. To facilitate efficient use of the scoring manuals and to guarantee unbiased, out-of-context evaluations, the responses have been sorted by their corresponding item numbers from the master list. For example, for the sentence stem "When a child will not join in group activities—", raters must evaluate all 15 participant completions to this specific item together before moving on to the next item.

Each response must be matched as accurately as possible against the examples provided in the corresponding scoring manual. If a response is compound in nature, raters must apply the specific scoring rules governing compound responses and explicitly document their reasoning (e.g., noting the use of specific rules like R2 or R3). In instances where a response is omitted (which is rare in this dataset) or is too incoherent to interpret, raters are instructed to apply the designated rule for unscoreable or missing data (e.g., Rule R5). Raters are urged to maintain strict objectivity. Standard items typically require less than a minute to evaluate, while even the most complex completions should take no more than a few minutes. Crucially, scores at the highest developmental tiers (C9 or C10) should only be awarded when the response cannot be matched to any lower-stage examples, when the elements cannot be accounted for by standard scoring rules (such as Rule M), and when there is unambiguous evidence that the completion aligns with the theoretical criteria of the highest-stage categories.

The primary score must be recorded in the first designated column. If a rater experiences uncertainty and wishes to note alternative potential scores in a secondary column, they are still strictly required to select one of those alternatives as their definitive "best estimate" final score. In situations where no adequate manual matches exist, standard scoring rules fail to resolve the ambiguity, or a specific item manual is unavailable, raters must deduce a stage score based on the underlying developmental theory and append the category label "undecided" (denoted as "u").

Once all individual item scores for the entire batch have been determined, they must be transcribed onto the individual protocol scoring sheets provided for each participant. Raters must meticulously double-check their data entry to ensure that the scores are recorded in the exact sequence in which the responses originally appeared on the participant's protocol. This original sequence is indicated in a specific reference column. Upon completion of this step, every individual protocol must contain exactly 36 distinct scores corresponding to the 36 items.

Raters must then compute several quantitative metrics on the scoring form. This includes calculating the simple frequency distribution of scores, determining the Total Weighted Score (TWS) by multiplying the simple distribution frequencies by their respective stage weights and summing the results, calculating the cumulative distribution, and deriving the automatic ogive score in the designated cells. Finally, raters must read through the entire assembled protocol from start to finish. This holistic review is necessary to confirm that the overall text genuinely embodies the qualitative characteristics of the final assigned stage. Raters are reminded that higher developmental stages inherently demand a greater diversity of themes, elevated structural complexity, and deeper psychological insight across the protocol as a whole.

# Ego Development: Nine Levels of Increasing Embrace

## Introduction

Ego development theory serves as a model illustrating the various levels of human development, specifically focusing on the evolution of meaning-making in individuals. It outlines the most prevalent stages observed in contemporary Western society, encompassing preconventional, conventional, postconventional, and early post-postconventional ways of interpreting the world. While often depicted schematically to demonstrate fine-tuned differentiations and its place within broader models of consciousness, the theory should not be interpreted as a simple hierarchy, a strictly linear progression, or a rigid staircase. It is merely one method of representing and displaying developmental models.

The stages of ego development parallel other action logic frameworks, and they can be compared as follows:

**Table 1: Comparison of Ego Development Stages and Action Logics**

| Ego Development Stages | Action Logics | % of US Population |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Post-postconventional, Transpersonal, Ego-transcendent** | | <1% |
| 6 Unitive | Ironist | |
| 5/6 Construct-aware | Alchemist | <2% |
| **Postconventional** | | |
| 5 Autonomous | Strategist | |
| 4/5 Individualist | Individualist | ~10% |
| **Conventional** | | |
| 4 Conscientious | Achiever | ~30% |
| 3/4 Self-conscious | Expert/technician | ~30% |
| 3 Conformist | Diplomat | ~12% |
| **Preconventional** | | |
| 2/3 Self-defensive | Opportunist | ~10% |
| 2 Impulsive | Impulsive | ~5% |

## Ego Development Theory and Stage Descriptions

Human development is frequently described as evolving in a spiral manner rather than in lockstep, with movement possible in multiple directions:
*   **Up:** Transformation, vertical development.
*   **Horizontal:** Expansion or transfer at the same stage (acquiring new skills or more knowledge), often referred to as "translating."
*   **Down:** Temporary or permanent regression.

The majority of growth in adults is horizontal expansion. Individuals learn new skills, methods, facts, and ways of organizing knowledge, but their fundamental stage or mental model of the world remains unchanged. In contrast, ego development theory details the sequence of how these mental models themselves evolve over time. Each new level incorporates the previous ones as subsets. A new level represents both a novel, coherent logic and a component of a larger, more complex meaning system.

Furthermore, the theory describes a psychological system comprising three interrelated components:
1.  **The Operative Component (Doing):** Examines what adults perceive as the purpose of life, the needs they act upon, and the ends they strive toward. It addresses behavioral dimensions such as coping, needs, ends, and purpose. It asks: How do people interact? What needs drive their actions? How do they cope and master their lives? What role do others play?
2.  **The Affective Component (Being):** Deals with emotions and the experience of existing in the world. It encompasses awareness, experience, and affect. It asks: How do individuals feel about things? How do they manage affect? What is their range of awareness and selective perception? How are events experienced and processed? What are their preferred defenses?
3.  **The Cognitive Component (Thinking):** Addresses how a person thinks about themselves and the world. It involves conceptions, knowledge, and interpretation. It asks: How does a person think? How do they structure experience, explain phenomena, and make sense of their lives? What logic underpins their perspectives on the self and the world?

It is crucial to recognize that each stage emerges from a synthesis of doing, being, and thinking, even though the term "logic" might imply an emphasis on cognition. Metaphorically, the theory provides an account of how individuals navigate human existence using common sense, increasingly complex maps, algorithms, and intuition.

**Table 2: Three Main Dimensions of Each Stage**

| Dimension | Characteristics | Associated Stages |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Knowledge** | Notice more pieces of the puzzle; Discover patterns, rules, and laws; Predict, measure, and explain; See further ahead and back in time; Know more and do more. | Self-protective (2/3), Conformist (3), Self-conscious (3/4), Conscientious (4) |
| **Wisdom** | Understand more deeply; Recognize assumptions; See the whole dynamic system; See within, around, and beneath; Strip away illusions; Empty mind. | Individualist (4/5), Autonomous (5), Construct-aware (5/6), Unitive (6) |

## Alternating Stages of Differentiation and Integration

Human development can be viewed as a progression of different ways of making sense of reality. These stages alternate between emphasizing differentiation over integration and favoring integration over differentiation. This pattern is observable both overall and from stage to stage.

Individuals individuate by continuously renegotiating the balance between differentiation and integration—a dual trend towards autonomy and homonomy (the desire to be in harmony with a unit extending beyond the individual self, such as a family, nation, ideology, or ordered universe). This dual need is also known as separation and participation, mastery and relatedness, independence and connection, or care and justice.

Stages of differentiation (e.g., Self-protective, Self-conscious, Individualist, Construct-aware) occur when individuals emerge from a previously shared worldview. They tend to focus on their differences from the prior stage, asserting their newly won independence while often expressing distress over the loss of connection.

Stages of integration (e.g., Conformist, Conscientious, Autonomous, Unitive) represent a more balanced state where individuals are in harmony with a new community that aligns with their current cognitive, emotional, and transactional needs. The sequence progresses from identifying with those who are externally alike (Conformist), to the like-minded (Conscientious), to the like-principled (Autonomous), and finally to the like-spirited (Unitive), where spirit is perceived as radiant in all people.

An overall shift from differentiation to integration also characterizes the transition from conventional to postconventional stages. The initial tiers of development represent a movement away from symbiotic embeddedness, an overall trend of increasing differentiation culminating in the abstract, analytic functioning of the scientific ego stage (Conscientious). The postconventional stages show an overall trend of assimilation and integration towards a conscious sense of belongingness and unity.

In modern Western contexts, fully functional adults typically view reality as preexistent and external, composed of permanent, well-defined objects that can be analyzed and controlled. This perspective relies on a maximal separation between subject and object, thinker and thought, epitomizing the traditional scientific mindset concerned with control, measurement, and prediction. Most adults operate under the assumption that subject and object are distinct and that analyzing parts reveals the whole, often lacking insight into the arbitrariness of defining objects or the idea that naming and measuring pulls the named entity out of an unnameable reality. The acquisition of this rational mindset is often viewed as the goal of socialization.

**Table 3: Comparison of Percentage Stage Distribution in Three Different Samples**

| Stage | 535 Managers & Consultants (UK) | 497 Managers & Supervisors (USA) | 4510 Mixed Adult Population (USA) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Impulsive (2) | <1% | 1.5% | 0.5% |
| Self-protective (2/3) | 0.4% | 1.7% | 8.2% |
| Conformist (3) | 11.3% | 21.1% | 47.8% |
| Self-conscious (3/4) | 36.5% | 33.5% | 34.8% |
| Conscientious (4) | 29.7% | 23.4% | 5.0% |
| Individualist (4/5) | 11.3% | 13.5% | 1.4% |
| Autonomous (5) | 4.9% | 5.6% | 4.0% |
| Construct-aware & Unitive (5/6 & 6) | 0.9% | <1% | <1% |

## Mapping the Expansion of Self-Awareness

Self-awareness expands in a regular pattern from ignorance to mature wisdom through increasingly complex perspectives on the self and others.

*   **Infant State (Stage 1):** Symbiotic. Con-fused, autistic, preverbal.
*   **Impulsive (Stage 2):** 1st person perspective. Rudimentary, physical self-labeling, basic dichotomies. Self-description in terms of own wishes; dichotomous thinking, self-serving.
*   **Opportunist/Self-protective (Stage 2/3):** 1st person perspective.
*   **Diplomat/Conformist (Stage 3):** 2nd person perspective. Single visible external feature; beginning comparisons. Several external features; vital stats, membership self; rudimentary internal states; concrete operations.
*   **Self-conscious (Stage 3/4):** 3rd person perspective. Clusters of external attributes, simple traits, beginning introspection; beginning separate self-identity; abstract operations.
*   **Conscientious (Stage 4):** Expanded 3rd person perspective. Self as a system of roles and clusters of traits; independent self, prototype personality; recent past and present, linear causality (Formal operations).
*   **Individualist (Stage 4/5):** 4th person perspective.
*   **Autonomous (Stage 5):** 4th person perspective expanded.
*   **Magician/Construct-aware (Stage 5/6):** 5th to nth person perspective.
*   **Ironist/Unitive (Stage 6):** Global/unitive perspective.

## The Preconventional Stages

The first tier of action logics, from Impulsive to Self-protective, represents normal maturation from birth to approximately age 12, as well as stages of "arrested" development in adults. These preconventional stages are rarely encountered in professional adults, as they are typically traversed in childhood. They reside as subconscious memories and serve as fallback positions during extreme duress.

### The Presocial or Symbiotic Stage (Stage 1)
*   **Perspective on the self:** None
*   **Cognitive:** Nominal actions
*   **Self:** Con-fused, confounded, undifferentiated self
*   **Other:** Not distinct, source of survival

In this initial stage, infants construct a stable world of objects, separating themselves out as objects in the process. The constitution of the self and outer reality are correlative and depend on language use. This stage can be subdivided into a presocial (autistic) phase and a symbiotic phase. Successful resolution of the challenges in these early periods is crucial for later adult adjustment, encompassing healthy ego, intellectual, psychosexual, social, and moral development. Strands of development that later become distinct are highly undifferentiated here. Adults at this level of undifferentiation are likely pre- or nonverbal and usually require institutionalization or complete dependence on caregivers.

### The Impulsive Stage (Stage 2)
*   **Cognitive:** Primary actions
*   **Self:** Rudimentary, physical self-labeling ("me," "mine")
*   **Differentiation:** Crude dichotomies
*   **Perspective:** First person perspective
*   **Other:** Fulfillment of needs on demand

This stage characterizes individuals showing early language use alongside an emerging ego, reflected in statements like "I want" and "mine." They are focused on safety and the gratification of basic needs. Children at this stage are governed by impulses, asserting their growing sense of self through the word "no" and concepts of ownership. Magical thinking prevails, along with a sense of unlimited power that is only curbed by punishment. Punishments are not linked to behavior but are perceived as random, retaliatory, and imminent in things. Others are viewed primarily as sources of need gratification—good people give, mean ones do not. There is cognitive simplicity and verbal undifferentiation. The impulsive individual lacks an adequate conception of adult life complexities and readily appears confused, anxious, and overwhelmed.

**Language Clues:** Simple statements, repetitions of the prompt stem, responding to past tense stimuli with present tense, and an inability to understand or respond to longer, complex prompts (e.g., "crime and delinquency could be halted if..."). Things are categorized simply as good or bad, clean or dirty, nice or mean. Affects are expressed in bodily terms rather than as feelings. Repetitiveness is a conspicuous sign, with many responses containing identical or nearly identical simple completions to various prompts.

### The Self-Protective or Opportunist Stage (Stage 2/3)
*   **Cognitive:** Preoperational actions
*   **Self:** Single concrete features, minimal action-based self-description in terms of desire
*   **Other:** Competing for goods, space, dominance, power
*   **Differentiation:** Beginning sense of others as having their own wants and will to get

This stage is reminiscent of toddler behavior, where everything becomes a test of wills and tantrums result from thwarted desires or conflicting needs. Adults at this stage exhibit similar behaviors. They are generally wary of others' intentions, assuming the worst, and view life as a zero-sum game or a war of wills. Their "I win, you lose" mentality causes friction and hurt feelings, and they are often perceived by others as manipulative and exploitative.

Individuals at this stage view the world solely from the perspective of their own needs and wants, lacking psychological insight into themselves or others. They believe the only way to achieve their desires is by controlling others and protecting themselves. They are also opportunistic, possessing a self-serving attitude, a keen eye for opportunities, and the energy to pursue what they want without reflection or delay.

Self-protective adults carefully maintain their fragile selves, which are synonymous with their will, ideas, and wishes. The self may be perceived as having two sides: an inner real self and an outer "false face" used for protection. They believe that if others knew their true desires, those others would have power over them. Consequently, they need to resist the will of others, test limits, and assert control. This stage marks the emerging awareness of others as separate people or "objects," requiring the rudiments of conscious social interaction ("If I understand what others are after, I can better manipulate them to get what I want").

Individuals at this level often inadvertently "get into trouble" by crossing boundaries. They try to anticipate trouble and avoid consequences. When they lose a test of will or overstep, they attribute the cause to external factors, becoming frustrated and displaying free-flowing anger and hostility. Blame is always placed on others, never on oneself. Their anger is projected outward, leading them to experience others as constantly angry and the world as a hostile, dangerous place. Rules are recognized but followed only for immediate advantage or to avoid punishment. Cleverness and seizing opportunities are deemed necessary for survival. Self-respect is tied to the amount of control achieved in opposition to others. They often feel isolated, like the "odd person out," but lack the skills to relate differently, creating a self-perpetuating dynamic of low trust and hyper-vigilance.

Morality is expedient; actions are only considered bad if one is caught and punished. When caught, they exhibit shamelessness and little remorse. They do not feel responsible for failures or trouble because they do not yet grasp linear causality (the connection between action and consequences). Blaming others serves as a protective mechanism. There is a heavy reliance on luck and magic for self-protection. (e.g., individuals in dangerous jobs who carry lucky objects and tend to be loners).

Self-protective people are not well-embedded in social networks and struggle to understand subtle human interactions not based on power. Relationships are volatile, and friendships easily rupture. Feelings are externalized and projected. There is little expression of or reflection on their own emotions, due to both a lack of insight and a need for self-protection. Showing weakness is considered dangerous ("The more others know about me, the more they can take advantage of me"). Consequently, they tend to express general hostility and a readiness for fight or flight.

*   **Cognitive style:** Thinking is concrete and dichotomous, based on global, undifferentiated judgments and simple ideas (black or white).
*   **Preoccupations:** Themes of domination, gaining control and advantage, or deceiving others. Conversely, they constantly fear being dominated, controlled, or deceived.
*   **Internal dimension:** Psychology is defined by current actions or desires rather than psychological states or long-term goals.
*   **Decision-making style:** "This is your problem, not mine."
*   **Organizational type:** Power and coercion.
*   **Defenses:** Use of blaming and distortion to minimize anxiety and maximize self-esteem. Coping styles are immature, commonly involving fantasy, acting out, and projection. All causality and significant psychological action are attributed to a constantly threatening external world.
*   **Depression:** Expressed as "There must be something wrong if I can't get what I want." They hate feeling controlled and constrained. Sadness is most likely expressed through aggression toward self or others.
*   **Sample character:** Individuals who are self-protective, bull-headed, possess tunnel vision, constantly blow up, and blame others for their circumstances.


---


# Developmental Stages of Ego

## The Rule-Oriented Stage
This stage represents a cognitive shift from self-protective thinking to conscientious dependence, serving as a logical bridge for mental growth. It is considered statistically rare. At this stage, individuals alternate between different viewpoints, wondering how they appear to others and how others appear to them. They begin to recognize external differences in features or behaviors. This stage often describes adults who are discovering the second-person perspective, making simple external comparisons, and using concrete mental operations.

Individuals become highly focused on social conventions and rules due to an emerging need for acceptance and likability. The desire to look "right" and acceptable places a strong emphasis on external attractiveness and appearance. People at this stage exhibit more trust, as they realize that acceptance and protection can be achieved by following rules. The world is perceived as less hostile compared to the self-protective stage. There is a desire for respect, which translates to being "seen" by others. Respect is gained by adhering to group norms rather than just through power. Individuals also show respect by paying attention to others, acknowledging mutual presence and listening. The discovery of the second-person perspective allows for simple feedback, enabling individuals to ask others for their opinions without fear of negative consequences, marking a significant milestone in social interaction.

At this stage, superficial similarities with others are discovered and provide comfort, alleviating feelings of isolation. This new feeling is enjoyed in loose associations, such as clubs, where similar action interests are shared.

**Language Clues:** Individuals make simple factual statements, referring to concrete behavior and single features of appearance. They passively respond to stimuli, attempting to avoid trouble but often uncertain how to do so. They may feel they have done something wrong without being able to articulate how or why. Overall, their expressions often lack emotional depth.

## The Conventional Stages
The Conformist, Self-Conscious, and Conscientious stages cover the developmental stages of most people after about the age of 12. Roughly 80% of adults populate these three stages, with most moving from the Self-Conscious to the Conscientious stage. In cognitive terms, the Conformist stage represents functioning at the concrete operational level, the Self-Conscious stage uses abstract operations, and the Conscientious stage relies on formal operations for meaning-making. At the Conscientious stage, individuals have a linear view of reality: objects have closed boundaries, causality is linear, and variables are treated as independent. The Conscientious stage is widely considered the adult stage in much of Western culture, supported and rewarded by society and institutions. It is a necessary precondition for democracy, as it requires a citizenry capable of rational deliberation and choice based on pertinent criteria to safeguard the whole while allowing changes in laws.

### The Conformist Stage
This stage describes individuals with an early adolescent mindset. Their self-identity is defined by their relationship to a group, leading to blurred boundaries between oneself and the group (e.g., family, team, or nation). Being part of a larger entity provides protection and shared power, with loyalty and obedience as the price for inclusion. Conformist adults thrive on dependency and may feel responsible in situations where they are not. Self-worth is tied to the group's status. There is total acceptance of the in-group and blind rejection of deviance and out-groups, creating a "them against us" dynamic. Ambiguity and ambivalence are not recognized, as they threaten the Conformist's existence.

Language is impersonal, often overly positive, and full of platitudes due to the need for acceptance. Conformists avoid rocking the boat, being singled out, or taking initiatives outside the group's benefit. Acquiring material assets and status symbols is important for indicating success and providing satisfaction. To be liked, one must have a pleasing social personality, emphasizing neatness, outward appearance, and cleanliness. The self is defined by the expectations of those to whom one belongs. Conformists accept norms without questioning, dividing their cognitive world into simple categories based on external distinctions.

Conformists are bound to those with similar tastes, beliefs, and expectations, feeling threatened by differing demands, perspectives, diversity, and complexity. In-group values are introjected as strong "shoulds," while differing values are denigrated. Negative feelings are split off and projected outward, experienced as "they hate me." Anger and disagreeable feelings are suppressed, rarely reaching awareness. Blind conformism, fundamentalism, and prejudice can express this mindset. Aggression against the self and negative affect are often countered by demonstrations of overly positive feelings and enthusiasm.

In summary, the Conformist lacks a separate adult identity, defined instead by others. Self-other boundaries are blurry, resulting in relationships with a co-dependent quality.

**Impulse Control and Character Development:** Rules are partially internalized but obeyed without question. Shame is a common response to undesired consequences. Life is governed by rules of what can and cannot be done. Differing views are morally condemned. Sexual and aggressive feelings are denied or suppressed out of fear of rejection and abandonment.

**Interpersonal Style:** Conformists conform to the rules and norms of their desired group. In workplaces, they smooth discontent and ensure a pleasant atmosphere, valuing being nice and helpful. Relationships are viewed in terms of expected behaviors rather than deep feelings and motives.

**Cognitive Style:** Conformists focus on concrete, visible aspects of experience, using superlatives and conventional clichés taken seriously. References to inner feelings are stereotyped, predictable, and aligned with cultural expectations.

**Conscious Preoccupation:** Great value is placed on appearance, status symbols, material possessions, reputation, and prestige. They are concerned with social acceptance, adjusting to group norms, and caring deeply about others' opinions. A sense of "shoulds" and "oughts" and the shame of unfulfilled expectations can be crushing.

**Internal Dimension:** Dependent on others' acceptance, Conformists worry about what others think and feel. They express simple "acceptable" feeling states (e.g., sad, happy, nervous, upset) without differentiating subtler graduations.

**Problem Solving:** Emerging problems are generally denied, relabeled, or whitewashed.

**Organizational Type:** Conformists seek acceptance and protection by a larger entity, drawn to organizations with clearly defined identities and hierarchical structures.

**Defenses:** Main coping mechanisms are projection (imagining others think/feel what they do) and introjection (swallowing others' definitions, norms, values, and opinions without questioning).

**Counseling Style:** Conformists like to give advice, telling others what to do, and tend to compare and evaluate others according to their own preferences.

**Character of Depression:** The most threatening experience is disapproval, desertion by significant others, rejection by the group, or disloyalty. The chief anxiety is the fear of being "not-me," a loss of self as accepted by others.

**Language Signs:** Short, stereotypical phrases, love of clichés, exaggerated positive affect, concrete descriptions, and factual information.

### The Self-Conscious Stage
This stage characterizes individuals able to step back and view themselves objectively from a distance, crossing a conceptual watershed by taking a third-person perspective. Individuals become capable of introspection and self-understanding, needing to differentiate themselves from their immediate family context and assert their newly discovered personhood. The third-person perspective allows operations with abstract objects and concepts. Individuals excel at seeing alternatives, offering numerous solutions and permutations for problems. They become aware of general "traits" in others, leading to an interest in sharing more of their inner nature and describing differentiated traits, norms, and conventional virtues. The focus shifts from likeness to individual differences, asserting personal needs and wants previously suppressed for acceptance.

However, they still desire acceptance by others because they tend to want to be better than others and stand out. The group is needed, but they stand at its periphery. Individuals often feel they have "figured it all out," knowing all the answers and what to believe. They feel righteous, often putting others in the wrong, with high moral standards and a strong sense of duty and responsibility. Compulsive and perfectionist tendencies are common, with a strong super-ego. Others are evaluated according to their own capabilities and standards, with severe criticism of differing thoughts as a form of intellectual aggression. A sense of superiority is evident. This stage is highly resistant and stable; individuals discredit counter-evidence or belittle others to maintain their beliefs.

In interpersonal situations, the "yes, but" syndrome and one-upmanship are common. On the positive side, they are adept at finding new solutions, better ideas, and more perfect procedures, seeing multiple possibilities and alternative solutions. However, they cannot yet prioritize among options or synthesize possibilities, not knowing when good is good enough.

Aggression, suppressed at the Conformist stage, reemerges, often as a hostile sense of humor or ridiculing others. While capable of looking inside, their defense is to be ultra-rational. They relish being movers and initiators, enjoying oppositional battles and feeling entitled to impose their views, appearing argumentative and opinionated.

**Impulse Control:** Societal shoulds and oughts are well internalized. Super-egos are strong and not available for inspection. Feelings of shame and guilt for transgressions are expressed simply or rationalized away.

**Cognitive Style:** Capable of abstract thought and operations, including multiple views, permutations, and careful comparison between pairs of items.

**Internal Dimension:** Psychology is understood in terms of stable moods and traits. They generate numerous solutions without prioritizing them, often hanging up the decision process.

**Organizational Type:** Engineering types, technocrats, bureaucrats, and professionals thrive here. Being in charge is a chief trait, with a clear-minded, pragmatic leadership style. They are necessary for running routine affairs and prosper in highly technological environments.

**Chief Defenses:** Intellectualization, rationalization, and explaining away what doesn't fit expectations. They often blame structures, tools, or others' incompetence for failures.

**Counseling Style:** As managers and consultants, they ask many questions, accumulate facts, and seek causes, which can be perceived as criticism and blaming. They offer advice and opinions without realizing they are not necessarily the clients'.

**Main Anxiety:** Having recently discovered their separate personhood, they fear losing their uniqueness, being reabsorbed into the mass, or losing their certainty and strong sense of self by opening up to others' views. This fear is counteracted by a strong front.

**Language Clues:** More complex syntax, beginning qualifications and conditions, contrast expressed in polar dimensions, passive interest in causality, beginning psychological vocabulary, references to the passing of time, consistent use of past tense, simple comparisons, and multiple options indicated.

### The Conscientious Stage
This stage is the target for Western culture, with educational systems aiming to produce rationally competent and independent adults. Democracy relies on the capacity of independent-thinking voters to make reasoned choices.

Individuals add linear time (past and future selves) to the third-person perspective, expanding their meaningful social context to society and those with similar ideologies and aspirations. They associate with people who have made choices about their ideals and goals, belonging to diverse groups without feeling torn by competing loyalties.

They are interested in reasons, causes, goals, consequences, and the effective use of time, curious about what makes people "tick." They seek truth about themselves through feedback and introspection, understanding themselves across time, describing past feelings, personal dreams, and future goals, with a future-oriented emphasis.

They believe in the perfectibility of humankind and the scientific method to uncover truth. Formal operations and abstract rationality peak. They believe proper scientific methods will reveal how things really are, including human nature. They work towards the betterment of the world, tolerating delays between actions and results, and recognizing the need for others to achieve goals.

Societal standards are internalized. They are aware of their ongoing growth and become truly introspective, concerned with living up to their beliefs. Guilt is a central emotion, and self-criticism can be severe when aggression is turned inward, leading to hypercritical, neurotic self-criticism due to single-minded, high-aiming plans.

Awareness of linear time allows observation of motivations across different periods. They seek root causes and reasons, believing truth about themselves can be found. They distinguish feelings from appearance and are motivated to figure things out.

Analyzing others and self-analysis become favorite pastimes. They cherish self-knowledge, possessing positive self-regard based on successes, independence, and self-authorship. They are less vulnerable to acceptance or exclusion.

Interpersonal relationships become intense and meaningful, with diverse and rich social contacts. Others are appreciated for different expertise, maintaining definite boundaries ("we agree to differ"). Increased self-differentiation does not mean egotistical behavior; others are valued independently of personal needs. Group membership is a contract to accomplish goals and partake in the enterprise's governance. Loyalty is to a chosen belief system rather than individuals. They function well in different teams and contexts.

Time is the medium to accomplish things, with a preoccupation on responsibility, conscientiousness, and expediency. They have a driven quality to accomplish or improve the world. The mood is earnest conviction, seriousness, idealism, and enthusiasm oriented towards action. They change minds through rational arguments and evidence. Self-esteem depends on achieving set goals rather than external affirmation. The drive to succeed can lead to over-extension and exhaustion, as limits are difficult to acknowledge.

They love the hypothetical and theories, convinced society can be controlled and improved. Rationality, progressivism, positivism, and reductionism are strongholds. They believe rationality will triumph and are interested in analysis. Truth can be approached by applying the scientific method and refining inquiry methods. They can be skeptical of unproven things but believe universal laws can eventually be figured out and proven.

**Impulse Control/Character Formation:** Societal values and morality are internalized if they fit self-evaluated standards. They tend to be self-critical, feeling guilt for unintentional consequences. They are self-reliant, conscientious, efficient, and preoccupied with attaining goals and ideals.

**Interpersonal Style:** They have a sense of responsibility and obligation towards others while pursuing their own agendas. They see themselves as indispensable, contributing members of society, yet separate and responsible for their choices. They appreciate mutual expression of differentiated feelings and ideas.

**Cognitive Style:** They think in formal operations, appreciating conceptual complexity and closed systems. They are aware of contingencies, alternatives, contradictions, and inconsistencies. They are concerned with consequences, priorities, and planful actions, capable of revision and reorientation.

**Conscious Preoccupations:** Achievement of long-term personal goals, ideals, and values is important. The money motive and focus on individual success fit well with capitalism. They reflect on motivations, reasons for behavior, and contradictory traits.

**Internal Dimension:** Interested in feelings, moods, traits, and motivations, seeking to understand causes of behavior to achieve goals.

**Decision Making:** They move with conviction when taking responsibility for a problem, concerned with efficient and effective strategies. They are willing to take risks and fail to a degree earlier stages cannot.

**Organizational Style:** Alliances are task-related or problem-oriented, with relationships depending on the specific task and role.

**Chief Defenses:** Intellectualization, rationalization, and suppression of the negative pole and shadow side defend against doubt and criticism. They prefer concentrating on the positive and acting when opportunity arises.

**Character of Depression:** Depression often manifests as guilt for unfulfilled goals and ideals, loss of the ability to accomplish them, and fear of losing control and independence.

**Fear:** They fear the Conformist mindset's dependency and submission, viewing blind obedience as "bad-me." They must ensure they are not subsumed in others' goals. Fear of losing progress, control, and separateness is overcome with action and forward movement.

**Counseling Style:** They ask many questions, including inquiries about feelings and experiences, with a greater ability to listen and restate expressions without adding interpretation.

**Language Clues:** Recognition and expression of conceptual complexity, explicit references to causal relationships, consequences, and priorities. Terms related to time (efficiency, planning, goals, future) and implicit references to time lapse. Differentiated psychological vocabulary describes complex psychological beings. "I" statements are revealing, containing contrast, self-criticism, and ownership of responsibility.

The major limit of the conventional mindset is its acceptance of facts and the external world as real, blind to the constructed nature of beliefs, especially conventional science. Underlying assumptions are rarely questioned. Knowledge, measurement, and prediction are taken for granted as means to control nature, self, and society. This attitude changes dramatically with postconventional development.

## The Postconventional Stages

### The General Systems Stages
At the first postconventional level, adults realize that the meaning of things depends on one's relative position, personal perspective, and interpretation. While objects are permanent, their meaning is constructed. Variables are interdependent, causality is cyclical, and boundaries are open and flexible, depending on system definitions. This systems view allows individuals to look at and compare systems of thought or organizations with distance. A main concern is laying bare underlying assumptions and frameworks. The Individualist stage represents systematic operations, while the Autonomous stage expands cognition to metasystematic operations, integrating different frameworks of the self into a coherent self-identity.

### The Individualistic Stage
This stage describes what a person can "see" with a fourth-person perspective. Individuals realize things are not necessarily what they seem, as interpretation depends on the observer's position. The concept of the participant observer, who influences what is observed, becomes a conscious preoccupation. Total detachment and objectivity are recognized as impossible.

The same object or event can have different meanings for different observers, contexts, or times. Individuals become interested in watching themselves try to make sense of themselves, marking a significant change in thought mode. Purely rational analysis is abandoned for a holistic, organismic approach, taking feelings and context into account. The process becomes as intriguing as the outcome. Relativistic or psychological approaches are favored over logical ones. The need to explain everything diminishes.

Individualists distrust conventional wisdom and hyper-rational tenets, distancing themselves from prior meaning-making. They reevaluate socially sanctioned role identities, redefining themselves uniquely based on personal experience. Realizing most prior meaning-making was socially conditioned breaks down scientific certainty and judgmental mindsets. They consciously scrutinize beliefs to test assumptions and relish novel mental freedom.

Since old identities are questioned, Individualists desire unique personal accomplishments independent of socially approved roles. They may withdraw from external affairs, turning inward to search for unique gifts or pursue burning questions. Given room, they provide creative input, novel problem-solving perspectives, and inspire others with their enthusiasm.

With the fourth-person perspective, paradoxes are recognized and enjoyed rather than explained away. Linear logic gives way to holistic understanding. There is no need to prove everything to live by it. A new, non-hostile humor directed towards the self emerges, based on the futility of framing things as right/wrong or provable/disprovable.

Individualists want to enjoy subjective experience, shifting from "doing" to "being and feeling." They become present-oriented, noticing how feelings affect the body and vice versa, developing a new body/mind connection. This reflects a shift from intellectual to organismic or embodied awareness.

Individualists do not impose interpretations on others; they seek to respect and understand them. Individual differences are celebrated. In groups, room is made for all voices and opinions. This can lead to radical postmodernism, where it is claimed with absolute certainty that there is no position from which to judge, failing to recognize the inherent self-contradiction. Diversity training may focus so much on differences that common humanity is overlooked. Egalitarian experiences prepare the way for deeper appreciation and tolerance of difference and similarity.

Individualists may or may not have a communal bend; some focus on their own meaning-making. Regardless, they recognize the relativity of all positions, are less apt to judge, and may appear "amoral" in letting others be and do what they want.

The focus on causality and goals is replaced by a fascination with the immediate present, understanding how things unfold. The focus turns from outcomes to processes, relationships, and non-linear influences. They watch how they and others change in different contexts, with an existential sense of how each moment differs, infusing the present with salience and poignancy.


---


# Individualist Stage (Continued)

Individuals at this stage often experience confusion arising from internal contradictions, frequently describing themselves as possessing multiple personalities or inner voices that resist synthesis into a unified whole. They might express this by saying, “Sometimes I act, feel, think one way, other times in another. There is struggle within myself, different voices competing for attention. And all seem real and important parts of me.” Some may refer to an “inner tribe,” asking themselves, “Who am I? What is going on?” Consequently, a primary source of anxiety involves the challenge of integrating these disparate aspects of the self. They may articulate fears such as, “I can be both my old rational self, and a new, different kind of person; I am afraid I am two or more personalities instead of one. I feel confused, split, ambivalent.” For these individuals, depression frequently revolves around these internal conflicts and a sense of despair regarding the possibility of ever discovering a true self. As they delve into their own feelings and motivations, they also become acutely aware of the human capacity for self-deception. The risks of defensive self-delusion and culturally biased distortions begin to be perceived as constant threats.

Conversely, this profound ability to connect with the self and engage in introspection fosters an enhanced capacity for empathy and a greater tolerance for diverse ideas, behaviors, and reactions. People at this stage are frequently admired, particularly by others at advanced developmental levels, for their energetic and uninhibited self-expression, spontaneity, and capacity to live authentically, free from restrictive societal norms. However, they may also be viewed with apprehension as unpredictable, or dismissed as impractical dreamers, especially within highly driven, achievement-oriented environments. Their interpersonal relationships are characterized by intensity and mutual fulfillment when based on synergy and personal choice. Yet, when placed in managerial roles, they can exhibit autocratic or highly idiosyncratic behaviors, leading others to perceive them as elusive, aloof, or unapproachable.

**Language Clues:** The syntax used by individuals at this stage features contingencies, sophisticated qualifications, and the juxtaposition of contrasting ideas. The conjunction “and” frequently replaces “or” and “but.” The use of phrases like “even though” or “despite” indicates advanced cognitive complexity. Their written expressions often include vivid personal disclosures, sometimes punctuated with exclamation marks, and employ a nuanced psychological vocabulary. A verbal rejection of cliché obligations in sentence completion exercises, such as responding with “How can I generalize?”, demonstrates an emerging awareness of cultural conditioning.

# The Autonomous Stage (Strategist)

The Autonomous stage introduces an expanded fourth-person perspective, situating the individual’s experiences within the broader context of specific worldviews and the entirety of a human lifespan. Equipped with this extended temporal horizon and broader social awareness, individuals at this stage can identify systemic patterns and long-term trends, a capacity that earns them recognition as strategic thinkers. Cognitively, they adopt a general systems perspective, enabling them to understand multiple, interconnected networks of relationships and processes.

Individuals at this stage recognize that they may exhibit conflicting traits or polarities across different times and contexts. However, unlike those at the preceding stage who might despair over their true identity, Autonomous individuals possess the ability to acknowledge and integrate these diverse facets of themselves, including previously compartmentalized sub-identities. A pivotal new capability is the realization of their own power to generate meaning and construct new narratives. This becomes possible because they understand that meaning is fundamentally an interpretation applied to experience; humans are continuously constructing stories about their reality. Consequently, Autonomous individuals make a conscious commitment to actively forge a meaningful existence for themselves and others through self-determination and self-actualization, navigating constantly shifting contexts. They maintain a robust, autonomous sense of self that is both highly differentiated and well-integrated, which includes the capacity to perceive and accept paradoxes and to tolerate ambiguity.

### Characteristics of the Autonomous Stage

*   **Realm:** Society and individuals sharing similar convictions and principles.
*   **Time Frame:** One's own history and lifetime.
*   **Cognition:** Metasystematic operations; functioning as a general systems thinker.
*   **Expanded Perspective:** The self is viewed as embedded within history and multiple cultural contexts.
*   **Preoccupations:** Personal development, self-actualization, self-fulfillment, and underlying processes.
*   **Positive Equilibration:** Achieves a well-balanced integration of body and mind; characterized as autonomous, tolerant, insightful, and growth-oriented, with high self-esteem and a focus on overarching principles.
*   **Truth:** Viewed as something that can be approximated. Higher developmental stages are considered superior because more complex arguments hold greater validity than weaker ones. Advanced development is seen as more adequate for navigating an increasingly complex global environment, emphasizing qualitative differences.

Autonomous individuals become highly attuned to their own psychological well-being, exhibiting an emotional tone that is notably less cynical and distrustful than that of the preceding stage. Relativism evolves into a personal commitment and a sense of responsibility for creating one's own meaning. Deeply connected to their inner workings, they can rediscover and embrace parts of the self that were previously rejected for being too confusing or threatening. By acknowledging the shadow aspects of the self to a greater extent, they achieve a new level of integration and wholeness. While they acutely experience role conflicts and dilemmas, they accept these as inevitable and view ambivalent feelings as entirely natural. Unlike individuals at even more advanced stages, Autonomous persons strive diligently to maintain composure and present themselves as reasonable and mature. They feel a strong responsibility to balance conflicting needs and various sub-identities, generally projecting an image of equilibrium.

Experiences are embraced for their immediate qualities even more fully than in the previous stage. Distressing emotions become more manageable; they can be internally acknowledged and openly shared with others. Because Autonomous individuals utilize a logical framework capable of integrating psychologically paradoxical elements, they expend less energy on defensive mechanisms. This efficiency fosters greater tolerance and spontaneity compared to adults at conventional stages. Both the self and others are accepted as complex beings possessing a mixture of positive and negative traits. Interpersonal exchanges facilitate connection with aspects of the self or behaviors that might otherwise be ignored. Constructive feedback illuminates blind spots and defensive postures, reinforcing the belief that the caring presence of others is essential for reaching one's full potential. Consequently, others are deemed vital to personal well-being, as deep self-knowledge and wisdom are cultivated through dynamic, intimate interactions. Mutual interdependence is recognized as inevitable and is approached with a sense of awe and a profound awareness of responsibility toward others. This deep appreciation of people as mirrors of the self occasionally allows Autonomous individuals to experience non-possessive, unconditional love.

The primary fear at this stage is the failure to fulfill one's potential or to uphold deeply valued universal principles, such as justice, tolerance, and human dignity. Depression typically stems from a loss of courage, a diminished sense of agency, and guilt over not realizing one's unique human promise. When employing psychological defenses, healthy Autonomous individuals rely predominantly on mature mechanisms like suppression, altruism, humor, and anticipation. If they resort to less mature defenses, they demonstrate self-forgiveness and resilience, reasoning, “Right now I need to be like this, but I intend to act more maturely again when appropriate.”

The internal processes at this stage are intricate and require careful observation. Despite their need for interpersonal connection, Autonomous individuals also require privacy and time for self-reflection. The self is perceived as a continuously unfolding entity subject to constant reappraisal, and observing this evolution provides significant satisfaction. Their life's work is dedicated to the pursuit of becoming the best version of themselves, making personal growth, self-actualization, and self-fulfillment their primary desires. They view others as actors in their own life dramas, finding these narratives equally fascinating. A powerful motivator is the desire to facilitate the growth of others, making this stage common among psychologists, coaches, consultants, and effective leaders. However, when their efforts to help others reach their potential meet resistance, they may experience impatience with the slow pace of others' development and frustration over a perceived unwillingness to grow. This tendency represents a central vulnerability of the stage.

Autonomous individuals are often the most steadfast believers that higher developmental stages are inherently better and should be actively promoted. They reason that increased differentiation and autonomy lead to a more objective, undistorted, and realistic self-identity, allowing one to claim an authentic and truthful self. Consequently, authenticity stands as a paramount value within their repertoire.

Many view life as an open-ended journey, rejecting the notion of a predetermined path applicable to everyone. They believe each person must forge their own lifestyle and bears the responsibility for their own self-fulfillment. They are deeply interested in psychological inquiries and strategies for resolving inner conflicts. Unlike those at conventional stages, they view seeking therapy, advice, or consultation not as a weakness, but as a vital necessity. They maintain faith in the human capacity to extract meaning from chaos and adversity, constructing personal significance without feeling compelled to impose it on others. They welcome alternative solutions to conflicts and deeply respect the autonomy of others. At times, they may find the mundane requirements of earning a living and other ordinary life constraints to be stifling, preferring to pursue their passions on their own terms. If their passion aligns with leading an organization to greatness, the pairing can be highly successful.

These individuals may experience genuine, principled anger and righteous indignation in response to global injustices. They are willing to oppose societal norms to express personal convictions or uphold their principles, though their anger rarely targets a specific victim. They live according to self-evaluated, internal standards, where "walking the talk" serves as a testament to their moral character and authenticity. Irreconcilable role conflicts are felt profoundly. Some emerge as charismatic moral leaders who, when defending a worthy cause, may disregard the negative repercussions for themselves. However, they can also become overly forceful in their convictions, particularly when driven by principled anger. If left unchecked, the combination of charisma and intense moral certainty can become a potent negative force.

An expanded awareness of their inner depths allows Autonomous individuals to utilize dreams, fantasy, and imagination much more freely than those at earlier stages. Their cognitive processes are unhindered, releasing profound creativity as their imagination breaks free from the constraints of strict logic and convention. They approach problem-solving by thinking outside the box. Furthermore, their acute awareness of human frailty as an intrinsic part of the human condition often manifests in existential humor, characterized by a lighter, more nuanced irony.

*   **Impulse Control and Character Development:** Guided by self-evaluated, internalized standards. Behavior serves as an expression of moral principles. Demonstrates tolerance for multiple viewpoints and a deep concern for conflicting roles, duties, and principles.
*   **Interpersonal Style:** Assumes responsibility for relationships while maintaining a strong desire for autonomy. Views relationships through the lens of inevitable mutual interdependence. Tolerates diverse approaches to conflict resolution and respects the autonomy of others. Employs non-hostile, existential humor.
*   **Cognitive Level:** Features an expanded time frame and social context. Possesses the capacity to perceive and embrace paradox and contradiction, alongside a high tolerance for ambiguity. Exhibits a broad scope of thought and perceives the self as the regulator of a complex system with interdependent parts within a larger context.
*   **Conscious Preoccupation:** Deeply concerned with individuality and self-fulfillment. Highly interested in the psychology of the self and the process of reconciling conflicting needs.
*   **Chief Anxiety:** Fears failing to fulfill personal potential, neglecting cherished principles (e.g., justice, tolerance), and losing courage. Experiences impatience with the slow development of others and their perceived reluctance to grow.
