*   **Internal Dimension:** Seeks to understand the self through the lens of inner conflict and polarities. Strives to integrate various situational selves into a coherent, unified whole.
*   **Defenses:** Primarily utilizes mature defenses such as suppression, altruism, humor, and anticipation. When employing less mature defenses, exhibits self-forgiveness and understanding, acknowledging temporary regressions while planning to return to mature behavior, stating, “Right now I need to be like this, but I will be able to act more maturely again later”.
*   **Representatives:** Often charismatic individuals willing to make personal sacrifices to defend and exemplify their deeply held moral principles.
*   **Language Clues:** Employs complex, flexible syntax to discuss a wide array of topics and concerns. Strives to capture the complexity of life in verbal expressions, presenting a coherent exposition of their thoughts and identity. Utilizes a differentiated psychological vocabulary and grasps the concept of circular causality in human relationships. Frequently uses terms related to identity, growth, higher principles, and self-fulfillment.

# The Unitive Stages

The highest levels of postconventional development encompass stages where individuals recognize that all objects and concepts are human-made constructs. This includes abstract notions such as the ego, three-dimensional space, and time, all of which are built upon successive layers of symbolic abstraction. They understand that even a commonplace concept like a "chair" is a massive simplification; no two mental representations of a chair are identical, nor are any two physical chairs exactly alike. Yet, the shared terminology functions effectively for communication. A defining characteristic of these advanced stages is the acute awareness of both the limitations and the extraordinary utility of language. While previous stages begin to deconstruct knowledge by recognizing the importance of interpretation and context, this advanced level perceives reality as an undifferentiated phenomenological continuum, a creative ground, or an underlying unity. A primary existential concern at these stages involves grappling with fundamental assumptions about human nature and the innate drive to impose order upon chaos. These final stages represent cross-paradigmatic and transrational operations, as only a novel way of knowing can hierarchically transcend and integrate all preceding epistemologies.

## The Construct-Aware Stage (Alchemist)

Individuals at the Construct-Aware stage recognize the overarching pattern of development that encompasses increasingly broad realms of experience and thought. They understand that the ego has served dual functions: as a central processing unit for stimuli and as the primary reference point for self-identity. Upon realizing this fundamental egocentricity, they begin to experience it as a limitation to further growth and comprehension. They question the ultimate value of constructing increasingly complex thought structures and integrations, recognizing the inherent absurdity and automatic boundaries of human map-making within the representational domain.

Unlike those at earlier stages, Construct-Aware individuals are highly conscious of the ego's vigilant and clever strategies for self-preservation. This marks the first developmental point where the ego becomes fully transparent to itself. They view definitive knowledge about the self or the universe as illusory and unattainable through sheer effort and rational thought, recognizing that all conscious cognition is constructed and therefore inherently separated from the underlying, cohesive, non-dual reality.

By directing their focus further inward, they deconstruct their own attempts at meaning-making, becoming acutely aware of the profound divisions and paradoxes embedded in rational thought. They independently arrive at the realization that the conceptual map is not the actual territory. The linguistic habit of dividing the world into polar opposites and attaching value judgments to them becomes fully conscious. Concepts such as good and evil, life and death, or beauty and ugliness are perceived as two sides of the same coin—mutually dependent and defining one another. Furthermore, they recognize that the incessant categorization of experiences as good or bad generates much of the tension and dissatisfaction prevalent in ordinary waking life. These individuals possess a dynamic, multifaceted understanding of human nature and the intricacies of social interaction. They actively confront their own deep-seated need for theories and explanations, seeking to uncover the limitations of the rational mind and to unlearn automatic, conditioned responses sustained by memory and continuous cultural reinforcement.

### Characteristics of the Construct-Aware Stage

*   **Realm:** Extends beyond one's own culture, encompassing a global and historical perspective.
*   **Time Frame:** Reaches beyond the individual's lifetime.
*   **Cognition:** Capable of perceiving unitive concepts; operates cross-paradigmatically.
*   **Preoccupation:** Focused on inner conflicts regarding existential paradoxes and the intrinsic limitations of language and meaning-making.
*   **Positive Equilibration:** Characterized by the acceptance of tension and paradox, a commitment to serving the self and others, and profound humility in the face of life's tasks.
*   **Truth:** Acknowledges that regardless of the level of abstraction or cognitive insight achieved, the individual remains separated from the underlying non-dual reality.

Construct-Aware individuals closely monitor their own emotional and rational processing. They observe their automatic tendencies to judge, analyze, and reflect in the pursuit of creating increasingly accurate theories of life. They understand these intellectual endeavors as partial defense mechanisms designed to avoid confronting the impermanence of the embodied self. Consequently, they see through not only cultural conditioning but also the fundamental predicament of existing within the confines of language.

Recognizing the filter of symbolic construction profoundly alters their relationship with language. They strive to remain conscious of the pseudo-reality generated by words, understanding that the quest for objective self-identification and rational explanations of the universe is ultimately futile—an artifact of the human desire to solidify that which is inherently fluid and immaterial. They recognize that the habit of language reinforces the ego's dominance whenever meaning is constructed through representation, regardless of the specific language, power dynamics, or cultural context. Simultaneously, they deeply appreciate the indispensable role language plays in human affairs, social interaction, and overall development.

As self-awareness deepens and reasoning becomes more sophisticated, these individuals gain increased access to intuition, bodily sensations, feelings, dreams, and archetypal or transpersonal material. These non-rational sources of knowledge become just as crucial as rational deliberation for interpreting experience and finding meaning. The ability to value and draw upon non-rational insights expands significantly throughout postconventional development.

Crucially, the consistent practice of inward observation often triggers spontaneous experiences of a direct mode of being, where the distinction between the knower and the known temporarily dissolves, and the personal sense of self vanishes. These occurrences, often described as peak moments, flow states, or bliss experiences, possess consistently positive qualities that encourage a yearning for further such encounters. However, the process of dismantling the conditional self can also be deeply unsettling, akin to the harrowing experiences sometimes encountered in alternative states of consciousness induced by intense meditation or other means.

Individuals at this stage frequently report functioning as a witness to their own internal experiences, observing the flow of thoughts and feelings without attempting to direct or control them. This witnessing provides moments of liberation from the ego's relentless drive for control and self-affirmation. Nevertheless, these experiences are transient; the moment the individual attempts to evaluate or judge the peak experience, the state is broken.

There is a natural reluctance to abandon familiar modes of functioning. People resist relinquishing the illusion of an enduring, separate identity that they have spent a lifetime defining and cultivating. Even when they comprehend that releasing attachment to the known alleviates familiar forms of suffering, deliberate attempts to let go often prove ineffective and generate intractable paradoxes. Becoming attached to the concept of non-attachment only strengthens the fetters. Similarly, taking pride in one's psychic abilities or ego-transcending journey merely reinforces the ego's dominance, illustrating how the ego can co-opt transpersonal experiences for its own aggrandizement.

A defining reality for Construct-Aware individuals is the scarcity of peers who share their developmental perspective. They may harbor fears that almost no one comprehends their complexity or sympathizes with their experiences, which in turn can induce feelings of guilt or hubris for perceiving themselves as "better" or more advanced than others. They acutely observe their own attachments and their reliance on increasingly elaborate self-theories to maintain a permanent identity. Conversely, they possess a profound appreciation for the necessity of others to make sense of life using the tools available to them. This fosters a deep tolerance for diverse life solutions, even as they clearly perceive the limitations of those solutions. Occasionally, they may experience envy toward the simplicity of earlier developmental stages, as their own reality is experienced as overwhelmingly complex. Yet, due to their advanced ego maturity, most achieve a dynamic and hopeful equilibrium amidst these fundamental conflicts. They pursue their chosen destinies with independence and courage, fully acknowledging their essential aloneness and existential despair. When integration fails, their depression centers on the fundamental isolation of humanity and the inability of rational thought to generate lasting meaning.

This is the first stage that interprets all experiences entirely through the lens of change and evolution. Consequently, they are even more sensitive than Autonomous individuals and possess a unique capacity to understand others in developmental terms. Those who engage in public or interpersonal work typically exhibit exceptionally refined interpersonal skills and a deep insight into complex personalities and relational dynamics. They listen with profound empathy and provide transformative, undistorted feedback.

Construct-Aware individuals have profound access to their own past methods of meaning-making, allowing them to tailor their interactions precisely to the developmental level of the recipient. For instance, they can effectively engage with individuals at self-protective stages by adopting a style that asserts superior insight through strength, meeting them on their own terms. As consultants or mentors, they adapt their approach to the client's needs, facilitating self-discovery through empathetic listening, strategically challenging ideas, and helping clients reframe experiences and construct new narratives. As counselors, they do not assume they can fully grasp the unique experience of another, yet they maintain a deep human affinity. They often harbor resentment toward the self-importance and subtle need for affirmation characteristic of the preceding Autonomous stage.

In fact, they may exhibit the least tolerance for the Autonomous stage's fervent drive to help others reach their maximum potential. They view the desire to change others as a fundamental flaw, reflecting an egocentric belief that one's own perspective is the valid measure of the world, and indicating a failure to live in the present moment. Because they are highly differentiated, they can be overly self-critical upon first recognizing their own ego attachments. However, with continued experience in the cycle of identification and release, they develop tolerance for their own vacillating complexity. They generally employ mature defense mechanisms—such as sublimation, non-hostile humor, and bracketing—while remaining highly insightful regarding their own vulnerabilities. They may consciously choose to utilize less mature behavioral styles or simply accept their raw, spontaneous responses when they occur. The conscious experience of non-ordinary states, where the sense of self and time dissolves, helps contextualize and diminish the ego's temptation to assume an exaggerated sense of power and responsibility.

These individuals can perceive the underlying structure of their own cognitive processes, compare them with those of others, and recognize the fundamental limitations of rational thought and language. While they often appear highly intelligent, they frequently experience impatience with their own cleverness, recognizing it as merely a cognitive tool. They are fascinated by the pervasive human need for meaning-making and understand the mechanics of how meaning is constructed through the segmentation and reification of the continuous flow of phenomena into complex theories. Consequently, they are deeply preoccupied with the boundaries of rationality and the challenge of living within the paradoxes of language.

In leadership roles, Construct-Aware individuals tend to establish novel organizations or work independently to make their optimal contribution to humanity. They embrace roles as catalysts or transformers but are quick to depart once they feel their transformative work is complete. They measure their success by their own dispensability—achieving their goal when the organization itself becomes self-organizing and inherently transformative.

*   **Conscious Preoccupation:** Focused on the limitations of rational thought and language, and navigating the tension of existential paradoxes.
*   **Counseling Style:** Highly adaptable; engages in mentoring and facilitates self-discovery through empathetic listening, strategic challenges, and reframing, all tailored to the client's specific developmental stage.
*   **Depression:** Characterized by despair over humanity's essential aloneness and the ultimate failure of the rational enterprise to create enduring meaning.
*   **Chief Anxiety:** The fear of being entirely misunderstood in their complexity, coupled with the fear that this very concern constitutes hubris.
*   **Language Clues:** Communication is complex, vivid, authentic, and playful. It is often more direct and unpolished than that of the Autonomous stage, as there is significantly less desire to impress or present an idealized self. They articulate a vast matrix of topics, insights, and questions, cleverly woven into complex sentence structures. Responses may exhibit a stream-of-consciousness or "thought-in-progress" quality, reflecting attempts to relinquish conscious structuring. They frequently use vivid expressions regarding the constructed nature of reality, pose fundamental questions about meaning-making as a defense mechanism, and demonstrate a deep concern with epistemology and the limits of definition.

## The Unitive Stage

Research into higher states of consciousness suggests the existence of stages beyond the rational, personal realm. While finer distinctions within this ego-transcendent domain are difficult to capture through standard assessment tools, the characteristics of the Unitive stage reflect a synthesis of these highest measurable levels of development.

The Unitive stage introduces a radically new paradigm for perceiving human existence and consciousness. The previous reliance on viewing reality exclusively through the lens of the self and the medium of language is fundamentally transformed into a universal or cosmic perspective. Individuals at this stage experience themselves and others as integral components of an ongoing humanity, deeply embedded in the creative ground and participating in the destiny of evolution. They successfully integrate the paradox of human existence: the simultaneous feelings of profound belongingness and distinct separateness are experienced without tension, viewed simply as shifting perceptions of the infinite possibilities of being.

At this level of integration, individuals perceive themselves and others across vast dimensions—geographical, social, cultural, historical, intellectual, and developmental. They effortlessly adopt multiple perspectives and shift their focus among various states of awareness. They feel deeply embedded in nature, viewing birth, growth, death, joy, and pain as natural, cyclical patterns within the flow of time. Rational awareness is no longer experienced as a limitation but as one of many phenomena that can move between the foreground and background of attention. They possess the ability to perceive the concrete, temporal aspects of an entity simultaneously with its eternal, symbolic significance. This unitive capacity allows them to recognize and cherish the essential humanity in all beings, feeling a profound sense of oneness with them. They respect the core essence of others and harbor no desire to change them. Crucially, from this perspective, higher developmental stages are not deemed superior to lower ones; all are recognized as necessary, interconnected components of a unified reality and an overarching evolutionary process.

Unitive thinkers accept themselves unconditionally and without the need for control. Regardless of the magnitude of their achievements, they recognize their efforts as merely a single drop in the vast ocean of human endeavor. Observers are frequently struck by the profound humility and grace these individuals exude. A defining characteristic of this transpersonal stage is a grounded simplicity that emerges on the far side of immense complexity.

### Characteristics of the Unitive Stage

*   **Realm:** The universe and the time/space continuum.
*   **Time Frame:** Eternity.
*   **Cognition:** Fully embraces unitive concepts.
*   **Preoccupation:** Focused on pure being, maintaining a non-controlling consciousness, and witnessing the continuous flux of experience and mental states.
*   **Perspective:** Highly multiperspectival; the center of identity shifts from the ego to a higher, transcendent Self.
*   **Positive Equilibration:** Exhibits an accepting, unassuming presence; demonstrates a fully empathetic, non-interfering capacity to simply be with whatever arises.
*   **Truth:** Perceived as immanent; truth is the experiential realization of interconnectedness and non-separateness, viewing existence as shifting states of awareness within a timeless spirit.

Despite their profound integration, individuals at this stage may sometimes be perceived by those at earlier stages as aloof or insufficiently engaged with the common pursuits and concerns of humanity. However, these critics often fail to recognize that Unitive adults function as powerful catalysts in the lives of others. By existing authentically and without apology, they challenge conventional perspectives and model a way of being that transcends evaluative, traditional notions of adulthood. They project a deep, ontological security that surpasses anything achievable through a rationally constructed self-identity. Unlike individuals at any other stage, they cultivate intense, non-demanding relationships with people across all developmental levels, ages, and backgrounds. By recognizing the inherent dignity in all manifestations of life, they possess a unique ability to make others feel entirely worthy and whole.

Unitive individuals operate from a completely internalized, transpersonal morality. Inner conflicts and competing external demands are accepted simply as aspects of existence that require witnessing rather than resolution or denial. They are no longer propelled by the desire to achieve specific states or to mold themselves into a particular ideal; instead, they easily relinquish the unattainable. While deeply concerned with global justice, creativity, and spontaneity, their non-attached, impersonal stance does not result in passivity. Rather, it enables them to take highly direct and powerful action when necessary.

Reality is frequently experienced as an undifferentiated phenomenological continuum or the unified ground of consciousness. Every thought, feeling, word, object, and theory is recognized as a human construct—an artificial boundary drawn where none truly exists. They understand the quest for meaning and connection as an essential facet of the human condition, feeling a deep interconnectedness with all sentient beings in their struggle to survive and comprehend their existence. This fosters boundless tolerance, compassion, and affiliation with all forms of life, recognizing that the simplest organism is, in its own way, as close to the ultimate truth as the most advanced philosopher. Peak experiences lose their extraordinary quality and become a familiar, integrated way of experiencing the world. The present moment is perceived as the intersection of past and future. This total openness allows them to align with truth and beauty, facilitating visionary experiences where reality is comprehended holistically and analogically, rather than solely through the rational mind.

They can effortlessly shift their focus, perceiving the entirety of a system simultaneously with its individual components. Operating within an expanded temporal framework that encompasses the entirety of Earth's history and future, they view life as a temporary, sometimes voluntary, separation from the creative ground, to which it will inevitably return. While conscious of their unique physical embodiment, they identify intimately with all living things. The perceived separation between self and other is understood as an illusion—a construct designed to satisfy the ego's demand for permanence and to defend against the fear of death.

Consequently, Unitive individuals transcend narrow ego boundaries. They maintain open boundaries, remaining attuned to whatever enters their awareness without becoming preoccupied by it. They engage in "witnessing" rather than mere observation, metabolizing experiences without the willful, conscious focus characteristic of earlier adult stages. They recognize that truth is immanent in the universe and can be apprehended through this open, receptive stance, though it remains forever beyond the grasp of pure rationality.

They possess an integrated sense of identity as active participants in cosmic evolution. They are aligned with their life's work, viewing it simultaneously as an expression of their unique individuality and as a contribution to shared humanity. Deeply invested in the fundamental dilemmas of the human condition, they advocate for justice, fairness, and universal benevolence. While they take responsibility for their own meaning-making, they do not view themselves as the absolute masters of their destiny in the way Autonomous or Construct-Aware individuals might. They recognize that the ways of being are infinite, with clues to this diversity found throughout human history, current cultural variations, and the potential for future evolutionary developments or alternative states of consciousness. Life is perceived as fulfilling a cosmic purpose, rendering it essentially simple. Thinking and doing are recognized merely as modes of existence, holding no intrinsic superiority over feeling, being, or even non-being—a concept that remains profoundly difficult for those operating exclusively within the personal realm to grasp. They embody the understanding that ultimate comprehension is itself an illusion.

In summary, Unitive adults maintain a beautifully balanced and integrated sense of both their individuality and their universal belonging, feeling themselves to be an active part of the universe's ongoing cycles of creation, destruction, and recreation.

*   **Language Clues:** Unitive individuals utilize a vast, global array of topics and concerns, often weaving them into a single response. Their unitive capacity provides them with an expansive mental landscape. Their communication is kaleidoscopic, effortlessly bridging profound contrasts: the concrete and the practical, the theoretical and the philosophical, the personal and the global, the serious and the humorous, the mundane and the sublime. Their responses to assessments may feature highly unique, vivid expressions and creative metaphors that articulate both ordinary and non-ordinary experiences. Conversely, they may also provide strikingly simple responses. This simplicity is not indicative of a lower developmental stage; rather, straightforward spontaneity is recognized as an essential hallmark of wisdom and ego-transcendence. While Construct-Aware individuals possess the most complex and differentiated view of reality within the personal realm, Unitive adults operate entirely outside the desperate need for meaning-making that characterizes all earlier stages, recognizing the ultimate futility of shielding and bolstering the ego.

# Summary

The developmental trajectory traces a path from the unconscious, undifferentiated state of infancy to the mature adult's conscious experience of profound embeddedness within the universe. This journey moves from the pre-rational to the meta-rational, and from pre-verbal unawareness to the dawn of post-symbolic wisdom. Immense freedom is attained when individuals recognize the fundamental interconnectedness of all phenomena and understand the constructed nature of boundaries, objects, self-identities, and the narratives woven about life and reality. Significant suffering is alleviated when automatic cognitive and emotional habits are unlearned—decoupled from the memories of the past and the desires for the future—and replaced by a mindful, non-evaluative, and unwavering attention to the reality of the present moment.


---


# Construction of a Scoring Manual for the Sentence Stem "A Good Boss—"

## Abstract
Ego development theory is the study of how an individual's processing of experience and meaning-making of the world develops. The tool used to measure ego development is a sentence completion test, and its scoring manual is the foundation for the high validity and reliability of the measurement tool. A modified version of the sentence completion test was constructed, and in order to continue the trend of strong psychometric properties, a scoring manual needs to be constructed for the new sentence stems found on the modified test. This project constructs a scoring manual for the sentence stem, "A good boss—".

The first step in creating a scoring manual was to sort the data, which consisted of 836 responses to the sentence stem, into categories within the different stages of ego development. Next, any new categories that seemed to emerge during the sort process were defined, and all of the existing categories were organized into a logical sequence within each stage. The final step was to write the scoring manual and perform basic statistics on the frequency distribution of responses within each category.

The results of this project include the construction of a scoring manual for the sentence stem "A good boss—", and a frequency distribution of the stem responses, which provides evidence that the sentence stem "A good boss—" has internal validity, as it seems to produce a similar distribution of response data as the assessment tool as a whole provides. This project enhances the reliability and validity of the modified sentence completion test and adds data and conclusions to the pool of research available for the study of ego development theory.

## Introduction
The purpose of this project is to construct a scoring manual for the sentence stem "A good boss—", which is a component of a projective measure of ego development. An introduction to this project would not be complete without a review of the concept of ego development, which will include an overview of the individual ego stages. This will be followed by a description of the original measure of ego development and its revised form. After a brief introduction of how these sentence completion tests are scored, the intent of this project will be discussed, including support for constructing an updated scoring manual for this sentence stem.

### The Concept of Ego Development
Early psychoanalytic theorists believed that the ego was derived from and explained in terms of drives originating instinctually. Yet, other theorists separated from this view, contending that the ego endeavored to spontaneously develop. The concept of ego development follows a continuum of earlier, similar conceptions, including moral development, character development, interpersonal relatability, cognitive complexity, and interpersonal integration. These conceptions have the following in common, they all: (a) embody normal developmental sequences that include dimensions of individual differences within any age cohort; (b) encompass a holistic view of personality and view behavior in the realm of meaning-making and purpose; and (c) are concerned with the personality characteristics of impulse control, interpersonal mode, and conscious preoccupations.

Extracting from the common domain of psychology and partly from these earlier writers, a new version of ego development was constructed. A program to measure ego development was proposed and then the data was used to construct stage-specific milestones, which embody the definition of ego development. These milestones represent the frame of reference through which a person makes meaning of the world and her or his experiences within it. This search for coherent meaning is defined as the "essence" of the ego, and the stability of the ego is tied into this "essence" in that the ego will selectively disregard the observations that are inconsistent with its current frame of reference. In other words, individuals will function at the level that is most egosyntonic for them and translate and respond to the world on a day-to-day basis from that frame of reference. Therefore, a projective test is a useful tool to help evoke the subject's own frame of reference on the test material, and this is the theoretical foundation for the sentence completion test. The cornerstone of ego development theory is related to the idea that language not only reflects human experience, but it also organizes and filters it. This description of the nature and function of language is an important contribution to ego development theory, considering that the sentence completion test measures the ego stages of respondents based on their verbal responses. The overall strategy of the sentence completion test is to identify the qualitative differences in the successive stages of ego development and to map the qualitative data onto a quantitative scale. Before describing how ego development is measured by the sentence completion test, it is necessary to introduce the stages of ego development.

The labels given to the stages of ego development have been transformed over the years since the first version of the sentence completion test. A fundamental aspect of the concept of ego development is that there are several dimensions of a person that evolve during ego development, including character development, impulse control, cognitive style and conscious preoccupations, and interpersonal style, and these features are woven into the descriptions for each stage.

The first stage is the Symbiotic stage, and the newborn at this stage is constructing for her or himself a stable world of objects. Some psychoanalytic theorists connect ego development to this period alone. Because this stage is characterized as pre-verbal or nonverbal, it cannot be measured with the sentence completion test and therefore will not be discussed further.

Stage 2 is the Impulsive stage and is indicative of the simultaneous emergence of the ego and the use of language. Impulses govern people at this stage, and others are seen as a source of supply. Cognitive simplicity is reflected in the language clues of using simple statements, using simple dichotomies (things are good or bad, clean or dirty), and instead of being expressed as feelings, affects are described using bodily terms. Individuals who are at this stage may seem confused, anxious and overwhelmed.

The Opportunist occupies Stage 2/3, and this person is wary of other's intentions and experiences friction due to an "I win, you lose" mentality. They perceive the world based on their own needs and wants and may be perceived by others as being exploitative and manipulative. Opportunists will attempt to avoid the consequences of their actions by placing blame on others. They are preoccupied with control and advantage, and their language reflects this with the use of words like "always" and "never".

The Diplomat is at Stage 3, and this stage is characterized by a desire to belong and conform to the rules of the group. Individuals at this stage are concerned with appearances, social acceptance, and avoiding conflict. They tend to view the world in terms of stereotypes and clichés, and their language is often characterized by the use of platitudes and conventional phrases. They value loyalty and cooperation, but may struggle with independent thought and decision-making.

The Expert is at Stage 3/4, and this stage marks a shift towards greater self-awareness and a focus on individual skills and knowledge. Individuals at this stage are concerned with competence, efficiency, and doing things "right". They value logic, reason, and objective facts, and may be critical of those who do not share their standards. Their language is often characterized by the use of technical jargon and a focus on details and procedures. They may struggle with ambiguity and multiple perspectives.

The Achiever is at Stage 4, and this stage is characterized by a focus on goals, results, and personal achievement. Individuals at this stage are self-directed, ambitious, and driven to succeed. They value planning, organization, and strategic thinking, and are able to balance multiple priorities and perspectives. Their language is often characterized by the use of action-oriented verbs and a focus on outcomes and consequences. They may struggle with delegating tasks and trusting others to meet their standards.

The Individualist is at Stage 4/5, and this stage marks a shift towards greater introspection and a focus on personal growth and authenticity. Individuals at this stage are concerned with meaning, purpose, and self-actualization. They value creativity, intuition, and subjective experience, and are able to tolerate ambiguity and paradox. Their language is often characterized by the use of metaphors, symbols, and a focus on inner states and emotions. They may struggle with balancing their own needs with the needs of others and finding a sense of belonging.

The Strategist is at Stage 5, and this stage is characterized by a focus on systemic thinking, collaboration, and long-term vision. Individuals at this stage are able to see the big picture and understand the interconnectedness of complex systems. They value diversity, dialogue, and mutual learning, and are able to navigate conflict and ambiguity with grace and skill. Their language is often characterized by the use of inclusive pronouns and a focus on shared goals and values. They may struggle with the slow pace of change and the resistance of others to new ideas.

The Magician is at Stage 5/6, and this stage marks a shift towards greater integration and a focus on transformation and transcendence. Individuals at this stage are able to hold multiple perspectives simultaneously and see the underlying unity of all things. They value wisdom, compassion, and spiritual growth, and are able to act with profound insight and intuition. Their language is often characterized by the use of paradox, poetry, and a focus on the ineffable and the transcendent. They may struggle with communicating their insights to others and finding a sense of grounding in the material world.

The Ironist is at Stage 6, and this stage is characterized by a focus on universal principles, unconditional love, and a deep sense of connection to all of humanity. Individuals at this stage are able to transcend their own ego and act with profound selflessness and compassion. They value truth, justice, and the well-being of all beings, and are able to inspire and uplift others with their presence and their words. Their language is often characterized by the use of simple, profound statements and a focus on the essential nature of reality. They may struggle with the suffering of the world and the limitations of human existence.

### The Sentence Completion Test and Scoring Procedures
The sentence completion test is a projective measure of ego development that consists of a series of sentence stems that the respondent is asked to complete. The responses are then scored according to a detailed manual that provides examples of responses at each stage of ego development. The scoring process involves matching the respondent's completion with the examples in the manual and assigning a score based on the stage that best fits the response. The scores for all of the sentence stems are then combined to calculate a total protocol rating, which represents the respondent's overall level of ego development.

The purpose of this project is to increase the psychometric validity and reliability of the modified sentence completion test and enhance the study of ego development by constructing a scoring manual for the sentence stem "A good boss—".

## Literature Review
It has been many years since the first publication of the sentence completion test, and since then, numerous studies have used this form of measurement in their empirical inquiries, volumes of books have been written about the concept and measure, countless evaluations of the measurement's validity and reliability have been conducted, and several revisions have been made to the test, its scoring manuals, and the labels for the stages of ego development. This literature review will provide a survey of the history of the test, the revisions and new forms that have been generated, the reliability and validity of the measurement tool, discussion of the concept of ego development as a "master trait", and the utilization of the test in peripheral areas of research.

### History of the Sentence Completion Test
To understand how the concept and measurement of ego development were arrived at, it is necessary to begin with studies of the personality patterns of mothers and of women in general. A research group created an objective measure named the Family Problems scale, which included items concerning the problems of family life throughout the day. The purpose of this study was to administer the test items to several samples of women and search for clusters of items that might be related to personality patterns or traits. This study led to a large cluster of items that highlighted a characterization of women resembling the "authoritarian" personality. The group constructed a scale around this cluster, which they termed the Authoritarian Family Ideology. In determining which group of women was representative of this ideology, the researchers noted that a continuum was necessary to define the cluster and that authoritarianism was not the extreme, but the midpoint. Therefore, they came up with a milestone sequence, which represented qualitatively different levels along a developmental course. This milestone sequence was proposed to be the concept of ego development and introduced it not as one interesting personality trait among several others, but as the "master trait". The use of the term "master trait" has sparked some controversy in the field of trait and developmental psychology, and this debate will be examined later. A program of how to measure this concept of ego development was also described.

To test this new idea, the group constructed a sentence completion test, adopting the conception of I-levels for interpersonal maturity and integration as well as guidelines for scoring the test. The four initial stages and codes were as follows: Impulsive (I-2), Conformist (I-3), Conscientious (I-4), and Autonomous (I-5). Early raters of the measure indicated to the research group that an intermediate stage was needed between Impulsive and Conformist, and therefore, another stage was added, coded Delta, to occupy a level between these two stages. In the beginning, the researchers had sentence completions to begin with, but no scoring manual. To devise scoring manuals, the group first tried to imagine what kind of person would emerge in each stage, based on the milestones, which resulted in possible examples of responses for each stage. This list of possible responses was termed their exemplar manual. After this manual failed with newly collected responses, the researchers tried grouping examples into categories, and this was termed the categorized manual. The final manual that worked best in terms of being most useful for new sets of responses was a combination of both exemplar and categorized manuals, the rationalized category manual.

For microvalidation of the manual, the researchers finalized ground rules for scoring, and re-evaluated the final scoring manual with a large and heterogeneous sample set. This resulted in the revision and addition of stages of ego development. For example, the Opportunist (Delta) stage was renamed Self-Protective, which was more reflective of the expressions in the item responses, and another transition stage was introduced just after Self-Protective, coded Delta/3, because there seemed to be a need for compromise between the Self-Protective (Delta) stage and the next stage, Conformist (I-3). A later stage emerged above Autonomous, named Integrated (I-6), and two transition levels were added, one originally called Conscientious-Conformist (eventually changed to Self-Aware, I-3/4) and the other termed Individualistic (I-4/5) for the transition between Conscientious and Autonomous.

The cornerstone of the method is classifying related but not necessarily linked answers into categories, treated as equivalent. Forming categories is an art that improves with practice, because there is constant feedback when the manual is next put to use. This statement provides insight into the creative methodology of constructing scoring manuals for sentence stems. For final confirmation to send the sentence completion test out for publication, the researchers settled upon 36 stems, finalized the unique scoring algorithm, and tested the scoring manual using a sealed sample and experienced raters. Data from this sample gave an interrater reliability of approximately .91 for rating the items and total protocol ratings, and the internal consistency (coefficient alpha) was .90. Training exercises administered to new, untrained raters proved that the written instructions in the scoring manual were complete.

The most distinctive and crucially valuable feature of the method of manual construction is the microvalidation of manual categories by repeated cycling, applying the manual to a new sample, "decoding" the ratings to see how well the parts of the manual are doing, and making minor changes before applying the manual again. By this method the descriptions of the several stages were shaped by the data, and the conception of ego development took shape.

Perhaps a limitation of this initial publication of the test and its scoring manual is that all of the item responses were obtained from women and adolescent girls. Prior to this manual being published, a prediction of future research needs led to a proposal of new versions for men and women, which subsequently became the original published forms of the test. As these forms were used in the field, problems began to emerge, which, in turn, provided the criteria for revising the test. These criteria which needed to be met included: (a) close comparability of forms for men and women (to keep the forms as equivalent as possible); (b) interesting and varied content (parallel stems may decrease interest and motivation of respondent); (c) first and second pages usable as abbreviated forms (a shorter form may be more attractive to users); (d) selection of some items originally composed for men and for which the original and only available manual was derived from male samples, for example "When I am criticized—" (this balances the use of stems and manuals devised for men and women); (e) selection of the most valid items; (f) avoidance of successions of items that suggest connected answers; (g) the first item on each page sufficiently structured so as to be relatively easy to complete (in hopes of preventing self-consciousness, factual reports, or objections); and (h) the last item on each page should be one that leaves a pleasant or bland aftertaste, that is, does not encourage hostile or self-derogatory completions.

Based on these criteria, new forms were devised for women, men, girls, and boys. With new forms of the test, it was a fundamental next step to revise the original scoring manuals and include samples from male and female subjects, considering the early scoring manuals only included responses from adolescent girls and women. Researchers who had used the earlier forms were requested to share their sample protocols in order to help with revising the scoring manuals. The researchers also took this opportunity to revise the coding for the stages of ego development.

In this revision of the scoring manual, several category titles within each stage were rewritten, more typical examples were chosen for category titles, and because exemplar categories had been used more successfully during the rating procedure, the majority of the descriptive titles were changed to exemplar category titles. Other changes in the format from the earlier version included a more condensed manual, the use of themes that appeared across stages for a given stem to help guide the rater to the pertinent area of the manual, and the "blurbs" for each stage were omitted. The resulting version of the scoring manual is the current published manual for the test.

Table I illustrates the codes assigned by the research teams, and also the corresponding numerical codes used in the representation of the ego development stages, which subsequently are the codes used in the scoring manual for this project. It is important to note that the Delta and Delta/3 codes were subsumed into "2/3" to keep the numerical codes consistent. While there was evidence of finer distinctions between the Delta and Delta/3 stages, these representations were combined because the earlier stages manifest themselves so infrequently with the sentence completion test. It is noted, however, that these earlier distinctions are important for research in the earlier, preconventional stages.

A new sentence completion test form and scoring manual were developed for use in the measure of ego development in children and youths. This was in response to questions raised about the use of the scoring manuals (devised from responses gathered mostly from adults) for rating the forms for boys and girls, as well as the minimum age at which those forms could be used. There is also an available appendix for using the test in translation to other languages. This seems to be a very resourceful tool in expanding the cross-cultural research on ego development. In fact, the test has been translated into 11 different languages in 19 different published studies.

**Table I: Stages of Ego Development and Corresponding Codes**

| Ego Stage | Original Codes | Revised Codes | Current Codes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Impulsive | I-2 | E2 | 2 |
| Self-Protective | Delta; Delta/3 | E3 | 2/3 |
| Conformist | I-3 | E4 | 3 |
| Self-Aware | I-3/4 | E5 | 3/4 |
| Conscientious | I-4 | E6 | 4 |

### Reliability and Validity of the Sentence Completion Test
A robust body of research with several hundred studies utilizing the test as a measurement tool supports the reliability and validity of the test. Two significant reviews describe the results of numerous studies concerning the reliability and validity of the test. Interrater reliability is high for this assessment when using the scoring manuals, with a Cronbach's alpha of .91. Similar results were consistently reported in research publications over a course of 20 years. The validity of ego development theory and measurement can be broken down into construct validity, discriminant validity, and predictive validity. There is considerable empirical support for the construct validity of ego development in regards to the three central tenets of the theory (unitary nature of the ego; the ego representing an integration of the personality characteristics of cognitive functioning, personal and interpersonal awareness, and character development; and the sequentiality of ego stages), as well as support for its relation to external criterion of alternative measures. Discriminant validity for the test indicated that the low correlations between ego development and verbal fluency and intelligence supported that ego development is distinct from those two variables, and it was also found that socioeconomic status and ego development are distinct. There was not enough empirical support to suggest further research into investigating the predictive validity of the test. An earlier review of the reliability and validity of the test presents very similar findings. The results of the review of 20 years of research were summarized as indicating that ego development may be regarded as a complex, but unitary, construct, with the ego developing in a hierarchical, invariant, and sequential manner. It seems safe to conclude that the test is a reliable and valid tool for measuring ego development.

### Ego Development as the Master Trait
Ego development theory and its original measurement tool seem to hold a solid position in the field of personality research. Yet, some ideas have met their share of criticism over the years, such as the use of the term "master trait". The conception of ego development is presented as the master trait in personality. It is contended that this frame provides the other, more specific, fragmented personality traits with their meaning, and it is around ego development that these other traits are organized. The indivisibility of the cognitive, affective and operant components of ego development is another reason given for saying no term less than ego development encompasses all of these phenomena.

Those that question the postulate of ego development as the master trait comment that the complexity of personality is being unjustly reduced to a single continuum and assuming that ego development holds a super-ordinate position in the overall collection of personality traits. It is argued that this leads one to think that all major personality factors could be seen as master traits, for they all condition the expression of the other traits. For example, some have wondered if the sentence completion test really is a measure of ego development or just a measure of differences in intelligence. It seems, though, that intelligence is a factor necessary but not sufficient for ego development.

A summary of 52 correlations between ego level scores and intelligence test scores only found a weighted Pearson correlation of 0.30, indicating that ego development and intelligence are conceptually and functionally distinct concepts. One might ask, then why do the two factors seem so related? It is necessary to be reliant on some kind of internal consistency in the measurement of ego development, and so the scoring and results will tend towards the most easily and reliably judged element, which seems to be the cognitive element. This is also similar to what happened with moral development, in that it was necessary to rely on stressing the cognitive and intellectual components of moral development during measurement. While agreeing that there is no proof of the indivisibility of ego development, there is also a caution against reducing the differences to intelligence only, for a formal notion of logic and cognitive structure will not suffice for an understanding of the contents and constituents of ego development, namely its "essence". There are also examples of individuals who score the same on intelligence tests but have very different levels of ego development, indicating an alternate possibility for another pacer aside from intelligence.

This debate stills begs the question of whether or not there are multiple lines of development, for if one does not believe that there could be a single master trait developing, one would have to research and define the other possible lines of development and discuss the correlations between them. Several lines of development have been mapped, but it is noted that the cognitive line of development seems to be a precursor to all other lines, including the self-line, for what one identifies with is determined by what one can be aware of. Other researchers explored this notion as well, describing cognitive development as affecting personality because what psychologically impacts an individual has to be known by her or him in some cognitive form. A case has been made that in fact the idea of lines of development may reflect an explanatory fiction, rather than an ontological reflection of human development.

In summary, if one is going to scorn the presentation of ego development as a "master trait," then it is necessary to approach the concept of multiple lines of development, which is currently a controversial one. In the study of personality development, are there master traits or are they all the same thing? The holistic and organismic view of the concept of ego development is stood behind, and the significance of the proposal to the field is described as helping to go beyond the fragmentation of trait psychology and to look at personalities as meaningful wholes.

### Relevant Research Areas for Ego Development
Research interest into adult development and organizational change in the workplace has been a catalyst for revisions of the names and stages of ego development as well as new forms of the test. Versions of stage names that are equivalent to the originally generated labels were introduced. A revised form of the test was used to measure organization transformation, and those revisions included decreasing the total number of stems and adding four stems that related directly to the work environment, resulting in a 24-item test.

The concept of ego development was advanced by showing evidence of emerging patterns at the later stages of ego development. One contribution to the concept of ego development was the replacement of the highest stage, Integrated, with two distinct stages, Construct-Aware and Unitive, and new scoring rules were developed for scoring these protocols. The test was also expanded and revised into a new form. This enhanced the original assessment tool in terms of allowing a better capture of professional subjects in organizational contexts. Studies on postautonomous ego development resulted in a modified test, which is the most finely tuned, cost-effective, and validated assessment tool to date for ego development in the field.

Versions of labels for the stage names originally proposed were introduced. The stage names were meant to be less pejorative and more semantically neutral, for example, "Diplomat" instead of "Conformist" which may have a negative undertone. Table II can be referenced to help distinguish the labels used for ego stages.

In closing, it seems useful to mention some of the other areas of interest in which the test has been used: successful organizational transformation; meaning-making in the management field; mature ego development and ego transcendence; transcendental meditation programs and postconventional self-development; optimal adult development and dynamics of growth; advanced ego development among adults; personality change in adulthood; differential patterns of gender-related personality traits; ego development and the transition from adolescence to adulthood; individual differences in personality; sex differences in the course of personality development; ego development in college; adolescent academic achievement; trait models of personality and the Five-Factor Model; counseling theory and model for treatment, including the Integral model; and coping and defense mechanisms.

**Table II: Comparison of Labels Used for Ego Stages**

| Current Labels | Alternative Labels 1 | Alternative Labels 2 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Unitive | Ironist | Unitive |
| Construct-Aware | Alchemist/Magician | Magician |
| Autonomous | Strategist | Strategist |
| Individualistic | Individualist | Individualist |
| Conscientious | Achiever | Achiever |
| Self-Aware | Expert/Technician | Expert |
| Conformist | Diplomat | Diplomat |
| Self-Protective | Opportunist | Opportunist |
| Impulsive | Impulsive | Impulsive |

## Methodology
This section will outline the methodology utilized in constructing a scoring manual for the sentence completion stem, "A good boss—". Sources used in determining this methodology included articles and chapters describing how the original scoring manuals for the test were constructed and revised, the original scoring manual for this stem and an updated version, publications on ego development and how it manifests at each stage, and also personal communication. Five sections delineating the course of construction will be described in detail below: (a) data collection, (b) sorting response items, (c) category definition and organization, (d) writing the manual, and (e) statistics.

### Data Collection
The responses used for this data set came from two different sources, an original manual attempt and a database with thousands of collected responses. Responses for the sentence stems, which included "A good boss—", were collected starting in 1982 and continued until 2000. When the manual attempt was updated in 2008, there were n = 358 responses for "A good boss—" included as examples in the manual. For this project, n = 478 additional responses to the sentence stem were incorporated into the manual, leading to N = 836 total items in the data set.

### Sorting Response Items
In order to easily organize the scored response items, each response was entered into a spreadsheet exactly as it appeared in the item response list, preserving any grammatical errors. All of the available data collected about the respondent was included for each response (age, sex, numeric item score, item score [assigned ego stage], ID#, the total protocol rating, and the total weighted score). Each item was assigned a new identification number, beginning with letters to distinguish the new identification number from the original identification number assigned by the initial researcher who organized the data. The letters were followed with 4 numeric values corresponding to what order the items were entered into the file. This version of the file was termed the "Master" worksheet.

Once all of the available responses were entered into the worksheet, the worksheet was printed out, and each individual response was cut out. The responses were sorted based on their original item scores for ego stage, and utilizing the existing categories in the current version of the scoring manual, each response item was matched to a category title. During this categorical organization, themes and patterns that emerged within as well as between each ego stage were discerned and highlighted so they could be described during manual construction. If new categories seemed to emerge, they were assigned a numerical code (the existing categories were numbered, and the new categories followed in numerical order).

An attempt was made to classify every response into a single category utilizing the sequence of scoring rules from the scoring manual. Rule 1: Match the completion with one of the listed category titles. Rule 2: Where the combination of two or more elements in a compound response generates a more complex level of conception, rate the response one step higher than the highest element. There are two types of compound responses. One type is a pseudo-compound response, which could be a cliché or repetition, but either way it constitutes a similar thought or single banality in common speech. The other compound response is a true compound, which would contain two or more contrasting ideas or alternative perspectives on or aspects of a situation. Rule 3: Where the combination of ideas in a compound response does not generate a higher level of conceptual complexity, rate in the less frequent category or rate in the higher category.

If a simple response or compound response could not be scored using Rules 1-3, then scoring continued in sequence to Rule 4: In the case of a meaningful response, where there is no appropriate category and Rules 2 and 3 do not apply, use the general theory to arrive at a rating. Rule 5: Where the response is omitted or is too fragmentary to be meaningful, it is rated Stage 3 (Opportunist) by default. During this initial sort, "Unassigned" categories for each stage were used if the rating for an item was uncertain. Along with the rules, an additional rule was applied that pertained solely to scoring responses at category 5/6, 1: Matrices (not merely lists) have to include 3 or more ideas from multiple perspectives, subject areas, and levels of analysis.

At this point, the numerical codes for the assigned category number for each response were added to the "Master" worksheet for each response, in a column titled "Category #". An extra column was also added for comments about the category assignment (if uncertain about category, if response might need to be moved to a different stage, etc.). This "Master" worksheet was copied into a new worksheet within the same file, and the "sort" function was used to physically organize the items by ego development stage. This worksheet was entitled "Ego stage sort." Following this step, one new worksheet was created for each stage, labeling each worksheet with its corresponding stage name. For each of these worksheets, the responses were sorted once again by category, using the numerical code for each category (the number "888" was used for items that were "Unassigned").


---


# Rewritten Content

After sorting the responses, the categories were organized within each developmental level and across all developmental stages.

## Category Definition and Organization

In scoring the protocols, the most difficult distinction for raters to make is between responses that share similar content to the adjacent stages. Therefore, providing a cross-reference for a category within one stage that may share a theme with categories at adjacent stages will assist the rater in distinguishing between stages for a single response. The original scoring manual for this stem has cross-references already in place, but it was necessary to confirm these and to determine if any new themes emerged across developmental stages that needed cross-referencing. A data table was created with the developmental stages across the top as the column headings, and within each column, the category title was listed. From this arrangement, any new themes were determined and confirmation was made that the cross-references from the original manual were still reflected and were the closest fit for efficient distinction purposes. If categories had similar content to adjacent stages, those category titles were given a cross-reference denoted by the stage number and category number for that adjacent stage. For example, if a category from Stage 3 had similar content to a category in Stage 3/4, the cross-reference might be: (3/4, 12). This would direct raters to Stage 3/4, category 12 to help them decide which stage the response best relates to. A brief description of the differing theoretical content between the similar categories was listed for many of the cross-references.

Based on communication with the framework developers, it would be helpful to determine if the categories within each stage follow some internal logic and then list these categories in the manual based on that internal logic. For organization purposes, the most common categories (based on frequency of responses) were listed at the beginning of the category sequence. After that, a determination was made as to whether or not the categories could be logically ordered. Utilizing the data table from the cross-references, it became evident that themes were emerging within and between the stages. These included themes of Relationships (R), Communication (C), Behavior (B), and Growth/Development (G). A column was then added to the data chart (entitled – "Internal Logic Category"), and an internal logic category was designated for each of the category titles within the stages. The categories were placed in the following order: (a) first, the most frequent categories overall (containing 10% or more of the responses within a category); (b) then categories grouped by internal logic category, and sorted from the most to least frequent; (c) and finally, those categories which were not included in the major themes (R, C, B, G) were designated as "Other" (O), and unless they contained 10% or more of the total responses for the stage, these categories were listed at the end of the stage. After determining the internal logic, all categories were renumbered within each stage, in numerical order.

## Writing the Manual

After sorting the responses, assigning each response to a category, and organizing the categories, a draft of the scoring manual was written. The item scoring manual included a "blurb", which is a short description including useful topics for the raters. The different types of responses that the stem "A good boss—" generates from responders across all developmental stages were described (based on the essence of each stage according to developmental theory). The blurb also described factors for the rater to keep in mind as they scored individual item responses, including any difficulties the rater may encounter when trying to distinguish the developmental stage of a response for this stem.

Following the blurb, each developmental level was listed in the following order: Impulsive, Opportunist, Diplomat, Expert, Achiever, Pluralist, Strategist, Magician, and Unitive. After listing the stage name, a short description was written about the general characteristics of this stage, any information relevant to adult development and meaning-making at this stage, and an explanation of the sequence logic for the categories.

Next, the categories were listed in numerical order (based on LC#), followed by example responses for each category. If a category title was exemplar (based on prototypic responses) it began with the numerical code, followed by a dash and a lowercase letter. For category titles that were descriptive in nature, they started with the numerical code, used a capital letter for the first word and described the included responses. Category titles were immediately followed by cross-references, if applicable, and asterisks were applied for the common category titles- using one asterisk for a category that included 10% or more of the responses in that developmental stage. Previous research also utilized asterisks to denote popular responses, although their asterisks represented different distribution percentages, for example, they used one asterisk to denote categories that included at least 2% of the responses and two asterisks to denote categories that comprised about 5% or more of the sample set. For this project, the sample set was not large enough to incorporate 2% and 5% for the cutoff levels.

The examples of responses that follow the category title were taken verbatim from actual protocol responses and typed in italics. The responses from the updated version of the scoring manual were included and differentiated by bold type font from the items in the project data set (in regular font). All items from the data set were included under their respective categories, because listing more responses, as opposed to fewer responses, will help the rater to make a more informed decision. The exception was omitting repeated, identical responses within a category.

## Statistics

Within the spreadsheet file, basic statistics were performed for each developmental level. The responses for each category were counted, and a percentage was determined for each category, based on how many responses fall into this category compared to the total number of responses for that developmental stage. These percentages were also used in determining the sequence of the category titles within a developmental stage (highest frequency categories listed first). An additional distribution graph was constructed which compared the range of responses among the developmental stages for "A good boss—" and a range of scores from a mixed sample set, showing the distribution of developmental stages within a general adult population.

## Results

### Item 12

**A Good Boss—**

How one views self in relation to other people can be explored through a variety of sentence stems, and with the stem, "A good boss—", it is possible to understand how an individual may view a relationship with someone in a supervisory or leadership position. Researchers first compiled data from this stem in early studies, and since then, it seems to have added a valuable dimension of work relationships to the study of development. This scoring manual was recently updated, affirming, "'A good boss—' has proven to be one of the most liked stems by professionals because everyone can relate to it and unlike 'a good father,' it does not have a personal (and overly Freudian) ring". It is also described how it is likely that most adults have had experience with bosses or may even desire to become a boss themselves.

The varieties of completions for this stem exhibit a wide distribution among the developmental stages, ranging from Opportunist (Stage 2/3) to Unitive (Stage 6). The topics of the responses include: social interactions, benefits of interpersonal relationships, conventional and differentiated traits of a good boss, communication, the atmosphere created at work, growth and development (of the individual and/or the company), efficiency and effectiveness, what behaviors a good boss might engage in, as well as many others.

One factor to keep in mind while scoring is that the quality of personal relationships with others seems to develop from dependency to allowing for a degree of autonomy and independence and even further toward mutuality and interdependence as one moves to the later stages. Also, it seems that the meaning of work evolves through the stages from a banal activity, to a means of achieving one's individual goals, onward to a more encompassing medium for growth, development and expression of one's beliefs and values, and finally as part of the global realm of existence. Additionally, researchers caution that we may get more "canned or rehearsed" completions even at the later stages, for people may provide quotes from leadership books and gurus. It is stated, "It is therefore important to be alert to 'espoused' theory and do get a sense of how much the respondent actually lives the experience of a boss."

In order to assist the rater in traversing the scoring manual, categories have been arranged in a certain order within each stage. Categories marked with an asterisk contain 10% or more of the responses within the stage, and these items are listed first among the stage categories. The remainder of the categories are grouped by overall theme (Relationships, Communication, Behavior, and Growth/Development), and then sorted based on which theme is most dominant for that stage. Categories not included in the four major themes for this stem are designated as "Other", and unless they contain 10% or more of the responses for that stage, "Other" categories are listed last. To help distinguish responses across adjacent stages, cross-references to similar categories in other stages are listed in parentheses next to category titles. Some cross-references also include a brief reason for distinction, if available. Item responses in this scoring manual include items from the updated version of the scoring manual. Those responses can be differentiated with their bold type font from the items in the project data set (in regular font).

### Themes:

**Relationships (Stage 2 – Stage 5):** The topic of relationship between boss and employee is first noticeable from Impulsive with "looks after me", then Opportunist, when a good boss "stays out of my way", and evolves to "gets on well with his staff" at Diplomat, "knows each person individually" at Expert, and to "knows his people and can relate to them" (Achiever). At later stages, interpersonal relationships have an essence of being valuable in their own right and being mutually interdependent (Pluralist and Strategist).

**Communication (Stage 3 – Stage 4/5):** This theme spans from Diplomat to Pluralist, in which it emerges as a theme of how a boss communicates with her/his staff. In the earlier stages, a good boss listens and talks to the employees, but later listens and understands, takes all factors into account, and becomes more receptive and engaging, allowing for input from employees.

**Behavior (Stage 3 – Stage 5):** The behavior of a good boss is another theme within this stem, emerging at Diplomat and persisting through Strategist. At earlier stages, behavior seems to have a more individual connection, for example, a good boss is helpful (Diplomat), or delegates (Expert), and can even begin to engage in contrasting behaviors at Achiever. At later stages, behavior seems to have a more encompassing impact on general systems, and psychological causation comes into play (Pluralist and Strategist).

**Growth/Development (Stage 3/4 – Stage 5):** Emerging in the middle of the conventional stages at Expert, a good boss facilitates growth by making life easier and being reliable when you need him/her. At Achiever, a good boss will motivate employees and support them in their efforts to learn and experiment, and even continues to learn her/himself. This develops into a good boss fostering growth and tolerating human nature and mistakes (Pluralist) and even further to a concern for autonomy (Strategist).

### Stage 2 / Impulsive

No responses in the data set were assigned a rating at this stage. Some of the characteristics of responses at this stage might be a sense of being dependent ("neediness"), simple statements, and hostility if one cannot get what one wants immediately. Previous item manuals based on data for "A good mother—" and "My father—" sentence stems were used to hypothesize on possible reactions to supervision relationships at this Stage.

1. -- is good, nice
*is sweet, nice*
*is a dead one*
*looks after me (dependent)*

### Stage 2/3 / Opportunist

At this stage, adults may seem egocentric and preoccupied with their own immediate wants and needs. It is possible that they might be opportunistic and aggressive at going after what they want, or be self-protective in the presence of a perceived threat. Others are seen as competitors for goods, attention, and power. Needs rules impulses at this stage, and rules may be seen as a loss of freedom. Language at this level includes simple dichotomies (good/bad, right/wrong). Because the first four categories each contained 10% or more of the total stage responses, the categories for this stage have been listed in order of most to least frequent categories.

### Stage 3 / Diplomat

The Diplomat has moved from a "me against them" to an "us against them" mentality, and norms rules impulses. Socially accepted behavior and a respect for the rules (obedience and loyalty) dominate behavior at this stage as the Diplomat seeks status and membership into a group. Appearances are also salient, and negative feelings and expressions are avoided. Responses may have an exaggerated positive affect and may include clichés and stereotypical phrases. The order of responses in this stage is most frequent categories, followed by "Relationships", "Communication", and "Other" themed categories, respectively.

### Stage 3/4 / Expert

A focus on likeness at the Diplomat stage has turned into a focus on individual differences, as Experts begin to assert more of their own needs and wants that were suppressed for the sake of the group in the earlier stage. An awareness of general traits in others emerges, and an emphasis on expertise, procedure, high moral standards, and efficiency surfaces. Experts may feel that they have it all figured out and are entitled to impose their views on others, which may come across in responses as "shoulds," qualifications and conditions, and use of psychological vocabulary and explanations. Unlike individuals at later stages, Experts cannot prioritize among options or synthesize/weigh out several possibilities. Craft logic begins to rule norms, experts in the field may be quoted, and clichéd responses may surface. Categories in this stage are sorted beginning with the most frequent categories, and then "Behavior", "Relationships", and "Communication" themed categories, respectively.

### Stage 4 / Achiever

Whereas the Expert seemed to rely on authority or received knowledge, the Achiever seems skeptical. System effectiveness rules craft logic, and Achievers might obtain effectiveness and truth through knowledge, the mutual expression of ideas, and the scientific method. With an expanded perspective, Achievers add a dimension of linear time to their concerns and seem very goal-oriented and action-driven. Achieving goals, planning for the future, and evaluating oneself in order to improve are some of the preoccupations at this stage. Achievers express complexities in the form of contingencies, contradictions, and inconsistencies. The most frequent categories are listed first, then "Behavior", "Relationships", "Growth/Development", and "Communication" themed categories, are listed, respectively.
