### Stage 4/5 / Pluralist

At this stage, Pluralists begin taking multiple perspectives into account ("everything is relative"), and this relativism rules the single system logic that may have emerged from the scientific method in the last stage. A more holistic, body/mind connection, being, feeling, here and now essence has been transformed from the causality, goals, action, and future-oriented drive from the Achiever stage. Interpersonal relationships are seen as mutually rewarding, and there is an awareness and tolerance for individual differences. There is discussion of how interpretation plays into meaning-making, as interpretation depends on the position of the observer. Context has become more important than content, and the Pluralist sees her/himself as self-in-relationship, interacting with a larger system. Categories are organized in this stage with the most frequent categories first, followed by "Relationships" and "Growth/Development" themed categories, respectively.

1. *-- fosters an environment conducive to help can grow (4,2)
*leads others by creating the conditions where each individual person will express his own talents to the best of his ability*
*creates the space for people to give their best and develop on their own accord.*
*leads others by creating the conditions where each individual person will express his own talents to the best of his ability*

# Stage 5 / Strategist

An expanded perspective allows individuals at the Strategist stage to understand their experiences within the context of their entire lifetime and their constructed worldview. While individuals at the previous stage can perceive this complexity, they are unable to take charge of it. In contrast, Strategists recognize complex, long-term trends and systemic patterns, enabling them to navigate these intricacies and assume leadership. Interpersonal relationships are viewed as interdependent, and core principles such as acceptance, justice, tolerance, and dignity for all take precedence over relativism. Strategists also acknowledge the need for autonomy in others and are deeply concerned with self-fulfillment and psychological causation. The responses for this stage are categorized and presented in order of their frequency.

## 1. Complex Psychological Causation and Mutual Influence

A good boss affects the morale of his staff and if they are happy this gratifies the boss and so the whole operation benefits. Such a person is someone who causes the right things to happen. This is one with whom I can have an intimate, gamefree relationship and above all I can trust, whilst for others maybe needs to be approachable and fair. A good boss is someone who can remain open, develop good relationships based on trust and respect and who will bring out the best in her/his subordinates, peers and superiors. This individual isn't really a "boss" at all, but rather a coach, a mentor, a friend, a parent, a sage - and all in a nice way.

A good boss listens to, solicits input from, and communicates information to his/her employees as well as keeping empathy, human, and a feeling of worthiness and teamwork in the workplace. A good "boss" is what I once was and what I hope to be again. Or, a good boss is able to nurture and direct his people in fair and effective ways, and is on to learning from his employees. This is one who with an ethic of service to his team questioningly seeks ways to add value to their work. A good boss has integrity, principles and love; and helps direct reports achieve their fullest on behalf of the enterprise.

This person is provocative and challenging, helps you to hold to what you believe in and see the wider issues whilst giving you room to be the best you can. A good boss has integrity, is straight with you, and both challenges you to do your best and collaborates with you to get the best out of you. This individual knows and treats employees and coworkers with a sense of balance between job's needs, the bosses needs, the employees needs and all their place in the firmament. A good boss believes in what she does, believes it serves a purpose, and empowers the folks who work for her.

A good boss is not too attached to his/her own agenda, but can deal with personal relationships, strategical goals, firm decision-making and analysis in a holistic and flexible way. This person leads by example and is the first to wade in. She is attentive in order to recognize and call on other's individual strengths and help them find ways to compensate for weaknesses, take personal responsibility. A good boss takes the time to listen, teach by example, is not afraid to challenge the status quo, and acts with a vision of quality first.

A good boss is a wonderful thing to have. It can be inspiring and enriching. It also helps having work be an extension of the self rather than just a necessary evil. This individual can recognize the daily routine and also more broad changes in and out of the office and work to motivate people within those changes; should be adaptable and open to change but not so adaptable that the office's mission is completely obscured. A good boss is one who can nurture and unfold his staff for the benefit of themselves and the organization that employs them.

This person stands her ground, gives positive affirming feedback whenever possible, constantly challenges and supports employees to develop their capabilities and skills, and always has her eye on the long-term vision of the organization. A good boss is able to balance company and staff priorities, whilst finding a way through the chaos to create a climate in which work is rewarding and successful. This is very rare! A good boss does not pretend to be the boss, yet still relates to his or her team as fellow human beings.

A good boss has a clear sense of self and a vision of a future state- both which are compatible with colleagues, co-workers and with the organization - and which are conveyed to others through example, not words. It is hard to generalize. I think one who ?? his staff and creates enthusiasm for common purpose (both individual and business growth) and drive to put together. A good boss works for the good of his company by way of its component parts - the people that make up that company, and within the environment in which the company is set.

## 2. Concern for Autonomy in Others Without Relinquishing the Role as Leader

A good boss keeps lines of communication open, and provides directions for activities without impinging on the employees own abilities and desire to direct his own activities. This person has a clear vision and purpose for the company but offers it without enforcing it upon his people. A good boss knows what needs to be done, gets it done, and still is able to see where changes are needed and makes improvements-all with everyone’s endorsement and contributions.

This individual is willing and has the skills to work others on a vision of how things could and should ideally be, to work with and support others in achieving these goals. A good boss respects his employees, has earned their trust, and demonstrates his confidence in them frequently allowing maximum opportunity for growth. This is someone who is wise enough to know when to move in and help and when to let go and leave others to learn for themselves.

A good boss knows how to listen to her/his employees and how to visualize progress and communicate it to her/his employees and integrate his visions with their needs. This person should be "tough" and "sensitive" a the same time - he should push his subordinates, be tough and aggressive to achieve the necessary goals within limits, yet at the same time he has to maintain an underlying sensitivity to be aware of the needs, desires and incentives of the employees. A good boss keeps lines of communication open, and provides direction for activities without impinging on the employees own abilities and desire to direct their own activities.

This individual empowers his/her subordinates to stretch themselves to do their best work and to have input and some control in their work and organization, while not relinquishing his/her own leadership position. A good boss is someone who is able to accept the creativity and independence of other people and to motivate and support them to develop themselves to their highest potential. This person provides guidance when necessary but permits his/her employees the opportunity to make own decisions and to maintain responsibility in their assigned work.

A good boss is a divine one - rare, but when you find one you only need a little at a time to get the full benefit of the riches within. This individual provides just enough structure and specificity to give a blueprint for success, provides enough latitude so when people accomplish things they know they did it themselves, and provides enough blessings and support so people can put their whole selves into their work without fear of reprisal. A good boss is one that gives me operational headroom and mutual respect, recognizes my commitment and runs interference in my career pursuits. He/she also acts as a coach, mentor, medic, wailing wall and leader.

# Stage 5/6 / Magician

While the Strategist still endeavors to appear reasonable and mature, their sense of self remains anchored around a "separate self." The Magician stage marks the beginning of recognizing this "separate self" as the central processing unit, noticing its clever attempts at self-preservation by projecting maturity to others. At this stage, the distinction between the "knower" and the "known" begins to blur, accompanied by increased access to feelings, dreams, bodily states, and intuitions. The constructed nature of reality and the complex matrices of existence become evident, which can also lead to anxiety and frustration. Magicians appreciate the need of others to make sense of life within their own capacities, possessing insight into the complex dynamics of people's lives through a long-term, whole-integrated focus. There remains a hope for the Magician to unlearn the automatic responses of language habits and the ingrained habits of mind and heart. The categories in this stage are organized according to established frameworks for this developmental level.

## 1. Complex Matrix of Content

This category involves a panoramic view rather than linear lists. It includes an array of contrasting possibilities, reasons, and responses provided in an attempt to approximate and express the complexity of one's insights, thoughts, feelings, and observations, which can sometimes appear abstract, distanced, or hyper-rational.

A good boss is one who is learning day by day and moment by moment, who encourages this kind of learning in others, who can also adapt to unique people and situations, and who fosters the inner and outer development and those she or he leads. This person can measure vision, action and strategy against one another and include those beneath him in the formulation of all, sometimes as a follower. They can see the patterns unfolding over time, see now as a sacred gate to possible futures while honoring seeing the system as an aspect of the largest system. Overcome the stagnation in success.

A good boss is one who exercises leadership by mobilizing others to solve difficult issues, being mindful of how best to position him/herself with the right balance of challenge and support to further the contribution and development of the people working for and with him/her. This individual may utilize technology and total integrity, usually hires people more skilled than him/herself, surfs the economic/cultural waves, and trusts his/her intuition when wise to do so. A good boss genuinely respects and values the people who he/she "bosses" and supports their development, creativity and initiative in ways that are appropriate to each individual, within the context of the organization and larger purposes of existence.

This person has leadership qualities of vision and direction-setting coupled with skills in planning, doing and communicating; as well as a sense of responsibility for and the talent to support the development of diverse individuals and teams. A good boss responds to the needs of his people and balances this with the business needs he/she has taken on; she/he engenders an atmosphere of trust and comradeship, and an understanding of what has to be achieved; he/she gets the right balance between hierarchy, collaboration and autonomy; she/he has an eye out for the future and the meaning of the work in a wider context, and assists his/her team/s to connect to these things also.

A good boss understands that the responsibilities of a work provider range from time structuring to a carer and nurturer of the souls; he is these and he holds the boundaries; he listens, and helps remove blockages to growth. This individual cares about his/her employees by listening to their ideas, concerns and assists in their development to grow within a role, then within the organization, and finally their body, mind and soul. A good boss is able to unleash the potential and abilities of staff by creating a situation in which they choose willingly to live of their best, having a clear understanding of goals, roles and their unique combination, within a supportive, challenging and ultimately liberating environment.

## 2. Exploration and Evaluation of Intrapsychic and Interpersonal Dynamics (Habits of Heart)

This category encompasses a wide range of personal thoughts, feelings, concerns, and deep insights into one's own and others' psychological functioning and interdependence. It reflects an awareness of the ambiguities, simultaneous benefits and costs, and double binds inherent in human relationships, as well as an understanding of the stages and processes of identity formation.

A good boss sees the brilliance, capability and potential in her employees, while confronting them with careful and growthful truths about their actual performance, all the while holding to the la. A good boss is made, not born, they say… so if we are to be of service, are we ourselves not the first "organization" that we must master? A good boss is a gift to be enjoyed, savored and an opportunity to learn, although such a gift may also be a limitation to anyone who aspires to run their own business, be their own boss, or author their own life.

This person embodies many characteristics that positively influence his ability to manage his subordinates, or manage with his subordinates, but an exceptional boss "challenges" his subordinates to develop their talents and weaknesses, and openly appreciates their value to the organization individually and as a team. A good boss recognizes and capitalizes on opportunities to coach and transform others, knows when to clown, when to threaten punitive action, when to be open, expresses both doubt and certainty and invites feedback and input on her own behavior goals.

A good boss listens; puts subordinates at ease, laughs and gives perspective (the "bigger picture") to help subordinates shape the details of his work. He worries most about the quality, motivation and development of his staff rather than the details of what they are doing. This is someone who is generous about revealing aspects of themselves (including insecurities, emotions, formative life events) as part of really getting to know their teams very well. Combine this with a high achiever with an ability to reward and create an environment of enjoyment and possibilities and you have the sort of role model that I would seek to emulate and learn from. They...

A good boss is one who seeks his/her employees as capable individuals, who treats them with respect, who appreciates good work and rewards it, who understand that cooperation and prosperity are intertwined. This individual symbolizes energy and movement in positive directions, and inspires others to learn and grow by providing them with freedom, challenge and love.

## 3. Exploration and Evaluation of Habits of Mind

This category shows evidence of observing one's own thinking processes and thought habits, such as automatic judging, circular reasoning, infinite regress, and defensive mind games. It includes an awareness of existential and logical paradoxes, commentary on various levels of one's own awareness or the felt lack thereof, and an intellectual understanding of the nature of polar opposites as necessitating each other.

A good boss is probably not the phrase I would use: 'A bosses B' sounds simplistic and foolish. A good leader is someone who has vision et is utterly present; who engenders respect yet is also vulnerable; who leads apparently effortlessly by their own commitment and open struggle.

## 4. Reference to Constructed Nature of Reality

This category involves specific references to and questioning of underlying assumptions, multiple perceptions, frames of reference, paradigms, and the process of meaning-making. It is explicit about the multiple, multi-layered, and changing meanings of words and definitions, demonstrating an awareness of the constructed nature of self and reality.

A good boss is a judgment from a point of view (i.e. what is good?); at this moment I feel myself a "good boss" if I can be/act in the spirit of Lao Tsu, wherein a 'good' leader has such finely honed sensitivity, and is so invisible that he seems to do nothing special, yet accomplishes goals in a way that "People say 'we did it!' " A good boss is a term that takes on new “bells and whistles” during this period of heightened technological and economic dominance; however, the primary concern for addressing the complex interplay of purposefulness and relatedness have remained similar since the time of the Greeks, Romans, Indians and Chinese.

A good boss is in the eye of the beholder, where the eye may be more than one "eye" (the subordinate, the peer, the boss him/herself, the superior, etc.) It remains relative until it is fixed in time and space by one or more than one. I don't like to judge or take too strong a position: It always depends where I am in my own evolution. Some say you need a "bad" boss to appreciate a "good" one - . If I were to say "ideally" (with my present state of consciousness) - a "good" boss would be like a good coach, friend, etc.: somebody who takes you where you are [continued on back of page] and puts you consciously or unconsciously on a learning curve, so that you become aware, accept it for yourself, have strategies of action and, finally, take action in the areas of development, tasks, achievement, leadership, etc. (here again, I said as short as I could - there are many things to say).

A good boss is an odd phrase; it may contain a fundamental epistemological flaw. "A good boss" - is most often defined by someone who "doesn't have one" rarely found from that frame of reference, and probably somewhat invisible if she or he (the boss) is really good. "A good boss" is a relative judgment - it varies in time, situation and observer. Lao Tsu says that empties hearts and fills bellies, but he wants running a cardiac surgery unit. A good boss is always a subjective assessment. A good boss is rare as hen's teeth. (Whatever we mean by good?) A good boss is a contradiction in terms.

# Stage 6 / Unitive

Unitive individuals experience a profound sense of belongingness to the creative ground. Unlike the preceding stage, consciousness is no longer felt as a burden. At the Unitive stage, individuals let go of the unattainable and recognize that complete understanding is an ultimate illusion. Responses at this stage tend to convey a greater sense of peace and acceptance compared to the often tormented responses of Magicians. Completions to the sentence stem can be remarkably simple or take the form of an all-encompassing kaleidoscope response that eloquently bridges numerous paradoxes and levels, sometimes manifesting as a creative metaphor. Unitive individuals are capable of cherishing human connections with those who are vastly different from them, feeling a sense of oneness. Elements of the fundamental dilemma of the human condition frequently surface in Unitive responses. The categories in this stage are organized according to established frameworks for the Unitive level.

## 1. Wide Range of Thought on Human Relationships

This category reflects a perspective where the self is not the center. It includes unique positive affect and gratitude for people exactly as they are, alongside an appreciation of their shadow sides and struggles as mirrors reflecting all of humanity.

## 2. Expression of High Tolerance and Acceptance

This category demonstrates an acceptance of the self and the world "as is." It shows an openness to life, change, process, rhythm, flux, and self-in-transformation. There is a letting go of the need to judge, accompanied by signs of non-attachment and the embracing of polar opposites.

## 3. Non-Trivial Expression of Universal Connectedness

This category involves expressions of the self as part of a larger world, humankind, history, or the manifestation of the creative process.

A good boss is each and all of her employees and employers. A good boss is someone who is honest with everyone in an ongoing routine way regarding praise as well as criticism, who sees and conveys the big picture of what's going on and how the parts fit together, who conveys a sense of meaning and purpose to the work, and, at the same time, sees people in the context of their whole lives rather than just their work roles. (Tall order!)

## 4. Fundamental Thoughts and Feelings About the Human Condition

This category encompasses thoughts on the wonder of being, creation and destruction, joy and suffering, life and death, sexuality, nature, global concerns, conscience, and consciousness.

A good boss is maybe whoever arises in the moment to create the conditions for the next step forward, who sees what needs to happen most deeply and offers the courage to manifest it. A good boss does not fear to lay bare his or her own soul while reaching for an awareness of others. A good boss can be a 'boss' if he needs to, or a coach, leader, shaman, or disappear. A good boss is a boss God, that is, uses authority without presence of self but rather Self and the resulting Wisdom and Compassion and Flow enriches the field and otherwise becomes invisible as each person tends to translate experience in a healthier manner.

## 5. Unitive Ability

This category highlights the ability to shift focus effortlessly between near and far (geographically, historically, developmentally), the mundane and the sublime, the somber and the ridiculous, and now and eternity. This shifting occurs between different states of consciousness without attachment to any single view or position. It often features a playful, light touch or illuminating metaphors, akin to "seeing a world in a grain of sand."

A good boss is someone who knows how to balance authority and freedom, who is able to embody and/or give voice to the deeper mission of the organization, while seeing it in the context of society, and can do this in service of a deeper Reality. A good boss is a clown, is a leader, a decider, a guide, an evoker, a motivator, a foil, a mistake-maker and visionary for her employees and company, sees what might be or experiments to find what might be. A good boss makes rules, a good manager sets standards, a good executive creates systems, but a good leader simply "is."

# Discussion

When the initial scoring manual for this sentence stem was constructed, there were no existing categories available to assist in sorting the data. It appears that other sentence stems from similar assessment tools, along with the underlying theory of ego development, were utilized to predict potential categories. The manual also incorporated descriptions from research literature on supervision and management to suggest what characteristics might define a good boss. The task of creating a scoring manual from scratch for a new assessment tool designed to measure a novel theoretical concept is a formidable undertaking. The current project involved creating a scoring manual based on pre-existing attempts and templates, which offered a less complicated task, though it was not without its challenges. This discussion explores the process of creating the scoring manual, the inherent biases in the scoring process, the personal growth and development experienced during the project, and the limitations and future directions of this research.

## Process of Creating the Scoring Manual

A fundamental step before engaging in this task was to become thoroughly familiar with the concept of ego development. Several sources were referenced, including original manuals, manual revisions, and literature detailing later stage distinctions. This preparation provided an opportunity to calibrate responses to specific stages and then utilize established scoring rules to further assign responses to categories within each stage. Examples of sample responses are provided to illustrate the scoring methods and observed trends, adhering to the scientific practice of clearly marking the chosen stage and category and providing a rationale for the choice.

The procedure for rating single stem responses begins with foundational ground rules: Every response must be rated as a whole, focusing on the level of meaning—what the person intended to say. Deep-level inferences about intent are inappropriate; responses should be taken at face value. Snap judgments are to be avoided, but excessive rumination does not improve rating accuracy.

With these ground rules established, the first step in sorting responses was to apply the rule of matching the completion with one of the listed category titles. For instance, if a category title was "-is respected, looked up to," a response such as "earns the respect of his workforce" would be a match. Cross-references were checked when necessary. Attempts were made to satisfy this matching rule for every response in the data set. If a match was unavailable or the response was compound, subsequent scoring rules were employed.

When the combination of two or more elements in a compound response generated a more complex level of conception, the response was rated one step higher than its highest element. For example, a response combining elements of listening, understanding, providing direction, and helping with development might generate a more complex conception than any single element alone, warranting a higher stage rating.

If a compound response did not generate a more complex concept, it was rated in the less frequent category or the higher category. For instance, a response combining the delegation of authority with supporting people to develop might not generate a higher level of conceptual complexity than the individual elements, so it would be rated in the higher of the applicable categories.

Many responses did not provide an exact match to a category title and could not be rated using the rules for compound responses. In these cases, the general theory of ego development was used to arrive at a rating. This was particularly common at the later stages, where responses are often unique and complex, making it difficult to find matches to exemplar category titles. Guidance from research on postconventional development and scoring was heavily relied upon for applying theory to responses in the later stages.

The postconventional "systems" stages included responses indicating a shift to a more systems-centered perspective, compared to the individual perspectives of earlier stages. Responses might reflect a new ability to take multiple perspectives and tolerate human nature and mistakes. For example, a response stating that interpretations of a "good boss" depend on the individuals and situations concerned embodies the perspective that interpretation depends on the observer's position. This response would be rated based on general theory rather than an exact category match.

For the postconventional "unitary" stages, responses reflected a recognition of the constructed nature of reality and a realization of a nondual universe. Research distinguishing separate stages at this level was referenced to categorize responses. A response demonstrating a perspective at multiple levels of depth—from the boss to the employees, to the individual, within the context of the organization and the larger purposes of existence—would be categorized under a complex matrix of content, distinguishing it from linear lists found at earlier stages.

If ego development theoretical concepts could not assist in rating a response, a default rating was considered as a last resort for responses that were omitted or too fragmentary to be meaningful. Responses that were difficult to place, such as those that seemed to avoid answering the stem or were too fragmentary, were assigned this default rating.

During the sorting process, new patterns and categories emerged within stage responses. Titles for these new categories were chosen to best represent the included items. It is crucial to perform microvalidation on these new categories to determine their robustness with an increased sample set. No categories were established without the presence of a cluster of items; the creation of the scoring manual was driven entirely by the response data.

## Overall Trends

Themes characteristic of responses for this sentence stem, such as relationships, communication, behavior, and growth/development, were useful for examining overall trends related to ego development. Two primary trends materialized: a movement from preconventional to postconventional functioning, and a shift in the frame of reference from integration to differentiation.

The trend of integration and differentiation manifests as two patterns throughout ego development. One pattern involves experiencing differentiation during alternating stages, as individuals focus on their differences from the previous stage. These stages of differentiation may also involve experiencing tension. Conversely, the alternating stages experience less tension and seem more balanced because the frame of reference involves identifying with others who are externally alike, like-minded, like-principled, or like-spirited.

These trends are evident in the context of interpersonal relationships, which change throughout ego development, with their salience holding different meanings at each stage. In the preconventional stages, where symbiotic interactions and impulsivity occur, relationships are viewed as a source of supply. This source can be of a dependent nature or viewed as fulfilling a need or posing a threat. The pattern of integration to differentiation is visible as individuals move from symbiotic needs to differentiating from those relationships.

As the transition is made from preconventional to conventional stages, reasoning becomes more logical, and achieving independence becomes a primary goal. Interpersonal relationships and acceptance are important, and individuals begin to differentiate by distancing themselves from the group while aligning with experts. Relationships are valued if they are productive, effective, and encompass moral standards. The frame of reference develops from concrete to abstract, and finally to formal operational functioning, which is the target stage for most adults in the Western world. At this stage, relationships thrive if they are supportive, like-minded, and assist in reaching goals. The vast majority of the adult population scores at these preconventional and conventional stages.

The shift to a postconventional frame of reference involves a transition from logical to holistic reasoning. At the initial postconventional "systems" stages, individuals realize that meaning depends on the observer's position, and the ability to take multiple perspectives becomes a tool for making sense of the world. Context becomes more important than content. Differentiation occurs as individuals recognize that their interpretations are unique. A good boss is seen as being aware of these multiple perspectives and their role in relationships. The trend continues as individuals integrate conflicting perspectives into a like-principled framework where systems are interdependent.

Finally, in the postconventional "unitary" stages, the separate sense of self becomes an object of awareness, and the idea that everything is a human-made construct is recognized. Individuals may experience tension with this new metasystematic view of reality as they differentiate from the previous stage. As integration occurs at the final stage, individuals become more simple and succinct, experiencing a non-dual existence as part of the creative ground. Responses at this level break through conventional definitions, reflecting a universal connectedness with others.

Throughout the spectrum of ego development, individuals vacillate between integration and differentiation from one stage to the next. They evolve from an integrated, symbiotic existence in early stages, to a differentiated, independent human in conventional stages, and finally to a postconventional stage where they are once again integrated into a new sense of oneness and belongingness.

## Statistics

The final sorting of all responses into categories allowed for statistical analysis. The number of responses within each category was tallied, including additional items from updated manuals to increase the sample size. Duplicate responses were included for statistical purposes but omitted in the final scoring manual. The percentage of total responses for each stage contained within specific categories was calculated to identify the most frequent categories.

It is important to note that as more data is collected, the frequency status of categories may shift. The statistical analysis also determined the distribution of total responses among the different ego development stages. For a sentence stem to be useful in measuring ego development, it must elicit responses across the range of stages.

The distribution of responses spanned from the earliest to the most advanced stages, with a peak at the stage representing formal operational functioning, which contained 43.8% of the total responses in the data set. This pattern of distribution is similar to that reported for a mixed adult population, although slightly shifted. The comparison data represents total protocol rating scores from complete sentence completion tests, not just a single stem. This similarity provides evidence of internal validity for the sentence stem, as it produces a data distribution comparable to the assessment tool as a whole. Further validity and reliability results were beyond the scope of the project.

### Bias in Scoring Responses

The scoring manuals used in this framework are empirically validated measures that rely on response data to craft the scoring guidelines. However, scoring bias can still impact the validity and reliability of the scoring process. The framework developers highlight potential sources of bias that raters might encounter during scoring. These sources of bias, which can stem from the responses themselves or be intrinsic to the rater, are examined below.

One characteristic of responses that appeared to be more of a distraction than a true scoring bias was the presence of typing errors. The responses in the dataset had already been transcribed by another individual, and some typing errors may have originated during that transcription. These responses were subsequently re-typed into a spreadsheet, potentially introducing further typing errors. There did not appear to be any instances where the rating of a response was questioned due to a typing or spelling error. In some cases, as noted by the framework developers, spelling errors originating in the written responses may influence a rater to score the response at an earlier stage.

The framework developers contend that bias regarding word choice should be avoided because it is not the specific words chosen that determine the developmental stage, but rather the evidence of the thought behind those words. They emphasize that the entire response is the only safe index of the subject's meaning, and raters must operate solely within the level of discourse of meanings. During the initial sorting of responses, a negative reaction was experienced when the pronoun "he" was used in a completion. A perspective on gender role stereotypes created an assumption that anyone using "he" in response to a good boss was discriminating against women's capability to be good bosses. Consequently, it was assumed that someone using "he" instead of "she" might fit into an earlier developmental stage. This bias was inappropriate for the scoring process. Fortunately, this observation was made early in the scoring process, and that awareness helped mitigate bias when scoring responses containing this pronoun.

The framework developers also caution against questioning the truthfulness of a response. A fundamental ground rule for scoring is that deep-level inferences about the person's true meaning behind a response are inappropriate. During the early stages of the scoring process, a significant amount of time was spent focusing on an item response that did not exactly match a category title, attempting to decipher what the person was truly trying to say or what they really meant by their answer. For example, process notes indicate questioning whether responders who gave the answers "is a leader" and "is a teacher" were referring to the same meaning, or whether "leader" and "teacher" meant "responsible" to these responders, and should therefore be rated higher at Stage 3/4 under the category "-- is responsible". This type of rumination not only consumed a considerable amount of time but was also unnecessary, as raters are not supposed to attempt to figure out the hidden meaning behind a response.

Attempting to understand the factual nature of a response also raises the concern that a given sentence stem completion might merely reflect a transitory "state" of the respondent, rather than an enduring personality trait. This is a valid apprehension, as the potential to access a non-ordinary, transpersonal realm is always available, even though the state itself is transitory and fleeting. It is possible that a person has experienced a state that influences how they respond to a sentence stem.

For instance, an individual might have experienced a peak moment that corresponds to one of the sentence stems in such a way that the provided response scores at one of the later stages. However, another sentence stem, such as "A good boss—", might provoke a memory of an unpleasant experience of abuse by a previous boss, causing the person to respond in a manner resembling an Impulsive or Opportunist sentence completion. These two responses would capture transitory states experienced by the respondent. The state can also be experienced in real-time while the respondent is completing the test. For example, a person might have just come from work to a counseling session and is taking the test, but just before leaving work, they had an argument with their boss. This event could completely alter their frame of reference regarding what a good boss would be, compared to the frame of reference they held the day before.

The framework developers explain that the factual status of any response cannot be known, indicating the difficulty of determining whether a respondent's completion reflects a state or a trait. Traits, unlike states, are stable positions or preferred stances from which an individual consistently processes experiences. The measurement of this developmental framework is based on the notion that an individual will function at some core level. When rating a total protocol, the intent is to estimate that core level of functioning, or center of gravity. While some sentence stem completions may capture transitory states, the use of multiple sentence stems, combined with a sophisticated scoring system, appears to buffer out potential outlier effects and provides a depiction of the individual's stable continuum of functioning.

Other indications of bias based on response characteristics include allowing the number of words used (response length) or the respondent's age to predict the developmental level. At first glance, it might seem that the number of words used and the assigned stage are positively correlated. However, this relationship may stem from the possibility that it often takes more words to describe more complex thoughts and relationships. This highlights why proper use of the scoring manual is crucial, as it provides rules for raters to follow to avoid making such assumptions. The age of the respondent was removed from the sorted responses, so it did not appear to influence the scoring of the completions.

Finally, regarding sources of bias arising from intrinsic aspects of the response, an "end of the rainbow" effect may occur when scoring responses at later stages. In this scenario, a rater might incorrectly assume that sentence completions conveying a sense of peace, contentment, or self-satisfaction should be scored at a later stage, as development through a sequence of stages is sometimes viewed as an increasing capacity to solve problems. However, within this developmental framework, the sequence seems more reflective of coping with increasingly profound problems. Therefore, responses at later stages may be fraught with questioning, feelings of torment, and tension arising from the awareness of significant, global, and universal conflicts that are not easily resolved.

There also appeared to be sources of bias resulting from the rater's own qualities, such as personal problems, salient interests, and the rater's own developmental level. As previously described, a salient issue that originated bias was the perspective on gender role stereotypes. Another source of bias stemmed from personal experience, specifically the rater's personal relationships with bosses. During parts of the scoring process, it became apparent that comparisons were being made between the responses in the dataset and personal experiences with "good" and "bad" bosses. An individual's perspective on what defines "A good boss—" seems to be shaped by their own concept of a good boss, based on unique life experiences. It is a challenging task to avoid using those experiences as a frame of reference during the scoring process.

Finally, it is important to consider the extent to which the rater's own developmental level might have enhanced or hindered the scoring process. When categorizing responses in the later developmental stages, it became increasingly difficult to distinguish whether a response was more appropriate for one stage or the next. It was insightful to observe the frame of reference from which the scoring was conducted. The distinction between the conventional Stage 4 and the postconventional Stage 4/5 was very evident. However, distinguishing between the later stages was a more difficult task, requiring responses to be shifted back and forth between stages numerous times. This difficulty might indicate that the rater's developmental level resides within the range of those specific stages. It would have been interesting to measure the rater's developmental level prior to this experience, identify ways to control for limitations or bias associated with their frame of reference, and determine if their developmental stage changed throughout the process by measuring it again at the project's conclusion.

It is understandable that the framework developers point out that the rater's developmental level may affect the rating process, especially for later-level protocols. However, they contend that through experience (such as scoring and understanding the underlying theory) and the use of a self-training program, the range of scoring effectiveness can be extended to later stages. This appeared to be the case in this project; when difficulties arose in distinguishing between later developmental stages, resources providing useful insight were consulted. Scoring sentence stem responses and placing each into a category relies on the rater's discretion, and different raters may score the same response in a different category or even at a different stage. With later-stage responses, there is even more room for interpretation, which is considered a natural part of the scoring process, as scoring inherently involves interpreting from one's own frame of reference.

### Personal Growth

This project, as a whole, promoted personal growth and development through humility and gentle self-inquiry. Experiencing and overcoming roadblocks was necessary, and these barriers surfaced at various times, not just when working with later-stage responses. These included instances where it was necessary to relinquish the idea of understanding every aspect of the theory to make the "correct" choice regarding item placement. Working from a framework that demanded perfection in sorting the items proved to be a barrier to the overall process.

Once the humility of the task was embraced, it became possible to appreciate the glimpse into the responders' worlds and understand that it is unrealistic to expect one "right" score for every response (especially for later-stage responses). Another individual might make a different judgment on the scoring of these items, operating from their own frame of reference. The intricate scoring process constructed in the initial scoring manual helps correct for some of these potential differences in scoring and has resulted in high interrater reliability. Once the personal stake in correctly scoring all items was withdrawn and trust was placed back into the scoring process, the project began to progress again.

A gentle self-inquiry was also interwoven throughout the project. An awareness developed that the rater's position within their own frame of reference, their perspective-taking, and the context of their lifetime experiences impacted the construction of this scoring manual. Certain assumptions were made based on salient life issues, as well as the impact of past, current, and hoped-for future relationships with "bosses" on the interpretation of responses to "A good boss—". Meaning is a constructed concept; it is an interpretation brought to an experience from the observer's position. The position as a witness to the responses shared for this stem is a unique perspective, and interpretation was brought forth from personal experience. This will differ for each individual undertaking such a task. While deep inferences about the meaning behind responses are inappropriate, and each rater approaches the task from a different frame of reference, a judgment must still be made to assign a score to each response item. It seems crucial for raters to be aware of how their unique experiences may influence those assignments.

### Limitations and Future Directions

To increase the reliability of the scoring process and minimize bias introduced from personal experience, certain steps could be taken to strengthen the construction of a scoring manual. Although the final dataset included 836 responses for the sentence stem "A good boss—", collecting and adding more responses would strengthen the manual's reliability. Interrater reliability has been shown to be excellent, partly because the scoring manuals are empirically derived from data collected from the sentence stems. Having more responses allows emerging patterns and themes to develop more fully. Even with the current dataset, new categories seemed to emerge as similar items clustered together with responses from earlier datasets. Therefore, the size of the dataset may be a limitation, as a larger number of responses would help strengthen the resulting manual.

Another limitation was the rater's initial ability to rate the items. The framework developers stress the importance of the self-training process for new raters, providing training exercises in the scoring manual. Completing these self-training exercises before starting the data sort section could have saved considerable time. While knowledge of the underlying theory provided a useful foundation, the experience of sorting through hundreds of responses seemed critical for building scoring capability. Completing the self-training prior to the start of the project could have enhanced the process.

Determining the interrater reliability of this scoring manual would be an invaluable tool to enhance its effectiveness. This microvalidation step could involve a new set of response items to the stem and several different raters. Each rater could use the draft scoring manual and compare scores to generate a measure of interrater reliability. It would also be useful for the raters to track their reasons for assigning a specific score and their certainty in that decision. If a newly established category does not hold up during the microvalidation process (e.g., it is too narrow, confusing, or better subsumed by another category), this could indicate that the category is not useful and should be omitted or revised. If low certainty causes a rating discrepancy, the group of raters could discuss a new rating. Scoring insights, categories, and cross-references that work well for the group could be retained, while those not confirmed by the new data could be altered or omitted.

### Conclusion

The reliability of the empirically derived scoring manuals is a major strength of this measurement tool, with decades of research reporting consistently high interrater reliability. It is necessary to continue generating reliable scoring manuals for each sentence stem, including new additions like "A good boss—". This project enhanced the psychometric properties of the measurement tool by utilizing data to construct a scoring manual for this stem, which future raters can use. Previous attempts had been made to create a scoring manual for "A good boss—", and this project helped validate those existing categories while adding five new possible categories. Studying the distribution of responses for this stem also internally validated the scoring device and the construct of measurement.

Regarding the study of this developmental framework and its benefit to the counseling field, sorting responses into different stages and categorizing within each stage tests the original concept and the reliability of the scoring manuals. The responses included in this scoring manual can be utilized in future research to further define and understand manifestations of development in sentence stem completions, particularly in the ongoing study of later developmental stages. Even the relatively few responses added to the scoring manual for the later stages will contribute to the current scarcity of postautonomous response data for sentence stem completions. This experience also positively influenced personal and professional growth, increasing awareness of how one's developmental stage can influence their perspective on how others translate life from their own centers of gravity.

This project has hopefully enhanced the foundation for establishing a scoring manual for the sentence stem "A good boss—", strengthened the psychometric properties of the measurement tool, increased the data available for study, and promoted the use of this developmental theory as a sound construct with a valid and reliable measurement tool.
